# Epitome

## CHAPTER ONE Prologue

## Prologue

I sat on the cliff edge where the eastern gun redoubt had been. It was all in shambles now and debris cluttered the area. But I was not interested in that at all. I was more interested in the ravaged land in the valley mouth.

Almost half a year had passed since the destruction of the Groundlands, and the whole place had changed tremendously. The shattered ruins were unchanged and the bodies of the dead Skylanders lay where they had fallen. The corpses of carrion covered the area. They had been unable to resist the feast before them but had all succumbed to the extremely elevated and caustic magic field.

Even as I watched Wild Magic manifested. Most of the events were simple elemental manifestations but I saw a few smaller spell-beasts form before their short lives ran out. Momentarily there was a hulking beast, bipedal and corpulent with a single eye and a massive horn. It had only taken a single massive step before bursting apart in a cloud of multi-hued butterflies. The nexus of converging ley-lines filled the valley with magical power. I had been able to sense the magical winds for days before I arrived. Nothing that lived could exist down there. Except the savages from the north. They had moved into the ruins, hundreds of them. Far fewer than had participated in that massive attack on the Groundlands, but far too many for me to be able to clear out the ruins myself. I spotted several areas where the savages had nested and I counted at least three hundred eggs basking in the magical power. I knew that I had already been spotted for some of them shaded their eyes and kept a watchful gaze in my direction. I did not fear them. It would take them minutes to climb the switchback to my location and I could fly away with my Angel armour in a moments notice and leave them far behind.

“This is quite a pickle,” mother said from beside me.

“Yeah,” I agreed without looking at her.

“Do you have a plan to drive them off?”

I was silent for a while before I answered. “No. Do you?”

I could feel her shrug, her shoulder rubbing up against mine. “Kill them?”

“Thanks. That’s really helpful,” I answered sarcastically.

“I know. But I’m not the clever one that comes up with all the elaborate schemes. I just kill things,” mother replied. “Your job is to come up with a plan, daughter. Do your thing. Build some stuff that will help us.”

“Build some stuff?” I echoed, finally turning my head to look at mother with an arched eyebrow. “Build some stuff,” I murmured again.

She was right. The magical field was dense enough even up here that I could build almost anything I wanted.

The switchback would be ease itself to defend.

The savages had shown time and time again that they did not grasp tactics nor strategy and that they could be goaded into a full frontal attack, even against superior entrenched positions.

“Mother. You’re a genius,” I exclaimed and jumped to my feet, plans already circling in my mind.

I would have this place sorted out in no time.

## CHAPTER TWO Barengar

I crouched lower into the bushes I was hiding among, breath caught in my lungs. Had they spotted me? I didn’t think so but that one female had been looking straight at me. With some effort I relaxed the grip around the haft of my axe. The deoch draoidheil that I had been stalking were dangerous foes. I had fought them before, of course, as part of my training but this was the first time that I was out here without a war party of my fellows as support. I was alone. The final examination of my courses in mundane combat, to bring back the head of the only known foe that would completely negate my magic.

But my intention was not to only graduate so that I could take courses in more advanced magic. My eyes were on the grand prize.

The headmistress had announced that whoever brought back a live specimen would compete for a post as assistant to the founder of the college.

Ever since she had stepped back as the leader of the school in order to focus on her research Lady Marissa had barely been seen. Only the oldest students remembered seeing her in the flesh.

I wanted that job. More than almost anything in this world. I wanted to learn, and Lady Marissa was arguably the most skilled Mage in the world.

Some would argue that Kendall the Builder still surpassed her, and he was undoubtedly more skilled in Construction Magic. But few could rival the lady in sheer versatility.

Every so often she delivered new spells to the college to be taught to the students. Spells that had never before been seen. How she could create them so quickly, and so thoroughly, was a source of much speculation among my peers. That was the secret that I wished to learn.

And I would. As soon as I managed to incapacitate a deoch draoidheil and bring it back to the south. I risked raising my head to gaze into the small congregation of the creatures.

They were ugly bastards. Even the females was as tall as I was and while their hips were narrower than my own their shoulders was broader. Their skin was gray, their heads crowned in a thatch of bony spines that shuddered with a life of their own. Their chins were narrow beneath maws filled with fangs. Their arms were elongated and packed with sinewy muscles. I’d been punched by one of their kind before and I knew that they were much stronger than they looked.

But the worst part of them were their eyes.

Violet eyes that shone with an inner light.

There where three of them in the camp, child’s play if I had been allowed firearms.

But I had only my axe and whatever enchantments I could infuse it with. Most enchantments were useless against the deoch draoidheil anyway since they would just leech the power straight out of it.

The deoch draoidheil squatted around a crude clay bowl of water, peering intently into it. I could not even begin to fathom their reasons. As far as I knew they didn’t need to drink water or eat anything at all. Only magic sustained them. Was there anything else nearby that I could use as a weapon? There where a somewhat large rock that I might be able to lift telekinetically but at this distance I doubted that I could bring enough force to bear to actually harm any of the three. But I wouldn’t have to actually hurt any of them. Perhaps a distraction would be enough to tip the outcome in my favour. With the element of surprise I might just pull this off.

I reached out with my magic to grab a nearby bush and after taking a steadying breath I rustled it.

The deoch draoidheil grunted and their baleful gaze fell on the shrubbery. The large male grunted and jerked his head and the smaller male grumbled but stood up.

He tromped over to the bush and peered into it, reaching inside with his hand to push the thick leaves aside.

I held my breath. He was just a step away from me. My palms were suddenly sweaty and I acted before I could lose my nerve.

I sucked in a deep breath before I rose to my full height and dashed closer. My axe sang through the air and embedded itself into the side of the head of the male with a crunch.

The axe head lodged in his skull and my arms were pulled down by the weight of the corpse. The other two deoch draoidheil growled and charged me, undoubtedly hoping to catch me with my weapon lodged and useless.

But now that the creature was dead his magic draining power was gone and I was free to activate one of the enchantments on the axe. Power coursed down my arm, through the crystal haft of my axe and into the head. The flesh and bone pressing into the sides of my axe sizzled and smoked a moment before the entire head of the corpse erupted in a shower of gore. My axe was suddenly loose and I spun and swung in a smooth movement.

The axe spun horizontally through the air and the large male only had time to widen his eyes in surprise before my axe blade bit into the bridge of his nose and bisecting both of his shining eyes. With a crack of broken bone he fell and slid across the grass. The female leaped his corpse with a growl and kept coming.

I ripped my knife from my belt and growled right back at her.

“Come one then, bitch!” I taunted her even though I knew that she would not understand me.

My surprise attack had worked beyond my expectation and the playing field was more even.

Even though the female was small compared to the males she was still as tall as I was with longer reach. And she would be stronger than me as well.

I was suddenly less confident with my knife than I had been.

But I was past the point of no return.

The female approached rapidly and she leaned into her charge, spreading her arms to gather me into a vicious embrace.

The deoch draoidheil had learned over the years that they were much stronger than us but that we were deadly at a distance. They always sought to close the distance so that they could overpower us.

She slammed into me and wrapped her long arms around my shoulders and twisted to wrest me onto the ground where she could pummel me to death with her fists.

But my Blood Magic enhanced limbs strained to absorb her momentum and slowly I turned that tables on her.

“Not today, bitch!” I growled into her ear before I slammed her to the ground before dropping to a knee and stabbed her in her thigh. I twisted the knife and sawed it to the left to mangle as much of the muscle as I could before I danced back and out of her reach.

She tried to stand but her leg would not hold her weight any more and she collapsed again. I quickly backpedaled and wrested my axe out of the larger male’s head.

I turned to face the struggling female with a cocky grin on my face. Now I just had to make her compliant so that I could bring her back home alive.

Hopefully this one would not die of traumatic shock like the previous ones had.

I walked closer and she swiped her claws at me in defiance. I held the axe close to the head for better control and I cut the back of her hand open.

She snatched her hand back with a hiss, cradling it to her shapeless breasts. I saw the opportunity, and I took it.

I lashed out with a powerful swing and it bit into her upper arm about halfway from her shoulder. I could feel the bone shatter through the haft of my axe but I didn’t manage to completely sever the appendage.

She started scrambling away from me but her useless arm and wounded leg made her clumsy and I easily kept pace.

I swung again and bit deep into her healthy leg, snapping her femur.

Now she was completely helpless. I leaned closer and she almost managed to take my throat out with a swipe of her remaining arm. I flinched back and bit out a curse before I cleaved her shoulder. As luck would have it my axe slid slid into her deltoid and managed to get into her shoulder socket, shearing the limb clean off.

With methodical strikes I literally disarmed her and removed her legs. She was bleeding profusely, I had to stop that.

I jogged away from her and activated the second enchantment on my axe. Within moments the crystal had heated enough to give of a haze. I ran back to the female and felt her drink the magic right out of the enchantment. But the axe head was still hot enough to cauterize the stump of her shoulder. I gritted my teeth against the wrenching howl she let out and I maintained contact until I was sure that the wound was more or less sealed. I ran back away and reheated the axe before I cauterized her other arm. Twice more and she was barely bleeding any longer. I was a bit surprised that she still lived. The others had died of shock when I severed their limbs. Now there were only one part of her that was a threat to me.

“Sorry about this,” I apologised before I flipped the axe and used the flat side of the head to smash her jaw from her body.

Her tongue lolled grotesquely out of the hole that her throat had become and her eyes were wide and panicked.

But I was not done yet. I knelt by her writhing form and with gentle but firm taps of my axe I struck the fangs from her upper jaw until only bleeding gums remained. Now she really was not a threat any longer.

With a rope I pulled from my satchel I fashioned a sort of sling that I looped around her waist and shoulders before I heaved her onto my back like the most morbid backpack that had ever existed. It was much easier to carry her like this than dragging her across the ground. With a deep sigh I set off on my journey home.

###

I walked through the northern walls of the college with my head held high, prize mewling on my back. The settlement was large by local standards. By global standards it was barely a small city.

Two thousand and change students, a couple of hundred teachers and all of the support staff required to run the school made the population total at less than ten thousand. Most of the internal area was packed with multi-story buildings meant for housing while the manufactorium area lay to the west. The keep that was the collage, and my home, lay to the east of the housing area, and that is where I went. It was not a long walk and I had long legs and a brisk walk. But my swift passing was not quick enough for people to not notice what was on my back and I received more than one horrified glare. I didn’t let the expressions of dismay and disgust deter me. I knew what I wanted, and I would do what I had to do to achieve it.

Of course, if my prey had not been a mere beast then I wouldn’t have maimed and tortured it so. Probably.

I entered the keep proper and approached the drop off point. It was a simple crystal workbench in front of a cluster of cages with thick crystal bars. To my dismay I saw several savages in them, glaring and growling at anything that moved.

My classmates had managed to bring back some specimens intact. That did not bode well for me.

The surly old man that manned the desk today gave me an indifferent glance until I unslung my living backpack and deposited it onto the table. He cursed and recoiled at he sight of the maimed and agonized creature.

He glared at accusingly but I just shrugged in response.

“Orders where to bring them back alive. Not intact.”

The man grunted before he dutifully jotted down my name and a short description of the state of the creature in a ledger before he grunted again and jerked his head in the direction of an empty cage.

I grunted in response and heaved the creature into the cage before I slammed it shut. The crystal lock fused into a single piece with a flash of magic and just like that my mission was complete.

Now I just had to wait and see if the teachers agreed with my interpretation of our orders.

###

The wide crystal disk that was slowly raising me through the tower rocked with a screech and I threw my arms out for balance. But I did not fall to my death and the lift kept rising. My heart slowly returned to a more normal rate, slightly elevated due to my excitement.

Three nerve-wracking days I had spent before I had received word that I was to meet the Lady Marissa.

The disk stopped and the indigo crystal doors slid to the sides with a whisper. Warm air blasted me in the face and I drew a shuddering breath in the sweltering heat.

Why would she keep her apartments this warm?

I stepped into the chamber beyond and looked around. I could not see much. Racks and shelves of equipment filled most of my view and I felt a powerful magical activity somewhere in this room. My magesense was not acute enough to make out any details farther than a few steps from me so I had no idea what was going on.

The sound of crystal on crystal reached my ears and I carefully threaded my way through the cluttered mess. I looked around a row of shelves and suddenly understood the heat.

This was not the lady’s apartments. This was her workshop. Her forge.

“Hello?” I called out when I could not spot the lady herself. I had been told that she would be here.

“Over here,” a feminine voice answered to my right and I turned in that direction.

That was the first time I saw the lady in the flesh.

She had her back to me and she was working on something that was obscured by her body.

She was short and petite with a long mane of soft brown hair streaked through with red and violet. It fell like a waterfall down her back to curl around her waist, right above where her waist flared into shapely hips.

With a grunt Marissa used a crystal wrench to tighten a large bolt on the contraption that she was working before she turned around to face me.

Whatever I had expected, it was not this. Her face was youthful and I would not have believed that she was much older than I even though I knew that she was in her mid fifties.

One of her almond eyes, the right one, was a lovely brown colour that almost perfectly matched the colour of her hair while the other was entirely made out of blue crystal that flickered with an inner light. The hair above her left ear had been shaved off and a row of crystals where embedded in her skin as if they had been sunk into her skull and her ear was scarred as if it had been burned. My eyes traveled down as I took her in. Her nose was small and cute and placed above full red lips and a pointed chin that brought a pleasant taper to her jaw. Her neck and shoulders where slender, and the same kind of scarring that marred her ear were also visible on the left side of her body and the three lower fingers of her left hand had been replaced by skeletal crystal prosthetics. I couldn’t tell if her whole side was scarred as well since her blouse had long sleeves. At least the hint of cleavage that tried valiantly to escape the constraints of the cloth was pale and creamy and the skin was utterly flawless.

Lady Marissa saw my eyes dip to her hand and she held it up and flexed her fingers, flesh and crystal both.

“An experiment that went awry,” she grimaced. “I have managed to repair most of the damage, but not all.”

I suddenly realized that I had been staring and I quickly gathered my wits and fell to my knee.

“Lady Marissa,” I began but she cut me off.

“Marissa will do. I’m no lady.” With a few graceful steps she flowed past me. “Tell me why I chose you to be my assistant.” She ordered.

I swiveled my head to keep my eyes on her. “Because I brought back a live sample, as instructed,” I responded confidently.

Marissa searched through a shelf in silence for a few moments before she grabbed something and turned around.

“As did several of your classmates. In better condition, I might add. Try again. Tell me why I selected you.”

She walked past me again and leaned over the thing she had been working on.

I forced my eyes to not stare at her ass, eminently showcased in her tight trousers. She was old enough to be my mother, and then some, damn it!

Though she did not look her age. She must truly have mastered the art of Blood Magic in order to keep her youth.

“I displayed some other quality that you sought in the manner with which I fulfilled my task,” I said hesitantly and Marissa snorted.

She uncoiled the length of crystal wire and pressed it into the join of the nut she had tightened. With a surge of magic the coil ignited as she welded the nut in position.

“It would behoove you to not be so vague in your answers,” she commented wearily.

“Then I do not know, my lady.”

“Better. There is no shame in admitting ignorance as long as you genuinely strive to remedy it.”

Marissa tossed the coil to the side and it floated back to the shelf on its own and she turned around to face me once more.

She rolled her eyes. “Get up off the floor. Fetch me that pipe over there.”

I hastened to bring her the pipe she had indicated.

She drew her hand along the length of the pipe and runes fizzled into existence along its surface.

“Inscribe those for me.”

“Right away, ma’am.”

I sat cross legged on the floor and drew my chisel from my belt. With a tap of my finger and a flex of my mind the enchantments on the crystal rod flared into life and a tiny jet of magic erupted from the tip. I started tracing the rune template she had marked for me while she knelt on the other side of the unfinished machine and reached inside the crystal guts.

“Tell me why I chose you to be my assistant,” she urged me again.

“Because of my grades?”

“No. While you are proficient in most of your classes there are several students that excel further. Try again.”

I thought about it. Really thought about it. What separated me from my classmates?

What had I done that put me apart from the others?

The most significant and recent act of mine had been completing the challenge of bringing back one of the savages alive, though there were several of the others that had done the same.

But only I had brought back one that was maimed.

Could that be it? I alone had brutally savaged my prey and brought it back.

“I was the only one that did not just bring back the deoch draoidheil intact. I took measure to reduce the danger.”

Marissa looked up at me across the machinery she was working on and smirked, her crystal eye flaring brightly for a moment.

“That is correct. You did not make assumptions about your orders. You were told to bring one back alive, not intact, and that is what you did. And you displayed ingenuity in your methods as well as determination not only bordering on ruthlessness but went way beyond that.

You fulfilled your orders in a creative way without regard for the outcome and with near blind loyalty. That is what I seek in an assistant.”

“I am honored by your words,” I responded humbly.

Marissa scoffed and returned her attention to the machinery. “Humility does not suit you, Barengar. Be honest and straightforward with me and I will return the gesture.”

I bowed my head and returned to tracing the runes on the pipe in my lap. “As you wish.”

We worked in silence until I finished the runes and the enchantments solidified, sucking in a wave of magical power and erupting into blazing light that softly dimmed to a dull glow.

Marissa had raised her head when she felt the surge of magic and she gave the pipe a quick glance. She gestured with her hand and a cluster of crystals drifted over to me. I plucked them from the air with my own magic and had one of them slap into my hand. I raised to my eyes and squinted at it.

It was not just a crystal as I had first assumed. One end of the oblong and faceted crystal was covered in a nest of gold and silver wire that fused together into a sort of bulb.

“Be careful with those,” Marissa cautioned me as she straightened up and put her fists into her lower back. She arched her back with a grunt and a series of audible pops before she walked around the machine to sit down across from me. Her knees almost touched mine. “Your first lesson,” she smirked and put a delicate finger on the bulb in my hand. “While materialized magic is a fine substrate for enchantments, and a power storage medium second to none, it is not always the best substrate.”

“But it is the only loss-less substrate,” I quoted my enchantments teacher.

Marissa nodded sideways and waved her hand. “While true, that is not quite what I said. If the substrate is not materialized magic, even when enchanted correctly, will leak magic and therefore degrade the spell. But the substrate may in some cases alter the effects of the enchantment. Effects that can not be achieved with any other substrate.”

She stroked the bulb up and down with the tip of her fingers while she spoke, almost suggestively.

I looked down at the thin gold and silver wires. “These wires are enchanted?”

Marissa nodded smugly.

I lifted the little contraption and held it close to my eyes and really strained to see the runes.

Marissa laughed. “Don’t bother. You will not be able to see them with your eyes. Try with your magesense but I doubt that you will have the acuity to see them. Not even I can. I doubt even mother could.”

Her last comment made me jerk physically. I had forgotten that Marissa was the adopted daughter of the legendary Bás Marbhadair and that she had been trained by old Kendall the Builder. She truly was a Mage of legendary lineage.

“Then how could you possibly have enchanted it if you can’t even sense the runes?” I asked dubiously.

“Magic,” she giggled. Even after her laughter subsided she kept smirking at my sour expression. “Seriously, by magic. I used a meta-magic spell that cast a smaller version of itself, and that one in turn and so on until one that was fine enough was cast. Then that spell cast an enchantment spell that enchants the wire,” she explained.

My mind spun when it tried to follow. “That must have been many layers of spell work,” I murmured, still attempting to wrap my mind around it.

“Thousands of layers,” Marissa agreed. “I will show you that spell at some point, but for now I need you to mount these bulbs within the pipe. Let me show you.”

She snatched the pipe out of my lap, the tips of her fingers brushing against my inner thigh, before she quickly slotted a bulb that she snatched out of the air into the pipe. She put it with the wire tracery facing the center of the pipe and the thing slid into it like a bullet into the breech. It fell until it reached the first ring of runes that I had inscribed where the crystal flared and was charged with power stored within the enchantment. The bulb stopped falling and remained suspended within the ring of magic. Marissa shook the pipe to show me that the bulb was held in place. She repeated the process with the other end before she casually tossed the pipe at my chest.

I grabbed it out of the air with magic and snatched it with my hand for inspection.

“Alright, now what?” I asked when she remained silent.

Her evil grin almost made me groan. “Now you finish the rest.”

“The rest?”

Marissa pointed over my shoulder where a stack of what had to be at least a hundred of the pipes lay in a neat pyramid.

“All of them?” I sighed.

“All of them,” Marissa echoed maliciously. “Stack the finished ones over there,” she continued and pointed at an empty area of the workshop. “Let me know when you finish,” she said cheerfully as she walked deeper into her workshop and vanished from view. A few moments later I felt a huge surge of magical power and heard the crystal clanking of something being assembled.

I looked at the huge stack of pipes and sighed. This was going to take a long time, and it was going to be supremely boring.

## CHAPTER THREE Marissa

I left my new apprentice behind and I smirked when I heard his deep sigh. I approached my latest project that was an extremely quick mode of transportation. If my calculations were correct, and they were backed by information from a pre-apocalypse tome, then it should be able to travel to any place on earth within ninety minutes with an error margin of ten or twenty minutes. I had not quite gotten it to work yet and practical testing would be supremely dangerous to perform and incredibly time consuming to verify. There were also two major issues with the method of transportation. It required ridiculously vast amounts of magical power in ratio to the payload and as far as I knew there where no method to return to your point of origin. It was, essentially, a one way trip. A dangerous one. One that included a quick stint in space. I shuddered at the thought of that relentless void that I had read about in those ancient tomes but I could not help but feel a twinge of excitement in my chest. The tome had spoken of a time when humanity had routinely sent equipment, and people, into orbit for prolonged use. From what I had gathered it had been supremely useful. They had used something they called a ‘GPS’ for various purposes, though I had not yet learned what those purposes where. I had only learned that it was some sort of global ephemeral field projected by ‘satellites’ that were placed in ‘geosynchronous’ orbits to cover the entire surface of the world. Now, when I say that I had read this in a ‘tome’ I am not entirely truthful. I had in my travels encountered an underground bunker of truly ancient origins. It had been filled with strange box-like machines filled with intricately shaped metal and what I believed to be some sort of sand-based substrate. It was only by carefully inspecting the written labels on everything that I figured out that the things fed on electricity and only an off-hand comment that Kendall had made when I was child that I connected the term ‘electricity’ with lightning. From there it had been a few misshaps when I tried to learn how to use lightning to power the devices. I had been supremely surprised when the machines sparked into life when I found the correct ratio of lightning power and flow and the dull glass contraptions had lit up. It had taken me years to figure out how the strange machines worked, but the wealth of information they contained… It had been worth it. Very much so. I had learned about metals and their properties, about electricity and how it worked. I had learned about space and the various non-magical designs that those ingenious people had crafted to reach the heavens. But the sheer size of industry was on a level that not even the Cities could achieve. The humanity of old had progressed centuries ahead of the steam power that were currently used by those not skilled in magic, though my school had advanced their technology in a rapid revolution the last few decades. Magic and machine had merged into something that was more powerful than the sum of its parts. And I had shamelessly used that in an attempt to reverse engineer the technology of ancient mankind. 

My goal was simple. It was the same that the Mages of old had sought to remedy when they unleashed magic upon the world.

Freedom.

What constrained the freedom of every human?

Resources. Food. Building materials for shelter. Anything and everything.

As long as there were scarcity of goods, then there would be suffering.

If there were no scarcity, then no one would need to work.

If everything and anything was just available whenever you needed or wanted it…

That was true freedom.

I had learned from the ancient machines that many attempts had been made to achieve it. A form of governance called ‘communism’ had been attempted where the government owned everything and in theory supplied everything to everyone.

That had ended in a death toll that was orders of magnitude greater than the Fall of the Skylands. It made my own personal and prodigious body count to be a mere rounding error in comparison.

The fundamental flaw in the concept of communism was that it required everyone, and I do mean everyone, in the country to be voluntarily aligned with the ideal of sharing everything and working for the common good even if you were not rewarded in proportion to your investment. Humans simply does not work like that. And so the communists had been forced to be tyrannical, murdering countless dissidents that would not comply.

Another system had been tried that they called ‘capitalism’, which had a much more favourable outcome for the majority of the people involved in the experiment.

But it had eventually failed due to something the ancient people called ‘corporatism’ which I had not yet discovered the meaning of.

But the base problem still remained: scarcity.

Even if the production of goods where completely automated by ancient tech, the world only contained so many minerals and only had so much arable land. Eventually it would run out.

But magic would have been the solution of that. Materialized magic can replace almost any material found on Earth, and the fungus that the Skylands grew could be efficiently farmed to feed an incredible amount of people.

The seemingly endless stream of magic produced by… Something, could in theory achieve that which has plagued humanity since times immemorial. Scarcity.

But the Mages of old had had no knowledge of Wild Magic, and so in their quest for liberation they ushered in the doom of their society.

But I knew. And with that knowledge, I could to better.

I was not arrogant enough to believe that I alone could avoid making the same mistake, to accidentally avoid the dangers of the unknown unknowns.

But I knew that I could stand on the backs of giants, another quote I had found in the records contained within those ancient machines, and build a future that was better than my present.

And if that future was flawed, then someone in the future could stand on my shoulders to make the world a better place.

As mother had told me so long ago, intent does not matter. Only outcome counts. And only the future may judge if the outcome was good or bad.

My goal was simple. I wanted to achieve what the Mages of old had intended. A powerful magical field that encompassed the world so that anyone could build whatever they wished with the magic that was available.

But the old Mages had made a critical mistake. They had merely ripped open the ley line crossings and let the magic diffuse across the world without regard of how it dispersed.

The general magic field had eventually grown so dense that Wild Magic started to manifest, and you all knew how that ended up.

I was proposing a more controlled approach, a more even distribution with safeguards that would not let the field grow dense enough to manifest Wild Magic.

A distribution and storage program that spanned the world by using satellites in orbit to store excess magic away from any place where the discharge of accidents would not harm anyone.

If I could achieve that, that would be wonderful.

But it would also bring dangers of its own.

With unlimited power that people could use at their will… There would always be people that should not have access to such power, who would use it to harm others or restrict their freedom for their own personal reasons.

Unlimited power required some sort of safeguard, and entity that would enforce the rights of the people.

Power corrupts, and absolute power corrupts absolutely. Another nugget of wisdom I found in the ancient machines.

Which was why I could not let the power to enforce freedom to lie in the hands of humanity. Something else had to do that.

I had a separate project for that, a project that I hoped taking on young Barengar as my assistant would free me from the mundane tasks of my research so that I could focus on that project.

I considered my new apprentice. I had only spoken to him once, but he struck me as a clever young man. Not the brightest, nor the most skilled compared to his classmates. But this was my school and even the worst of my students was on par with the brightest and most skilled Mages of the Skylands. So Barengar would be enough for the purposes that I intended for him, without a doubt. What I was more interested in was his dogged loyalty and the creative way he had fulfilled the orders I had given to him. That single-mindedness in his strive to complete his tasks would serve me well.

I smirked when I thought about that sigh of his that I had heard. I wondered how long it would take before he figured out that I was testing him and he would build a spell to enchant and mount all of those pipes. I could have made that spell myself with little effort and have the work with the pipes completed much quicker than he would, but I wanted to gauge his skill and initiative before I introduced him to my more esoteric projects.

Physically he was not harsh on the eyes. Quite the contrary. He was tall which was something that I found attractive in a man. He was not powerfully built, his shoulders were not broad nor was he bulging with muscle. But he had chopped a deoch draoidheil into pieces so he must have some strength in those sinewy muscles of his. That did not matter much though since Blood Magic and enhancement Runes could sculpt a body almost arbitrarily. If I needed brute strength at some point that could be fixed. Even though Barengar looked nothing like Zandor with his dark hair and eyes and his skinny physique he somehow reminded me of my long lost lover. The man that I had loved and betrayed and who had most likely died when the Groundlands was destroyed. His bike had been missing after the bombardment of the Bizhjerai fleet and even decades later I still held out hope that he had made it out alive. I doubted it though since I had not heard or seen anything of him in nearly thirty-five years. I sighed wistfully at the thought of my former lover and forced myself, as I had so many times before, to focus on my current project. I channeled a massive amount of power into the materialization spell that would produce the magical fuel for my rocket engine. I believed that this design would be almost as close as I could come to the ancient engines of old that had propelled tonnes of equipment into space. But even with this engine I had a long way to go before any type of large scale operation would be viable.

I sat down by my desk and pulled a sheaf of indigo paper closer to me. It was filled with mathematical equations. Who would ever have believed how much math was required to travel into space?

I still had a ton of equations to figure out and I got to it.

As usual I lost track of time when I worked and I had no idea how much time had passed when Barengar politely cleared his throat behind me. I jumped, startled, before I put my pen down and twisted in my chair to face him.

My crystal eye homed in on the faint magical emanations that wafted from his skin. It had taken me years to learn to interpret the subtle streams of power that leaked out of every sentient Soul but I was certain that this particular pattern was amusement. At my expense for being startled.

“I couldn’t be arsed to do all of those pipes by hand, so I automated it,” my apprentice announced with a smug smirk.

“Good. I expected that you would figure that out.”

The energy around him solidified into a shell around his body. He was obviously displeased with my casual dismissal of what he undoubtedly considered to be a significant achievement.

And for his level of training, it was. But he would have to realize that I was decades ahead of him in experience and learning and that he would have to step up his game if he wanted to keep up. And the sooner he did, the better it would be for both of us.

“So… What next?” Barengar asked me when the silence drew out.

I opened my mouth to answer but before I could make a sound a crystal on my desk lit up with a bright and angry red shine and a sharp trill pierced the air.

I winced. I had ordered Headmistress Alana to not call me unless there was a crisis of some sort. I had stepped back as Headmistress so that I could focus on my research and left the school in her small but very capable hands.

I reached over and grabbed the crystal handle and put it to my ear.

“I’ve told you not to call me unless the shit’s hit the fan, Alana,” I growled into the handset.

This was another innovation that one of my earliest students had devised. The young man had come from the cities and had had some mechanical training before he decided to learn magic. During a discussion with me I had explained the ancient concept of ‘telephones’ that I had learned in that bunker and he had come back to me months later with a magical equivalent.

A weak artificial ley-line connected this handset with another in the Headmistress’ office and it used a clever spell to send shock waves down the stream of magic that was interpreted and translated into sound on the other end. It was an incredibly complex and clever spell and I had been a bit miffed that I hadn’t figured it out myself.

“I know, Marissa, but I think that this might qualify,” Alana’s clipped tones informed me.

I sighed and pinched the bridge of my nose. “What is it?”

“There is a man in the courtyard, demanding that we train his soldiers in the use of magic. He won’t take no for an answer and threatens violence if he is not allowed to speak with you. He apparently believes that you will take his side against me,” Alana said with some amusement.

“Then he is a fool, Alana.”

I sighed again before continuing. “Alright. I will be right down. Meet me at the courtyard.”

I replaced the crystal handset and looked up at Barengar.

“Have you been trained in the use of Angel armour?”

He nodded. “Of course. All of us have. Though I might be a bit rusty. It has been a while since I’ve flown.”

“We are just hopping down to the courtyard, can you manage that?”

“Yes.”

I nodded once before I stood up and stalked toward the wall. With a wave of my hand a panel opened up to reveal an Angel engine pack. Another gesture opened another panel with a prototype armour.

It was essentially the same, except it was slightly more efficient. I hadn’t tested it yet, so I would be taking that one today and Barengar would use the standard Angel armour. A design that had barely changed in thirty years.

“An old trick I learned from Uncle Kenny,” I answered my apprentice’s unasked question. “Even though we can materialize an Angel armour in a few minutes, it is much quicker to already have one on hand.”

He didn’t answer, instead he just nodded in acceptance and strapped on the armour. I did the same before I led him across the room to the opposite wall, the one facing the open air. I spread my arms and reached out with my magic and a hidden door opened to reveal a breathtaking view of the valley that had once been home to the Groundlands and that was now occupied by my school.

I leaped into the open air with Barengar on my heels, wings spreading to catch the wind and angled to bring us to the main courtyard of the keep.

###

The wind howled in my ears and messed up my hair as we glided down toward the courtyard. We had to curl around the tower from my workshop and when the side of the tower gave way I could see our target and my eyes widened slightly in surprise.

My true eye could barely make out any details, but my crystal eye… I could see the faint swirls of auras and I could already tell that there were quite a few upset people down there. I could already see Headmistress Alana emerging from the keep. She must have sprinted down from her office to arrive that quickly after having just spoken to me a moment ago. There were a thick cluster of guards, students and teachers around a smaller, though still significant, number of people that I assumed were our visitors. When Barengar and I drew closer I could make out the fact that several of the people on both sides had assumed threatening stances. I sighed. I didn’t want to deal with whatever this was. I had actual work to do.

My heels struck the crystal paving stones with a resounding clack and I flexed my thighs to absorb the shock. Silence fell heavily across both groups at my sudden arrival and the majesty of my entrance was only diminished by Barengar’s stumbling landing beside me. I rolled my eyes and ignored him. I would have to teach him to fly properly at some point.

“What is the meaning of this?” I barked.

Alana jogged up to me, her hair in slight disarray and and a faint blush on her face but her breathing was even and calm. Even though she was older than me by almost a decade she was an extremely fit woman. Not as talented in Blood Magic that she could halt the aging process to the same extent that I could she still had the faint wrinkling of a distinguished woman and her hair had thicker streaks of gray than mine. 

“This man makes unreasonable demands of the College, Lady Marissa,” Alana announced with a wave at one of the men, the one with the most opulent clothing and the most magnificent mount of the lot.

I narrowed my eyes at the man, taking in his pointed helmet, gold embroidered coat, dark eyes and dusky skin.

A man from the far south. I had rarely seen his kind this far north, mostly traders and traveling scholars.

“Unreasonable?” The man protested loudly while urging his horse forward. Presumably to loom over my diminutive stature.

“Yes, quite,” Alana agreed.

I let out a loud sigh. “What to you want?” I asked him tiredly.

“Word has reached me that this is the finest school for Mages in the known world. I have come to have my warriors trained.”

I nodded. “Very well. Have them sign their names in the ledger and we will schedule an acceptance interview and mind probe,” I responded.

That was standard procedure. My school would not train just anyone in the art of magic.

Anyone could learn to wield magic. Not everyone could learn to wield magic wisely. 

“Acceptance interview?” The man queried with affected puzzlement. “I think you misunderstand me, lady. I have come to have all of my men trained in magic.”

“I believe that you have misunderstood, sir,” I started. “This is my school. My rules apply. I will not train just anyone. Furthermore, anyone who attends this school will swear an oath, enforced by magic binding, to serve the College primarily and to never use their power to harm or restrict others unless in protection from the same for others.”

The man gave me a radiant smile. “Of course that will not apply to my men. That would make them quite useless to me. I am sure that you understand.”

I gave him a firm nod. “I understand, sir, and that is your choice. You may now leave my lands. Good day.”

I turned from him and started to walk away while exchanging a glance with Barengar.

“Don’t turn your back on me, woman!” The man barked and I stopped, my back stiffening.

I turned my head to give him an arched eyebrow over my shoulder.

“I have come to make my men into Mages, and so it shall be done!”

“You are not in a position to negotiate, sir,” I told the arrogant man sternly. “This is my school. Not yours. We are not beholden to anyone, least of all you.”

“Do you know who I am?” He asked with an arrogant toss of his head.

“No. Nor do I care to know. You are just an arrogant man that make demands of people he has no right to demand anything of. Good day.”

I turned my back at the man again.

“I will not leave until my men are enrolled in this school!” The man spat at my back.

I closed my eyes and drew a deep breath before slowly letting it out.

“Then you are trespassing, sir, and I will be forced to remove you, and your men, from our premises by force.”

“You are not allowed to harm me or mine unless we attack first, you said so yourself,” the man announced smugly. “I am the Qoch of Mukhtar, and I will not be denied!”

“You’re a cock alright,” I muttered under my breath while I turned back around.

One look at the haughty fool told me that he would not back down.

Nor would I. My school was very selective in who it chose to train. Less than one in a hundred applicants made it in, and even fewer lasted more than a season. 

Only the best were allowed to delve into the secrets of arcane lore. Many of my students would in time surpass me in both skill and power. 

That would be my legacy. To have that competence and ability in sworn service to the improving the lives of others.

I opened my mouth to give the order. The words ‘kill them’ was on my tongue, but then I reconsidered. While I had no doubt that my people would be able to kill these interlopers, there would be injuries on my side. Possibly even deaths. But at the same time, actual combat experience was invaluable.

I decided to spare them that. I would do this myself. I didn’t waste time with some grandiose statement, or honourable warning.

Instead I just activated my combat spells.

Three and a half decades ago I had believed that I had forged my body and Soul into a perfect weapon, grafting construction and destruction into an unstoppable tool.

How young and naive I had been.

Back then I had been nothing to what I was now.

I had unlocked many secrets of magic since then. Deadly secrets.

My crystal eye projected onto my field of vision several panes of information, and interfaces for controls. Scrying pulses exploded out of it in invisible waves, gathering information about my adversaries and allies alike.

With a thought I disabled the swarm of information about my allies. I did not need those at all.

Weapons were outlined on the men on horses, armour as well. Even their states of mind. Most of them were wary, and a little bit scared. They did not wish to fight us. Most of them knew that they couldn’t win.

Only the fool leading them had utter confidence in his abilities, that he would be able to cow the largest congregation of Mages in the known world with a small show of force.

His men were crowded in close, fingers on revolvers and carbines.

With a sweep of my arm I sent out a stream of tiny, pathetically weak, spells to adhere to the Souls of each of the interlopers. Those spells were mere markers. I finished the sweep with my arm raised high and I clenched my fist, summoning a spell out of one of the spell stones in my gauntlet. An artificial ley line shot out of the focusing nexus and speared down toward me. It lanced into the waiting spell template and ignited it.

The meta-magic spell lit up with a flash and spawned thousands of tiny materialization spells. Within seconds an enchanted orb solidified and fused together before the enchantment lit up. A powerful dome shield surrounded me and the interlopers and the indigo sphere split off a strand of the artificial ley line. It repeated the process on a smaller scale above my head, forming a shield to protect me. Nothing short of a Skyship would be able to harm me through a shield powered by sixteen ley line crossings.

Now all I had to do was to activate my meta-magic framework. The double row of crystals embedded in my skull along the side of my head. They were not purely for decoration.

Each of them contained a highly complex spell, a variant of the spell-inventing spell that I had created in my youth after Valia had given me key insights. But instead of finding new spells, they were loaded with a vast array of spells by using a compression spell that allowed the templates to overlap inside the crystalline matrix, and the meta-magic spells were complex enough to let them solve problems for me.

My current problems were these interlopers. The spells I had used to mark each and every one of them as an enemy was the trigger. Now I just had to feed in a little power.

Each of the fourteen crystals got to work, coordinated by the larger fifteenth crystal by my temple. Each was assigned a target using the scrying pulses from my eye, identified the most efficient way to eliminate the target and executed it by using as little of my own personal power as possible.

Ordinarily it would have been beyond my power to kill thirty men like this. I was not a particularly powerful Mage. I was skilled, but not powerful.

When a Mage uses magic, they channel their own personal power into the spell and is left drained until they can soak up enough power from the general magical field. There were several limits to this method of powering spell.

First, there were the Mages total power capacity. Once it was drained, the Mage would pass out or die depending on the severity of drainage.

Secondly, the Mage can only channel a certain amount of power at a single moment. Exceed that limit and your Soul will tear and your flesh will melt. My mother learned that lesson personally when she fought an undead liche that did not exist with the same constrains.

But spells did not exist by those rules. It required some clever application of meta-magic, but I could bypass my own physical limitations. Meta-magical spells are inherently complex and can’t be cast quickly. Not by a human.

But again, spells does not abide by the same rules. A meta-magic spell that casts meta-magic spells that in turn spawns the effects you want… That could be abused for spectacular effects.

And that is what I did. Each crystal in my skull was a complex meta-magical spell, almost intelligent one could argue. Each of them spawned the spell frameworks for whatever spell they had chosen to take out their targets.

Each framework was charged with just enough of my personal power to be able to draw power form the artificial ley line that I had created to sustain and power themselves without further input from me.

The result was an array of spells that in turn spawned the actual offensive spells by drawing power from the rapidly thickening general magic field within the barriers.

With a flash the spells discharged. I saw a man stripped of his flesh tumble from the saddle in a wet splash of gore.

Another had his head torn off by a telekinetic blast. A third was basically inverted when his spine was ripped out of his back.

Another just clutched at his chest and keeled over.

The arrogant man himself caught fire. A fire so bright that he cooked and burned into ash before he even had time to scream. His horse fell screaming to the paving stones, a large hole seared into its back to expose its ribs and spine.

Within seconds, thirty men had died and my spells winked out of existence. The satellite shield projectors fell to the ground and started to melt away. 

I sighed at the carnage and turned away.

Alana was already calling for a cleanup crew to mobilize and I left it to her very capable hands.

“Come, Barengar,” I told my apprentice who just stared at me with wide eyes. “We have work to do.”

Without waiting for him to respond I took to the air on my Angel wings and I could hear him scramble to follow suit.

I had already forgotten about the slaughtered delegation when I landed in my workshop and my thoughts were consumed with my secret project in my actual workshop.

###

Barengar spent most of our days with me while I was working on one of my later projects. He was not skilled enough to join me in my work so I assigned him various tasks that would challenge his limits so that he would learn. 

I could tell that he was getting impatient with the seemingly menial tasks I kept giving him. Most of them were repetitive but I could see his skill increase for each assignment that he fulfilled. Soon I would have to show him what he had actually learned. But that could wait for a few more days while I finished up my old project. I tightened the nut on the rivet that would keep a section of crystal plating secure and stepped back while wiping my brow with the back of my wrist. The project I was working on would be the next iteration of the Skylander armour.

It had started many decades ago as a simple crystal replacement of the ancient metal armour worn in battle long before the Apocalypse ravaged the world.

Then it had evolved into the type of armour that mother used that could be manifested from a power stone on demand. That had in turn been developed into the Valkyrie armour that would allow the wearer to jump high into the air and glide long distances.

Only a few years later the Valkyrie had turned into the Angel that allowed actual, though limited, flight.

And now this, the creation in front of me, would succeed the Angel. The Archangel.

The difference between the Valkyrie and the Angel were slight, only an extra set of engines attached to the back of the breastplate.

The difference between he Angel and the Archangel were, however, massive.

It stood more than twice my height and several times my width. In fact, it was more of a vehicle than an armour.

I had taken inspiration from one of my former students who had spent the last ten years trying to develop a flying machine that did not require lift bags like a Skyship.

He had proved that one could fly by using forward momentum to deform the air in order to provide lift.

Unlike the Skymmer that Uncle Kendall had created, this flying machine only used magic for propulsion, not lift.

I had shamelessly stolen that idea and applied it to the Archangel.

The original machine was slow, only a little faster than a Skyship and with far shorter range, and it was thrust forward by the use of a rotating crystal propeller.

It had taken him almost a full year to create a propeller that did not shatter when rotating at such velocities. 

I had done away with the entire need for a propeller. Like the Angel, the Archangel was propelled by the use of magical jets. That allowed it to reach far higher velocities with fewer moving parts, though the magical power cost was significantly higher. But that would be fine. Opening up more founts when necessary was no longer an issue, only distributing the power was. But I had plans for that as well. I just needed to make it safe.

Besides, this prototype of the Archangel, and the one next to it, was only meant as a personal transport for myself and Barengar. I would not be able to deploy them full scale without first solving the power distribution issue.

“Marissa?” Barengar’s voice echoed through the doorway.

“Yes?” I called out while I hurried through the door toward my assistant.

I found him just outside, searching for me.

“It’s the Headmistress on the tube for you,” he said with a disinterested tone.

“Oh,” I said eloquently. I had expected him to tell me that he had finished with his assignment and was ready for a new one, not that Alana had called to disturb me again. “Very well, thank you. Return to your task.”

Barengar nodded sullenly and strode purposefully into the gloom of my workshop. I watched him leave in bemusement for a few moments before I hurried over to the tube.

My assistant was not the talkative sort, something that I appreciated, but even I found his blunt demeanor strange at times.

“What is it now, Alana?” I asked the woman that I had made Headmistress.

“It’s the Council of the Cities,” Alana sighed and I could hear the weariness in her voice.

“What do those assholes want this time?” I asked and pinched the bridge of my nose.

“They’ve demanded your presence in two days time at noon for a council meeting.”

“Did they at least tell you what this was about?”

Alana sighed. “You know that they did not.”

“I know, old friend. I know. I’m inclined to ignore this summons as well. We have not signed the treaty of the Cities.”

“I suggest that you do attend this one,” Alana protested. “Even though we are not part of the Union, we are not yet fully self-sufficient. We still rely on the cities for some material and provisions and further sanctions by them could be painful.”

I closed my eyes. I really hated dealing with those pompous pricks.

“Marissa?”

“Yeah, yeah. I hear you. I’ll go to their damned meeting. But I would also like for you to divert resources to make sure that we become self-sufficient sooner, I don’t care if we have to construct additional Skyships for long distance trading or actually commit to building that railroad. I don’t like relying on the Union of Cities.”

“Got it, Marissa. I will see to it.”

“Thank you, Alana. I don’t know what I would do without you,” I told her earnestly.

“You probably would have a much larger cleanup crew. Your style of diplomacy is… Let’s just say that it is different from mine.”

“It sure is,” I chuckled.

I didn’t wait for a response from Alana. We had worked long enough together, and been friends even longer, that we didn’t need to exchange pleasantries any more. I heard the crystal echo of her replacing the lid on her speaking tube even before I had. I reached up to my choker and stroked the gem embedded above my throat, changing my clothing into travel clothes.

My violet blouse and deep purple trousers misted away only to be replaced with thicker versions, reinforced by light armour.

“Barengar!” I called out into my workshop. “I have been summoned to the Lahar to meet with the Council. You’re coming with me.”

Barengar appeared almost instantly, his dark eyes shining with excitement. “I am?”

“Yes. You are my assistant. You go where I go.”

“Very well. I will gather provisions…”

I cut him off by slashing my hand through the air. “No need. We will be taking my latest project for a test run. We will be arriving sooner than you think. Just arm and armour yourself and meet me in there,” I said and pointed back into the room that I had been working in. “I need to make some final adjustments.”

Barengar nodded once and promptly left. I hurried into my workshop and went over the two crystal machines to ensure that everything was in order.

I was ready and waiting when Barengar practically sprinted into the chamber and skidded to a halt in front of me. His eyes went wide when he took in the massive contraptions that I had painstakingly built over the course of the last few months. They were the result of years of research and centuries of accumulated work by my spell-finding meta-spells. It would not have been possible to achieve in a single lifetime if I had not been able to run thousands of the meta-spells in parallel. I looked Barengar from top to bottom and back. He had clearly been inspired by my chosen level of arms and armour when he chose his own loadout. A coarse crystal shirt draped loosely around his torso and thick padded trousers would protect his legs from most slashing attacks. Armoured pauldrons and mail sleeves protected his arms and I could just barely make out the head of his axe peeking above his shoulder. A thick belt protected his hips and lower belly and a long dagger and a revolver hung from it to the side.

“No rifle?” I asked with a cocked eyebrow.

“I’m no marksman, Marissa. I prefer to get up close with axe and magic,” he grumbled.

Not a marksman, eh? We will have to see about that.

Not only was a rifle deadly in its own right, but given the right kind of preparation and ammunition it would also increase the range of any Mage by a large margin. It was simply a tool that was too powerful to eschew.

But I would have to take care of that flaw in his training later.

“This,” I said and slapped my gloved palm onto the side of the Archangel next to me, “is the Archangel. It is the next iteration of flight armour.”

“This… This is an armour?” Barengar asked while letting his eyes roam across the sinister looking machine.

“It is,” I confirmed. “More of a vehicle than an armour, admittedly. While it can move while landed it is rather clumsy and the protection it provides to the wearer is limited. It is primarily focused on flight.”

Barengar’s eyes flared when he focused on his magesense in order to see the multitude of spells enchanted into the frame.

“Fucking shit… Did you enchant all of this yourself?”

“I sure did,” I said with a smug grin.

“That is not possible,” he protested. “There must be thousands of spells in this thing.”

“Tens of thousands,” I corrected him. “And you are correct. It is not possible for a human to do all of that. But with the correct use of meta-magic…”

His chest rumbled, almost like a growl. “You keep using that phrase, yet I have not yet seen how this could be accomplished even with meta-magic.”

“All in due time my assistant. Your skills have progressed far in the few weeks that you have spent with me. Soon I think that you will be ready to aid me with my most prized project.”

His eyes lit up with interest and he bared his canines with a hungry grin.

I had seen many men give me that exact hungry expression before, but usually their eyes were fastened on my tits instead of my eyes. This was the first time I had seen such a voracious hunger and lust in someones eyes and it was not directed at me. It was the thought of such knowledge that excited him. I returned his grin. I might have just found a kindred Soul in this young man from the east of the Bizhjerai lands.

Very suddenly, Barengar spun away from me and walked closer to the Archangel and inspected its hollow belly.

“Is this… Is this an Angel attachment in here?”

“Yes. In case the pilot must bail out of the Archangel.”

“Smart. I would not want to escape death in this machine only to fall to my death.”

“My thoughts exactly,” I smirked.

“How does it work?” He asked with a frown of concentration.

I couldn’t help but laugh at his stoic enthusiasm. “That is for another time, my eager apprentice. Today you will merely be a spectator. I will slave your Archangel to mine for they are not easy to learn how to fly.

He turned his head to squint suspiciously at me. “And you know how to fly these?”

“Of course. I built them,” I scoffed.

“But I have never seen you test these. If they make even as much noise as an Angel does, and my estimate is that it would make even more noise, I would have at least heard it around school.”

“I have not tested these particular examples, but I have tested the earlier prototypes. Though not here at the school.” I hesitated for a moment. Was I ready to reveal to him that I had a primary workshop outside of the college grounds? Probably. “I have another laboratory far from here where most of my actual work is done. Perhaps I will bring you there soon.”

He practically salivated at the thought.

“But enough about that for now. Watch me, and learn how to enter the Archangel.”

I didn’t wait to make sure that he was paying attention. I merely trusted that he would.

I reached up my arm and sent out a streamer of magic to connect to the large blue crystal embedded in the headpiece of the Archangel. It sucked the power inside and the enchantment around it flared into life. It sucked magical power out of the dense field contained inside my workshop and I could feel the connectors to the artificial ley lines outside open up to replenish what was stolen. One by one the enchantments on the Archangel activated and cycled through the diagnostic routines. The machine straightened up and the hollow belly opened. I turned my back to the machine and backed into it, placing my feet in the stirrups below the knees. When my lower back struck the brace for my waist the pressure triggered the mounting sequence and a thick belt snapped around my hips and pulled snug. Cuffs ensnared my ankles and knees before armoured plates slid around my legs to keep them safe from wind and harm. I stuck my arms into the gauntlets and was similarly secured before the shoulder brace sank down around my head to encase my torso in protective plates and to keep me firmly secured. Finally the helmet lowered to settle around my head and I was cast into pitch darkness. The machine whirred and I was raised higher into the innards of the Archangel, though I was still exposed near its front. The bulk of the machine was above and behind me and I was cradled along its stomach, partially submerged into it. The sensor spells activated and the inside of the helmet lit up with an illusion spell that perfectly replicated the outside. I took a sideways twisting step to look at Barengar and the Archangel emulated my movements. I saw to my great pleasure that his jaw had fallen toward his chest.

“You should probably stow your axe to the side, it will not fit inside the pilot nest,” I told him and my voice rang out through the exterior auditory spells, slightly distorted by the crystal emitters.

Barengar nodded slowly and stiffly drew the axe from his back and hooked it to the side of the pilot nest on his own Archangel. His awestruck expression quickly wore off and he confidently activated his own machine and mounted it just like I had.

The intent-control interface that I had installed in the machines picked up on my thoughts and our machines connected with a pair of artificial ley lines.

The ley lines simply transferred magical power between the machines, but that was not the primary function of them. Through that connection it was possible to send modulated pulses of magic that could be interpreted as instructions. The machines quickly negotiated a master-slave relationship and control of Barengar’s Archangel was transferred to mine. That was one of the reasons that I was not confident to deploy these machines on a large scale. Anyone that managed to insert themselves into the ley line and had knowledge of the method of communication between them would be able to issue commands to the vehicles and hijack them from their pilots. I had not yet figured out how to secure the communication so that an outsider would not be able to do just that. But that was a problem for another day. As it was today, only the students of my College had even the vaguest notion that it was possible to communicate through ley lines. Even I only knew about it because I had translated the knowledge of how the ancient civilizations had done the same through electricity and copper wires to a magical version. Perhaps they had solved the problem of intercepting communication? I should return to the bunker and peruse the vast store of information again.

Later. Now I had some asshats to attend to.

“Your Archangel is slaved to mine, just sit back and try to enjoy the ride,” I chuckled and my voice was transmitted into Barengar’s helmet.

When he did not answer I spoke again. “I can’t see you, so you have to verbally acknowledge, Barengar.”

“Got it, Marissa. I will sit back and try to enjoy the ride.”

“And… Try not to vomit. There are no drainage systems in your helmet.”

An audible gulp was his only response. Good enough, I guess.

With a series of mighty clanks I turned my Archangel and walked stiffly toward the wall. The intent-control interface picked up on my thoughts and a vast swathe of the outer wall slid to the side on cleverly disguised cogs. I positioned myself by the edge and waited for Barengar’s machine to assume position beside me.

“Here we go,” I grinned and ignited the jets.

The roar of the engines was high pitched yet thunderous and I slowly lifted off the floor before I eased my way out of the tower to hover outside. Barengar slid up to me before I rotated my Archangel and kept climbing.

Hovering like this exhausted vast amounts of magical power but we were still fueled by artificial ley lines from the massive collector nexus below us. I touched a gem inside my left gauntlet and the Archangel clanked and twisted around me. My arms slid up to nestle along my ribs with my hands by my shoulders, my waist was tucked back into the upper part of the Archangel and my legs was secured to reduce drag. The arms of the machine slid up and back to form sleek wings and the engines realigned to point backwards. We started falling, but a moment later the jets truly engaged and I felt as if a massive hand pushed me deeper into my harness. The ground shot past us with ever increasing speed and our fall evened out until we started climbing. We were traveling far faster than a Skyship already, but the Archangels had even more to give. The roar of the engines increased twofold and the pitch rose until I could barely hear it any longer.

I heard an agonized moan from Barengar and an audible swallow.

“You okay over there?”

“Yeah. Just a bit unused to these motions. This is amazing!”

“I know, right? You’re gonna love this!” I laughed and pitched us both hard to the left and set a course for Dun Traelak. We had more than enough power to take that relatively slight detour to Lahar.

We sped across the sky and the trip that would take several days by ground, possibly even a week depending on weather, took us only a couple of minutes. We soared across the mountain range that separated the plains from the hills where the Cities lay and hooked east to fly one of the paths that mother and my brother had used to patrol. My brother had been fast, but even his mighty wings were no match for the Archangel. We screamed past the roost of the Grey dragonflight and their matriarch raised her massive head to roar a challenge at us. But before the sound of her scream could reach us we had left them far behind. Even before Dun Traelak was visible on the horizon our Archangels had started to slow down and I brought our machines into their hovering configurations and we slid into a hover above the massive city the ancient fortress had become. It was even bigger than Lahar, the largest of the Cities, and stretched far beyond what had been the Skylander encampment during the siege. An amalgamation of stone, wood and crystal, it was the perfect mix of Skylander and Clan architecture to bring out the best of both. Even from our altitude I could make out dozens of people on the street that looked up at us and waved, for who else but I would come here in such a manner. I was tempted to land on the roof of the old fortress to visit Uncle Kenny, but I knew that he would insist that I spend time with his adopted children and stay for a meal. We did not see one another often enough these days. While I thoroughly enjoyed spending time with my adopted cousins, this was not the time for it. I made a mental note to make time for a proper visit soon. Perhaps Uncle Kenny could spot the flaws in the Archangel design that I could not find.

“This is where I grew up,” I told Barengar wistfully.

“I know,” he responded simply. “Though I have never been here before. Will we visit?”

“At some point,” I said vaguely and kicked our Archangels back into gear.

From Dun Traelak it would be less than a quarter of an hour flight to Lahar and we spent the first few minutes of it in silence while taking in the scenery.

“You were not born in Dun Traelak, right?” Barengar asked suddenly.

“No. I was born on the Skylands, far out into the ocean.”

“Hm,” Barengar grunted. “We were taught about that place. A crystal monolith suspended in the sky on a great geyser of magic.”

I swore under my breath. I had given strict orders to not teach about the Skylands. That was a place that was dead and buried and no good would come in digging into the history of that forsaken place.

I just grunted noncommittally.

“Why did you leave?”

“Both of my parents had died and I had been adopted by a mercenary, a friend of my fathers. She was sent out here on a scouting mission and sort of went native. She shipped me to Dun Traelak before the war started.”

“Would you have stayed if given the choice?”

“No. I hated that place. Everyone did.”

“It was truly that bad?” He asked incredulously.

“Yes.”

He seemed to infer from my curt answers that this was not a conversation I wanted to have.

“Will we visit the Skylands? Even though you say it is awful, I’d like to at least see it.”

“No. No one can visit the Skylands.”

“Why?”

“Because my mother destroyed it. Sank it into the depths.”

Stunned silence was his response and it lasted almost until we reached Lahar. Even when we zoomed past one of the smaller Cities he did not comment.

“So you are the last Skylander?” Barengar finally asked.

“I am,” I confirmed.

“The last of your kind…” he mused. “That must be so lonely.”

“Not really. I am glad that I am the last one.”

“That’s a horrible thing to say,” he responded but the tone of his voice implied that he was expecting a response that would contradict his statement.

“Perhaps it is. My people were not good people, Barengar. There were valid reasons for their behavior, but those reasons were no longer valid once they left the Skylands. They were incompatible with the others and countless people died because of it. They could not be reformed, and so they were destroyed. Mother killed most of them herself, all but a handful of thousands that survived the destruction of their colony on the mainland.”

“What happened to the survivors?” He asked almost immediately, curiosity evident in his voice.

“Some of them actually reformed and assimilated wherever they ended up. But most of them gathered in the valley where the school is.”

“At the school? But we are there now… What happened to them?”

“I killed them. Now shush, I must focus on landing us near the palace.”

Barengar shut up and I turned my attention to bringing us down. Lahar was a big city, almost as large as Dun Traelak had become, but the palace was far larger than the old keep. It was built out of white stone that shimmered in the sunlight and the towers and buttresses struck an imposing skyline. I had never flown here before, in fact this was the longest flight I had ever performed with an Archangel armour, and the higher vantage point disoriented me for a moment. I managed to get my bearings as I brought us low in our hovering configuration and with a glance at the power level gauge I decided to land as fast as possible. Power was draining fast now that we were entirely reliant on the stored power in our internal cores and our little joyride to Dun Traelak had drained our cores more than I had anticipated. I plopped us down on the innermost courtyard with a clang of crystal on marble. I set us down a little too roughly and marble splinters shot out in all directions as the ground cracked.

“Oops,” I muttered.

Almost as soon as we touched down armed guards streamed out of the palace interior. They wore full steel plate and carried bolt action rifles. Weapons technology had developed quickly in the last thirty years as the engineers and weapon smiths across the world sought to replicate Skylander magitech. While we had automatic weapons that could fire incredibly rapidly, most of the non-magic tech lagged behind a few decades. Many mechanical problems were ease itself to solve with magic, but without magic… I didn’t even want to think about it. 

The intent-control interface reacted to my thoughts and I was lowered to the ground, the helmet retracted and my limbs and torso was released one by one from their restraints. I blinked in the sudden bright light and lightly hopped to the ground. Barengar stumbled out of his armour and instantly spun around to snatch his axe into his hands.

He took a step in front of me to block as many rifles as possible and a shield bloomed out of his body.

I smiled proudly at his back. He was facing almost thirty bolt action rifles with only a revolver and an axe and he did not emit even a whiff of fear. I used my crystal eye to peek at his aura and saw only complete confidence in the two of us. I shook my head wryly and stepped around him, my high heels click-clacking on the marble.

I scanned the row of tense men aiming their weapons at us until I found one that I thought that I recognized. Though his name had escaped my memory long ago, his insignia clearly marked him as a commander.

I pointed at him. “You. I know you.”

“Commander Ola, Lady Marissa,” he responded and gulped.

“Do you mind?” I asked with a casual wave at his warriors.

Ola blinked sheepishly. “Stand down,” he barked and his soldiers obeyed instantly by lowering their rifles and shouldering them before standing at attention.

“Inform the Council that I have arrived to answer their summons,” I asked of Ola gently.

“Yes, ma’am. Right away ma’am,” he bowed before snapping an order to his aide. 

The young woman snapped out of her wide-eyed stare at me before she dashed back into the palace, the click-clack of her low heels fading into the distance.

“Barengar, put your weapons away. You are scaring the soldiers,” I chuckled.

Reluctantly he complied and reached the axe behind his back and slid the head into the hoop on his pauldron.

I let my gaze wander across the faces of the soldiers but they were all staring stiffly straight ahead and refused to relax for even a moment. But I knew that they were ready to snap into action at a moments notice.

Idly I considered what would happen if they chose to attack. I might be able to hold my own for a while, perhaps even escape if I used the power in the cores of the Archangels to fuel the same kind of spells that I had used back home against that arrogant upstart. But thirty soldiers with rifle versus just me and Barengar… That was not good odds. But they did not know that. They only knew the stories that had been told about me. About the woman that had destroyed the Groundlands and killed eleven thousand Skylanders in a single day.

Somehow the stories neglected to mention that I had had the bulk of the Bizhjerai fleet at my back and that I had spent months infiltrating and sabotaging the Groundlands to prepare for the savage deathblow.

They did not mention the friends and lovers that I had betrayed. The friends and lovers that had given their lives to protect me even though they had known what I was planning.
