They did not mention Alissa. The Aunt that I had been forced to kill. Forced to shoot her in the head publicly with my mothers own revolver. The same revolver that was to this day strapped to my hip.

I still had nightmares about that event. The greatest regret of my whole life. 

I shuddered at the thoughts and pushed them away, like I always did.

## CHAPTER FOUR Barengar

Marissa shuddered beside me, but I did not believe for a moment that it was out of fear of facing thirty armed and armoured soldiers.

I had seen her face down a larger number of mounted men just a few days ago. And she had killed them all in moments seemingly without any effort.

I had heard many stories about her from my classmates since I started to attend the school but I had always believed them to be inflated rumours.

It was undeniable that she was a genius, the sheer number of spells that she had seemingly conjured out of thin air and the machines she built effortlessly stood in proud testament to that fact.

But the stories in which she killed thousands of Skylanders and destroyed their settlements had always struck me as hyperbole.

Until that day. The sight of her, that tiny woman with the aura of sadness, spawning dozens of spells in moments to strike a whole company of trained soldiers dead in the blink of an eye…

That had been harrowing. That such power could be contained by such a tiny woman was unbelievable. Unbelievable but for the fact that I had beheld it with my own eyes.

No, these soldiers posed no threat at all to her and with that knowledge I felt no fear at all in our present situation. I had only stepped in front of her because she had not raised a shield of her own and the guards had seemed skittish at our sudden appearance. Even if they did not seek a fight one of them might panic and fire anyway and I didn’t think that our meeting with this Council would go smoother if Marissa slaughtered the welcome delegation.

It was a bit odd to just stand there with the staring guards. The commander, Ola, did not even attempt to make small talk. And while I detested small talk, it was still odd.

My eyes wandered toward the door where that pretty little aide had vanished and I found myself hoping that she would return, preferably with permission to allow us to enter.

This was boring.

I only had to endure the boredom for a little while before the aide returned, red in the face and breath heaving. She fell in beside her commander and whispered something into his ear and the stout man gave her a firm nod without responding.

What was that all about? I guess it was back to waiting then.

And my guess was correct.

For almost fifteen minutes we stood waiting. I was tempted to practice a bit on my spell work but I worried that any visible spell might be interpreted as a threat or an attack.

I better not.

When the waiting finally came to a close it was in the shape of a young man with piercing blue eyes, shoulder length brown hair and white robes that was almost entirely covered in silver thread embroidery.

He would have been handsome if it wasn’t for the arrogant smirk that seemed perpetually fixed on his face. His eyes narrowed at me for a short moment before he sneered and turned his attention to my mistress.

His disdainful expression vanished as if it had never been and a dazzling smile graced his features. I did not miss how his eyes dipped momentarily to take in the cleavage that her ceremonial light armour exposed.

“Lady Marissa! We were not expecting you for another two days,” he exclaimed before he took her proffered hand and bowed over it to press his thin lips against the back of her gloved hand.

“Councilor Talmud. Always expect the unexpected,” Marissa replied tersely and I could easily read from her posture that she did not like this man.

Good. Then we were in agreement regarding our assessment of his character.

“I received your summons barely an hour ago and decided to take the opportunity to test one of my latest contraptions,” Marissa continued with a casual jerk of her thumb over her shoulder at the Archangel armour.

Talmud’s blue eyes widened momentarily. “Barely an hour ago? And you are here already?” He asked incredulously.

“Yes. The Archangel armour proved to live up to my expectations,” Marissa said casually.

“So it would seem. Come, join me and I will escort you personally to your chambers and make sure that you are provided for until the Council meeting.”

“Don’t bother, Talmud, I do not intend to stay. I came to hear what you want to say and then be on my merry way. Lead the way to the Council chamber if you wish, otherwise I remember how to get there.”

I suppressed a smirk at the affronted look on his face but he quickly schooled his features into an expression of puzzlement.

“But the meeting is not scheduled until another two days,” he protested meekly.

“And that means that all of the members of the Council have been here for days already. I don’t have the time nor the patience for your games, Talmud. The Council requested my presence and I am here now. We can start the meeting right now or you can hold it without me,” Marissa responded firmly as we walked through the palace.

I tried to focus on what they were saying but the opulence all around me fought for my attention. I could not understand why people would wish to surround themselves with such expensive and ultimately useless items. I was far more interested in utility than style, something these people seemed to prefer the exact opposite of.

“The Council has decided to convene in two days time and the proper preparations must be completed before we can…”

“Cut the bullshit, Talmud. I know that your agenda was set weeks ago, two more days won’t change anything. Summon the other Councilors now, I intend to be on my way within the hour,” Marissa said, glowering.

“The Council must respect the decisions of the Council…” Talmud tried again but Marissa cut him off with an annoyed gesture.

“I’m not a part of your Council so I must not respect anything. You asked me, asked me, to come. I am not beholden to you in any way. We are barely even allies. I’ve come here to help you, presumably, because you have nothing that I want. If you want my presence at this meeting then you will hold it now.”

She had stopped while she spoke and Talmud listened with increasingly gritted teeth. I could practically hear them grind together. With a final irritated scoff he snapped his fingers and a beautiful young blond appeared as if she had stepped out of thin air.

“Summon the Councilors. The meeting starts as soon as they all arrive,” he snapped at the poor girl who curtsied and started to hurry away.

“The meeting starts in ten minutes, regardless if they are all there,” Marissa called after the poor girl.

I was amazed that she could order the rulers of the thirteen Cities around like this. They must really be desperate for her help.

I found myself increasingly curious about what they would want from her.

Marissa watched the girl vanish down the corridor before she spun on her heel and started to purposefully stride down the corridor.

I hurried to keep up and was a little annoyed that Talmud managed to sneak in between me and Marissa. 

My fingers brushed the grip of my revolver but I restrained myself. It would not be a good start to shoot a Councilor in the back on a diplomatic mission.

“This is most unseemly, Lady Marissa,” Talmud seethed as pleasantly as he could but it was quite obvious that he was fuming on the inside.

I could just barely suppress a dry chuckle while I walked behind him.

The Council quite clearly needed Marissa, if they didn’t then I was quite sure that they would have us ejected from the palace by force.

Talmud kept trying to talk to Marissa while she pointedly ignored him as if he did not exist at all. The hallways that we had been traversing suddenly ended in a pair of double doors and with a gesture from Marissa they opened on their own.

Talmud gritted his teeth at the obvious display of power and followed her inside. I stepped through the doors and beheld a large chamber, well lit by lanterns suspended on chains from the high ceiling, and it was dominated by a large horse-shoe shaped table with comfortable looking chairs along the outer rim. Marissa marched up to the one closest to the doors and promptly sat down, slouching back and throwing a leg over one of the armrests.

Talmud sputtered in indignation but wisely kept his mouth shut. I took up position behind Marissa and made sure that my left side was exposed by the tall wooden back so that I could draw my revolver and fire from the hip at an instants notice at any corner of the room. Just as a precaution I raised my personal shield to envelop me and Marissa both and the telltale blue shimmer that surrounded us made Talmud bristle.

“No magic in the Council chambers!” he snapped at me.

I stared at him impassively, not caring at all about his offense.

“Leash your dog, Lady Marissa!”

Marissa finally deigned to look at Talmud and after a long pause she gestured for me to comply.

I did so without hesitation and without looking away from the annoying Councilor. I wanted to make clear who was in charge in this room, and she sat before me.

Before Talmud could spit out the retort that was visibly on his lips a couple of older men entered the chambers. They gave Marissa a surprised glance before the gave her a polite bow each that she responded to with a smirk and a nod before the men hurried over to their seats.

The next group of men that arrived was visibly upset and glared angrily at my mistress before they sat down.

Next came a woman in her thirties and she would have been lovely if not for the haggard expression on her face, the haunted eyes and sallow skin. It looked as if she had not slept in years and carried a mountain on her shoulders. She smiled warmly but with a bit of restraint at Marissa before she assumed her seat at the table.

Talmud sullenly sat down next to Marissa and within a few minutes all the seats were taken.

Then an old man burst into the chamber with surprising alacrity given his frail form. He was tall and gaunt and had a silver beard that tapered into a scraggy point across his chest. It was as if all of the hair that used to be on his head had slid down to take up residence on his wrinkled chin.

“You are in my seat, Talmud,” he said to the younger man with a surprising baritone.

“Lady Marissa took mine, Councilor Aldan,” Talmud responded like a petulant child.

Aldan merely arched a fuzzy eyebrows and stared down at the younger councilor.

Talmud defiantly stared back but his posture said that he was anything but defiant.

“Quit wasting our time, Councilor Talmud,” a portly Councilor at the apex of the horse-shoe told him sternly.

“But where will I…” Talmud began but the chubby Councilor cut him off.

“You will stand, Talmud, since you so graciously offered your seat to our guest.”

Talmud shot to his feet so quickly that he was a blur and the older gentleman sat down with a sigh of relief.

“Of course, Councilor. I offered it as a gesture of good faith,” Talmud said as he expected everyone to eat his bullshit.

I rolled my eyes and wondered if the young man had been given this position so that he would not bother the leader of whatever City had sent him.

The portly Councilor banged the table with the hilt of his dagger twice in rapid succession. “I now pronounce the Council convened. The agenda for this meeting is quite extensive but out of respect for our esteemed guest we will begin with the matters that require her presence.”

“I’m glad to hear it, Ira,” Marissa responded pleasantly. Almost the polar opposite of how she had treated Talmud.

Ira inclined his head before turning his attention to one of the men that had been angry when they arrived.

They still seemed displeased.

“Councilor Pael, you have the floor,” Ira told one of the men.

Pael stood up and slowly walked over to the open end of the table, which I assumed were the speakers area. Pael was a short man of middle years that did not strike me as a man that had done a single day of honest labour in his life. While he was not fat, he would soon be. Soft was the best way to describe any part of him. From his hands that completely lacked callouses, to his face and plump lips and down to his slippered feet.

“I wish to speak of the war to the north, my esteemed colleagues,” he began.

That piqued my interest. The war had been raging for years and had been locked in a stalemate almost since it began. It was especially close to my heart since it was my people that were the aggressors. The war had broken out just before I decided to leave my homeland to attend the College. It was in fact the reason for my choice. I had wanted to become a powerful Mage and fight for my people, but I had since come to embrace the philosophy of the College. To build and mould the present into a better future. Not to destroy the present to make way for that better future.

“I know that you all already know about this, but let me state for the record,” Pael continued with a gesture at the young scribe in the corner furiously scribbling down a transcript onto a large sheaf of paper. “The stalemate between the Bizhjerai and the Ca’el has led the latter to make forays into the territory of Ukai.”

Marissa tilted her head in my direction. “Pael’s home,” she whispered up at me.

I nodded in appreciation that she kept me in the loop.

Pael glared at her but did not halt his speech.

“So far only skirmishes has been fought with only light casualties, but we all know the nature of war. Soon they will attempt to move their forces through our lands in order to flank the Bizhjerai and in so doing they will plunder and burn what they can to sustain themselves. Ukai will suffer without the farms and villages that sustain our glorious City. But we can’t stand alone against the Ca’el, we need your help. Should Ukai fall on hard times, all of us will suffer. The metal we mine to the north sustain the forges of Yra, and the components from Yra supply the engineers of Toha who in turn enable the Skyshipyards of Lahar.”

Pael drew a deep breath. “I call the Council to a vote regarding sending military aid in defense of Ukai in order to preserve the wellbeing of us all.”

Ira tapped the table with his dagger again. “A vote has been called regarding sending military aid to Ukai to defend the border against the Ca’el. Let me remind the Council that any joint military action sanctioned by the Council must be granted with qualified majority and is binding to all, even those that vote nay. What say you?”

The Councilors all raised their fists and glanced around. Ira counted to three and roughly half of the Councilors raised their thumbs. Two held theirs sideways, abstaining I assumed, while the rest held their thumbs down.

After discounting those that abstained I realized that only two of the nay-sayers would have to be swayed before the vote passed.

Pael glowered at those who voted nay and I noticed that the fatter one among them was gloating openly.

“What could make you change your decision?” Ira asked the nay-sayers.

The gloating man raised his hand to demand the word and Ira gave it to him with a gesture.

“Why should Raha aid Ukai when Ukai refuse to strike fair trade deals with us? Why should we send our young men to die in your war when you steal the food out of our mouths with terms of trade that is practically theft?”

Several of the Councilors groaned in exasperation but none of them intervened.

Pael must have been desperate for he closed his eyes for a moment and took a deep breath. “Ukai commits to renegotiating any deal between Ukai and Raha after the threat of the Ca’el is neutralized in exchange for military aid during this conflict,” he finally said as if every word pained him physically.

The as of yet unnamed Councilor could not hide a malicious grin as he leaned forward and greedily rubbed his hands together.

What an absolute fucker.

Slowly he raised his fist and shifted his thumb in an upwards direction.

Meanwhile the haggard looking woman had been whispering to the elderly man next to her, one of the abstainers. Their whispered conversation seemed to draw to a close and the man raised his thumb. The woman leaned back and closed her eyes and for a moment I thought that she had passed out, but then a tiny smirk curled her lips.

And just like that the Cities had decided to go to war.

I knew that the Cities could bring fearsome power to bear if they chose to. They had numbers and they had technology that had been unsurpassed in the known world until the Bizhjerai and the other Clans adopted Skylander magic. But the Ca’el were powerful, a vast empire to the northeast. Most of it was sparsely populated and only a few dozen large cities comprised most of the population but my people easily outnumbered the Clans and the Cities individually, but not put together. If the Cities and the Clans allied against the Ca’el in this war, then my people would most likely not win. Not when fighting on foreign territory and all of the logistical challenges that comes along with that. But from what I had gathered during this short visit to the Council of the Union of Cities, they were not interested in engaging the Ca’el alongside the Bizhjerai. They only wanted to protect their own interests.

It made sense from a tactical standpoint but while I did not truly understand why my people had so suddenly been roused to war by a foreign demagogue I was more than half convinced that they would not stop after they had conquered the Clans. Their greedy eyes would eventually turn south toward the Union and once the Clans had fallen… The Union of Cities would not stand a chance against the Ca’el.

I shook my head in disappointment, but it was out of my hands. I had sworn oaths that forsook my old allegiance to my people and shifted my allegiance to the College and its mission. Oaths enforced by magic to the point of being unbreakable.

The mood in the room had shifted dramatically and all of the present company wore tense expressions on their faces, as if they had not truly grasped the gravity of the decision they had just made until it was made.

Only Marissa seemed relaxed as she sat slouched into the tall-backed chair.

Councilor Ira tapped the table twice with his dagger with a resigned sigh. I got the impression that he was not at all surprised by the outcome of the vote and his next statement proved my suspicion correct.

“Councilor Elan, as the most experienced among the Council in the art of war, please present the plans for the defense of Ukai that I asked you to draft.”

The only Councilor that was not a soft man, or a woman, stood up. He was tall and broad shouldered with a jaw that seemed carved out of granite. His gray eyes were sharp and hard and despite age softening his body he stood with his back ramrod straight with an obvious core of iron. His silver hair was cropped short and as he stood his hand dropped to cradle a weapon that was no longer fastened to his belt. He grimaced briefly when his fist closed around nothing before he walked around the table to the speaker position. He was clearly a former military man, probably a general or a Skyship captain. He assumed his position and clasped his hands behind his back before he cleared his throat.

“Councilor Ira asked me to draft hypothetical plans in case the Union would vote to assist Ukai in its defense against the Ca’el. These are my findings.

He nodded to someone behind me and I pivoted sharply, hand on my revolver, but all I managed to see was the door swinging shut behind someone. I had not even heard anyone enter.

I swore under my breath at myself. I still was not used to relying on my magesense. If I had been using it properly, as I had been taught, then I would not have been taken by surprise like that. I was in charge of not only my own safety, but that of Marissa as well. I could not afford to be lax in my duties. I focused on my magesense and reached through the door and into the hallway outside. A couple of servants were preparing a large board on wheels. Probably a map. My assumption was proved correct when the doors opened and the servants rolled the map around the table so that Elan could reference it easily.

“Ukai is here,” he said and pointed at a marker a good distance to the south of the mountain range that separated the Clan plains from the Union foothills. “Ukai itself does not have much in the way of natural defenses, it is mostly lowlands with flowing hills. Nothing in particular to focus a defense around or to use as a choke point for the Ca’el hordes.”

He let that sink in for a few moments. “This mountain range that separates us from the front of the current war has several passes, however, that would be easily defended by a smaller force. If we construct defenses here it would cut the access of the Ca’el into Ukai. That will put a stop to the raids and buy us enough time to fortify and mobilize.

Furthermore, the river of melt water from the high peaks to the east cuts down here,” he continued and trailed his finger down the thick river bisecting the territory of Ukai. “If we seize and fortify the viable crossings it will force the Ca’el to use the more dangerous crossings where they are vulnerable to counter attacks and harassment by dragoons. Their losses would be significant given that we can maintain a steady supply of powder and shot for our skirmishers.”

Elan looked each of us in the eye, even me, before finishing up.

“Any questions?”

The female councilor raised her hand and Ira gave her permission to speak with a wave of his hand.

“If the mountain fortifications will be small and garrisoned with only a small force, what would stop the Ca’el from simply sieging them and taking them? Their numbers are immense and they can afford to throw their soldiers at our walls until we drown in them.”

Elan waited patiently for her to finish before he nodded.

“A fair point, Councilor Raha. But as long as they are in a deadlock with the Bizhjerai it would be too costly to send that many men to besiege our fortifications, especially if all they want is to raid the farms of Ukai. If they sought to actually invade then the mountain forts would only be a delaying tactic.”

“What if they just bring artillery and pound the forts to dust?” Another councilor asked without asking for permission.

Ira glared at the man, who took no notice.

“I have been to those passes. We can build in such locations that we will always have the high ground and thus have longer reach with our own guns. No besieging artillery will be able to fire upon our walls without counter-fire.”

I noticed out of the corner of my eyes that Marissa sat with a bored expression on her face while cleaning her nails with her knife.

“What about Skyships?” Raha asked.

“We must station our own such that they can respond in a timely fashion. I have already made offers to several mercenary fleets, the Fists of Heaven among them.”

Marissa suddenly stared right at the councilor, her personal grooming efforts forgotten.

“Raistlin’s old troop? I never trusted that bombastic fool,” one of the councilors grumbled.

“Say what you will about Raistlin,” Elan told the man sternly, “but he knows war. His fleet is one of the finest out there, better than many of our own fleets.”

“Because he stooped to use Bizhjerai tech,” the man grumbled but I could tell that he had already given up.

Elan gave the man a final hard stare before he continued with his plan.

“First order of business would be to construct the forts and improve the roads that leads to the mountain passes so that supplies and personnel can travel there more quickly. The College of Mages will supply Construction Mages to manifest the forts and staff them with on site Mages for repairs and for magical defenses…”

“No,” Marissa interrupted him sternly.

Elan stared at her for a few moments before he blinked slowly. “No?”

“You heard me. The College of Mages will not take part in this conflict.”

The fat councilor that had forced Ukai to renegotiate their trade deals leaned forward. “The College of Mages is in Union lands, Lady Marissa. The Union voted to support Ukai, therefore you will…”

“No. The College is not a part of the Union. Never has been, never will. We will not take part in your wars.”

“But the valley where the College…”

“Is mine. The College and the Union has no military pacts. We are not a member of the Union. You claim that the land belongs to you. I say differently. It was completely unoccupied when the Skylanders settled there and you did nothing to stop them. I did. I set in motion the events that lead to the destruction of the Groundlands. I destroyed them. I was there. I fought there. I killed them all. None of you, nor none of your people were there. That makes that land mine and if you want to persist in making us a part of the Union against our will then you will have to come and claim us.”

She stared the fat Councilor in the eye and her crystal eye flared balefully. “I can assure you that that is a conflict that you will not win.”

The absolute certainty behind her voice sent chills down my spine. I believed every word she had just said. If they forced this issue and invaded then she would kill them all. And then she would raze each and every City that participated in the attack. She was sworn to, bound by the same magic that enforced my own oaths.

“Come, Barengar. We are done here.”

Marissa stood up and walked toward the doors.

“If you leave this chamber without committing to aid us, then we will have to cancel all of our trade deals,” Ira said tiredly.

Marissa paused without looking back. “So be it. We will manage. Good day, Councilors.”

And then she led me out of the Council chambers. The doors closed behind me with a thud of finality.

###

The doors behind us opened almost immediately and I could see through my magesense that it was Councilor Talmud that was hurrying to catch up to us. I could hear Marissa let out a frustrated sigh when she sensed the same but she didn’t even glance over her shoulder at him or slow down to let him catch up.

But in just a few long strides he did and immediately started chattering at her while his guards closed ranks around us. Talmud alternated between demanding that we returned to the council chambers and haughtily begging us, though his begging was mostly made up of thinly veiled threats. I tuned out his words and focused on his guards. They were all armed and armoured and posed a much more significant threat than the arrogant man. The only armament he wore was an ornamental dagger at his belt and I doubted that he had ever wielded it in anger. Marissa would be able to deal with him without even looking at him if he tried anything. Marissa let Talmud’s words flow over her as she walked toward the large courtyard where we had touched down with surprisingly swift strides for her short legs. I was watching the guards so closely that I barely noticed when we emerged into sunlight and the sudden light blinded me. I blinked my eyes rapidly to adapt when I sensed Talmud’s face harden and he made a gesture behind my back with his hand. Cold dread coursed down my spine and time seemed to slow down. I poured power into my magesense to expand my range as much as I could and at the edge of the expanded area I suddenly sensed a slight movement. Without thinking I leaped forward to put myself between Marissa and the sniper on the roof. I started to raise my shield but I knew that it would be too weak to properly absorb the shot. My right hand twisted behind my lower back to grasp the haft of my axe and my left kept pouring power into my shield. The rifle barked and time snapped back into its normal pace. The shot slammed into my shield with a bright flash and the deformed bullet grazed my left bicep, carving a furrow in my flesh. Pain lanced up my arm and shoulder, but Marissa was safe. The guards all around us milled around with their weapons raised uncertainly but not a single one of them returned fire. Several of them even pointed their rifles at me and Marissa. In their panic they might shoot either of us and while we both had our shields up we would not be able to absorb more than a few shots each. I did the first thing that came to my mind and I spun around and swung my axe one handed at Talmud. His eyes widened in fear and panic but the half-moon blade did not crash into his temple as he undoubtedly had expected. Instead the haft of my blade slammed into the side of his neck and the beard of the axe hooked around his neck. I twisted and pulled him around while tripping his legs with my own. He fell heavily onto his knees with a curse and I darted in behind him, my left hand fisting in his hair and my right sliding up the haft of the axe to grip it just below the head to keep the edge firmly against his neck.

The sniper fired again and the shot slammed into Marissa’s shield and to my horror it flashed brightly, far more brightly than a normal shot would have. It broke through her shield but had been deflected enough to embed itself into her shoulder instead of her chest. She was spun around with a curse and fell to a knee.

“Marissa!” I shouted and cast a spell at the sniper on the roof. He was far beyond my range, but my spell was not designed to take him out. Instead the spell exploded into a thick mist that would obscure us from his sight and foul his aim.

“I’m fine,” Marissa grunted and stood up straight. When she turned around I saw that the shot had punched through her shoulder armour and blood was seeping out of the ragged hole in her flesh. But there was something wrong with the blood. It was a far darker red than it should have and it hissed and bubbled as if it was acid. There was also far less of it than I had expected and even as I watched the trickle dwindled into almost nothing. Acrid smoke boiled out of the hole in her shoulder. Marissa rolled her shoulder with a grimace before her eye snapped up into the cloud of smoke that I had created. Power exploded out of her as a multitude of spells erupted out of the gems on the side of her head. A contraption akin to a catapult formed around her and she was suddenly launched into the smoke and I had to follow her with my magesense. All of the guards were aiming their weapons at me by now and I firmed my grip on Talmud and my axe.

“Shoot me and I will slice his neck when I fall,” I growled at the soldiers.

A few of them glanced around at the others nervously and I could feel my axe wobble when Talmud swallowed nervously.

A series of flashes illuminated the smoke followed by a single scream before a man came hurtling toward the ground. He ended up suspended spread eagled a few feet above ground.

A large telekinetic pillow appeared out of nothing and Marissa fell down upon it. The pillow collapsed under her weight until it deposited her on her feet and her eyes narrowed at the suspended man, her human eye glared balefully and her blue crystal one flashed ominously. She acted as if she hadn’t just been shot.

“I must admit that I underestimated your stupidity, Talmud,” she hissed angrily. “I wouldn’t have imagined that you would try to have me assassinated in your own home. This was clumsy, even for you.”

“I don’t know who this man is!” Talmud protested wildly.

“Don’t play coy with me, Councilor,” Marissa sneered, spinning around to face him. “I saw you signal for him to take the shot. Even your own guards did nothing to retaliate.”

Talmud wisely shut up.

“Shall I kill him?” I asked Marissa.

“No,” she sighed. “We have no proof that he did it, and even if we did I am certain that his actions were sanctioned by the council. The only reason these guards haven’t finished the job is because you hold that idiot hostage.

“Fuck it,” I replied, “let’s kill them all and be done with it.”

After having seen Marissa’s unexpected magical strength just now I had no doubt in my mind that we could kill all of these assholes.

I had been taught that she was of average strength, but far above average in skill. But the sheer amount of power she had just expended in launching herself and then creating that pillow over such distances contradicted what I had been taught.

“You can’t kill us!” Talmud protested. “The Union will go to war with the College for that!”

I pressed my axe into his neck, drawing a faint line of blood. “You should be more worried that the College might go to war on you, fool. You’ve given us more than enough reason to,” I growled.

“You have no proof,” he managed to get out.

I must say that I had underestimated his courage. He actually dared to argue from his current position. Or he was just stupid or arrogant enough to believe that he still had the upper hand. On the other hand, he did have a dozen armed guards with their rifles trained on us.

“Not yet,” Marissa said softly. “But I am sure that I can get the truth out of this guy,” she continued with a wave at the struggling assassin.

“He belongs to the Union,” Talmud choked out, almost in a panic. “He committed his crime in the Cities, therefore he must be tried according to our laws!”

Marissa sighed and rubbed her forehead with her right arm.

“He must be locked behind bars and interrogated, you can’t take him with you. Tampering with the accused will not be tolerated!”

“Fine,” Marissa snapped. “Toss him into the dungeon all you want after I take what I need from him.”

With that she drew her revolver with a single smooth motion and shot the man in the heart. Her gun was back in its holster before the sound of the gunshot had rung out and the man threw his head back and screamed.

Light blazed out of his eyes and mouth and he seemed to shrivel from the inside, his chest imploding and smoldering with blue flames. He finally went limp and Marissa raised her hand. The assassins chest exploded in dry tatters and her shot smacked into her palm and she closed her hand around it and dropped it into her pocket.

Then her eyes looked into mine. “Barengar, thunder,” she commanded.

I knew exactly what that meant and I twisted away from her and plugged my ears with two layers of shields and emptied the space between them of air. Even though my eyes were closed the flash of light was nearly blinding and the rumble of the blast pounded into my chest.

I pushed Talmud aside and sprinted after Marissa. I used my magesense to navigate and had to shoulder between two stunned guards that were stumbling around with their streaming eyes unseeing and their hands clapped over their ringing ears. I leaped over a fallen rifle and quickly stashed my axe into the Archangel armour I had flown here in. It quickly pulled me inside and took off to follow Marissa. The last I saw of Talmud was him sitting on the ground and rubbing his eyes, and then the Archangel shifted into flight mode and we shot into the sky. We flew in silence and I tried to wrap my mind around that enchanted bullet Marissa had used to kill the assassin. I had never seen or heard of a spell effect like that. It looked almost as if it had drained the Soul right out of the man, but not only his magical power like a zombie round would. The damage to his body had been far too severe for such a draining. That man had nearly disintegrated completely. What had that spell done to him? And why had she retrieved the bullet from his corpse?

Perhaps it was a complex spell and she wished to reuse the enchantment to save the hassle of recasting it?

But if she just wanted to kill the man a normal kinetic round to the heart would have worked just as well and would have been much more efficient.

I was so absorbed in my thoughts that I didn’t notice that we were approaching home until the Archangel shifted into hovering configuration and we slid into the opening in the tower and landed. I was suddenly deposited from the armour and faced a stern-faced Marissa.

“That spell you saw me use to kill that assassin… It was a prototype of a spell that I have been working on. One that I am not yet ready to reveal to the world.”

“I understand, Marissa. You secret is safe with me.”

He face twisted before hardening into an unreadable mask.

“I wish that I could trust that, Barengar. I really do, and don’t misunderstand me when I say this: I don’t trust you.”

My gut twisted when I heard her say those words. Had I not proved myself to be an able assistant?

“I don’t doubt that you will honour my secrecy, but there are always some circumstances where a man has a breaking point. Torture. Extortion. An offer that is just too good. I will need you to swear another Oath.”

My relief was immense. It wasn’t that she didn’t trust me, specifically. It was that she did not trust the nature of mankind. Good. Neither did I.

“Of course, Marissa.”

“I don’t think you understand. This Oath will be similar to the one you’ve already sworn to the College, but to me personally.”

My eyes widened at that. The Oath sworn to the school was magically binding. It simply erased the will to act against the Oath and forced the one who swore it to obey. But I trusted her, just as much as I trusted the school.

“Very well. I trust you Marissa.”

She gave me a firm nod. “Very well. Follow me.”

And then she spun on her heel and marched out of the room. “I had hoped to spend more time on your training, to really assess if you were suitable for this but now I will have to fast track my plans for you, Barengar.”

I didn’t respond. I just listened.

“Take off your shirt and lay down on the table,” Marissa ordered with a wave at a few shelves.

I looked in that direction in confusion, for there were no table there. Before my eyes a spell template formed out of nothing and hundreds of materialization spells appeared and withing moments a rudimentary table big enough for me had been constructed.

Even after all the time I had spent in this workshop I was in awe at the ease with which Marissa could manifest her will. If I ever got half as skilled as her I would be beyond pleased with myself. I watched Marissa walk over to the shelfs to rummage around with her hands above her head to reach the highest shelf while I stripped out of my light armour and shirt. I dropped the final layer of garments onto the floor just as she made a satisfied sound and turned around with a vial of glowing turquoise liquid clutched in her fingers. I sat down on the rudimentary table and waited for her.

She stood beside me and dropped the vial only to catch it in a cradle of magic before she peeled off her left gauntlet and glove.

Before I could stop her she had drawn her knife and slashed her palm with it.

“Marissa!” I gasped and started to rise but she pushed me back gently with a thick and soft wave of force.

She held her fist above the vial and squeezed blood into it, polluting the azure glow with streaks of malignant crimson.

“The blood will bind the Oath to me, personally,” Marissa explained.

I nodded as if I understood. In truth, I had not really grasped the Oath magic yet, very few students did.

It was just too complex and I could not even begin to fathom how Marissa had figured it out on her own.

Marissa swirled the ink and blood mixture around in the vial until it meshed into a dark purple liquid that glowed dimly, angrily.

“Lie down on your stomach,” Marissa ordered.

I complied. I trusted her, even though the concoction she had just made looked like distilled evil.

With my magesense I saw her reach the vial over my left shoulder blade and she slowly poured the liquid onto my back.

My flesh tensed at the unexpected warmth of the liquid, barely colder than my skin and thicker than water.

Like freshly spilled blood, I realized. But blood did not make my skin tingle with magic tremors.

Instead of running down my side and back as I had expected the liquid stayed in a shimmering puddle on top of my shoulder and while Marissa sketched symbols in the air above it the liquid flowed into an intricate pattern that my mind could not even begin to follow. It was all graceful swirls and harsh angles and I knew that I should have been able to read it. Individual segments I could parse, I saw one that was reminiscent of intent-control magic and another that was similar to a Soul Drain component but when I tried to wrap my mind around how the various components interacted it felt as if my brain would boil.

I watched as the liquid seeped and coiled around my shoulder, partially encircling and meshing with my Oath tattoo for the College on my upper arm.

“Brace yourself,” Marissa whispered suddenly.

“For what?” I asked dumbly just as the sizzle of fatty meat thrown into a hot griddle reached my ears. I had just barely lifted my head from the table when the pain hit me. I gritted my teeth and my whole body locked up, but I did not cry out. I had faced worse pain. And before I could remember that old painful experience it was all over. The sizzling sound abated and the pain faded almost from one moment to the next. My body relaxed and I was left panting and sweating on the cold crystal table.

Gloved fingers landed on my back, softly stroking my trembling muscles.

“I’m sorry about the pain, Barengar,” Marissa whispered regretfully. “I hope that the next iteration of the spell will include something to alleviate it.”

I enjoyed the sensation of her fingers on my skin for a moment before I pushed up and sat on the table.

“That’s fine, Marissa. I am no stranger to pain,” I grunted and wiped my forehead with the back of my hand.

She squeezed my shoulder and when I glanced at her face she had an expression of old sorrow lining her usually smooth face. In that moment she almost looked her years. It was mostly the haunted look in her natural eye that struck me to my core.

“Tell me about it, when you are ready,” Marissa breathed and I could only nod in response.

Then her face smoothed out and she once again looked like a young woman. Scarred and with prematurely graying hair, but young.

“Now get dressed and put the table in the discard spell. We have a lot of work to do.”

I leaped to my feet and sketched a bow at her retreating back, knowing that she would be able to see it with her magesense. 

I could not keep my eyes from taking in the sway of her hips and how her snug trousers hugged her shapely rear and I knew that she would catch that as well.

Chuckling to myself I got dressed and hoisted the table into the air with my arms. Sometimes it just felt good to do things the old fashioned way instead of with magic, just as Marissa had said earlier. I carried the table over to the large crystal container that would dissolve any object that was made out of materialized magic and return the magic to the distribution net. 

Once done I decided to seek Marissa out. She had said that there were work to do, but her workshop was a labyrinthine mess. It was not overly large, but it was cluttered with a multitude of work areas and spells that blinded my magesense with bright pillars of power. I walked through it all, peering into cubbies and aisles but couldn’t find her. Then it struck me. She must be in the hangar, the only area that I had not even given a cursory inspection.

True enough, she was stood before the Archangel that I had flown in. She was bent at the waist with her head and shoulders deep inside the machine and I could sense that she was casting spells in there, probably modifying some aspect of the propulsion system. I leaned against the wall and waited, watching my mistress and trying to learn as much as I could with my limited vantage of what she was doing.

The spellworks inside the machine was blinding my magesense at several areas but I managed to twist and turn it in place so that I could get a murky view of what was going on in there. She was indeed casting spells. Her body was locked in place, fingers splayed and cradling a large power core in the heart of the machine. Hundreds of tiny ley lines connected her fingers and palms to the core and connected to distinct points inside. Slowly the coils pulsed but I could not make sense of what it meant. I twisted my magesense to look at her face and was suddenly struck by her beauty.

I had of course, being a man it was impossible not to, noticed her beauty the moment I first lay eyes on her. But to see her like this… In her natural habitat, not knowing that she was observed… It was as if I truly saw her for the first time. Her full lips were tightened, her nose slightly scrunched up and a frown of concentration wrinkled her forehead. Her natural eye was closed but I could tell that it was moving rapidly beneath the lid while her crystal eye was wide open and glowing brightly. But there was something more than just concentration in her expression. I got the sense of a profound grief, doubt and regret along with a well concealed hope. I don't know if it was my imagination playing tricks with me or if my magesense was somehow acute enough to pick up emotions as hers seemed to be, but that was the impression that I got. 

Quite suddenly the magical light inside the machine winked out and Marissa’s eye flashed open, though she could not see my presence right in front of her face. Her head twisted a little to the side and a small grin curled her lips mischievously. Somehow she was even prettier when she smiled when she did not know that someone was watching.

“Enjoying the view?” She asked loudly and I was momentarily disoriented by seeing her lips move but hearing the sound of her voice from a completely different direction. I released my focus and my vision twisted as I switched from full magesense back to my flesh eyes. And found that my gaze had been firmly stuck on her proffered bottom.

“I… I was watching you work,” I stammered while she shimmied out of the narrow duct she had been reaching into.

She stood up and tossed her head to flick her hair over her shoulder before giving me a look over her shoulder.

“Sure you were,” she grinned.

“I swear, I was in full magesense and didn’t…” I trailed off uncertainly.

She sashayed up to me and patted my arm reassuringly. “Relax, Barengar, I ain’t no prude maid. I don’t care if you stare and I know that you would not presume to do anything more. All is good.”

I nodded slowly, not certain if there was a deeper meaning behind her words.

“What were you doing with the Archangel?” I finally asked. “I could not make sense of the spell you were casting.”

She waved her hand in an exasperated motion. “I wasn’t casting a spell, precisely. I was modifying it. That core you saw is the main spell core of the machine.”

Her eye unfocused momentarily as she stared over my shoulder before she shrugged a little.

“I suppose that you could call it the brain of the machine. It contains the spells necessary to coordinate the engines, distribute power and all that kind of stuff.”

“A brain?” I asked incredulously.

“Kinda,” she shrugged. “Not as complex as a human brain, nor capable of individual thoughts but the analogy works. You give it abstract high-level instructions and it can translate them into actions. Like ‘land over there’ or ‘fly fucking fast’ and it will sort it out on its own.”

I stared at her for a little while, trying to figure out if she was pulling my leg. “You’re serious?”

“Yeah. It’s just meta-magic, really. In itself nothing special.”

“Nothing special?” 

“Mhm,” she nodded, already turning from me and approaching the other Archangel.

“Fucking hell,” I muttered under my breath. “’Nothing special’, she says…”

“Anyway, I modified the core so that I can give you basic control of the Archangel but keeping it slaved to mine so that you may fly it on your own, but mine will take over if you make any major mistakes. I think it is time that I teach you how to fly one of these things.”

Excitement rushed through me. “Right now?”

“Sure. We are going for a flight anyway, might as well use it to teach my apprentice.”

She drew a deep breath before continuing. “But before we go, I must clarify a few things about your Oath to me.”

I nodded and indicated for her to go on.

“The Oath binds your will to me. Any intent you may have that might betray me in any way… You will simply lack the will to do so. The magic will mould your will so that you won’t even want to question it. So here are your first orders: you will not reveal anything that I teach you to anyone else without my explicit say so. Anything I give you permission to use in front of others or teach others will include any components of those spells unless I explicitly state that that component should remain a secret. You will tell no one what we are working on unless I give you permission. The only exemption from these rules are if you must use your new skills to save your own life, or my own, or someone or something that you believe, truly believe, must be saved. Got it?”

“Understood, Mistress,” I told her earnestly. For once she did not comment on my use of the honorific.

“Great,” she smiled. “Then don your armour, Barengar.”

She did not have to tell me twice and I practically leaped into it. This time only excitement filled me when the crystal machine cradled me into itself and I relaxed as it walked after Marissa toward the opening walls. A few moments later and we were screaming north across the mountain range.

“Where are we going?” I asked, hoping that Marissa had opened the communication ley line.

“My other workshop,” was the cryptic response.

Finally! She had teased that I might one day see it, but I had not anticipated that it would be this early.

“And you will not reveal its existence or location to anyone, even if it means your death, or mine. There are secrets there that the world is not ready for yet. They are better forgotten forever than unleashed too early.”

“Understood, Marissa.”

We flew in silence for a few more moments and when the mountain range gave way for the Clan territory I could not wait any longer.

“How about that partial control?”

A tinkling laugh echoed through my helmet and I could not help but smile fondly.

“So eager! I approve. Very well, I will transfer basic controls to you, but first I will give you some instructions.”

I waited impatiently for her to continue.

“The Archangel is almost entirely intent controlled so you must school your thoughts so that you do not give it false commands. For that reason there are some that are motion controlled. The arms and legs are controlled by your own limbs, just move normally and the Archangel will move along with you. The arms and the legs are locked in flight mode and only available when hovering or landed. You may activate the sight mode if you wish and in that mode the Archangel will fly in the direction you are turning your face. Twist your head side to side to roll, turn it to yaw and nod to pitch. Be careful, the higher your velocity the greater the effect of the wind will be and a twitch of your head might throw you into an uncontrollable tail-spin. I suggest that you use sight mode until you can train your intent control in a more safe environment.”

“And the limited controls won’t allow me to crash the thing?” I asked nervously.

When the silence dragged on I had to speak again. “Marissa?”

“Possibly. Probably. I just made those changes to your suit and I have not tested them. So be careful.

I had just a moment to feel a twitch of anxiety before she spoke again.

“Transferring control.”

My Archangel twitched and rolled to the side. I could see Marissa vanishing from the illusion that represented the front view of my Archangel.

I cursed and focused all my will on ‘sight mode’ but my belly lurched when I started to rapidly drop toward the plains hurtling past far below.

I closed my eyes and focused my entire being on switching modes.

My Archangel suddenly dipped its nose toward the ground and my eyes flew wide open. Only a sea of green grass was visible.

This was it. This was how I died. Crushed into a pulp inside a tangled mess of shattered crystals.

My world suddenly spun when the Archangel righted itself and my stomach lurched at the sudden change of direction.

“Seems as if my modifications worked, albeit a little too slowly,” Marissa mused.

But I could hear her rapid breathing trough the connection between our machines. She had been scared.

“Remind me to tweak the settings before we return.”

“Alright, Marissa. Did I activate sight mode?”

“I believe so. Check the bottom of your illusory screen. There should be an ‘S’ along the bottom right.

I glanced down and sure enough, it was there. “Yeah, I can see it.”

“Good. Experiment with the sight controls. Carefully. Small movements of your head until you feel something happen.”

I took a deep breath before I slowly inched my head to the right. Nothing happened. Gritting my teeth I kept turning my head until the Archangel started turning.

Letting out the breath I had been holding I grinned. I had done it! I had controlled this impressive machine and not gotten myself killed.

I tried nodding my head and felt he Archangel dip before I tried angling the wings a little.

Gaining confidence I slowly ramped up my movements and I barely noticed the sea of grass below us give way to the sea of water. We were still heading north but I was having too much fun flying in loops around Marissa.

I laughed excitedly, momentarily forgetting that Marissa would be able to hear my childlike glee.

I was brought back to reality by a feminine giggle and I grinned sheepishly to myself.

“Glad to hear you enjoying my machine, Barengar. Are you ready to take it to the next level?

“Next level?”

“Controlling velocity as well,” she clarified.

“Oh, hell yeah!”

###

I don’t know how long I spent playing around with the flight controls of the Archangel, gradually learning how to maneuver through the air but once I grew confident in my ability to not crash with my limited controls I finally took a look around.

And saw only water below us in all directions and only sky above us.

“Uh, Marissa?”

“Yes?”

“Where are we?”

“To the east of the Bizhjerai lands, far out into the ocean.”

“Uh huh, and where are we going?”

“To my private workshop.”

That was not very forthcoming. Nor was it reassuring since I could clearly see with a glance at the charge gauge that the Archangel was down to almost half power levels. If we did not turn around very soon then we would not have enough power to get back to the school. I estimated that we had been flying for a little more than two hours, which meant that our destination would have to be within a two hour flight from where we were. If it wasn’t… Then we would fall from the sky to drown in the ocean.

But I trusted that Marissa would not throw our lives away so I tried to relax and to not look nervously at the power gauge every few minutes.

The huge storm clouds up ahead did not alleviate my fears at all.

“Marissa?”

“Yes, Barengar?”

“Those storm clouds, are we going to fly through them?”

“Possibly, I can’t tell from here how high they are. We might be able to fly above it.”

“Shit,” I mumbled. I had just barely learned how to fly this thing and I did not relish the challenge of my paltry skill that flying through a storm undoubtedly would be.

“Transferring controls of your Archangel to mine, just in case. Just relax, Barengar, and let me guide us through this little storm.”

Relax she says. Easy for her to say, she was the one in control of this situation. I did my best to obey her and forced my tensing body to relax as much as it was able.

And then Marissa went ahead and shattered my attempts to relax.

“The storm seems to be growing rapidly, let’s kick this up a gear and try to pass it before it grows too much.”

And then the nose of our Archangels angled higher and the high-pitched whine of the jets turned into a scream from the deepest pits of hell.

I was violently pushed back in my harness, the crystal braces pushing into my groin painfully. I could do nothing but stare ahead at the storm as it rapidly loomed closer. It was difficult to breathe all of a sudden, as if there where not enough air inside the helmet.

“Marissa?” I croaked.

“I know,” her strained voice returned almost immediately. “The Archangel is not designed to fly at these altitudes. But I wold rather risk it than dipping into that storm right now.”

I tried to answer, but no sound escaped my throat.

Relax, Barengar, I told myself. The more you panic the more air you will need. Just trust Marissa and relax and try to breathe as little as possible. Focusing on my breath became my whole world, one painful breath at a time. In and out, inhale and exhale. After some indeterminable length of time my focus was broken by a curse.

“Fuck, I’m about to pass out. Barengar, we are going into the storm for the final push. Brace yourself.”

I barely had time to react before my Archangel twisted sideways and dipped. I let out a ragged moan instead of the scream that was building up inside of me and then my illusory viewport went dark.

We must have dipped into the thick clouds. A few moments later we plunged out of the cloud cover into gale winds and rain almost as thick as a waterfall. A flash of lightning almost blinded me and while I blinked the spots out of my eyes I could feel the winds buffeting my Archangel.

“Gotta slow us down, these winds are to strong,” Marissa muttered. “And why is that glare filter not working? That lightning bolt nearly burned the eye out of my socket.”

I didn’t have the presence of mind to answer, nor did I care to. I just closed my eyes and prayed that the flight would be over soon. 

Every time the Archangel veered from a gust of wind my stomach lurched and I was convinced that I was being blown out of the sky. But every time it somehow managed to right itself.

“Almost there,” Marissa murmured and my ears popped as we descended.

I forced my eyes open but could see nothing but the rain. Rain and the thickest bolt of lightning I had ever seen. Wait, that was not a bolt of lightning. It didn’t fade instantly, and it seemed to be flowing upwards. My eyes widened with realization.

It was a magic fount!

But one that was unlike any other. It was huge. Impossibly huge. Hundreds of feet thick and thousands of yards high.

Was it… No, it couldn’t be?

Had Marissa really situated her private workshop on top of the Great Geyser? The location of the legendary Skylands?

It had to be. That magic fount was far too large to be any other. But that would mean that the workshop was right there in front of us. But where?

The storm forgotten, I searched the illusion in front of me. It had to be inside the geyser somewhere. There! Bright crackling interference heralded the presence of a powerful shield that kept the caustic currents at bay but it was so bright that I could not see through it. We were heading straight for it and slowing down and without warning I felt myself pushed around as the Archangel shifted to hovering mode. The wind instantly seized my less aerodynamic form and tried to whisk me off to the side. The jets of the armour strained against it and just barely persevered. I could not see Marissa anywhere around me but I knew that she must be close or else she would not be able to guide my armour. Slowly we inched closer to the bright shield and to my complete lack of surprise a hole opened. My armour dashed inside and Marissa suddenly appeared through the waterfall that had formed over the hole in the shield. The hole snapped shut and we were suddenly cut off from the storm. We drifted the hundred yards toward a floating structure of deep purple materialized magic and my crystal feet touched down on a large outcropping. Marissa crunched down next to me and I could hear a deep sigh over the communication spell.

“Welcome, Barengar. Welcome to the former Skylands. My birthplace.”

## CHAPTER FIVE Marissa

I had such mixed feelings about this place. I knew that it was not actually the Skylands, that horrific monolith had been sunk into the deep ocean by mother, and good riddance.

I had built this workshop from the ground up with the same spells that the Skylands had originally been built with, and that somehow made the two structures similar to me. Even though it was most definitely not the Skylands, this was still the area where I had been born. Where my mother had died when I was a baby. Where father had died fighting the dragonkin horde when I was a child.

His death was at the same time one of the worst events in my life and one of the best.

If he had not died that night then I would most likely not have been adopted by the legendary Dragonbane. I would not have moved with her to the Outside. I would never have had mom, Malkareth or grandma, Uncle Kenny, Auntie Alissa, Marie or any of the others. The price for my life had been the life of my father, and it killed me inside to think of it that way. I often asked myself that if I could go back in time to save my father, would I?

And the worst part about that is that the answer would be: I don’t know.

I didn’t know if I would save the life of my father if I had the opportunity, knowing what I now know about what would come to pass.

Logically I knew that I shouldn’t save him, that without his death then I would die along with him and all of the other Skylands when mother destroyed it. But maybe if I hadn’t joined her on the Outside then the knowledge that dad and I was still there would have stopped her. And if it did, would the war ever have ended?

So many what ifs that I could not possibly answer.

The only answer I had was: I didn’t know if I would save the live of my father. 

What kind of daughter would ever think like that?

I shook my head and forced those old thoughts out of my head, knowing that they would return later like they always did.

“Welcome, Barengar. Welcome to the former Skylands. My birthplace.”

“Thanks,” he responded hesitantly.

Despite his tone the sound of his voice comforted me. I had never brought another living person here. Perhaps not being completely alone would keep me off the disturbing pattern my mind tended to dwell on when I was here.

“Transferring control to you, Barengar. It is time that you learn how to walk these things. Just don’t fall over the edge,” I cautioned him.

“I’ll try not to,” he responded wryly.

Keeping my secondary illusory viewport trained on him I walked my own Archangel across the landing pad and the massive door responded to my presence and opened, magical lights flickering into life inside the hangar.

Keeping an eye out for Barengar in my secondary illusory screen I kept walking toward the workshop entrance. With some amusement I observed his first tentative steps in the cumbersome armour but with the alacrity I had come to expect of my apprentice he soon gained confidence and caught up with me.

“Is this really where the Skylands used to be?” He asked with awe in his voice.

“It is.”

“Why out here? It is so far away.”

“Precisely why I chose this place. That and there is magic in abundance here.”

He didn’t answer, nor did I care to elaborate. I led the way into the hangar and then shed my Archangel.

Barengar stumbled out of his own just as two artificial ley lines lanced out of the crystal mountings in the wall to connect to the nearly depleted power cores.

Barengar retrieved his axe and slung it on his back before using his longer legs to catch up to me. The inner doors swung open when they sensed my presence and I could hear Barengar take in a sharp breath.

I could not suppress a smug grin at his reaction. This place was like the workshop at the school, but at a far larger scale. The massive chamber that we were in were covered from roof to ceiling in thousands of spells, each one cycling through hypothetical spells to try to gauge their effects, if any. Even as we watched one of them pulsed a schematic through a network of crystal cables as thin as my hair into a contraption that was about my height and three times my width. It whirred and hissed before spitting out a sheaf of crystal paper with glowing diagrams on it. I walked up to it and retrieved the paper. I saw that there where dozens more of them in the hopper. I glanced at the glyphs and to my disappointment I identified the spell as a variation of one that had already been discovered. The shapes of the diagrams were completely new, but the effects could be achieved in a much more efficient way. Even so it was a valuable finding, though not immediately useful to me.

“Is that…” Barengar breathed, not being able to finish his sentence.

“Yup,” I answered casually. “New spell. Or rather, old effect but new method.”

“And all of this…” His voice trailed off again and he gestured wordlessly at the multitude of spells.

“Yes. The source of my ‘inventions’.”

“How does it work?”

I couldn’t help but smirk at him. I did appreciate his thirst for knowledge. I raised my hand and pulled a crystal into my hand and casually tossed it to him.

He caught it smoothly without using magic before peering at it.

“You tell me,” I challenged him. “That’s a copy of the schematic for the spell. Figure it out and then explain to me how it works.”

I turned from his flabbergasted expression and slid the useless paper back into the printing machine. It ate the paper hungrily before a loud hum emerged from beneath the floor. That was where the massive control spell framework was located. The lights in the chamber flickered as the massive spell consumed almost all of the available power to do its calculations before countless of the fine cables pulsed brightly. Each of the spells connected to the pulsing cables immediately flickered out before they cycled on to the next set of inputs. It would just be a waste of time trying to find the optimal solution for a spell whose effect was already known.

I summoned a crystal wand into my hand and quickly invoked the spell contained within. When I made the wand I had never really envisioned using it, but it had been easy and now proved to be useful.

The only purpose of the wand was to designate a volume to become a living area, a small two-room suite with an adjoining bathroom. There were currently only one of those in my workshop, mine, but now that Barengar would be living here with me I needed more accommodations.

A surge of magic responded to the spell I had cast and in my magesense I saw the area behind the wall evaporate and shift to create the necessary space and furnishings.

“Your bedroom will be over there, it should finish constructing in an hour. You may claim any free area as your own personal workspace and you may use any of the wands on that rack to create whatever infrastructure you need,” I told him with a wave at a rack of staffs and wands that I used for general construction. “Under no circumstances may you use that elevator over there without my express permission, got it?”

“Got it,” Barengar gruffed, obviously displeased that I would keep secrets from him even after his new oath. Perhaps it was foolish of me since I could now trust him implicitly but paranoia had kept me alive this long. I had known a lot of people that had been less careful than me, and most of them were dead now.

“Food will be served thrice a day, unfortunately nothing spectacular. Only traditional Skylander fare, the only thing that I am able to produce out here. Your first job is to figure out how that spell works. If I am downstairs when you do then you may hail me through yonder speaking tube and I will come and check up on you.”

“Understood, Marissa.”

“Good. Get to work.”

Without further ado I left him there and strode onto the elevator. I touched the stone on my choker and set my armour to change my armour into something more comfortable. The elevator sank into the floor and took me much farther down than one would expect. The master control spell framework for the meta-magic spells was far larger and far more complex than what one might think and it occupied almost the entire stretch of the floor and was almost a dozen meters thick. I had not built it myself of course. Actually I had, but not all of it. What I had created was a self-modifying spell that would grow and adapt as was required and it was now almost ten times as large and complex as I had originally made it. It was actually what had given me the knowledge and inspiration to create my current main project. One that surpassed even my orbital power distribution grid in priority.

The elevator slid from the ceiling of the chamber below and I was offered a top down view of the area below. The chamber was a large sphere, the inner surface of which was covered in spells drawn mostly by my own hand. A long bridge led into the center of the sphere and culminated in a circular platform. On that platform was a large orb that contained my experiment. Complex modules in varying shapes protruded from the sphere and I saw that one of them had melted and was smoking and sparking. I sighed. I had hoped that my latest alterations to that module would have been more stable but I had evidently been mistaken. The elevator slid into a socket on the floor and became almost indistinguishable from the platform. I walked toward the start of the bridge, my high heels click-clacking on the crystal floor. Just as I was about to cross the arch that demarcated the start of the bridge a form flashed into being by one of the columns. It was a spectral vision of a woman, casually leaning on the column with her arms crossed beneath ample breasts and one heeled boot raised to rest on the crystal pillar.

I stopped suddenly and beheld the image of the woman. Even though I had seen her often during the last thirty years I still could not believe that she was still with me. For a decade I had believed her… No, I had known her to be dead. I had seen her own memory of her death countless times. Yet here she was.

Her stern expression suddenly melted into a happy smile and she uncrossed her arms.

“Mother!” I exclaimed and rushed into her embrace and I was suddenly a little eleven year old girl again.

“Daughter,” Kyra Zorantis, Dragonbane, Bás Marbhadair, whispered into my hair and I felt her lips press through it and into my scalp. “All is well?”

“As well as it could be, mother,” I responded. “I’ve a new apprentice,” I told her cautiously.

“Oh? Is he or she here with you?” Mother asked with a sly gleam in her forever glowing violet eyes.

“He is. He is upstairs, bound by Oath not to come down here unless I invite him.”

“Is he handsome?”

“Mother!” I protested weakly.

“You will forgive this old ghost for being impatient. You’re nearly sixty years old and I want grandchildren!”

“I’m not sixty!” I protested with a pout.

Mother threw up her hands exasperatedly. “Fine. You are only fifty-nine. Not even close to my estimate.”

“I may be only fifty-seven, mother. Neither of us knew how old I was when you brought me Outside,” I groused.

“Still,” mother persevered. “I want grand-children. And you will not make any if you are cooped up all alone with your dead mother most of the year.”

“What has happened here?” I asked and abruptly changed the subject. During the last few years mother had been talking of grand-children more and more often, a subject that made me increasingly uncomfortable.

In all of my life I had only met a single man that I had considered procreating with. And he have been dead for three and a half decades. At least I believed him to be dead, I had never actually seen his corpse, unlike Valia’s, but he could not have survived the destruction of the Groundlands. Either he had died at the hands of the Bizhjerai Uncle Kenny had brought, or he would have died of exposure in the valley shortly afterward.

No, Zandor had been gone for a long time and I had not met his equal since.

“I don’t know, Marissa. I don’t understand half of what you are doing down here. One day that module just exploded and she has been silent ever since.”

A twinge of panic twisted my belly. “Silent?” I queried.

“Yes. Silent. Not even a twitch of illusion.”

“Shit,” I breathed and started walking across the bridge.

Mother fell in beside me silently, the click-clacking of her heels joining mine.

I had envied her for years. Even in life she had managed a grace on those ridiculously high heels of hers that I had never been able to replicate.

It was not fair. She was tall enough as it was, she really did not need the extra height her heels gave her. I desperately did. But at the moment I was more concerned with the broken piece of machinery.

I reached the large crystal sphere and sank my fingers into the grooves along the side. I quickly climbed up to the damaged module and inspected the damage.

After a few moments of investigation I let out a relieved sigh. The base of the module was not damaged at all, neither was the interface framework. Only the module itself had melted. I planted my feet securely before I grasped the module with both hands and twisted the large structure out of its socket. It was heavy, but nothing my enhanced physique could not handle. I twisted and heaved the broken module and tossed it down to mother. With a wave of her ghostly hand she caught it with magic and brought it gently down to the floor. I leaped off my perch and slowed my descent with magic, something that would have been monstrously wasteful of power in any other location but here and possibly at the school. I landed softly and sat down next to the module. It was almost the size of my torso, or it would have been had it not been partially melted.

“What could have caused this?” I asked aloud.

Mother sat down next to me. “A malfunction,” she deadpanned.

“Thanks for that illuminating observation,” I said and gave her a sidelong glare.

“You know better than to ask me those questions,” mother replied before a familiar expression of grief overcame her features. “I wish that your mom was here. She was always better at this kind of magic than I was.”

The old grief that ever boiled in my heart threatened to well over. Mom, Anjali Bhizjerai, had taken to magical construction like a fish to water even though she had not known a single spell before she met mother.

Yes, she would have been able to help me. But she was dead. Truly dead, her life ended by a stray bullet to the head at the battle of Utopeia.

I checked the connecting socket in search of interference of the spells there. If I had placed them too tightly they might have interfered and degraded and if that had happened to the main power input… That could have caused a power surge that destabilized the whole spell matrix which could have resulted in this mess. But the frameworks that I found was intact. Sadly, I could only inspect the physical substrate of the spells, the enchantments themselves having long since faded. I could glean nothing from this melted lump of crystal and with a disgruntled sigh I discarded it to the side in the pile of previously broken components. Luckily I had a few spare components. I had learned early on to never make a single instance of a component so that I would save time if and when they actually broke down. I walked over to the cart with spare modules and hoisted a copy of the broken one out of its depth. This one would have the same flaw as the other one, but at least it would work for a time before failing and maybe this time I would be able to tell what might be wrong with it. I just had to observe the failure.

I handed the component over to mother before I climbed back onto the machine. She sent it up to me in a cradle of magic and I slotted it into place and twisted it into a locked position with a grunt.

Then I carefully connected the main power input and observed the module flicker to life. When I was satisfied that it was igniting properly I glided back down to join mother and waited expectantly.

The components flashed brightly, magic flowing between the protruding crystals where the arcs combined into a single bright ray that shot toward us. But it did not burn through my body, instead it halted a few steps from me and a small focusing crystal started forming in the air above my head. When it was large enough it seemed to suck the magic into itself before refracting it in a dazzling array of colours. The colours coalesced as the module learned how to form illusions before the swirl of energy formed a glowing humanoid shape. It was reminiscent of mother in its method of materializing, but it lacked her intrinsic method of interfacing with the world. This creation was in essence a ghost while my mother was something more. The light took the shape of a woman, something between mother and myself. She was taller than me, but shorter than mother. She had narrower hips than me, but broader than mothers. Smaller breasts than mine, but larger than mothers even though the margins between us was slight. It was as if the light had chosen a form in the direct middle of mine and mothers. Which I knew that it had. Unlike mother this shape had no colour apart from the blue-white glow of pure magic and it had nothing more than the most rudimentary of facial features. The eyes appeared more almond shaped than last time and the lips a touch fuller with a little more of a pout to them. The hair was longer, but it still floated around her head as if she was submerged in water.

“Kyra. Marissa,” a voice greeted us, but the lips of the construct before us did not move and the voice came from no discernible direction.

“Epitome,” I smiled at the construct. “I am glad to see that you are safe.”

“Why would I not be?” Epitome answered with an emotionless voice.

“You communications module had broken. I feared that it would have harmed you.”

“It did not, Marissa. Each of my spell modules have a surge-protection framework installed that will isolate any malfunctioning part of me. You should know this. You made them yourself.”

“I know, Epitome. I know. But even so I worried.”

“Why did you worry if you knew that it would not harm me?”

I sighed. Even after all the decades I had worked on Epitome she still struggled to understand emotions. And she was still utterly incapable of having them herself.

“It is a thing that humans do,” I answered lamely. I had learned years ago that it was useless to attempt to explain emotions to a creature that could not experience them. It was like explaining how to do magic to someone who had not even extended a tendril of their Soul before.

“I see. Humans worry despite knowing better. Thank you for teaching me this, Marissa.”

“You’re welcome, Epitome,” I answered automatically.

How would I help her understand humanity? For years I had tried conversing with her, and for several more years I had waited for my meta-magic spells to produce something that I had never even considered that might push me in the correct direction. But it would appear that the solution I had worked out a few years ago might be the only remaining option, distasteful as it was. I just needed to solve the last piece of that particular puzzle.

“Are you well?” Mother asked Epitome suddenly when the silence dragged on.

“I am fully operational, Kyra. Does all of your organs function at optimal efficiency?”

Mother chuckled. “No, Epitome. I am dead, remember? I am a mere apparition.”

“Yes, of course I remember. I was merely attempting to perform the concept of ‘politeness’ that we spoke of.”

I sighed. It did not appear as if Epitome had evolved since last I was here.

“Epitome, does a man have the right to rob another?” I asked the construct suddenly.

“In general, no.” She responded instantly.

“In general?” I prompted her.

“In general, taking the possessions of someone else is wrong,” the construct reiterated.

“When might it be right?”
