“If, for instance, the man being robbed have more resources than he needs and the one doing the robbing will die without it. If he does not rob then there will be only one man left.”

“And why would that be a bad thing?”

“All life have value. You taught me that.”

“But I also taught you that everyone has a right to the product of their own labour.”

“You did. But two lives are better than one.”

“Even if the resources is not enough for both of them to live well? Is two miserable lives better than one good one?”

“Yes.”

“What if the first man defends himself? What if he will fight to the death to keep his resources?”

Epitome was silent for a while. “Murder is wrong and his attackers life will be forfeit.”

“And then there would be no lives instead of two.”

“Yes.”

Epitome stared blankly at me, uncomprehending.

I sighed and turned to face my mother. “She is only reciting what she has learned. She can learn by observation, but she does not understand. Can’t extrapolate.”

Mother gave me a wry grin. “Perhaps you were hasty in naming her ‘Epitome of Morality’.”

“Perhaps I was.” I took a deep breath and slowly let it out. “I must return upstairs. I have one final solution I’d like to try with her. Would you keep talking to her and observe that communications module? I need to know how it is malfunctioning so that I can fix it.”

Mother’s face softened when she noticed my weariness and her crystal hand reached out to smooth my hair behind my ear.

“Of course, my daughter. Whatever you need.”

“Thank you, mother,” I whispered and leaned my cheek into her hand.

Then I abruptly walked back to the elevator and returned to the ground floor. I found Barengar by a desk he must have constructed while I was downstairs and he had extracted the meta-magic spell from the crystal and kept it just below ignition level so that he could inspect the three-dimensional symbols.

“Have you worked it out yet?” I asked him while expecting him to throw his hands up in defeat. It had taken me a long time to perfect that spell and I would not expect him to figure it out in less than an hour.

“Not completely,” he grumbled. “These parts makes sense,” he said and indicated almost half of the spell.

I could barely keep my eyes from widening in surprise.

“Explain those to me,” I commanded while approaching him rapidly. A chair spun around and slid beneath my rear even as I was sitting down, one of the gems in my skull reacting to my unconscious desire to sit down.

“These parts are power input, very basic. These are some sort of sensory cluster. I can see heat, light, tactile and a few other rudimentary sensors. But these ones I have no idea what they do,” he admitted with an exasperated sound. “And over here we have some sort of complex iterator. I think it might be some sort of greedy heuristic to plot a line through three-dimensional spells? I assume that it tries to form glyphs but I can’t make sense in how it actually implements it.”

During the time he spoke I felt my eyebrows climb my forehead. I was impressed with his progress and I realized that I had severely underestimated the man. He may look like a warrior but beneath that fierce exterior lay a keen mind.

I made a snap judgment. “Alright, you have impressed me, Barengar. You can put that spell aside though I want you to keep studying it when you have time. But I have something else to show you now.”

“Right now?”

“Yes, right now. I want to introduce you to the project before it is time for supper.”

“Very well,” he responded and dismissed his spell by swiping his hand through it.

“Follow me,” I said and stood up to lead him through the workshop and into the back. I led him through a large door into a massive open space where a huge crystal cylinder lay. Massive torrents of power was being funneled into it, materializing into liquid magic.

“What is this?” Barengar gasped when his magesense was blinded by the power in the room.

“This, my dear apprentice. This is the future of humanity.”

###

Barengar was so engrossed in my explanation of my orbital power distribution net that he refused to pause for dinner. Instead we brought it into the workshop with us and discussed as I ate.

He continuously pointed out aspects of my plan from angles that I had not anticipated that made me revise and rethink parts of my strategy and before I knew what was going on I was huddled over a desk doing calculations on a piece of crystal paper with Barengar looming over my shoulder. Mostly he just struggled with keeping up with my calculations but every now and again he questioned a formula and more often than not he was correct.

Eventually I added the final swirl to the last Rune and leaned back into the chair and rubbed my gritty eyes.

“What time is it?” I asked, not really expecting an answer.

“An hour after midnight,” Barengar answered.

“We should get some rest,” I sighed wearily. I was tired, but satisfied with our progress.

I stood up and started to leave when Barengar spoke again.

“Marissa…”

“Yes?” I turned around to hear him out.

“This thing is supposed to be the main lifting engine of this space vessel of yours?”

“Yes,” I agreed. We had gone over this several times.

“And its sole purpose is to lift the actual vessel into space where it will disconnect and another engine will take over.”

“Again, yes.”

“This all seems terribly complex, why not just cast the spell into the void and then channel the magical power directly to it? Construct the thing in space where it is supposed to be in the first place?”

I opened my mouth to dismiss the idea and found that I had no words.

Why hadn’t I thought of that? I was supposed to be the best construction Mage in the world, dammit!

I knew the reason. I had gotten stuck in the old ways. The ancient bunker I had found had contained the secrets of ancient civilizations and I had merely taken their already existing solution and adapted it to function by magic.

I had not even considered that it might even be easier to just go wholesale magic.

“You’re right,” I finally breathed. “That would probably be easier, and cheaper.”

I hurried back to the desk and sat back down, my fatigue forgotten. I didn’t question Barengar when he dragged a spare chair over and sat down next to me, I just scooted over so that he could have some area of his own to work with.

He stared intently at the paper before me as my hand flew across the pages, drawing up the formulae.

Without speaking he noticed one of the formulae that I left for later while I forged on to the more crucial areas. Barengar started working on that formulae.

He couldn’t possibly understand the grand scheme of the system I was sketching out but he was more than proficient enough to understand most of the parts.

My mind was spinning, but somehow the math flowed out of me and before I knew it dozens of pages was filled with script.

Barengar diligently worked on his own schematics but a quick glance at his work told me that he hadn’t made any major mistakes.

I rubbed at my gritty eyes and glanced up at the clock on the wall. Almost noon. We had worked non stop since midnight. Fatigue crashed into me and a sudden yawn cracked my jaw.

Decades of use of Blood Magic had honed my control and with just a twitch of my mind I reinvigorated myself. I didn’t want to disturb Barengar in his work so I leaned back and stared at the components.

In the end it would be relatively simple to place a construction spell in geosynchronous orbit above my workshop. It would require a tremendous amount of magical power. Far more than the rocket approach but during such a short time that the total was down to almost three quarters. The key insight had been that it would not be enough to just launch the spell straight up or it would come right back down. It also had to be given a massive momentum in the direction of the rotation of the earth in order to stay up there, but it could not accelerate too quickly or it would be pushed out of the artificial ley line and fall right back down. It had to accelerate in a rate proportional to the angular velocity for the earths rotation at that height. Without the ancient knowledge there would simply have been no possibility at all for the two of us to figure this out in just a night and a morning. It had taken the ancient civilizations decades to figure all of this out. We truly were standing on the backs of giants.

This method was all well and good in placing things in a single geosynchronous orbit, but if we wanted to alter the orbit then the satellite would have to provide its own propulsion. Which the rockets that I had already designed could certainly do.

So the seed spell would have to contain a focusing lens to absorb power, a control spell that could interpret advanced commands, a materialization spell that could create rockets and fuel and a focusing lens that could project an artificial ley line back to the surface. And a storage spell that would store excess power in a dense power core that would expand and evaporate on demand. All but the first component that I had worked years developing. 

A sudden thought stopped me in my tracks.

I had already figured out, aided by ancient knowledge, how to send communications through an artificial ley line. Was it possible to send spells in the same way?

If it was, then once we had a proper satellite in orbit and connected by artificial ley line, then we would be able to pass new spells to it. We could send new modules directly to it for construction, almost like a shipyard in space.

But that would take further years of research and trials to figure out. There were simply too many variables involved for a human to keep track of.

I put my face in my hand. I really was tired.

How had I not had that thought earlier?

This was a task best suited for meta-magic. Trial and error on large scales. 

The old humans hadn’t had the same type of rapid prototyping capabilities like I did.

When they needed to test something their researchers first had to run endless simulations, perform countless calculations before their material engineers had to craft the components to exacting standards before the mechanical engineers could assemble it. It had to be tested over and over and adapted in a process that could take years or even decades.

I could modify and create new components in hours by materializing magic. Out here I had the full power of the Great Geyser to draw from, an almost inexhaustible source of power.

I just had to offload the whole feedback loop to a rather intelligent meta-magic spell.

One such the one I had in the basement.

“I have to go downstairs,” I suddenly announced.

Barengar just grunted in response, still totally engrossed in his work.

I rushed from the room and descended to the bottom level, my foot tapping impatiently at the slow elevator.

Mother gave me a single look and deduced from my expression that she should not interrupt me. Instead she just sat cross legged on the floor and regarded me expectantly with a small smile on her full lips.

“Epitome!” I called out before I was even halfway across her bridge.

The illusory female appeared before her core housing and a smile graced her lips. “Marissa,” she greeted me and this time her lips did move and her voice appeared to come from her avatar.

“Mother taught you how to speak?” I said, surprised. I had been trying to do that for months.

“She did,” the spell responded. “Though I suppose that is not the reason you came down here again.”

“It was not,” I replied, remembering my mission.

“I have a mission for you, Epitome.”

“I am eager to serve,” she replied without emotion.

I knew that she was not truly self aware and that the response was just a platitude. She did not have choice. Was incapable of even comprehending that she might have a choice.

“I want to give you the ability to connect to the workshop and learn how to construct things with materialized magic, and to cast spells of your own.”

“I do not know how to cast spells,” Epitome answerer placidly.

“That’s fine,” I said and waved her concerns away. “I will give you access to a vast array of meta-magic spells that are discovering and cataloging spells. You should be able to divine their purposes and use them for yourself. You are allowed to use as much power as you need to, any excess power that me or Barengar or any of our constructs are not using. They have secondary priority, primary priority is to keep the workshop floating and the fungus farms active. Understood?”

“Understood, Marissa.”

“Furthermore I want you to be cautious. Never use a spell with enough power that you are uncertain of the outcome except in small increments.”

“I will consider safety a paramount concern,” she responded calmly.

I shouldn’t have been as impressed as I was that she had managed to discern the core of my worry without me having to express it explicitly. I always forgot that she was entirely capable of learning, and that she did so far faster than a human. She just was not imaginative and had no initiative of her own. Neither did she understand emotions or morality, which was the whole point of her existence.

But until I could figure out how to teach her that she would serve as the mastermind of my workshop.

A slow smile spread across my features. “Very well then, giving you access to the main control node should be simple enough, hold on.”

I closed my eyes and focused my intent on one of the stones on the side of my head. The meta-magic spells embedded within reacted and spells flew out of it.

Why hadn’t I considered giving Epitome access to my workshop earlier? I had basically already done something similar with the cores that I had implanted in my brain. They served as a similar interface between my brain and magic as she would be between me and my workshop and by extension the power net that I planned to build across the globe. 

I could already sense the workshop shifting slightly, a channel being bored between floors to connect the workshop control core with Epitome’s communication module. An artificial ley line projector and receiver was already materializing in the control node, crystal cables already snaking into the core to connect to the correct areas. Soon she would have full control over everything.

“Mother, would you oversee the connection? I’m dead on my feet here.”

Mother shimmered and seemed to dissolve before reforming in front of me. She had gotten used to being awake and projecting out of her dagger that lay safely nestled in the most secure area of the workshop.

“Of course, my daughter. I don’t sleep any longer, but you do. I will take it from here.”

I threw my arms around her and felt her crystal fingers stroke my back. It was almost like when she had still been alive. She just lacked the warmth and the solid physical presence that she had had when I was a child. I still missed her, but this spectral version of mother was all that I had had since her death and I was more than happy that she hadn’t truly died along with mom.

She gently pushed me away. “Rest, daughter,” she admonished me.

I yawned and nodded before I gave Epitome one last look. She smiled and inclined her head at me.

I went to bed.

###

I woke up at dawn the next day and wearily left my bedroom to attend to nature’s call. I drank my fill of stale desalinated water and then stretched my back. I may look like a young woman, but I felt far from it.

I guess I should have been thankful that I truly did not feel my age. I still remembered grandmother complaining about her aching joints when she was my age but the only issue I had was a stiff back in the mornings. I set my choker to manifest comfortable clothes and padded barefoot across the cold crystal floors to fetch breakfast. Yet again I mused that I should install heating spells for the floors and yet again I knew that I would prioritize something else and forget all about it until the next morning when I repeated the same walk across icy floors.

To my surprise I found Barengar outside inspecting the meta-magic spell array. I would have expected him to still be resting. He was eating a plate of fresh fungus absently while I scrutinized the spells.

“Good morning,” I greeted him.

“Morning,” he gruffed. “Is this all there is to eat?”

“I’m afraid so,” I answered his complaint with a wry smile. “It is the only thing that can grow out here. It is the very same type of fungus that used to sustain all of the Skylands.”

“It’s terrible,” he stated flatly.

“It is a bit bland, I agree. Perhaps we can bring more palatable provisions the next time we are on the mainland. Until then, the fungus will have to do.”

“I suppose that it will.”

I left him there to find sustenance for myself and I returned shortly with a plate of my own.

“I finished with the calculations before I went to be,” Barengar reported. “Do you want to double check it?”

“No,” I shook my head. “Not now at least. From what I could see before I left you you had it well in hand. I trust that you would have alerted me if you were uncertain.”

Barengar stared at me with his brown eyes for a while before he nodded, accepting my words as the simple truth.

“I think that we have enough knowledge to try to send a spell into space.” He was silent for a few moments before he shook his head with a chuckle. “That’s a sentence I would never have believed that I would say in all seriousness.”

I chuckled. “I would have thought the same a decade ago. There’s been a lot of preparation for this moment, Barengar. It started centuries ago, before the apocalypse.”

His brow furrowed in confusion and I realized that I had not explained to him the source of my knowledge about spacecraft.

“Alright, Barengar. Sit down.”

He did and focused his full attention on me. I put a piece of fungus in my mouth and chewed it while I considered where to start. I grimaced at the stale taste of the horrid food and then launched into my tale.

I told him about the bunker I had found while exploring the mountain range in search of more savage nests that I might have to clean out.

About the strange dormant machines and how I had figured out from the labels that they were expecting current. I told him about how I had in my youth read a tome in Dun Traelak that the ancients had ‘harnessed thunder’ and that this ‘electricity’ that they ancients had used was the same force that compelled lightning to strike. From there it had only taken me a few months to manage to cast a spell that would convert magical power into electrical power in a way that the machines had accepted. And the knowledge contained within…

Barengar’s eyes lit up when I told him about the information contained within the machines.

“May I see these machines?” He asked excitedly.

I nodded. “I have already made plans to move them here, if possible, but I have not dared to do so. If one of them were to be damaged in transit…”

“I understand,” Barengar responded, her brow furrowed in thought. Then his lips stretched into a boyish grin. “Perhaps we do not need to move them here, perhaps we only need to move the information.”

“What do you mean?” I asked, curious.

“You said that the information was stored on some sort of spinning metal tablet that these machines could interpret somehow?”

“Yes,” I agreed. “They seemed to read the tablets using light and electricity.”

“That sounds similar to how spells are stored in crystals,” he pointed out. “What if we can translate this old mechanism for storing knowledge into the magical method, then we could use the same kind of communication spells that the Archangels uses to transmit the information here and store them in power cores.”

I tapped my lower lip while I thought. “That might just work. But how do we translate the information? I have used my magesense to inspect these metal tablets and all I can find are alterations in the electric charge in the metal itself. It is pure gibberish.”

“Yet the machines translate it into words.”

“True,” I drawled, not really understanding what he was getting at.

He pointed at the array of meta-magic spells all around us and a dim light lit up in the back of my mind. 

“Go on,” I urged him.

“Posit a spell similar to these ones. We manually translate a heap of information into magical format and feed it to the spell, then we translate the patterns in the metal disks… Somehow… Into a format that the spells will understand. Then they can work out the translation and the patterns used.”

“But?” I asked with an arched eyebrow since I could tell that there was more coming.

“But I don’t know how to do that translating, nor how much of it might be needed for a comprehensive lexicon.”

I nodded slowly. “I might have a solution for that, one that bypasses the translation problem.”

“But?” Barengar prompted me with sly grin, turning my own methods against me.

“It has its own issues that needs solving. But at least those problems I have an inkling as how to solve.”

Barengar popped the last piece of fungus into his mouth and frowned around the mouthful. “Tell me?”

“All in due time, my apprentice. First I have a task for you.” I raised my hand to forestall his protest. “Relax, it is a part of the solution for our little knowledge migration project.”

I paused and waited for him to settle back down. “I need you to create a body out of materialized magic. Nothing fancy, something humanoid that is capable of navigating and interfacing with the world. It should be equipped with senses equivalent to those of humans, though touch is the least important. Actually, smell and taste are the least important. Touch, sight and hearing are essential. It should also be able to connect to an artificial ley line, you can steal the construct from the Archangel for that part.”

To his credit he did not ask me why. Instead his face lit up with interest at the challenge.

“What are you going to do?” Was the only question he asked.

“I am going to prepare for a space launch,” I winked and stood up.

I caught a glimpse of his excited smile before I turned and strode into the hangar area. I stopped in front of the massive rocket and with a wistful sigh I deactivated the construction spells and invoked a massive deconstruction spell that would return all of the energy bound into the massive construct to the power cores in the lowest levels of my workshop. With another few thoughts I instructed the control module of my workshop to expand the buffer crystals a tenfold and scale up the power gatherers accordingly. We were going to need a lot of magical power in the coming days.

Now it was time for the difficult parts. I needed a spell construct that would be able to create a massive artificial ley line, one that would be able to remain stable up to the altitude of geosynchronous orbit. According to the information in the bunker that was almost thirty-six thousand kilometers. Almost three times the diameter of the Earth, according to the same data. I shook my head slowly, suddenly doubting myself.

Had I finally fallen prey to my own hubris? The longest artificial ley line I had ever heard about was the one from the Skylands to Utopeia, and that one was barely two thousand and change kilometers. And that one had had several relay stations to refocus the ley line. How the fuck was I supposed to have a ley line cover fifteen times the distance? Would I even be able to have relay stations? They would require much power each to keep suspended, maybe even more than the Great Geyser could output. I gritted my teeth. It had to be possible! I had worked for too long on this project to give up now. If it was too expensive to keep the ley line open for long stretches of time then I would simply have to optimize for quick transfers. Oversize the projectors and receivers to transmit a massive storm of magical power in a rapid torrent, store it in an inefficient incorporeal buffer spell and then sedately store the power in a dense crystal on site. That might work.

But what about Wild Magic? A burst that powerful was bound to manifest in spontaneous and unpredictable ways. Perhaps it would not matter? Perhaps the burst would be over so quickly that any damage the Wild Magic would cause would be negligible? Or could I contain the ley line in shields powerful enough to restrain the events? But that would make the whole transfer even less efficient.

I knew roughly where the breakpoint of dangerous wild magic was, and it was far below the power levels I was contemplating. I pushed the thought aside. A problem for another day.

Now I had a massive projector to design and construct.

I focused through my crystal eye and had it project my schematics suite that outlined my workshop. I shifted into prototype mode and started sketching out the spell constructs that would be required, and the extensions to the workshop. It would be a whole new upper level. A launch deck.

I idly connected to the control module and instructed Epitome through the newly connected ley line that her first task would be to construct a copy of one of the Archangels in the hangar bay.

We would need a third one soon.

## CHAPTER SIX Barengar

It had not been simple, but I finally believed that I was finished with that avatar construct that Marissa had tasked me with building. Her requirements had been rather loose, so I had chosen the form and spells by myself.

I had modeled it all after a human skeleton. Those had been proved to work for interacting with the world and I had observed Marissa’s prosthetic fingers enough times to remember how the motive spells worked.

What I did not know was how whatever was supposed to animate this avatar would deal with balance and staying upright, but I assumed that Marissa had some sort of meta-magic spell that would learn how to interface with the control nexus embedded within the skull. I had included a bunch of sensor spells that would gather spatial data via rapid fire scrying pulses and that data could possibly be used to create a sort of sense of balance.

The construct itself was rather simple, just the basic outline of a human skeleton with some minor differences. The torso was covered by a crystal plating, jointed like an armour to afford maneuverability, and inside instead of lungs were two large power cores to maintain functionality in low magic zones, at least for a while. The skull was mostly filled with sensor clusters and a large blank spell stone that could be inscribed with any spell that Marissa had in mind for the purpose. That stone itself had cost an almost astronomical amount of power, but Marissa had casually waved my concerns aside. I still had not gotten used to how much power we really had at our disposal out here. The rest of the skull was composed of a magic to voice modulator, a design I had shamelessly stolen from the communicator that was installed in the Archangels.

I looked over the design one final time, checking the hinged bones and the telekinetic enchantments that would motivate them. I already had plans for an upgraded version where each limb would be supported by a crystal framework that should be able to hold position by passive friction, I just had not decided if that was a good idea or not. This version would constantly consume power just to remain standing but would hardly consume any extra power while in motion. The other version would barely drain the power cores while stationary but would cost significantly more in motion to overcome the friction. I wanted to discuss that with Marissa before I applied the upgrades I had planned out.

And that is what I decided to do, so I stood and stretched my back. I was awarded with several loud pops and I groaned in satisfaction. Then I started hunting for my mistress.

I was starting to get a little frustrated when I could not find her and I assumed that she was downstairs again, working on that secret project that she still had not told me about.

I was a little annoyed that she still did not trust me enough to tell me about it. I was sworn to secrecy, literally incapable of betraying her trust. She should have told me.

Calm down, Barengar, I told myself. She will tell you in due time. 

I shook my head to clear it of the negative emotions and started walking toward the speaking tube to check if she was downstairs.

On my way over I saw a door that I had not noticed previously. I thought that I had explored the entire workshop, barring the forbidden lower level.

Curious, I walked over to the door and gently pushed on it.

To my surprise it slid open easily and I stepped inside. I instantly realized where I was. This was Marissa’s personal workroom. Large panoramic windows gave a breathtaking view of the ocean stretching as far as the eye can see. It only took me a glance at the sun to verify that the view was facing west. Toward the coast of the Bizhjerai lands. Marissa’s home.

I started turning around to leave, not wanting to invade this private space, when my eyes caught on a display cabinet. Mesmerized, I walked over to it and peered through the transparent crystal.

It was beautiful. It was an axe, similar to the one stowed in my own chambers, but bigger. Not too big for me, but on the verge of being too cumbersome. The head was large, the edge curved and the beard of the axe looped back to serve as an excellent hook. The top of the handle held a sharp spike that would easily pierce mail armour if enough weight was placed behind the thrust. The back of the axe had a hammerhead to counterbalance the blade. A perfect all-around weapon. The head to bite into flesh, the hammer to deal with armour and the spike for added versatility. Upon closer inspection I saw that the pommel had a dagger attachment about the length of my hand and curved backward. Perfect to use with a push cut or a thrust if the axe was ever out of position. The wielder of this weapon just had to be careful to not hurt themselves with it when they swung the axe around.

And I have not even mentioned the enchantments yet!

There where several spell stones embedded in the haft, some of them blanks, but I saw at least one heat-based attack, one cold-based and one that I thought would produce lightning.

There where also some enchantments on the haft itself that I could not really make sense of. I got the impression that they had something to do with telekinesis and spatial coordinates but before I could ponder them more I saw that there where more objects in the display case. 

An armoured sleeve that would cover the right arm as well as a very thick left arm vambrace.

The sleeve had the same kind of spatial and telekinesis enchantments as the axe as well as several power cores to fuel it all. I reached out my fingers to touch the sleeve but my fingers were stopped by cold crystal.

“Beautiful, is it not?”

I jumped at the sound and spun around guiltily, discreetly shuffling my feet so that my body would obscure the fingerprints I had left on the crystal.

“Yes. It is. But you do not strike me as the type of fighter to prefer an axe, especially not one of that size,” I blurted.

“No, I am not. I made it a long time ago for a friend of mine,” she said sadly.

“Oh, and you did not give it to him?” Too late her sad inflection registered in my mind and I realized how stupid my words had been.

Her gaze dropped to the floor and she turned away, strolling toward her desk.

“No. He’s been dead for thirty-five years. I don’t even know why I made that weapon for him. Maybe I was foolishly hoping that he had survived but I have not found him despite my searching…”

Awkward silence descended between us, her standing by the window and staring out at the ocean with her back to me.

“You should take it. It serves no purpose in that cabinet.”

“Are you certain?” I asked, surprised beyond belief that she would gift me something so valuable. Especially something that held great sentimental value to her.

“I am,” she responded resolutely and waved her hand. The cabinet sprang open and I reverently walked closer to it.

Slowly my fingers reached for the sleeve and I lifted it down gently. I slid my arm into the sleeve and struggled with the strap that was supposed to go across my chest to join with a buckle to the one that would go around my arm. But it was impossible to reach on my own.

“Let me help you with that,” Marissa murmured from next to me. I had not even noticed her approach.

Her fingers gathered the malleable crystal straps and brushed against my side as she buckled them up and tightened the straps.

I flexed my fingers inside the articulated glove and marveled at how the crystal plates locked together without restricting me. I glanced at her face and saw a wan smile grace her lips before I turned my attention to the axe.

“Go on, pick it up,” she urged me.

I was more than eager to comply. The haft fit my hands as if it had been made for me and I took a few steps back to take a few test swings with it.

It sang through the air, perfectly balanced. It was heavier than my own axe, but not so heavy that I would not be able to bulk up enough to swing it properly. Especially not with a little application of Blood Magic to aid my muscles in their growth.

“Did you see the enchantments on the haft and the sleeve?”

“Yes,” I responded quickly, eager to learn what they did.

“When you activate the enchantments on the sleeve it will connect with the axe and pull it toward you. If you ever drop it, or throw it, in combat then you will not be without weapons for long.

My brows rose of their own volition.

“Truly? What about the exponential drop off in magic efficiency?

Her smile brightened at my question.

“That is still a concern, but the enchantments are precise enough to focus the power to an extent that it will reach the axe as long as it is within a reasonable distance.”

“What is a reasonable distance?” I asked immediately.

“Farther than you can possible throw the axe,” Marissa chuckled. “Just don’t drop it down a cliff or into the water. A few dozen steps, no more.”

“Understood. What about the bracer?”

“Glad you asked. Put it on.”

I hastened to obey and slipped the thick brace into my wrist. It covered my entire forearm and was almost seven and a half centimeters thick. It was also rather heavy and unbalanced, but the point of gravity was close enough to the bones of my arm to not throw off my axe swings dramatically. Especially not with some muscle grafting and practice.

“What’s up with all of these crystal layers?” I asked.

Marissa stepped back with her natural eye gleaming. “Hold your arm in front of you, make sure to keep your face away. Then activate the enchantment in the vambrace.”

Puzzled, I did as instructed. And almost shit myself in surprise when the layered crystal unfolded rapidly and interlocked to form a large round shield in a flash of magic.

“Pretty neat, huh?” Marissa smirked at my astonished expression.

“I guess…”

“I learned a long time ago that even though you can project your own shield, or materialize one if you have the time, to block most attacks, sometimes you need something that is faster.”

I grunted in response. I’d had the same thought a few times since the day that we were fired upon in Lahar.

“This particular shield I designed decades ago, so it might not stop all modern ammunition. But look behind the boss of the shield, there’s another set of activation and control runes there.”

I peeked behind the boss and true enough, I saw several runes there. I channeled power into the largest one and a magical shield projected out of the boss to cover the shield.

“The control runes will allow you to expand the magical shield to cover a larger area, though it will not be as efficient as the crystal shield,” Marissa explained. Then her smile broadened smugly. “The main perk of using that shield is that it is powered by a core in the vambrace and as such you will not suffer any backlash from impacts.”

My eyebrow climbed my forehead at that. That would be extremely useful in a fight.

“This will be invaluable, I am sure. Thank you for this gift, Marissa.”

She waved my words away and averted her gaze, obviously uncomfortable with my gratitude.

“It is nothing. Actually, you are doing me a favour in putting it to use instead of leaving it here to collect dust.” She cleared her throat and appeared to gather her wits for a moment before continuing. “I assume that you were looking for me when you came in here?”

“Ah, yes. Yes, I was. I finished with the avatar you asked me to build.”

The slight awkwardness between us evaporated and she leaned closer with an excited grin. “Really? That quick? May I see it?”

I was a bit taken aback by her surprise. Was she astonished that I had completed what she believed to be a task more difficult to me than it was? Or had she expected me to be less skilled than I was?

I decided to not presume anything so instead I shrugged. “Yes, of course. I would love your feedback on the design.”

I led her from the room over to the workbench I had constructed for this particular project. It was a long gurney and I let her pass me to inspect it. She immediately lifted one of the arms and tested the articulation of the hand and elbow before she peered closely at the motivation spells. Her crystal eye flashed blue and illuminated the creepy crystal skeleton.

“Yes,” she muttered. “This is good spell work, Barengar. But what is this?”

She leaned forward until she was almost pressing her forehead against the blank faceplate of the avatar. “Ah, control nexus, good. Good. The sensory cables seems well placed… I did not teach you that.”

She twisted her head and looked at me over her shoulder, still bent over the avatar. “Did you study the crystal cables of the meta-magic array to implement this?”

“Yeah,” I admitted and scratched at the back of my neck.

“Excellent. I wouldn’t have thought of that, I think. I would probably have connected them by a tiny artificial ley line but this is much more efficient and stable. Very good.”

She paused for a moment before she stood back up and tapped her lower lip. “Although, the cables might be knocked loose in combat and that would prove incredibly detrimental.” She hummed.

“You did not tell me that this was to be a combat model?” I griped gently. If I had known that then I would have made the internals of the construct more robust.

“Hm? Oh, no. This is a civilian model. More of a proof of concept actually. But I’d like to build some combat models at some point.”

She paused again before she graced me with a warm smile. “I really like the forethought of placing a blank spell stone as the brain. That was really clever of you.”

I inclined my head in gratitude, basking in her praise. Just the fact that she admitted that I had figured out a solution that she would not have thought of made my heart swell with pride.

“May I ask what this is for?”

Her face went suddenly blank and her eyes, flesh and crystal both, seemed to stare into my Soul.

“I suppose it is time for you to be introduced to my primary project.”

My belly lurched when I heard her words. The fact that she was so hesitant in introducing me to her project almost made me sick with apprehension.

“Come, follow,” she ordered me and walked around the gurney toward the elevator. One of the stones on the side of her skull flashed and belched forth a spell. The gurney lifted slightly off the floor and drifted along with her and I hastened to follow. Cold churned in my stomach as the elevator slowly started descending, and it only got worse when Marissa remained silent. For long agonizing moments we descended into the darkness until it eventually broke into a brilliant light. We emerged from the roof of a giant chamber, spherical in shape and festooned with hundreds if not thousands of enchantments. A smaller, but still large, sphere dominated the very center of the chamber, connected by a bridge to a walkway that encircled the equator of the outer sphere. Marissa walked purposefully onto the bridge and I followed hesitantly. Was the sphere her experiment?

“So you finally decided to bring the boy down here?” A voice said from behind me. I spun on my heel, hand snaking up behind my back to curl around the haft of my new axe.

But what I saw was not what I had been expecting. By the sound of the voice I had expected a woman, and that is what I saw. 

What I had not expected was that she would be a ghost.

She was tall, almost as tall as I was, composed entirely out of magic and slightly translucent. Her shining violet eyes bored through my skull even as her ephemeral lips where spread into a slight smile. She was clad in the manner of Skylanders, all indigo crystal cloth, and armoured with a light corset and armour plates on her limbs. Her hair was a darker shade of violet than her eyes and half of her scalp was hairless and the skin was marred by burn scars. Much like Marissa’s.

My eyes narrowed in suspicion. I had only heard of one Skylander that would fit that description, especially the skeletal crystal hand that jutted out of the severed stump just beneath her left elbow.

“Barengar, meet my mother. Kyra Zorantis. Mother, meet my apprentice.”

Kyra Zorantis, the legendary Bás Marbhadair, smiled at me and offered me her hand in greeting.

“A pleasure to finally meet you, Barengar,” she murmured.

I stared at her proffered hand for too long before I let go of my axe and I clasped her wrist in the warrior’s handshake. I had expected my hand to pass straight through her incorporeal form but my palm struck not-quite flesh. She was not as warm as a breathing woman would be, not as substantial as one. But she had a definite presence in this world.

“The pleasure is all mine,” I lied. In truth, the ghostly woman sent chills down my spine.

The dead were supposed to stay dead.

“I believed you dead,” I commented tactlessly.

The ghost winced and averted her burning gaze. “I am. As close as one can come to death without actually entering the void fully. An old enchantment keep me coherent in this Archon form.”

Archon. That would explain so much. She was not truly dead. Her body had burned away from the magical power that coursed through it, leaving only a disembodied Soul maintained by its own prodigious power.

But such power was fated to burn away and the Soul fading along with it. How had she managed to stay coherent for almost forty years?

A question for another time, I decided when I saw that Marissa had kept walking. I turned from the Archon and hurried to catch up and I was more than a little surprised when Kyra fell in beside me.

“What do you think of my daughter, young man?”

“She is a powerful and ingenious Mage, Bás Marbhadair.”

“Take no offense, Barengar, but that is my name among the Bizhjerai. Of which you are not. Please, call me Kyra.”

“As you will, Kyra.”

“And in regards to your answer, she is. But that was not the answer I sought. What do you think of her?”

I walked in silence for a few steps, gathering my thoughts, before I spoke.

“She is troubled by her past and obsessed with making amends by turning the future into some sort of utopia.”

“A sentiment shared,” Kyra murmured. “I too held such dreams once, before the cruel reality of the world struck such thoughts from my mind.”

“What happened?” I asked, belatedly remembering her story.

Kyra turned her face away from me. “My wife was torn from this world.”

I did not know how to respond to that. Thankfully I did not have to since we had caught up to Marissa at the central platform. 

“Epitome!” Marissa called out to no one in particular. “I have a task for you!”

A flash of light formed into the shape of a woman with indistinct features. It immediately struck me that the glowing entity bore significant resemblance to the two women in my company.

The blank eyes of the entity turned toward me and it tilted it’s head as if curious.

“I am working on the tasks that you have already set before me, Marissa.”

“Good,” my mistress smirked at the entity.

“I have a query,” it continued.

“You don’t have to state that you have a question, Epitome. I have told you this. Just ask it.”

“What is this creature that stands before me?”

“What?” Marissa asked with widened eyes.

“It bears resemblance to you in general structure, but the overall shape is distorted.”

A muffled snort of laughter from the side caused me to shoot a curious glance at Kyra. I only caught a glimpse of her amused expression before Marissa sighed and drew my attention back to her.

“This is a man, Epitome. He is human, like me. Just the other gender.”

“I see,” The entity said before the vague outline of plump lips spread into a close but not quite emulation of a smile. “A pleasure to meet you, man.”

“My name is Barengar, Epitome,” I answered politely. I had learned at an early age to not antagonize strangers with unknown capabilities.

“I was named Epitome of Morality by my creator,” the entity said and it struck me that it’s lips had not moved when she spoke and the voice did not appear to come from any specific direction.

“Remember when I taught you to use your avatar’s lips when you speak, Epitome?” Kyra admonished the entity gently.

“I can’t forget, Kyra,” Epitome responded without turning her gaze from me.

“Then do it. And it is considered polite to look at a person when speaking to them.”

Epitome flickered and when she returned to focus she was facing Kyra and smiling at her. “Of course, Kyra.”

This time her lips did move, though their motions were not quite in sync with the sound of her voice.

Then it tilted its head to the side with a curious frown furrowing the blank light that comprised its brow.

“Are you a person, Kyra?”

I could sense no malice in her words, only curiosity. Like a child that asked a very insensitive question without grasping the full meaning of their words.

“I believe that I am. I may be dead but I am still everything that comprises a person,” Kyra responded patiently.

The entity looked down with an expression of intense concentration on her almost blank face.

I watched on in rapt fascination. What was this entity?

“You and I are similar in almost every way when it comes to our capabilities, Kyra. Does that mean that I am a person?”

For the first time I sensed the hint of emotion in its voice. A note of melancholy and uncertainty was there. Faint, but present.

“No,” Marissa answered gently. “A person understand and experience emotions. But I hope that in time you will become a person, Epitome.”

“I understand. I will devote every spare resource I possess to understand the concept of emotions, Marissa.”

“Glad to hear it,” my mistress responded. “In the meantime, about that task I mentioned?”

Epitome flickered again and was suddenly hovering in front of Marissa.

What was she? How could she move like that? I spread my magesense out to try to learn more. But the chamber was so thick with magic that I was almost blinded the moment I focused on my other sense and I quickly retreated back into my own skull.

“My apprentice has crafted this body for you. I want you to learn to interface with the control nexus within the skull.”

“As you wish, Marissa. Will this task take priority over the others?”

Marissa seemed to consider that for a few moments before she slowly nodded. “Yes, that is probably for the best. I must return to my workshop to work out a problem I must solve before the next phase of the plan.”

“I will be here, waiting for your next visit,” Epitome smiled.

Marissa gave the entity a final long look before she swept past me and I fell in step with her.

“Walk with me, mother.”

I looked over my shoulder and saw the entity wink out of existence, but now that I knew that it was down here I could still sense its presence. It was a peculiar sensation to be observed by something unseen. 

We walked in silence until we reached the other end of the bridge and had stepped onto the elevator. It slowly lifted us into the darkness of the shaft between floors, the only illumination provided by Kyra’s faintly glowing form.

“Would you keep an eye on her, mother?”

“Of course, little one. Anything in particular that I should keep a watchful eye for?”

“If she seems to be overwhelmed by having a physical body, or spins out of control… Destroy the avatar.”

A flash of magic and a revolver in it’s holster materialized slung low on Kyra’s hip. An exact replica of the big revolver that Marissa always carried.

No, not a replica, I realized. The very same one. The same gun that her mother had used in life, and now after her death.

“I hope it will not come to that,” Kyra said through gritted teeth. “But if it does, then I will do what I must.”

Before the last echo of her words had faded the woman had vanished from sight and leaving only a few swirls of rapidly fading magic.

“I am sure that you have many questions.”

“I do.”

“Come to my workshop. I will explain everything.”

###

My mind was reeling from everything that Marissa had explained to me. The sheer scope of her ambitions was… Staggering to say the least.

But I also felt some hope in my heart. Perhaps it could work. The already tried and true methods of governing had obviously failed to bring prosperity to all and far from equal control over their lives. Only the Clans had something that amounted to freedom but even they followed their Chiefs, and they were elected for life. The only way to get rid of a Chief gone bad was war and internal strife within the Clan.

Marissa had spent years creating Epitome, yet there were a tremendous amount of work remaining before she could be put in charge of anything. She did not grasp emotions.

Her cold analytical mind would sacrifice a child if it would be logical to do so. There must be some way to impart human values to her. I realized that I had somewhere in Marissa’s explanation stopped thinking about Epitome as an ‘it’ and instead thought of the entity as a ‘she’. I wondered why it had chosen a female avatar. A question that was answered by a single glance at Marissa. Until today Epitome had never been in contact with a man at all.

“Do you have any questions?” Marissa asked and leaned back in her chair, observing me.

“How would you teach her emotions?”

She shook her head slowly. “I don’t know. I am not convinced that it is possible, though I am not yet ready to give up.”

I nodded at that. That was in truth the only question I had at the moment, regarding Epitome at least.

“I have more questions, but not about this latest revelation of yours.”

“Ask them, and I will answer.”

“When we were in Lahar and were fired upon, you used much magical power. Too much. Are you more powerful than what the legends say?”

Marissa shrugged. “I know not what the legends say of me, but I can tell you this: I’ve spent my life forging my body into a weapon.”

I leaned forward with interest. “Could one learn how to do what you did?”

“Of course. And I will tell you how.” Marissa leaned forward and rested her elbows on the crystal surface of her desk, interlacing her fingers to lean her chin on the back of her hand. Her crystal fingers drew my gaze and for a moment I saw a flicker of light within them. As if she had momentarily lowered a shield of illusion that shrouded them.

“Your fingers…” I murmured. “They are power cores fashioned as prosthetics!”

Marissa smirked at my dumbfounded expression.

“Not just my fingers. Over the years I have replaced all of my bones with materialized magic in the power core configuration. I’ve kept the marrow, mind you, since we mortals require such to provide blood. But the ossified mineral was gradually replaced with crystal and charged with power.”

I leaned back heavily, my mind heavily entrenched in thought.

“Clever. That would give you much power at your disposal. But how do you channel it all without destroying your flesh?”

“I do not,” Marissa answered and raised a slender finger to tap one of the crystals embedded in her skull. “These spell stones contain a spell framework similar to Epitome. Far from her complexity, they act as a buffer between me and the world. The meta-magic contained within is intelligent enough to react to the intent control nexus slaved to my mind and choose spells from the major memory crystal to fulfill my desires.”

“And they bind the resulting spell from the power in your bones,” I mused aloud.

Then my scattered thoughts clicked together. “You don’t actually channel the power. You circumvent your mortal frailty by casting relatively weak spells that in turn control the flow of energy.”

She pointed at me with her index finger and grinned. “Top grades for my apprentice.”

I gazed down at my hands for a few moments before I raised my eyes to look into hers, flesh and crystal both.

“Then anyone may be as powerful as you with the correct preparation.”

“A true observation, my friend. Which is why I have withheld such information from the masses so far. There are too many men, and women for that matter, that should not wield such power.”

I recalled the conversation about Epitome of Morality we had just had.

“According to you,” I accused her.

“Yes. According to me. And I am a flawed moral entity, as is any singular human. I can only act in the manner I believe is right. Once I am convinced that Epitome truly understands she will have access to all of the knowledge that I have accrued and she will have the power to distribute it as she see fit.”

I nodded slowly, unable to find flaw in her arguments.

“When you where shot, smoke billowed out of the wound but I sensed no magic to cauterize it.”

I framed my words as a statement, even though it was a question.

“I have used Blood Magic to alter my vitae. It is now a caustic agent that reacts to the touch of air and cause it to boil. I no longer need to spare any thoughts toward battle triage.”

Marissa smiled smugly before continuing.

“Furthermore, one of the stones on the side of my head is dedicated to my own health and is complex enough to be able to administer triage if needed. The wound was cleaned and stitched before we even entered our Archangels.”

“Your stones appear to be very useful indeed,” I mused while stroking the stubble covering my cheeks.

“They are indeed. Though not without pain to install.”

I focused my gaze on hers. “I am no stranger to pain,” I said with a challenge to my voice.

She shook her head sadly. “You are to this kind of pain. The intent control interface directly with your Soul. It is unlike any pain you have ever suffered.”

“Pain is transient,” I countered.

“True,” Marissa admitted. “If you wish it, then you will have stones such as these in time.”

“I have one final question.”

Marissa inclined her head.

“You killed that assassin and it seemed to me that you drained his Soul from his body. Then you retrieved the shot from his chest. Why?”

“Your assessment is correct. I ripped all that he was from his body and it was stored in the bullet. I have since extracted his Soul and dissected it to peruse his memories.”

I felt my eyes widen. “You can do that?”

Marissa nodded before leaning forward again with interest. “You seem more fascinated than horrified,” she observed with a twitch to her lips.

“I had not thought such a thing possible. What did you learn from his memories?”

Marissa scoffed. “He was, as suspected, hired by the Council to kill me.”

“Those fucking bastards! We should burn the Council Hall to the ground,” I seethed.

“To what end? They are fools beneath our concern, especially now that the school has severed ties with them.”

“What if they invade like they threatened?”

Her eyes narrowed. “Then they will face the fury of ten thousand suns and meet the same fate as the Skylanders did.”

“You really mean that, don’t you?”

“I do. I have destroyed a city and killed thousands. I can do it again if I have to.”

“And I will stand by your side.”

Silence settled between us for a few moments before Marissa tilted her head and spoke.

“Do you have any further questions?”

“Not at this moment.”

“Then come, I have something else to show you. I would have your thoughts on the matter.”

Marissa stood up and left the room through a door near the corner of her office, a door I had never entered before. I hurried to catch up and drew up short at what I beheld on the other side.

Shields covered the walls to keep the elevated magical field high. Cages lined one of the walls, and within the cages…

Snarling deoch draoidheil glared balefully at us. And on a gurney in the center of the room lay one without arms or legs, secured by a strap around her neck.

“Is that…”

“The one that you caught for me. She has been very illuminating.”

“Why did you have us catch them?”

“Cast a spell at her, and have your answer.”

I cast a simple tongue of fire at the creature and just as expected the power was leeched directly from my spell and the flames petered out long before they could as much as char skin.

“They devour the magic just as expected.”

“Yes,” Marissa stated simply, regarding me with an expectant expression on her face.

Realization struck me and a grin tugged at my lips.

“You seek to replicate this ability.”

“Precisely. My mother discovered decades past that dragons are artificial creatures, a complex spell bound in flesh wrought by Blood Magic. The deoch draoidheil were once created by a similar process, though they were originally human like us. Therefore their magic devouring aura was once a spell and thus it follows that we will be able to replicate it with enough study.”

“And why do you need this ability?”

“Defense. Why shield yourself from harm, expending power to do so, when you can steal the power form any magical attack in order to turn it upon your adversary?”

“I can’t argue against that.”

“Yet so far I have been unable to crack this problem and I would have your thoughts on the matter.”

I leaned over the female that I had dismembered all of that time ago and I peered into her snarling face.

Her sharp teeth snapped closed inches from my face but I had ensured to not lean within the reach of her neck.

“These creatures were once human?” I asked with some interest.

“Not these specifically, but their ancestors were.”

“Then I agree with you, their peculiar abilities should be just a spell that is somehow embedded within their form.”

“Have you heard of the Reavers?”

“The savage tribes of the Clan territories? Only in passing.”

“I’ve seen them. Fought alongside them. And against them. They have similar abilities to these creatures, but instead of devouring the magic out of spells they leech the power from your Soul and use it to cast their spells. Without a sacrifice they can’t use magic.”

“That strikes me as a severe weakness,” I observed, straightening from my bent over posture.

“It is, but they were made such. The liche Grasax that once controlled Dun Traelak created them. He was powerful and nothing could stand against him and live.”

“Except your mother,” I interjected.

“Yes. Except my mothers,” Marissa corrected me. “They both faced him along with a squad of Ithlaki horse lords. My mothers were the only survivors and they were both gravely wounded in the battle.”

She drew a deep breath before continuing. “Grasax was powerful, but he was just one entity. A single magic user to cover the vast territory that he held.

It does not matter how powerful you are if you can only apply your power at a single location at a time. He dared not train his slaves in the arts of magic lest they grow too powerful and break the magical bonds he had placed on them. Instead he tried to create his own Mages. He succeeded in the Reavers and he bestowed upon them the limitation to only be able to use their power in combat. That way he ensured that if they sought to rise against him then he could quash them before they gathered enough power to be a threat.”

“They could have sacrificed their own in advance of betrayal,” I pointed out logically.

“They could have,” Marissa smirked, “but Grasax knew how most people worked and assumed that they would be unwilling to resort to such tactics.

For some unknown reason Grasax and the Reavers parted ways, my personal belief was that they were discarded as a failed experiment yet they somehow survived and flourished outside his domain.”

“So the Reavers have a similar ability to the deoch draoidheil, reinforcing your hypothesis that we should be able to recreate the spell.”

“Yes. Thoughts?”

I stared at the caged beasts and mulled it over before I shook my head with a sigh. “No, not at this time. But I will think on it and let you know whenever inspiration strikes me. May I come here and study them as I wish?”

“Yes. I have no more secrets from you, Barengar.”

“I am honoured, mistress.”

For once she did not command me to use her name and she graced me with a smile.

“Come then, and let us have a meal before it is time for bed.”

“Ladies first,” I said with a smirk and a bow as I let her lead me out of the chamber.

When we walked by her large desk I for the first time noticed a figurine on its surface. It was a crystal dragon, lying on its paws with its snout beneath its wing as it slept. I lingered and beheld it, marveling at the fine details on the figuring.

“My brother,” Marissa said softly from beside me. “At least as I remember him, sleeping in the sun on the courtyard of Dun Traelak.”

“It is marvelous. Did you make this?”

“I did. Detailed art is simple once you can translate memory into physical form with just a thought.”

I gently placed the figurine back onto the surface and saw Marissa’s mournful expression.

“You miss him dearly,” I stated.

“Every day for almost forty years.”

Without saying anything else she spun on her heel and left the room. I respected her need for silence as we prepared the evening meal, even more of that abominable fungus.

I could not fathom how the Skylanders could stomach the stuff for their whole lives, and I had only been forced to endure it for barely a month.

We sat down to dine while my mind was consumed by thoughts of deoch draoidheil and dragons and the conundrum that Marissa had placed before me.

I thought of Epitome and the struggle she had with comprehending emotions. Epitome. Dragons.

A light went on in my head.

“Marissa?”

“Yes?”

“You said that dragons were a spell enshrined in flesh.”

“I did,” she said, curious where my train of thought had lead.

“And Epitome is a complex spell to emulate a mind.”

“Still correct.”

“So she is essentially a dragon, without the flesh.”

She stared at me blankly for a moment with a furrowed brow before her eyes widened and her crystal eye flashed bright once.

“You are correct. My brother had no issue with emotions. In fact, he felt many of them with more passion than most mortals.”

“That means that your quest is not an impossible one. In fact, it has already been solved.”

Marissa leaned her chin in her hand and nearly impaled herself on her fork and she stared at it in confusion for a moment before she dropped it.

It drifted gently onto her plate on streamers of magic, seemingly without her conscious thought.

“I wish that my brother was here so that I could ask him and examine him,” Marissa complained softly.

“So do I, Marissa. So do I.”

“We must find a dragon to study,” she proclaimed suddenly and made my jaw drop. “I will entreat the Red Flight at first opportunity, though I do not believe that they will see us.”

“Why not? I thought that they were allies of Clan Bizhjerai,” I said with a pointed glance at her shoulder where I knew that her Clan Rune was.

“They were during the war with the Skylands, but after my brother died… They blamed the Clan for his death.”

“But he gave his life willingly for the Clan… At least that is what the legend claim.”

“It is true. But he was an adolescent dragon under the care and protection of the Bizhjerai. In the Flight’s eyes that means that they were responsible for keeping him alive. And regardless of the circumstances, they failed.

To make matters worse his body was entirely destroyed by his death and there would be no rebirth of his spirit. For what might have been the first time ever, a Greater Dragon actually died.

And not just any dragon either. The reincarnation of Malkareth the Great. The Patriarch of the Red Flight.”

I let out a low whistle. “I had no idea that your brother was of such an esteemed lineage.”

“He was a king among dragons,” Marissa said with a sniffle, yet her eyes were dry.

“Tell me about him,” I prompted her.

I was genuinely interested. I had only ever glimpsed a single dragon when I was on the road from my home to the School and it had been a bare speck on the horizon sky.

I could not fathom what it would have been like to have one as your adopted brother.

And told me about him she did, for the rest of the evening.

She spoke of how she had first met him on the plains outside of Utopeia and how she had within minutes of meeting him climbed on top of him and been taken for a ride. 

She told me how they had played on the courtyard and how they had explored the mountains behind Dun Traelak.

Even though I was listening intently I found myself consumed by the expression on her face. That wistful cast to her features that spoke of an old grief whose piercing sting had faded until she could see the happiness in the memories through the tint of sorrow.

The clock in the main workshop suddenly struck midnight and Marissa jerked in surprise before she smiled sheepishly.

“I’m sorry, Barengar. I did not mean to prattle on. We should retire for the night.”

“Don’t be. I am glad you told me all of this.”

I stood up to clear the table but Marissa stopped me with a hand on my forearm.

“Thank you. Thank you for listening.”

I put my hand over hers. “Anytime, mistress.”

She graced me with a trembling smile and then she gently withdrew her hand from my grasp and retreated to her bedchamber.

In a suddenly good mood I cleared the table, smiling to myself. It felt as if we had grown closer during this evening than during the the rest of the time we had spent out here at her primary workshop and I had never been happier that she had chosen me to be her apprentice.

###

I groaned when the light of dawn fell upon my face and I tried to wiggle back beneath the covers.

“Good morning, Barengar,” a voice said from beside my bed.

Instantly awake I spun in my nest of blankets, a deadly spell forming in my left hand.

I squinted suspiciously at the shadow next to my bed and it tilted its head quizzically.

I dismissed the spell I had formed and let my head fall back into my pillow.

“You can’t just barge in here like this, Epitome.”

“Understood, Barengar. It will not happen again.”

It was odd hearing that mellifluous voice flow from the blank faceplate of the avatar that I had constructed for her.

“Marissa and Kyra is awaiting you. I have some news to share with all of you.”

I sat up in the bed and let the blankets fall down to my waist.

“I did not think that you had a flair for the dramatic, Epitome.”

Again she tilted her head and I was astonished how she had mastered the mannerisms of a mortal. Her posture was feminine with most of her weight on one hip and her back arched as if accounting for the weight of breasts, even though she currently lacked that part of the female anatomy.

I stood up and started to put my clothes on, I paused before I put on the armoured sleeve that held my axe and I decided to skip it for now. I was not expecting combat out here.

Then I remembered that Marissa was always armed with Kyra’s old revolver and even though that was most likely a sentimental gesture I decided to do the same. I slung my own revolver to my hip, but that would suffice in the ways of weapons.

“I have often wondered why Marissa and Kyra always carry weapons, even here where there are no enemies. I see that you do as well. Is this a common trait for humans?”

“No, not really,” I sighed and buttoned up my shirt. “Just us, and warriors in general I suppose. If you are always ready to fight then you will never truly be taken by surprise.”

“An oddly logical reasoning for a human.”

I glanced at the crystal avatar and found that she was watching me. But something was off in her mannerisms. Her posture was perfect, an uncanny mimicry of female relaxation.

Then it struck me. She was completely still. There where no twitches or fidgeting or the gentle motions of breath. Of course, to her every motion would be deliberate and a conscious act as a direct result of a decision.

Not at all like us humans.

“That sounds like a compliment, Epitome,” I grinned while straightening my collar before I walked around her to enter the main room where the others would be waiting.

“I place no judgment by my words. It was merely an observation.”

I led her into the main room and saw Marissa sitting by the table where we had spent the previous evening, Kyra sitting beside her. A plate of fungus and a crystal cup of water awaited me opposite the two women.

“Good morning, ladies,” I greeted them cheerfully.

“You seem to be in a particularly good mood today, Barengar,” Kyra commented while a slow smile spread across her lips and her shining eyes drifted from me to Marissa and back.

“Mother!” Marissa admonished her but Kyra’s grin only grew wider.

I glanced from the older Archon to the younger Mage and then shook my head. I had not understood the interaction and I had many years prior reconciled myself to the fact that I would never truly understand women.

“Just glad to see that Epitome has taken to the avatar I built for her so well.”

“A much impressive feat,” Kyra agreed. “If we had had such in the war many of my Clansmen would have survived.”

“We would not have had the magical power to keep an army of these functioning,” Marissa pointed out.

The Archon waved her concerns away. “After your mom had figured out her scouting spell bugs raw power was not truly a concern any more. We could open up more crossings with ease.”

“And the Hand of Glory could have been modified to accept even more power from the Skylands. What if they would have reverse-engineered these constructs?”

“Fair point, daughter. But we are not here to debate. We are here because Epitome have something to share with us?”

We all turned expectant faces to the construct.

“I do, Kyra. I am here to report success on several of the tasks set before me. First, and most obvious, is that I can now command this avatar fully. I find it intriguing to interact with the world in this manner. To physically touch and manipulate reality manually instead of with magic.”

Epitome paused for effect, which was a strange thing for an entity that did not understand theatrics to do.

“Secondly, the space launch power core now spans most of the new lowest level and is several meters thick. It is fully charged with magic and according to my calculations it should now contain enough power reach orbital altitudes. A spell that fulfills the constraints given to me has been designed and stand ready to be launched.”

Stunned silence greeted her. Had she actually accomplished all of that so quickly?

“Does the artificial ley line require stabilization coils?” Marissa finally asked.

“No. I took the liberty to experiment, within the constraints given of course, to learn more about the nature of magic. I have found a method to focus the magical energy in such a way that it will maintain coherency at much longer distances than the method used in most focusing towers. It will degrade with distance, of course, since the magic will interact with the particles in the air but I am currently running a series of experiments to verify a solution to such issues.”

She paused again. “I am optimistic about the results. I believe that we have reached a possible solution for extremely long range energy transfer that could be suitable to transfer energy extremely long distances without significant losses.”

I grinned at Marissa. If Epitome was correct then her dreams of a world spanning energy distribution network might be plausible.

She returned my smile with an excited one of her own.

“Show me!” She breathed and Epitome inclined her head.

“Follow me to the upper launch level.”

“You’ve built an entire new level?”

“It was required for the infrastructure,” Epitome stated as if her feat was of no consequence. “Most of the output of the Great Geyser was consumed for a significant period of time in order to complete the construction in a timely manner.”

The implications of her words were astounding. She had designed, developed and constructed a massively complex spell frame in a staggeringly short amount of time.

She led us to the elevator that we used to travel down into her chamber but instead of sinking us into the floor it instead lifted us toward the ceiling. I glanced upward and saw that a new shaft had been carved through it. We rose into the relative darkness of the shaft though it did not bother any of us. Our magesenses were more acute than our eyes and we could see everything perfectly fine. We emerged from the floor and I looked curiously around.

The level was large and spanned the entire width and length of the workshop and it was absolutely cluttered. Intricate crystal frameworks and bulbous spell cores filled the area, all of it nesting and warping in a spiraling pattern toward a massive spell stone in the center of the room. It was wide at the base and narrowed to a fine point at the top, each facet of the crystal glinting in a particular hue of the rainbow. The domed ceiling had a circular opening above the main crystal with a slightly longer diameter, though I could not see through the darkness nor could my magesense reach that high.

“Ah, I see,” Marissa whispered and I looked over at her. Her flesh eye was blank and unfocused while her crystal eye blazed a brilliant azure. She must have been using some property of her eye to see through the roof to behold what was up there.

“What do you see?”

“A radar dish,” she murmured.

I shot Kyra a a glance and saw my own incomprehension mirrored on her features.

“Would you mind elaborating?” I asked my mistress.

“It’s an ancient technology that I learned about in the bunker we spoke of. They used it to focus electromagnetic waves to transmit information vast distances. It was what gave me the whole idea of all of this to start with.”

“I see,” I said even though I did not.

“Yes, this all makes sense,” Marissa continued. “Though it would have taken me years to figure it all out myself, not to mention actually building it all.”

“I have the advantage of being able to create a multitude of spells to aid me in the design phase and use an evolutionary process to cull dead ends. I consumed vast amounts of magical power in order to compute all of this,” Epitome stated without hint of smugness.

“I assume that you have not tested this in large scale yet?”

“I cannot. The minimal viable testing scenario would still require enough power to destroy this entire facility. I deemed it useless to test in a smaller scope since the results would not reflect reality.”

“I agree, though I fear to actually use this. If there is an error and the magical power leaks…”

“This workshop will be destroyed in the process. You should retreat to a safe distance during the trial.”

Marissa spun around to face the avatar that Epitome was piloting. “But then you will die!”

“That is a possibility that I have considered,” Epitome answered without trace of emotion.

I had to remind myself that she did not have emotions, and as such she did not fear death. She could probably not even appreciate her own existence. She just… Was. And the concept of not being did not register to her as anything worthy of note.

“And that does not concern you?” Marissa whispered.

“It does not, Marissa. As I have told you before, I am unable to place value in anything. Including myself.”

Marissa closed her eyes and rubbed her forehead with the tips of her fingers.

“You may not value yourself, Epitome, but I do. I won’t have you risk yourself for this.”

“I can create a spell core to hold the schematics for your entire workshop in its current configuration, including myself, if you wish. You could use that spell core to recreate the workshop in the case of catastrophic failure.”

“Do it. Just in case. But I would also like you to move the lower power core and this level to a separate satellite at a safe distance. If this experiment goes sideways then we will not lose the entire facility and most importantly: you.”

“It will cause a significant drain on our power input to maintain levitation of a satellite facility at such a distance.”

“Doesn’t matter. Do it. Scale up the power gatherers if you have to. The Great Geyser once supported the whole Skylands, it can support our current needs.”

Epitome was silent for a few moments as if considering. “As you wish, Marissa. If you would please leave this level for your safety then I will commence reconstruction immediately.”

With a sharp nod Marissa led us back to the elevator and we descended back into the main workshop. 

As soon as it settled into the floor I felt a surge of power course through the whole workshop, blinding my magesense and causing me to wince. A slight shudder rumbled the floor.

“Forceful deconstruction of the lower floor was more time efficient than evaporation based disconnection. I apologize for the lack of warning.”

Had she just blown the most massive power core in existence from its moorings?

A single mistake and we would all be ash…

For the first time I started to doubt my mistress. She had given tremendous power to an entity that could not understand the full implications of her actions. An entity without regard for her own self preservation.

I pushed my doubts aside. 

I trusted Marissa’s judgment.

## CHAPTER SEVEN Marissa

It took Epitome only a day and night to split off the lower and upper levels of my workshop and join them together into a separate structure. Another day to set up the levitation foils and shielding it would require and then a full night to slide it away to a safe distance and construct a bridge between the two facilities. Barengar, mother and I were in a fortified bunker on top of my workshop that had been constructed specifically for this purpose. Just in case of a catastrophic failure I had mother’s crystalline prison secured within my own flesh and the two Archangels stood ready behind the bunker so that we may escape if we had to. Epitome stood beside me, waiting for my command.

I drew a deep breath and slowly let it out.

“Are you ready?” I asked Epitome.

“Ready as I’ll ever be,” Barengar responded with a shrug. Despite his casual lean against the wall I could sense a tension in the set of his shoulders. I could sense mother giving me a short nod from behind.

But I had posed the question to Epitome, so I waited for her response.

“Everything is ready, Marissa. Diagnostics have all passed successfully.”

“Then you may begin, Epitome.”

The avatar inclined its head and my magesense was suddenly utterly blinded by the most massive spike of power I had ever sensed.

The storm of magic boiled and churned and then suddenly a massive and impossibly bright lance of power surged to the sky. The thunder of the displaced air reached me almost immediately and made my ears ring and caused me to wince in pain. The air itself around the lance of power combusted and burned as it speared ever higher. The tip of the lance was far beyond the range of both flesh vision and magical senses and I was powerless to do anything more.

I was about to suggest that we retreat back into the workshop to relax and wait for the procedure to finish when I sensed an oddness through the blinding storm of magic.

“Epitome?”

The avatar turned her head toward me and then I saw only whiteness.
