A moment later the shock wave blasted through the view ports of the bunker and my ears popped before everything went silent. The floor shook violently and threw me from my feet to crash heavily into the wall. My magesense was washed out by a thick wave of power yet I sensed a tiny event of wild magic manifest in front of me. Before I could identify it it winked out, only to be replaced with two more. I struggled blindly to my feet and found that the floor was askew. Slowly vision returned to me and I could see Barengar attempting to get back on his feet.

Mother was utterly gone and Epitome was a twisted mess of shattered crystal, the enchantments on her avatar completely overloaded and burnt out.

I straightened out tentatively on the skewed floor and forged my way to the viewport. As expected the launching satellite was utterly gone and the outside superstructure of my workshop was covered in crystal shards. Some had struck so hard that they pierced the material of the walls and were embedded deep within the core among fractures.

“Fuck!” I swore. “Are you alright?” I asked my apprentice.

“Yeah,” he nodded. “Just bruised. The experiment failed, I take it?”

“Catastrophically,” I agreed sullenly.

“Good thing you had Epitome move it from the workshop.”

“Quite. Let’s inspect the damage,” I grumbled and gingerly made my way to the elevator.

It was dead, of course, the enchantments burnt out just like Epitome’s avatar.

Worry churned in my belly. If my workshop had been decimated like this then I feared for her. Her chamber resided within a powerful shielding spell bolstered by physical enchantments, but that blast had been supremely powerful.

I reached out with my own power and winced at the lance of pain through my skull, but the elevator slowly sank us down into the bowels of my home. After what felt like an eternity we emerged into Epitome’s chamber and I let out a relieved sigh. The enchantments burned brightly, but her communications module had of course melted and exploded again. Luckily I had a few spares on hand since they tended to malfunction periodically. 

I wasted no time in directing Barengar in replacing it and while we worked mother slowly reformed next to me. Her form was even more ethereal and unsteady than usual and she wore a pained expression on her face.

“What happened? I remember standing in the bunker, waiting, and then suddenly… Nothing. Until I reformed down here.”

“It blew up. The magic surge fried everything, including your form. It must have sent you back into the containment spell.”

“I see. And Epitome?”

I glanced at the large sphere that was the brain of my creation.

“I think that she is fine. The reinforced shield seemed to have protected her. But her communications module blew and her avatar is fucked.”

Mother puffed up her cheeks and blew out a breath of frustration. “Any thoughts of what failed?”

“None,” I admitted. “I hope that Epitome would have sensed something.”

Mother looked at the large sphere and Barengar crawling up it with the replacement module.

“Then we wait.”

“Yes.”

We did not have to wait long. Barengar snapped the new module in place and it instantly lit up, power coursing through it and activating all of the enchantments one by one.

Before he had climbed down Epitome and rejoined us the ghostly avatar of my creation had appeared.

“There was a weakness in the power connector between the main power core and the focusing dish, as well as several negative feedback joints in the spell frame,” she said without preamble. “The acceleration calculator twisted apart from the kinetic feedback and that caused a chain reaction that ended up with the main power connector melting which in turn fractured the power core.”

“Hence the explosion,” I mused.

“I have already redesigned the components and begun construction of a mark two of the facility. I have also started repairs of this facility which I assume have priority over the construction of the replacement satellite.”

“You assume correctly, Epitome. And make sure that you craft a replacement Avatar for yourself when you have spare time.”

“I will, Marissa.”

With that, we left her behind to deal with the cleanup. Having an intelligent workshop was proving to be a time-saving invention. My only regret was that I had not given her full control earlier.

###

It took Epitome only a week to recreate the launch satellite and repair the workshop. She even found time to recreate her avatar.

She did have the advantage to be able to split her mind into incredibly specialized aspects that were perfectly suited for the assigned task to work on multiple projects simultaneously.

She could also work around the clock since she did not need sleep.

And then she had the further advantage of not having a limited magical strength or channeling rate of her magic.

I shook my head at the marvel before me as I watched the bridge connect to the new launch satellite. We were back in the bunker for the second attempt to launch a magical construct into orbit.

Again mother, Barengar and Epitome was with me and I gave her the command to initiate launch.

I watched with bated breath as the spike of power lanced into the sky. This time Epitome had added crystal shaders to the view ports so that we would not be blinded and our ears were protected from the thunder of displaced air. Long minutes stretched on as the power surged into the sky.

When fifteen minutes had passed I deemed it safe enough to leave and carry on with my day. But I did not.

Just in case something happened, this bunker would be the safest place to be. Possibly even safer than Epitome’s chamber since shrapnel from any exploding modules on her would be like getting shot.

An hour later the bright lance of power dimmed and an auxiliary projector disk lit up with a much slimmer and more tightly focused magic beam reached for the sky. It panned around for a few minutes.

“We have contact,” Epitome announced. “Minimal viable orbital satellite diagnostics complete.”

I felt my lips stretch into a broad smile. It was a good day.

“Commence construction of receiving disk and initial power storage. Once that is complete continue on with a transmitter.”

“Understood, Marissa.”

“Is the third Archangel complete?”

“It is, Marissa. It is waiting in the hangar.”

“Perfect. Let us be away then.”

“We are going somewhere?” Barengar asked with barely concealed excitement.

“We are,” I affirmed mysteriously and almost let out a giggle at his frustrated scowl. 

But he had learned by now that he would get nothing further from me and so he just fell in line.

We took the elevator down to the lower level and walked to the hangar.

“I have never flown before,” Epitome stated without emotion. “Nor will I be able to maintain a connection with my avatar.”

“That is what the transmitter on the satellite is supposed to fix for you. Align it with these coordinates and scan for any returns.”

I sketched the coordinates in the air and left the numbers as glowing runes.

Epitome did not even glance at them, her magical sensors picked up the runes in an instant and her avatar handed me a spell stone.

“This is the seed spell for the transceiver you asked me to build.”

With a thanks we all climbed into our respective Archangels. I started mine up and transferred limited flight controls to Barengar but kept Epitome’s Archangel slaved to mine.

She did not know how to fly, nor would she be able to once we passed outside of her range. We were going quite the distance after all. I walked to the large doors and they swung open unbidden. We walked out onto the launch pad and the jets of our Archangels lit up and lifted us into the air. I carefully drifted us close to the edge of the shield that opened before us. One by one I brought us out into the maelstrom of magic that surrounded my workshop.

We were instantly tossed about and the illusory monitors around me started flickering from the interference. I quickly reconfigured our suits for flight and we sped away form the Great Geyser. When we were out of range of the interference and full control returned to me I found that we had lost a great deal of altitude. I corrected that quickly enough and set a course north-west.

“You still with us, Epitome?” I asked.

As I had suspected, I received no response. We were already outside of her range and her avatar had powered down. 

It was a rather long flight and I’d had Epitome install extra power cores so that we could reach our destination and return without refueling.

Even Barengar grew tired of playing around with his Archangel and I could hear him grumble under his breath through the communication spell.

Just when I heard him inhale to complain I started to nudge us down closer to the ground. We were drawing close. 

Just as well. It was drawing close to noon and my belly was grumbling angrily from neglect. The thought of consuming the provisions of fungus that I had brought turned my stomach and I entertained the thought of sending Barengar out hunting for proper meat as soon as we touched down. My belly rumbled in agreement.

I took us down low, only a couple of hundred meters. We were almost by the farthest reaches of the mountain range that separated Clan territory from the rest of the world but we had long since left actual inhabited Clan territory behind us. Nothing lived this far to the north-west. Only the ruins of ancient civilizations lived here and the ghosts contained within them. In the distance was a sprawling ruin with the burnt—out shells of impossibly tall buildings reaching for the sky like skeletal fingers.

“By the Maker,” Barengar whispered when he laid eyes on it.

“It is a city from before the Apocalypse,” I responded. “It has been dead for centuries.”

“It’s so huge…”

“I’ve read records that a single city back then could be home to tens of millions of people.”

“How do they feed that many people?” He asked with wonder in his voice.

“They were powerful and knowledgeable beyond belief. They had technology that was vastly more powerful than magic.”

“Why would they ever forsake such glories for magic?”

“Because magic, in combination with their technological prowess, allowed them to reach heights never before even dreamed about.”

“Until the world ended,” Barengar concluded grimly.

“Until the world ended,” I agreed as I shifted our Archangels into hover mode.

We drifted up to the mountains and I set us down on a dug out platform a ways from the base of the mountain.

An old road paved with a thick black substance mixed with rock that was more than half claimed by nature slithered up the mountainside.

Asphalt, the substance was called I suddenly remembered.

I set our Archangels down next to the hull of a Skyship that I had landed here years ago. It was dormant and lacked the lifting bags that had hoisted it to this remote location, long since disassembled to make the mobile laboratory a more permanent fixture.

“Is this the ancient bunker you spoke of?”

“It is. The ancient repository of knowledge. A gift from a civilization long passed.”

Barengar disembarked his Archangel and immediately drew his axe from the storage compartment. The axe I had made for Zandor all those years ago in the vain hope that he would seek me out when it became known that it was I who had founded the renowned School of Magic.

He had not, and I had reluctantly concluded that he was dead. It still stung my heart whenever I saw that axe or thought of my lover of old, but it was an old wound and the ache had faded with the years. Though it had never completely healed, and I suspected that it never would. I disembarked from my own Archangel and quickly entered the dormant Skyship.

With a push of my power the laboratory powered on and magical lights flickered to life, illuminating the interior. It was cramped, as all Skyships were, and was dominated by a multi-purpose fabricator and a small work bench.

I slid the spell stone that Epitome had given me into the fabricator and powered it on before I switched it for exterior builds. The side of the laboratory slid open and the fabricator swiveled out of the hull and began construction. I hoped that the power remaining in the Skyship’s core would be enough to fully construct the machine we required and I quickly checked the power gauges.

It would be tight, but if it was enough then we would have more than enough power for whatever we needed. But power was not the main mission of this little escapade. Knowledge was.

I stepped back out into the sunlight where Barengar was securing the area, his axe held ready and a revolver in his left fist. He was taking no chances. I approved.

“All clear,” he called back when he checked every nook and cranny of the plateau.

He jogged back to me and paused, casting furtive glances at the large steel door that blocked the entrance to the bunker.

“Not yet, Barengar. We have to wait for this machine to finish. In the meantime we should eat.”

Barengar grunted in disapproval.

“If you want something to do, then take your Archangel down into the valley and hunt for a meal. There should be no dangerous predators here, but I have on several occasion spotted mountain goats in the area.”

“It would be nice to eat something that is not that abominable fungus you are so fond of,” he groused.

“Believe me, I am far from fond of the fungus,” I chuckled.

“I agree, that fungus is the worst,” mother said as she suddenly appeared next to me.

She glanced around before she grimaced.

“I don’t like this place. Not enough magic here for me to remain manifested for long.”

“I am sorry, mother. Retreat to your stone and I will call on you when the environment is more comfortable for you.”

Mother nodded before she faded from view again and I could sense her presence going dormant once more.

“I don’t think I will ever get used to that,” Barengar muttered before he slung his axe to his back and walked over to his Archangel. “I think I will search for these goats you speak of.”

I waved him farewell and watched him fly his machine over the precipice and vanish.

I found myself with nothing in particular to do, a rare occurrence for me, so I decided to lean back in the midday sun and take a nap.

###

I awoke to the scream of jets in close proximity and I opened my eyes and saw Barengar touch down gently with his Archangel, a mountain goat strapped to the belly of his machine. I winced when I saw the fresh blood trickling down the previously clean crystal plates but I quickly washed my face clean of disapproval when the machine disgorged my apprentice. 

“Hunting went well I see,” I greeted him.

He awarded me with his usual rakish grin and retrieved the dead goat and hurled it with casual ease to slide to a halt by my feet.

“It did. It was not even afraid of me. Just stood there staring at me.”

I inspected the cadaver and saw that its neck was nearly cleaved from its shoulders. Quite a clean kill. Death would have been instantaneous for the beast.

“Did it let you get close enough to chop it down?”

“Not really. I threw the axe. The recall spell worked flawlessly,” Barengar enthused.

“Glad to hear it. Did you try any of the elemental imbues?”

“Naw. A clean throw was all that was needed to bring this mighty beast low,” Barengar chuckled as he knelt next to the corpse while he drew his knife.

“Where did you learn to hunt?” I asked curiously. We had barely spoken about his life before he attended my school.

Barengar stuck his knife into the goats belly and slit it open all the way to the neck in a single smooth cut. Without hesitation he stuck his hand inside and started pulling out long coils of intestine.

“I’m from a province near central Ca’el. My father was a rancher, raising livestock to provide the closest city with meat, milk and eggs. There were a large forest near our home where we used to hunt. He taught me to track prey there.”

“Was?”

“He passed away a year before I left to attend your school. Lungrot.”

“I am sorry to hear that,” I said sincerely.

“It is how the world is. Industry caught up with him and the smoke finally got him.”

I was silent for a few moments as Barengar started skinning the goat.

“And your mother?”

“Raiders got her when I was a child. Nomad bandits attacked the ranch and made away with a whole lot of father’s cattle. They killed mother in the barn. Father would not let me see her body.”

“I’m sorry.”

“Don’t be. Such was life in Ca’el. It got a little better after the revolution, and then it quickly got worse again when the war started. They seized my ranch to feed the army and sent me on my way.”

Barengar ripped the skin free with a grunt and then set to butchering the goat.

“I was certain that they were going to conscript me into the army and while I was not reluctant to join the army I knew that I was more than just another grunt. If I was to fight then I wanted to really contribute to the war effort.”

“So you traveled to my school to become a Mage.”

“Aye, that I did. At the time I did not know about your Oath, yet I took it willingly once I finally understood the true purpose of power.”

That piqued my interest and I leaned forward and focused my full attention on him.

“And what is the true purpose of power?”

Barengar looked up at me and stared me straight into the eyes.

“To make the world a better place. For yourself, for others. Doesn’t matter. As long as you improve things without hindering others, that is a net gain. If I have to use my abilities in war then so be it. But I will not go to war unjustly, like my people have done.”

I tilted my head, considering his words.

“Why do you believe that the war the Ca’el wage are unjust?”

Barengar’s head snapped back up to face me.

“You believe that your people provoked mine?”

“No. Yet I would know your thoughts on the matter?”

“The Bizhjerai didn’t provoke us. None of the Clans did. I don’t know the true reason behind the war, but it is not one of territory or resources. It must be ideological, but which ideas are conflicting I do not know. But I do know that the Clans have never taken aggressive action toward my people without first being provoked.”

Barengar sighed and wiped his brow with the back of his hand.

“I only know that the anti-Clan sentiments started cropping up at about the same time that Lord Zaar appeared to whisper into the ears of the Emperor.”

I nodded in agreement. His words aligned with what I knew was true.

“The war is costly. A not insignificant part of the population is at the front lines and the rest are struggling to support them. Not only with food but with weapons and ammunition. The whole war is folly.”

I could hear the frustration in his voice, and I could sympathize.

“I know,” I agreed and Barengar sent me a baleful glare.

“So why do we not stop it?”

“What do you mean?”

Barengar laughed mirthlessly and spread his arms. “Does the School not possess the largest gathering of Mages in the world? Could we not construct weapons superior to the ones currently in use and give to the soldiers in the front line? Participate ourselves and turn the tide of battles?”

“We could,” I agreed readily. “And then what?”

Barengar stared at me, flabbergasted, and his butchering forgotten.

“What do you mean ‘and then what?’”

“Let’s say we do what you propose. Which side should we support? Which side in this conflict deserve to win? And how would we even judge that? Besides, do we really have the authority to just march down there and decide how the war should end? Does that not make us just as bad, if not worse, than those who started this senseless conflict?”

“It makes us better!” He protested hotly. “We would be ending the slaughter! Stopping a war is always a good thing to do.”

“So might makes right, is that it?”

“Of course not,” Barengar scoffed and stabbed his knife into the hind joint of the goat. With a savage twist of his wrist he popped the joint out of its socket.

“And in less clear cut conflict, lets say a war about trading rights between two of the Cities. A war of greed. Which side do we support?”

“Whichever side has the better claim.”

“And who are we to decide that if not even those involved can do that without taking up arms?”

“We are the Collegium of Mages!”

“And now we are back to the might makes right argument,” I stated flatly.

“Then we just go there and kill the armies of both sides!”, Barengar snapped angrily. “The world will soon learn that war is futile and they will solve their problems peacefully.”

“Or perhaps the whole world will mobilize to kill us instead.”

“Let them try!”

I shook my head slowly. He was too young to understand. Had not seen war with his own eyes yet. He had not been forced to kill people in cold blood, especially not people he loved.

“I know that you believe that you believe in the right things, Barengar. Just as I believe that I believe in the right things. But do I know that I believe in the right things? Not at all.”

Barengar looked at me in confusion. “What do you mean?”

“I have done horrible things, Barengar. Because I believed them to be right. I set in motion events that killed eleven thousand people, because they held to the Skylander ideals. Ideals that were irreconcilable for me. But did that give me the right to kill them all? Once I was certain. I am not so certain any more.”

“But the Skylanders were evil,” Barengar protested.

“According to you,” I pointed out. “If they believe the same of me, would they have the right to kill me?”

Barengar could not answer that.

“It is all just opinions, Barengar.”

“But they were hurting others,” he protested weakly and I could tell that his resolve had broken somewhat.

“As was I. I killed thousands, Barengar. Thousands of corpses by my hand, because we had different opinions.”

I let the silence linger for a moment. “Is there even an objective truth? In any given situation, is there a course of action that is the right one?”

I took a deep breath. These were all conundrums that I had struggled with for most of my life. Ever since I had exterminated the Skylanders.

“Or are all actions some measure of immoral?”

Barengar’s sullen silence was punctuated of the sounds of his knife slicing into flesh.

“That is why you created Epitome, isn’t it?” He finally asked softly. “Because you don’t trust anyone to know what is right. Even yourself. That is why you are so keen on her understanding emotions.”

“It is,” I agreed readily.

“Why do you believe that she would be more trustworthy than a regular human? Will not her judgment be as flawed as ours?”

“Perhaps. But I hope not. I hope that she, as an artificial construct, will be able to separate her emotions from logic but still be able to take both into consideration in order to arrive at the closest one can come to objective truth.”

Barengar finished slaughtering the goat and tossed the final scrap of meat onto the pelt he had spread across the ground.

“I suppose that we will have to wait and see when she is finished.”

“That we will,” I agreed wistfully.

Barengar sighed and almost rubbed his face with his blood-drenched hands before he stopped himself and instead looked around.

“Not much firewood around here,” he commented idly.

“Just cook it with magic flames,” I said hungrily.

“It’s not the same,” he grimaced. “The smoke of wood gives the meat a taste that I miss dearly.”

He was correct, of course, but as he had stated there were not much firewood around here. Only a few shrubberies that managed to eke out a living from the harsh soil, twisted by winds.

But that did not stop him. He stomped over to a shrubbery and felled it with a single swipe of his axe.

Within minutes he had gathered a respectable pile of them and he quickly cut them down to size to make a small cooking fire that he set ablaze with a casual wave of his hand. Strips of meat lifted into the air and hovered in the flames and soon the air was filled with the sound of sizzling fat. Far less than I had hoped for. These mountain goats were lean creatures with not much meat on them at all but it would be enough for the two of us. And a welcome break in the endless string of meals composed entirely out of Skylander fungus.

We ate in silence, contemplating our recent discussion of morality.

Barengar seemed to be ready to speak once we finished, but timing would have it so that the machine that had been constructing had finished while we ate.

Just as he opened his mouth to talk a loud whirring from behind drew both of our attention. The machine was a box about waist high for Barengar, which was just slightly below chest height for me, with a transceiver disk on top. The whirring sound was the gears inside turning as the disk aligned itself in search of a signal. Suddenly it stopped and I waited with bated breath. A brilliant spear of magical power lanced through the sparse cloud cover and slammed into the disk that absorbed it almost losslessly. I saw a few design flaws that would cause the machine to degrade in time but almost before my thoughts had arranged themselves one of the spell cores in my skull reacted and cast the necessary modifications for me. The machine, like most of my contraptions that was intended to last, included a self-reparation state. My unconscious spell altered the reparation spell and it suddenly perceived the machine to be ‘broken’. In fact, it was only the specifications for the machine that had changed, but that hardly mattered. Almost instantly it started to modify itself to get rid of the inefficiencies and ensure that I would not need to return here to fix it in a few weeks. The machine started charging up its internal power cores and when that was done in a minute or two it projected a containment shield over itself and it started to elevate the magical field density inside. When the required density was achieved the shield started expanding to maintain the same density as more power was pumped into the field. It grew rapidly at first but soon started to visibly slow due to the square-cube law: that the volume of a body grew exponentially in proportion to its surface and given a constant flow of power... The results were predictable. But the plateau was relatively small and before long most of it was covered in the thicker field. Mother suddenly formed beside me without me having to summon her.

“Ah, that’s better,” she said with a languid stretch. 

I noticed with some amusement that Barengar watched her ghostly curves with some interest and I made a mental note to stop by somewhere that he could take care of his needs soon.

I knew what happened when you locked a man in a remote area with only a woman, a ghost and a sentient machine as company for prolonged periods of time. And that was a complication that I would rather not deal with myself.

While Barengar was undeniably attractive, he was less than half my age. I may look like a thirty-something woman and as such it was easy to forget that I was almost sixty years old. Or I might already have passed that mark. I had never known exactly how old I was since my father had not put much stock in the passage of time after my birth-mother had died when I was but a toddler. Even if I had not been almost three times my apprentices age, I still would not have wanted him in that way. I had been down that road before decades ago and it had only ended in heartbreak. No. Epitome would be the only child I brought forth into this world.

And what a child she had the potential to become. I just had to figure out how to unlock it.

So far Barengar’s suggestions had been the best one. To study the dragons. They were essentially already what I wished to create with Epitome, yet even dragons were too tied to their emotions for my purposes.

I did not need Epitome to feel with passion, only to understand emotions and adapt as morals changed and developed over time without adapting too much or too quickly. Slow evolution would be the key to progress.

“So the thing worked, huh?” Mother said with a sideways grin at me.

“It did,” I responded smugly.

“I never doubted you, daughter. You have surpassed even Kendall in magical construction.”

I felt a slight blush creep up my neck at the praise. Even as a near sixty years old woman a simple compliment from the woman I idolized could make me blush like a little girl.

“Shall we?” Barengar asked impatiently, itching to explore the bunker.

“Let us wait for Epitome,” I said with a nod at the third Archangel.

Barengar grumbled but did not protest. We did not have to wait for long before the Archangel surged into life and disgorged Epitome’s avatar.

“Long range remote control of this avatar established, Marissa,” she told me placidly. “Where are we?”

“We are to the north-west of the workshop, in a bunker made by ancient man. They have vast repositories of knowledge here that I would like for you to learn and transcribe into a more modern storage format and store in my workshop.

“It will be done, Marissa,” she said without complaint.

With a wave of my hand the enchantments that connected the electrical wire components in the bunker to the magic to electricity converter I had so painstakingly constructed a decade ago woke up.

With a groan the metal doors started to slide to the sides, revealing a dark pit leading into the heart of the mountain.

“Shall we?” I asked and without waiting for a response I walked into the darkness, letting my magesense guide me where my eyes could not.

I did not even have to use my magesense to tell that my companions were hard at my heels.

## CHAPTER EIGHT Barengar

I was finally here! The first tangible sign I had ever encountered from the old world.

It was a strange sensation to see the heavy metal doors open without a trace of magic. The technology of the old world was truly wondrous that they could achieve such a marvel without magic at all.

The inside of the bunker was dark but my magesense could make out forms along the walls and ceiling reminiscent of light spell enchantments but they were all dormant and dark.

It did not matter. A Mage was never truly blind and I focused on my magesense to let it guide me.

For a short while I was envious of the ability of some Mages to always have their magesense at hand.

I knew that Marissa does, and most likely her mother as well.

But not me. I had to focus to actually use it beyond a few paces from my body. And that is what I did then.

We followed the dark corridor for what felt like an eternity before we emerged into a rectangular chamber.

It was filled with rack upon rack of strange contraptions. A nest of metal wires connected them in orderly rows and banks of glass planes lined every available surface.

Marissa grinned beside me and reached her hand to the side and flicked a small switch on the wall.

I heard a high pitched keening that rapidly increased in frequency before I heard a series of sharp sparks and clicks.

Then the world was bathed in blinding light.

I blinked teary eyes and slowly regained my vision. She had turned on the light by flicking that switch.

Marissa smiled at me, her eyes closed presumably to avoid the same blinding that I had just endured.

“A little warning next time?” I complained but she just chuckled at my dismay.

Still with her eyes closed she walked to a larger box mounted to the wall and she pulled the metal doors open before she reached inside and turned several large levers.

A loud whirring sound erupted all around me and I fell into a combat crouch, magic and axe ready at hand.

“Relax, Barengar. It is just the machinery waking up.”

I hesitated before I reluctantly put my weapons away. 

Now with my vision finally fully restored I looked around and found Kyra leaning against the wall and Epitome standing next to one of the racks of whirring machinery. 

Her crystal fingers reached for one of the machines, not quite touching them.

“Are these… Like me?” She asked hesitantly.

Marissa closed the switch-box and turned to face her.

“No. You are far beyond these constructs. You are a sentient entity of your own. These are just a repository of knowledge. A library of sorts.”

Epitome turned her head to look at her creator. “You wish me to learn all contained within?”

“I do. Most of the information here pertains to space flight. The humans of old launched tens of thousands of machines into space to aid them in coordinating the various civilizations that spanned the world.”

“Interesting. I will learn everything I can.”

“Good. Let me show you how to operate these things,” Marissa said and kicked a wheeled chair over to one of the machines.

Epitome did the same and gingerly sat down on the edge of the chair, folding her legs back beneath it and crossing at the ankles with her hands resting on her thighs just by her knees. A supremely feminine posture. One that she must have learned by observing Marissa. 

I walked over to look curiously above Marissa’s head at what she was doing. Her fingers flew over a flat contraption with a multitude of buttons on it and the sound of her tapping filled my ears.

It was oddly soothing to listen to the sound, yet I could find no pattern to what she was doing. The buttons were each marked with a symbol that I could not decipher.

Letters of a dead and forgotten language, I was sure. The glass pane in front of Marissa had lit up and similar strange symbols covered most of the screen.

“How did you learn this language?”

“Grasax’ library in Dun Traelak contained many truly ancient tomes of knowledge. Some of them were written long before the Apocalypse in this tongue and he had several lexicons to decipher them with. Kendall forced me to learn the language, English he called it, when I was a child. Apparently it was the most widely spoken language in the world at the time, by a small margin. Now I am glad that he taught it to me though I hated him for it back then.”

“I know this language,” Epitome said softly. “You included them in my cores?”

“I did. It was not difficult and I thought that it might come in handy one day.”

I shook my head. Every day my mistress impressed me. ‘Yes, of course I transcribed this ancient and dead language that barely anyone knows into a spell core. What do you mean that is an impressive feat, it was not a big deal’.

I watched Marissa tap away at the keys while Epitome watched impassively, soaking up the knowledge, I was sure. I soon tuned out their conversation and grew bored.

This ancient ruin was markedly less interesting than I had expected. 

“It is quite dull when you understand nothing, isn’t it?” Kyra commented from my side.

I started in surprise. I had not noticed her approach. Then yet again, she might not have. She tended to do the same type of flickering relocation that Epitome was so fond of.

One of the minor perks with being an incorporeal ghost.

“Yes. Very.”

“I once believed myself to be a rather proficient Mage,” she commented idly. “Not a genius by any stretch of the imagination. But skilled.”

I waited for her to continue. “And then I met Red. My wife,” she clarified at my confused expression.

“The Red Terror,” I murmured.

“She hated that name,” Kyra winced.

“You still miss her, don’t you?”

“Every day. More than you can imagine,” Kyra sighed. “She was my everything.”

Her entire form wavered and flickered before solidifying again. “Anyway, I thought that I was skilled before I met her. I had used magic for more than a decade. I had fought and killed more Mages than I could count on the Skylands. She had never used magic before she met me. Within a few months she was creating original spells that defied comprehension. She was a natural.”

“And then you’ve seen what Marissa’s become,” I finished for her.

“Yes. That was after my death, of course. I was dormant, locked within that dagger for more than a decade. The last time I saw her in the flesh she was just a fourteen year old girl, crying that her mom was dead and her mother and brother left her behind, never to return. The next time I saw her she was a woman grown, infiltrating the bastion of our enemy in a desperate bid to kill them all. And the things she could do with magic…

Even back then it was staggering. Now…”

“Yes. If I only become half the Mage that she is, I will consider myself very happy.”

“As you should.”

I glanced back to Marissa and Epitome and saw that it was now Epitome tapping away at the buttons as if she had never done anything else with Marissa standing behind her with a hand on a crystal shoulder.

My mistress looked like a proud mother watching her baby take the first steps of life.

“You gonna be alright here on your own, Epitome?” Marissa asked after observing her working for a little while.

“I am not truly here. I am still in the workshop, connected remotely.”

“Right,” Marissa said with a grin. “I can’t believe that actually worked out.”

“You had laid significant groundwork, Marissa. All I had to do was to smooth out the rough edges.”

Marissa stared at her creation, shocked, for almost a full minute before she backed away and turned to me and her mother.

“Let’s leave her to it and get out of this place. I need to step by the School and check in on things, then we can return to the workshop.”

I nodded in agreement, still trying to figure out why she had looked so surprised just then. We walked in silence through the now illuminated corridor for a long while before Marissa spoke.

“Did you hear that?”

I was instantly on high alert, reaching for my weapons and straining my ears for any sound. “What?”

“What Epitome said,” she clarified and I relaxed. She had not heard a threatening sound after all.

“What do you mean, little one?” Kyra asked her daughter.

“The last words she spoke before we left. Was that modesty?”

Kyra’s face lit up in understanding, and then her brows drew down in determination. “Don’t read too much into it, Marissa. It might have just been something she picked up from observing the two of us interacting.”

“But what if it was not? What if she is learning emotions?”

“I think your mother is correct,” I interjected gruffly and immediately regretted my words when I saw the hurt expression on Marissa's face. “Let’s just think on it,” I hastened to add.

“Have you added any new knowledge about emotions recently?”

“No,” she responded resolutely.

“Have you modified her spell cores?”

“No,” she admitted, this time reluctantly.

“Then it would stand against reason that she would not have spontaneously developed a sense of emotions. Am I correct in this statement?”

Marissa mulled it over while we walked several more steps. “Generally, yes. But the spells that comprise her are so complex that it is almost impossible to predict how they might interact and alter her behavior. Just like us humans, we are too complex to predict the reaction to any input.”

“But such a massive development is unlikely to just happen,” I said. And I was fairly confident that my words were correct. Marissa had allowed me to inspect the schematics for many of the spells she had used to create Epitome and while I did not understand even half of them I had a good enough impression to believe that my claim was justified.

Marissa sighed and rubbed her face with her hands. “You are probably right. It’s just that… For a moment…” Her words trailed off as her mothers spectral hand clasped her shoulder.

“It will happen, little one. Some day we will figure out how to make it happen. And by ‘we’ I mean ‘you’.”

“Don’t sell yourself too short, mother.”

“I’m not,” Kyra protested when we emerged into the late afternoon sun. “I am not even close to your level. I can aid you no longer in your spell craft.”

“Perhaps not. Yet you still helped mom even after she surpassed you in magical construction.”

“She was yet a novice. A novice with great aptitude and talent but a novice nonetheless.”

The Archangels reacted to our presence and cycled up as we approached.

“What about the third one?” I asked.

“We will bring it with us. We can discard Epitome’s avatar here once she’s done. She can always build a new one.”

I nodded in response. That made sense. I unslung my axe to stow it in my massive flying armour before I let it devour me.

The illusory screens came to light the moment the helmet settled onto my shoulders and I saw Marissa and Kyra facing each other in front of Marissa’s Archangel.

“I will see you later then,” Kyra stated with a warm smile.

“Yes. Sleep well, mother.”

“Always do.”

Her physical form had faded even before she had spoken the words and Marissa mounted her own Archangel and we lifted into the sky.

Then we went home.

###

Marissa had barely lifted her communications device when she slammed it back down and came racing back to me.

I had been looking forward to a proper cooked meal before we left for the ocean workshop and I sighed heavily when the expression on her face informed me that it was not to be.

“We are going. Now!”

“What? Where?” I protested but she had already darted past me.

Her Archangel was already firing up its jets when I entered the hangar and I practically threw myself into the crystal embrace of my own machine. I leaned my head forward as I raised my fists to adjust my altitude and I found that Marissa had reclaimed the flight controls from me.

“Apparently Uncle Kenny had left a message for me, almost a week ago. It’s bad, Barengar. I have to see him.”

Uncle Kenny? It took me a moment to figure out who she meant. Kendall the Builder! The legendary Skylander that had been adopted into Clan Bizhjerai. Old and trusted friend of the Dragonbane!

As far as I knew he had been on the front lines of the war for years, building fortifications and defending his people from my people.

Were we going to the front lines?

“We are going to the front. Uncle Kenny would not have called me if it was not urgent. He knows that I want nothing to do with the war. Something must have happened to him!”

She sounded as if she was on the brink of tears and I felt a sudden jolt in my belly when our velocity surged to unsurpassed heights.

“If he has died because I was hiding out in the ocean…”

“Calm down, Marissa,” I barked harshly. “He’s not dead, and even if he is it will not help him if you are too upset to fly these things and crush us into the ground!”

I heard her take a few deep breaths yet our velocity did not abate.

“You’re right, Barengar, I’m sorry. Yeah, he’s not dead. Just concerned. He probably just need my thoughts on some defensive design he has,” she rambled.

I shook my head within my suit, yet I could not help feeling excited that we were going to the front.

The war was the whole reason that I had gone to the School, even though I no longer held the same motivations.

I had deserted my conscription so that I could participate in a grander manner, and now that I could I was finally going there.

A grin stretched my lips at the thought of what I would show the soldiers.

We swooped low shortly after the front lines crawled above the horizon. Trenches stretched as far as the eye could see beneath me, which was very far indeed at this altitude.

At some places the trenches were only a few dozen paces from one another and I could see soldiers scurrying about like ants down there. Here and there the fighting was going strong, the soldiers fighting hand to hand in the trenches themselves. Artillery shells detonated on both sides in a steady stream and men carried the wounded to the healers in relative safety.

Marissa looped us between a pair of Bizhjerai Skyships and changed our Archangels into hovering configuration to bring us down to the ground.

Barely had our mechanized feet touched the ground that she shot out of her machine and dashed across the mud. I clambered out of my machine, withdrew my axe and jogged after her.

She must have applied Blood Magic in order to move that quickly. When I finally caught up to her I found her in embrace with an old and tall man, his gnarled fingers entwined in her hair.

His eyes opened and they shone with power and mental acuity that belied his weathered face and gray hair. His armour sat on his thin frame if it had been made for a younger, or more muscular man.

The old man gave me a small smile and extended his hand to me.

“Marissa’s apprentice, I take it.”

I nodded and shook his hand. “Barengar.”

“Kendall,” the man said casually and I felt my eyes open wide.

This was Kendall the Builder?

I bowed deep from the waist. “My lord,” I began but Kendall interrupted me.

“None of that now, boy. I’m Kendall, or General if you must.”

“Yes, sir General!”

Kendall rolled his eyes and finally pushed Marissa back to hold her at arms length. “It’s good to see you again, kiddo,” he smiled warmly at his short ward.

She smiled up at him through wet eyes. “You too, Uncle. I just received your message, you said it was urgent…”

Kendall’s happy expression died instantly and he looked out over the ravaged no mans land.

“It is. I am going to be frank with you, Marissa. We are losing the war. Slowly, but surely. We need you. The Bizhjerai need you. I need you.”

Marissa looked heartbroken for a long moment before her face hardened.

“You know my mind on this, Uncle. I can’t bring the School into this war. We have sworn to not take sides,” she said resolutely.

“I’m not asking for the School, Marissa. I am asking you.”

“I’m just one woman. I can’t turn this war,” she said through gritted teeth. I could tell from the look on her face how much it hurt her to deny the old man.

“You are not just one woman, little one. You are Marissa Zorantis Bizhjerai, the finest Mage in the world and one of the best fighters I have ever seen. You brought down the Groundlands almost single-handedly.”

“Did you lure me here just to try to get me to join the war again?”

“No,” Kendall smiled, “though I was loathe to at least not try. I actually have a schematic that I would like your thoughts on. Your research on artificial ley lines will undoubtedly let you find a flaw in my design.”

“That, at least, I can help you with, old man,” Marissa smiled.

“No time to waste, come,” Kendall responded and promptly spun on his heel and led us toward his command post. “Tea?”

“Please,” Marissa responded.

I just shook my head when Kendall looked at me inquisitively.

“He does not speak much, does he?” Kendall chuckled.

“No,” Marissa agreed. “Only when necessary.”

“If only more people in this world could adhere to the same,” Kendall commented while gesturing for an aide to bring the tea to Marissa.

“That’s rich, coming from you, old friend,” Kyra said as she flickered into being behind Kendall.

The old man’s eyes widened before a wide smile cracked his leathery face and he spun around to gather the specter into his arms.

“Kyra! It’s been too long. Still dead?”

“Yeah, that is not about to change. Are you still old?” She retorted with a wry grin.

“Even older, and that will just progress from this point,” Kendall chuckled. “Sometimes I envy you, Kyra. You are as young and beautiful as when you died while I… You know.”

“It ain’t worth it, Kendall.”

“Perhaps not when your body was still young and strong, but this old meat shell has started to fail me. I’ve had lung rot twice this last year and I fear that the next one will actually get me.”

The aide returned with a steaming cup of tea for Marissa, who thanked the woman quietly.

“Nonsense, Kendall. You are too stubborn to die from a stupid disease,” Kyra responded while casting a long glance at the aide.

The redheaded woman stood at ramrod attention, staring at the space of nothing between Kendall and Kyra.

Kendall finally chuckled and addressed his aide. “It’s alright, Jani. Say hello to your Aunt and cousin.”

The aide instantly relaxed and the smile that erupted across her face turned her from merely pretty to radiant. 

My attention immediately perked up when I saw her smile and I paid more attention to the woman.

Older than me by at least half a decade and short. Taller than Marissa but far shorter than Kyra and her armour hid her form well, but not well enough that I could not determine a more generous than average swell to her hips and bust.

“Auntie,” Jani greeted Kyra and flowed smoothly into the ghosts arms.

“Jani. You’ve grown since I saw you last,” Kyra laughed and pushed her away to take a good look at her. “How long has it been?”

“Six years,” the young woman said while turning toward Marissa. “Cousin,” she said and embraced Marissa.

To my surprise Marissa did not return the embrace as warmly as Kyra had.

“Kendall, remind me please. Is she the second generation of your strays? Or the third?”

“Second,” the old man responded with a warm look at his adoptive daughter. “From Clan Azwail.”

Kyra nodded sagely. “That would explain the tits and the hips,” she mused.

“Auntie!” Jani squeaked indignantly as her neck and chest turned a lovely shade of red.

“Stop tormenting the poor woman, mother,” Marissa admonished her Archon mother before turning her attention back to Kendall. “You had some schematics for me to look at?”

“Marissa! You have not seen Kendall for years and you go straight to work? I thought that I taught you better manners than that!” Kyra protested.

Kendall raised a hand to cut her off. “It’s fine, Kyra. We are fighting a war after all, we must focus on keeping our people from dying.”

Kyra settled down. “There’s not enough magic here for me to sustain myself much longer. Call me if you need me, or before we leave. Whichever comes first.”

And with that she was gone.

“Right,” Kendall said and rubbed his arthritic fingers together. “Jani, take care of young Barengar here. We shan’t be long.”

“Yes, father,” the redhead chirped and Kendall and Marissa retreated into an adjoining room.

I found myself alone in the room with the beautiful redhead and I regarded her with interest that I hoped was not too obvious.

She slowly sidled up to me and offered me a shy smile.

“Uh, hello. I’m Jani,” she introduced herself with a proffered hand.

“Barengar,” I responded and engulfed her dainty hand in my massive paw.

“A pleasure,” she beamed before her previous uncertainty returned. “Are you, uh, Marissa’s apprentice?”

“I am,” I said and then quickly cursed myself when the conversation lapsed.

I wracked my brain for something to say, anything.

“Kendall called you and Marissa cousins, but you must be less than half of her age,” I finally managed to get out.

“Yeah… It’s a complicated story, my family tree.”

“I’ve got time,” I shrugged.

“Father and his husband always wanted children, but they obviously could not have any of their own,” Jani began. “So after the war against the Skylanders they adopted a pair of orphans.”

“A noble act, worthy of commendation.”

“Yes. They are wonderful parents. But when my older siblings grew up my fathers found their home cold and empty. So they adopted three more children.”

“You among them.”

“Yes,” she smiled. “I guess it was not as complicated as I had assumed,” she giggled and then blushed again.

I could not help but grin back. “That sounds rather straight forward. And that is how you ended up as the General’s aide?”

Jani nodded vigorously. “He wanted someone who could read his smallest expressions and assume his desires before he knew them himself.”

“A wise man. A lesser man might have chosen someone else to avoid complaints of nepotism. And an even lesser man would have not cared at all.”

“Quite so.”

The conversation lapsed once more.

“How goes the war?” I asked suddenly and then mentally berated myself. That was not a good subject to discuss with a pretty girl.

“Not well,” Jani winced. “We are not giving ground, but neither are we taking any of worth. We kill more than we lose but the Ca’el… There’s just so damned many of them. For every one we kill three appear to take their place.”

“My kind likes to breed,” I shrugged, hoping to push her thoughts to a more pleasurable pastime.

Instead of the desired reaction her face paled and she took half a step back, her hand falling to the hilt of the sword slung to her hip.

“You’re Ca’el?”

I raised my hands placatingly. “I once stood as such. Now I am of the Collegium of Mages. I hold no loyalty to my old homeland.”

Slowly she removed her hand from her sword, but her eyes were still wary.

I was about to say something to defuse the situation when I heard gunfire. Very close gunfire.

Jani darted toward the door just as a messenger burst through the opening. “General! They’re attacking!”

Marissa and Kendall practically sprinted out of the inner chamber and I barely reacted in time to fall in step with them. Kendall was the first to exit the command center and a powerful urge caused me to tackle Jani to the side and throw myself headlong aside Kendall. The shield Marissa had gifted me snapped open and I felt a powerful impact on the crystal plates. I landed heavily on my shoulder and rolled to my feet, triggering the enchantment on the shield to expand its protection.

“Sniper!” I called out in warning and reached for my axe.

“Got him,” Marissa stated flatly from behind me and then I heard the sharp retort of her rifle. I had not even noticed her taking it from her Archangel.

Power surged around her, manifesting her full Angel armour.

Belatedly I activated my own.

I glanced over my shoulder and saw Kendall raising his staff, a powerful blast of magic lancing out of it to shoot across the no mans land to detonate among the charging Ca’el. Clansmen peeked up from the trenches to squeeze off shots, but the slow rate of their bolt action rifles were not enough to take down the oncoming horde. Dozens fell only to be trampled beneath the feet of their comrades.

Marissa fired shot after shot, each detonating in a horrific blast of magic that cut down clusters of men, but to no avail.

The horde would surge into the trenches and then flow along it, claiming it for themselves.

Magic streamed out of Jani, tossing her red hair about her head like a storm gale while glyphs and runes formed on the ground. The mud hissed and boiled and dried into cracks where the power touched it. She slammed her fist down and the world went white a split second before I heard a thunderous boom. It was the smell of ionizing air that finally informed me what had happened. She had called a bolt of lightning down from a clear blue sky. I blinked my eyes until my vision cleared and stared at the girl in wonder. How the hell did she have the power to reach into the sky like that?

Later, Barengar. Focus!

I spun around again when I felt a bullet ping into my shield. The Ca’el were leaping into the trenches in droves and I could already hear the screams and clang of steel on crystal from the melee down there.

We had to stop the spread of the Ca’el somehow.

I looked around and saw a group of Bizhjerai and Ithlaki lugging one of the new machine guns on a tripod along the trench.

“You! Set the machine gun up there,” I screamed at them and pointed.

I looked at Marissa and saw that she was hovering far above with her Angel armour, scouting the advance of Ca’el. Kendall was speaking into a communicator, coordinating an artillery strike from the sound of it.

Only Jani was left with me, crouching behind my shield.

“Can you fight?”

“Yes,” she said defiantly.

I did not doubt her. “Go, take the right flank. Keep them from spreading through the trench, block it somehow. Force them to climb up the side and into the killing ground,” I said and pointed at the machine guns.

To her credit she just nodded once before she tensed her legs and leaped impossible high into the air. A surge of magic caused her to sail toward the far edge of the trench.

I watched her retreating form with open-mouthed awe. She had just made a leap that would require Valkyrie armour, but she had none.

I shook my head to clear it and made my own leap aided by my armour.

With axe held high I crashed into the trench and my former kin.

###

I claimed the first life even before my boots touched the floorboards. My axe clove the Ca’el mans head from temple to jaw in a single stroke and I slammed my shoulder into his limp body to force him out of the way. The next man raised his rifle to spit me on his bayonet. I let go of my axe with my left hand and grabbed the barrel of his weapon and wrenched it aside before I slammed the top spike of my axe into his throat. I swept his legs from under him and left him to bleed to death behind me. That was foolish of me since even a dying man could still fire his rifle, so I just strengthened my shield to my rear and forged on.

The next Ca’el that I encountered was locked in combat with a Bizhjerai woman and before I could aid her she had clubbed him in the side of the head with the butt of her rifle.

With a scream she raised her weapon above her head before bringing it down on his head with a wet crack. Like the veteran she was she had heard the sound and immediately dismissed the dead man and spun to face another foe. But she was a tad too slow and the bayonet of another warrior entered her side. She cursed and kicked his kneecap with a crunch. The man staggered back and took my thrown axe to the face. It clove through his forehead and the bridge of his nose and he dropped limply. I pulled the axe through the air and back into my hand with a meaty thwack as the Bizhjerai woman fell to a knee, hand pressed tightly to the wound in her ribs. I dashed up to her and pulled her hand from the wound. Blood oozed out of the ragged hole in her flesh and I winced. He’d gotten her right in the kidney and I was beyond impressed that she’d had the wherewithal to fight him through the immense pain she must have been in.

“I’m sorry,” I screamed over the din and then cauterized her wound with a blast of magic.

The sinews in her neck bulged with her scream but I could barely hear her over the din of battle.

“Go,” I screamed at her and tossed a spell directly overhead. It detonated with a bright flash, hopefully blinding any Ca’el up there that might shoot her. I dropped my axe in the mud and grabbed her under her arms and aided by a touch of magic I hurled her up on the safer side of the trench. The axe returned to my hand and I charged onward. I turned the bend in the trench and beheld chaos.

The fighting was an absolute cluster-fuck, no lines at all. Ca’el and Clansmen were mixed and they were fighting in small clusters. It was quite obvious to me that this segment of the trench would be lost. An explosion further down the trench heralded Jani at work. At least I hoped that it was Jani. I threw myself at the nearest Ca’el and managed to grab her neck from behind. With a roar I clove her head with a savage chop and tossed her corpse aside.

A fireball launched from my off hand incinerated the next. I threw my axe into the back of a large warrior that was about to kill an Ithlaki soldier and then I had to deploy my shield to absorb a fusillade of bullets. I collapsed the shield and clapped my hands together, pouring magic into the sound to amplify it. The little firing squad tumbled into the mud, thrown about by the sudden clap of thunder. A burst of my Angel armour sent me flying and I landed with a stomp on the head of a Ca’el woman. A chop of my axe killed another and then I suddenly felt a sharp pang in my thigh. I spun around and saw a woman lying on the ground clutching the dagger she had just stabbed me with. A flex of magic sent lightning coursing into her body, causing her hair and armour to smoke. But it was too weak to kill her, so I did that with the dagger extension of my pommel. A quick stab right between her breasts, straight through her armour.

I was a bit surprised that the blade cut through solid steel as if it was butter, but I should not have been. Marissa did nothing in half-measures and a weapon crafted for a dear friend of hers would be of top quality. I limped to the side and cauterized the wound with magic. 

“Barengar!” I heard the warning cry and spun around, a moment too late.

A Ca’el woman leaped into the trench from above, her bayonet aimed squarely at my chest. My shield was focused to my front, and more powerfully to my rear. I had not been clever enough to guard my top side, forgetting that I was fighting in a deep trench in the ground.

A large projectile struck the woman, wrenching her to the side. A flash of red billowed in the air as the pair tumbled through the mud, snarling and hissing at one another.

Jani. She had saved my life. I raised my arm to throw my axe, but Jani was blocking my throw with her body.

Somehow the smaller woman ended up on top, straddling the Ca’el female.

With a howl Jani raised a small dagger and jammed it into the other woman’s throat, again and again until she stilled.

Jani straightened up, covered in mud and blood with her red hair grimy and stringy plastered across her face.

I had never seen anything more beautiful.

“Stop fucking around!” She screamed at me before her entire form lit up and she blurred past me, leaving a faint outline of herself in her wake as she ran.

She crashed into the fighting like a howling banshee, dagger and magic claiming lives in equal measure.

I was stood there like an impressed fool for only a heartbeat or two before I charged after her. Most of the Clansmen in the trench were already dead, but just behind the next bend I could see that the trench was collapsed. Jani had already accomplished her side of the task. Suddenly Kyra appeared next to me, already aiming down her ethereal sights. Her revolver spoke and claimed a Ca’el soldier, his Soul draining into the shot and raising his corpse as a zombie.

Marissa dropped down into the trench beside me, magic bursting out the gems in her head, taking down Ca’el soldiers with deadly precision.

She gave me a sidelong glance and a grin.

“Let’s show them what we can do.”

I stared at her for a moment before I threw my head back and howled.

As one, we charged the enemy. The Ca’el were numerous, but three Mages skilled in combat was more more than a match for them.

I lost count of how many I killed and my conscious mind retreated into the back of my head.

Each of us acted like a part of a greater unit, defending one another when needed and changing adversaries when the moment came.

Several times I felt a strong urge in my chest that caused me to deploy and extend my shield to deflect a shot or bayonet from Jani or Marissa.

Slowly but surely we pushed the Ca’el back toward the collapsed earthworks where Jani had destroyed part of the trench.

The Ca’el was pressed close, scrambling to get away from us when suddenly a handful of metal spheres rolled down the side of the trench.

“Grenades!” I called out and fired my Angel jets. With my magesense I could see Marissa doing the same and Jani launching herself into the air on a flare of magic.

I looked down and caught sight of a multitude of terrified faces in the trench just before the grenades exploded.

Fire, mud, dust, debris and parts of bodies was hurled toward us but with far from enough power to hurt us.

A severed arm bounced off my thigh, but that was the worst of it.

The brisk breeze quickly dispersed the dust and smoke in the air and to my surprise I saw that many Ca’el had survived, shielded from the blast by the bodies of their comrades.

Several of them milled around, shell shocked, while others stared up at us with terrified cries on their lips. One of them pointed at us.

“It’s the Ghost of Death!” I heard someone shout in the tongue of the southern provinces of Ca’el, referring to the name my people had for Marissa. Even in the far reaches of the Empire the story of the Groundlands and its destruction had spread.

And suddenly my kin were fleeing, scrambling to get out of the trench.

Those that made it topside were riddled with bullets from the machine gun nest. Many did not make it up.

We waited for a few moments until the machine gun stopped firing before we drifted down toward the command post. We soared high above the battlefield and I surveyed the enemy lines. They were less than fifty paces away from our trench and I could see the battered survivors flee back into their holes. But one figure towered above the rest, standing on a raised platform to survey they field of battle.

Clad all in black, hooded and with a familiar mask to cover his face.

“It’s the Demagogue!” I screamed to Marissa and she swung around to fly backwards.

I couldn’t believe it. The man who had manipulated my people into starting this war was right there. The foreign lord who so suddenly had claimed the Emperor’s ear and turned my people into this warmongering horde.

“Jani!” Marissa screamed. “Get back to Kendall, tell him that the Demagogue is here.”

“What are you going to do?” The beautiful redhead screamed back.

“We are going to end this. Barengar, come!”

And then she was zipping toward the enemy lines, powerful magic surging out of her into a multitude of massive spells.

I gulped. This was suicide. Two Mages against hundreds of Ca’el?

I spun around to face Jani in the air. “Tell Kendall to send help! Quickly!”

Jani paled but nodded before falling toward the command post.

I gritted my teeth and pushed my Angel armour to try to catch up to Marissa.

Suicide or no, I had sworn an Oath to her. I would not, could not, break it.

Not for anything.

## CHAPTER NINE Marissa

I cursed myself while I soared toward the hulk of a man that stood defiantly among his soldiers.

I had sworn many years ago that I would not partake in this war. That the Collegium of Mages were a neutral agent in this world.

But when Kendall had pleaded for my help my heart had almost broken. That man had raised me after my adopted parents had died.

He had given me everything, and I had denied him in his hour of need.

I had intended to keep denying him no matter how much it hurt me. I was doing it to protect my students, to keep them from becoming pawns in the wars of others.

Yet when we had been attacked by the Ca’el I could not just stand aside and watch my people get slaughtered.

And when the Demagogue somehow showed up within sight, I could not turn away.

Kill him and the Emperor might come to his senses and stop this madness.

I might be able to end this war without embroiling my students into an endless meat grinder that would undoubtedly claim many of their lives.

And so I sent myself toward the demagogue, preparing to unleash everything I had at him.

I knew next to nothing about his capabilities, but I was not taking any chances. I would obliterate the man and leave nothing behind but ash.

Spells surged out of the gems in my head, every manner of death and destruction that I knew formed around me and power surged out of the stores in my skeleton.

Strike hard, strike first and your opponent will be too dead to retaliate.

There is no overkill. Only kill.

At least in situations like these.

I prepared to unleash the full fury if my Soul, just a few more yards of flight to get within optimum reach.

The man just stood there, hidden eyes staring at me through the eye sockets of his dark engraved mask. His back straight, shoulders back as if daring me to strike him.

Not an ounce of fear radiated from him.

For a moment, I hesitated. Even a non-Mage would be able to see and feel the power of my spells, yet he stared defiantly at me.

What did he know that I did not?

I quashed the doubt and angled my wing jets forward to swing myself around to slow down.

And I unleashed all that I had.

The air flickered and wavered as my spells ripped apart the area between us and the Demagogue vanished in a cloud of fire, plasma, lightning and even more sinister forms of destruction.

The men that had been clustered around him was torn apart in an instant, bits and pieces of them and their equipment sprayed out across the mud.

I hovered above it all, panting and staring at the destruction. A slow and satisfied smile spread across my lips.

The Demagogue was dead. The war might very well be over.

Barengar drifted in by my side.

“Is he dead?” He called, axe hefted tightly in his armoured hands.

“Yes. He is dead,” I proclaimed with weary satisfaction.

It would take me a long time to recharge my skeleton. I still had a little bit of power left stored within it, but compared to what had been it was almost nothing.

I was about to turn around and head back to Kendall to report when I saw a flicker of movement in the fading storm of magic.

“What the fuck?” I muttered and focused my crystal eye on the whirlwind of magical residue that covered the area.

My crystal vision filtered out a lot of the interference and I could make out the vague outline of a Soul in there. One that burned bright with magical power.

The fuck? Ca’el were not supposed to have any Mages in their ranks.

“Dodge!” I called to Barengar just as a flash of light blinded me and a massive force impacted my chest.

I could feel my breastplate crack along with my breastbone, the jagged edge of my armour cutting deep into my right breast, nearly cutting it off.

The skeletal wings of my Angel armour shattered and I lost control of my flight. My head snapped forward when my body was launched backward and I felt several of the muscles in my neck tear.

A moment later the ground slammed into my back with the force of freight train, cracking at least three of my ribs and dislocating my left arm in blinding stabs of pain.

I could see nothing, feel nothing. Even my magesense was a blinded mess from all of the power that I had unleashed in my cataclysmic blast. I took a ragged breath and I felt a broken rib poke into my lung, causing a fit of coughs that only made it so much worse.

I did not even have the mental acuity to use Blood Magic to dull the pain.

A lifetime of pain later vision returned to my crystal eye and I saw the world through a blue tint.

The Demagogue was casually striding toward me, his blank face cold and unforgiving. I tried to reach for my revolver, but my dislocated arm had gone entirely numb.

The large man towered above me, looking down in what struck me as malice.

“I knew that waging war against your people would eventually bring you forth,” he said with a gravelly voice and a mocking tone. “I just had not imagined that it would take so many years before I found you.”

I tried to speak but could barely draw breath to sustain life, much less to speak.

“Ah, speechless are you? It does not matter. I did not come here to break words, only to kill you and avenge my people.”

My eyes narrowed at him. Did he know me?

“Oh, I forget my manners,” he chuckled. “You probably don’t recognize me with this mask on. Yet again, you might not recognize me either way.”

He reached up and pulled his mask and hood off. He had once quite obviously been very handsome, yet now he was horribly scarred. Half of his face appeared to be completely burned and melted to the bone, teeth peeking through the tattered remains of his cheek and his cheekbone was laid bare to the world. His ear was simply gone and only a fused hole remained on the side of his head. Most of his scalp was similarly ravaged and whatever tufts of hair that sprouted on the other side of his head was almost completely white with only a few splashes of faded green. One eye was milky white and more than half hidden by a melted eyelid. The other was a deep green. That was what spurred recognition within me.

“Zandor?” I wheezed through gritted teeth.

He smiled, his burned flesh twisting that once so gentle grin into something from a nightmare.

“So you do remember me, Marissa? I feared that you had cast me from your thoughts just as you cast me aside so long ago.”

It was him. It really was him. I had thought him dead at the destruction of the Groundlands. For thirty five years I had mourned his death.

“You destroyed everyone and everything I had ever loved, Marissa. And now I will kill you, just like you killed Valia.”

He raised his massive foot to stomp me into oblivion, and I could do nothing to stop him.

A part of me did not want to.

That part of me still believed that what I had done back then was wrong, that I deserved this.

I closed my eyes, and welcomed death.

His foot rose and Zandor paused to savour the moment. And that was when mother decided to step in.

She appeared with a wild cry and swung at Zandor with her ephemeral, yet deadly, sword.

“Begone specter,” he muttered and gestured with his arm. Mother flinched and her form wavered. She took half a retreating step and then she abruptly vanished.

“Mother!” I gurgled through the blood in my lung, devastated to see her banished that easily.

Where Zandor had learned this much magic was a mystery to me, but it did not matter. I was about to die. Zandor started to bring his foot down toward my face when a blazing light entered the range of my magesense.

Like a burning comet it zoomed close and collided with Zandor with a powerful impact that sent him flying back a dozen yards.

Kendall landed from his rebound next to me, Barengar touching down behind him.

“Get the fuck up, kid,” Kendall snapped at me. “More Ca’el are coming, we gotta get out of here!”

His words snapped me out of my stupor and the modifications I had wrought on my body had worked to restore functionality to my limbs. The spare power in the power cores embedded in my breasts had splinted my ribs and breastbone and my caustic blood burned in my lung, stopping the bleeding.

I coughed a cloud of smoke and sat up. Barengar knelt beside me and with a grunt he popped my shoulder back into its socket.

I barely held back the scream.

My mind was clear enough by then that I could use Blood Magic to dull the pain. Not dull it completely since that would render my limbs utterly useless, but enough that I did not want to scream at the slightest twitch of my arm or twist of my torso.

“We have to retreat,” Barengar yelled as he surveyed the approaching Ca’el warriors.

I looked at the oncoming foe and saw that they were not the regular infantry of the Ca’el Empire. These wore Skylander armour and their bodies were wreathed in magical shields.

My crystal eye told me that they were not powerful Mages, but Mages nonetheless.

I desperately wanted to go after Zandor, force him to explain to me why he had done this.

The choice was taken from me when he came barreling toward me, deadly spells forming in both of his hands. I gritted my teeth through the pain and drew my revolver with my recently dislocated shoulder and shot him three times.

The enchanted bullets flattened on his shield and I had to scramble out of the way of his charge. His spells surged into me. I deflected the first one with a weak shield, the strongest I could muster. The second one was robbed of just enough power to not kill me, instead it made my heart stop for the briefest of moments before the monitoring enchantment jolted it back to beating. The world returned to focus just in time to see Barengar’s axe, wreathing in actinic lightning, impact Zandor’s shield. It bounced off it with an impressive shower of sparks but without doing any damage.

The axe zipped back into his hand and with a frustrated growl he spun to face the approaching Mages. His shield deployed to deflect a fireball and he launched his axe in response.

That was the last I saw of him before Zandor blocked my view with his massive body. With my magesense I saw Kendall hovering above our heads, launching powerful blasts of magic that incinerated two of the approaching Mages.

Zandor’s hands suddenly erupted in fire. He had always preferred fire as his weapon of choice.

He launched a tongue of flames at me and I responded with a cone of cold to smother the flames.

I staggered from a sudden bout of dizziness.

“You are weak, Marissa, but you are still skilled in magic. But I will not need magic to defeat you in your current state,” he sneered and advanced on me with his fists raised. 

I tried to blast him with a concussive force, but his shield brushed the weak strike away as if it was nothing.

And then his big fist was approaching my face and I had to raise my own arm wrapped in a shield to deflect his punch.

The force of impact was immense and my shield collapsed. The remaining force of his blow struck my forearm and dislocated my shoulder again. I stumbled to the side with a cry and had to repeat the procedure with my other arm.

This time the shield held but my reserves were running dangerously low. The next punch landed solidly in my chest and broke my breastbone open further. My left leg slid far back to brace the impact and Zandor did not hesitate at the opening. His leg lifted and his heavy boot crashed down on my thigh, snapping my femur and shattering the crystal it was made of. Searing pain ripped through me and I landed hard on my dislocated shoulder and the world went black for a moment. When my vision cleared Zandor was approaching and I could see Barengar roar and strike the head from one of the other Mages with his axe, spinning around and charging at Zandor’s back.

Zandor was limping, his left boot a smoldering mess and oozing blood through the ragged leather.

I was done, out of magical power and drained of stamina. Pain lanced from my hip in sharp stabs that made my breath hitch and I could barely breathe due to my cracked breastbone.

I took a tally of my wounds.

Dislocated shoulder. Broken ribs, still splinted. Cracked breastbone, the spells were weakly trying to shore up the fracture. A dozen cuts that were already boiling and sealing themselves.

And finally, I saw with my magesense, my right leg was gone and my hip was a shattered ruin. 

Zandor didn’t waste his breath on words this time, he just drew a dagger from his belt and knelt beside me, hand lancing at my throat. At the last moment he froze and threw up his left hand to project shield just as a powerful beam of magic slammed into it. The weight of it lifted him to his feet and pushed him back a few steps, right into Barengar who slammed his axe into Zandor’s thigh from behind.

Zandor screamed in pain and fury and fell to a knee but his shield did not waver. Barengar slid through the mud to a stop beside me, his hands already moving over my body to try to stem the bleeding from my hip.

“Barengar,” I gasped out. “Zandor…”

Kendall stepped over me, his staff held aimed at Zandor as he poured his prodigious power into pushing the big man away.

“Get her out of here, boy!” He shouted at Barengar.

“No,” I whimpered, unheeded.

“Go! I will hold him off!” Kendall shouted as his beam of magic petered out.

Barengar slid his hands under my armpits and grabbed firmly across my chest as he lifted me from the ground almost effortlessly. I screamed in pain when that caused the broken ends of my breastbone to grind together.

Zandor launched a flurry of magical bolts at Kendall who expertly deflected them but I could see that the old man was weakening rapidly.

“Kendall,” I gasped and coughed, unable to make my voice heard.

Zandor advanced on Kendall and they exchanged a few blows, magic wreathed fists versus staff. Barengar fired his Angel armour but we only lifted a foot from the ground, his power core running low and not having the power to lift us both from the ground.

“Leave me,” I whispered but I did not know if he heard me. My eyes were fastened on the duel before us and the few Mages that survived formed an ever tightening circle.

Two of them launched attacks at Barengar and me but he deployed his shield to protect us both.

When it collapsed again I saw Zandor duck beneath Kendall’s staff and straighten up within his guard.

I knew what was going to happen even before I saw it.

Zandor reached out and grabbed the top of Kendall’s helmet and wrenched his head to the side, exposing a gap between his helmet and pauldrons.
