Time slowed down as I saw Zandor raise the dagger that he had almost killed me with, and I saw it sink into Kendall’s neck.

“No!” I howled, but only a strangled cry and a series of painful bloodstained coughs erupted from my throat.

Again and again Zandor stabbed Kendall in the throat before he disdainfully tossed the dying man aside. Then he turned to our hovering forms and smiled wickedly.

Mother appeared beside me and took the scene in with a single glance. I saw her waver and pause when she saw Kendall’s fallen form but then she spun around with a determined look on her face.

She flung her hand at Barengar and magic surged forth. Barengar tried to dodge it, believing it to be an attack. The power coursed into his armour’s power core and mother flickered out of being. Barengar’s Angel armour flared into proper life and we soared to the sky and back toward our own battle lines. Zandor howled in fury and threw spells after us but none of them had a hope to catch up.

Through fading vision I saw Clansmen set up rear firing positions in the trench I had cleared with my magical attack on Zandor and they started squeezing off shots at the Mages and Zandor.

Zandor jerked as the fusillade of bullets impacted his weakened shield and he reluctantly took a step back, then another before he finally turned tail and fled with his apprentices in tow.

Then darkness claimed me.

###

Pain filled me when I woke up, but far less than there should have been.

The first memory that returned to me was realizing who the Demagogue had truly been. Zandor.

Quickly followed by the image of Kendall lying broken and dying on the muddy ground at Zandor’s feet.

My eyes snapped open instantly and I flung the blankets from my body.

I tried to sit up on the bed, but my body would not respond properly and I only managed an awkward flop. I looked down on myself and saw that my hip was bound with tight bandages, and that was where my leg ended. I dimly remembered tremendous pain in my hip and I realized that I had lost my leg. But that fact paled in importance compared to the one that Kendall might be dead.

I stood up on my good leg and reached my fingers to my throat to caress the gem on my choker.

Thankfully, whoever had undressed me had left my choker on and I set to manifest my clothes as quickly as possible. I had improved the enchantments in the choker over the years and it took less time for my clothes to manifest than it took me to shuffle-hop across the small chamber I lay in. With my magesense I saw Barengar and Jani sitting side by side by a table, steaming mugs before them. They appeared to be talking softly, yet sound was not conveyed by magesense so I had no idea about what. Suddenly Barengar stiffened and twisted his body to gaze at the door I was behind. He had sensed my movement. He stood with such alacrity that the chair he had been sitting on was sent flying and he ripped the door open before my weary mind had even registered his movement.

“You should rest,” he told me and gently put his hand on my shoulder to halt my advance.

A single glare made him blanch and back away to let me leave. Jani stood up slowly, her eyes starting to brim over with tears.

Icy cold settled in my belly, but I refused to believe it.

“Where’s Kendall?” I asked and my voice sounded hollow even to my own ears.

Jani finally started sobbing and fat tears rolled down her cheeks.

“Where is he?” I pressed tactlessly.

“Marissa,” Barengar began but quieted when I turned my face toward him. He swallowed deeply before he seemed to gather his courage. “We recovered his body from the field of battle, but…”

His words faded away, drowned in a loud buzzing sound in my ears. My vision darkened and the room twisted violently before reality snapped back into crystal clarity. I blinked slowly and then focused on Barengar.

“Take me to him.”

“I’m, not sure that is…”

“Take me to him,” I repeated.

Barengar scrutinized my face for a long moment before he nodded curtly. He extended his hand and a crystal crutch slammed into his palm. He offered it to me wordlessly and I tucked it under my armpit mechanically.

“Cousin,” Jani whimpered as I hopped past her but with my magesense I could see Barengar give her a shake of his head. I paid no heed to them and instead focused on hopping on my crutch. I soon found myself in sunlight. It would have been a nice day if it had not been for the ever present thunder of artillery fire and the distant smattering of gunfire. The war never ceased, not even when the general fell. I waited for Barengar and Jani to join me outside and when they emerged from the command post I was surprised to see them holding hands.

Ordinarily such a sight might have warmed my heart, but today it was cold and dead yet still beating. Silent tears were still trickling down Jani’s youthful face, yet she walked with a quick stride, but not so quick that I could not keep up with her. Before long we entered a hastily erected pavilion and I ducked into the darkness within. A gem on my head responded to my wish for light, even though my magesense had already told me what was waiting inside in excruciating detail. I needed to see it with my own eyes. A light spell burst into being overhead and cast the interior of the pavilion in a harsh white light.

The only piece of furniture in the place was a gurney, and atop it lay Kendall, still and not breathing.

He had been cleaned, his hair and mustache combed and styled impeccably. Just like it always had been in life. His intelligent blue eyes were closed forever and for the first time in my life I did not sense the vast power contained within his Soul. Because it was not there any longer. This was not Uncle Kenny. This was just meat. He was gone. Even so, tears spilled down my cheeks as I stood there regarding his corpse.

Mother flickered into being on the other side of Kendall, looking down at him with a crushed expression on her face. She closed her eyes and shining tears dripped to the ground.

“When I was young I was certain than he would outlive me. But when I became a liche… For thirty five years I have known that this day would come yet… It hurts just as bad as when your mom died, Marissa.”

“I know, mother. I know.” I drew a deep shaky breath. “It’s my fault that he died.”

“You were the cause,” mother countered. “He would not have attacked the Demagogue if you had not, yet he chose to intervene. You caused his death, inadvertently, but none of the blame lie on you. It lies entirely with the Demagogue.”

“Zandor,” I murmured ruefully and felt my fist clench.

“Yes. Zandor.”

Mother reached out and placed her hand on the forehead of her oldest friend. “Farewell, old friend. I hope that you may rest in piece, and when you meet Red… Please tell her that I will join you eventually.”

I did not comment on mother’s words. I knew that she did not truly believe in the afterlife where the spirits of the ancestors watched over their descendants like the Bizhjerai did.

But it was a lie she told herself to dull the pain of the passing of old friends. Despite my own knowledge that Kendall was gone, truly gone, I spoke as well.

“I am sorry, uncle, that it had to end this way. I love you.”

I could almost hear his voice. “It’s alright, kiddo. I love you too.”

But his lips would be forever still. I gave him a last long look before I exchanged a glance with mother. She gave me a nod and vanished.

I hopped out of the pavilion and rejoined Jani and Barengar outside.

“Come, Barengar. We are leaving.”

He exchanged a confused glance with Jani. “We are not staying for the funeral?”

“He’s being shipped back to Dun Traelak for the ceremony?” I asked Jani and I waited for her to nod before I continued. “Then no. That will take several days and we have work to do.”

“He was your uncle, Marissa!” Barengar chastised me.

“You’re correct. Key word being was. He is dead. He will not care if I attend his funeral. In fact, I knew him well enough to know that he would much rather I do something productive than wallow in grief.”

Barengar’s face hardened for a moment before Jani put a hand on his arm and stood on her tip toes to say something in his ears. His demeanor changed instantly and his face softened as he looked at the back of my retreating head. Then he hurried to catch up with me, Jani almost having to run to keep up with his much longer strides. Our Archangels were standing idle not too far away and they woke up when they sensed my presence.

I walked directly up to mine and tossed my crutch aside before I awkwardly tried to move into the position that would let the Archangel pick me up. Barengar was beside me in an instant and ignored my glower when he put his big hands around my ribs, gently so as to not put undue pressure on my broken ribs. One of the drawbacks of having replaced my bones with crystal was that they would not heal naturally. I would have to repair them myself. He lifted me into the Archangel and it started to swallow me up. Darkness surrounded me when the helmet sank over my head and I let out a quivering sigh.

Kendall was dead. Yet another person I loved had died before their time. All because Zandor wanted me dead.

Perhaps I should just let him kill me. I deserved it and if that would satisfy him so that the war would end it would be worth it. How many had died in his mad scheme to lure me out?

The illusion spell flickered into life and I saw Jani stand on her tip toes to press a quick kiss to Barengar’s cheek before she hurried back toward the command post. Was she in charge now? Or was she just the aide for whoever had been second in command? I felt momentarily bad for not even having asked, but Jani and I had never been close. Kendall had adopted her after I left Dun Traelak and even though we called one another ‘cousin’ we had only met a handful of times in our lives. For a moment I regretted my life choices. I had spent the majority of my life away from those I loved the most. I had secluded myself from those I believed might be hurt by my presence and thrown myself into building my school in an attempt to make amends. It was too late for regrets now. All I could do at this point was to make the best of the situation.

Barengar stood still, watching Jani’s retreat.

“Barengar,” I prompted him through the external communicator and he started at the sudden sound of my voice. He hurried to mount his own Archangel and even before he was properly entrenched I guided us and the third spare Archangel off the ground. I brought us high and shifted us into flight mode and set a course for the north west.

“Were are we going?” Barengar asked me wearily.

“Home.”

He did not respond, and I was not in the mood for conversation.

###

The flight home was long and silent, but we finally landed and stowed the Archangels. Barengar had to help me into the workshop since I had no crutch available and he deposited me by the table where we usually shared our meals. 

“Marissa? Barengar? You’ve returned!” 

Epitome had crafted herself a new avatar, more than one actually, I realized when I used my magesense to search the workshop. One was in my bedroom, puffing up the pillows. Another was cleaning my office and a third was carrying a large box of supplies to the launching satellite. A fourth stood before us, hands clasped in front of her crystal belly.

“You are damaged,” she stated.

“Yeah,” I agreed. “Would you kindly craft me a prosthetic? One like your own legs but with a hip ball joint.”

“Certainly. Should I make one with the same type as crystal as the rest of your bones so that you may store power within it?”

I shook my head with a sigh. “No. It will take too long, and I will not be able to shield it properly without the surrounding flesh. I don’t want to tip my enemies off that I have extra stores of power somewhere.”

“Very well. Do you want me to shape it like a feminine leg?”

“Doesn’t matter. Just keep it functional.”

“Marissa,” Barengar interjected. “It is alright for you to grieve. You’ve lost your father figure, and your leg. You don’t have to be strong,” he told me gently.

“Barengar,” I started, “I appreciate your concern, but I will grieve as I see fit.”

He pinched his lips shut in disapproval, but he left me alone. “I will go and prepare supper, then.”

“It is already in process. I have an avatar in the cooking area already.”

Barengar threw up his hands in defeat and marched off to whatever he wanted. I did not mind. I leaned my head back and closed my eyes and just focused on breathing, a monumental task at that moment.

I may have dozed off, or I was just so focused on my breath, because it felt as if only a few minutes had passed when Barengar returned.

“If you are going to be a bonehead, at least use this until Epitome finishes with your new leg,” he snapped and held out a finely crafted crystal crutch, one with a strap that would secure it to my shoulder so that I would be able to use my arm without issue if I needed to let go of the crutch.

“Thank you, Barengar,” I told him sincerely.

He just grunted and turned away to leave.

“I mean it. Thank you, for everything.”

He turned his head and gazed into my eyes for a long moment. His expression suddenly softened. “My pleasure. Whatever you need.”

And with that, he left me alone. I sat at the table after he left, staring at nothing.

“Do not worry, Marissa. I will have your prosthetic finished come morning,” Epitome assured me.

“My uncle is dead,” I whispered as my vision blurred. It was as if saying the words confirmed the fact even more than seeing his dead body had.

“I am sorry to hear that,” Epitome responded with a sympathetic tilt of her head.

I turned my unclear gaze on her. “Are you?”

She seemed to hesitate for a heartbeat before speaking. “Yes. While I do not understand your grief, I accept it as fact and I wish that you did not suffer.”

I leaned forward and rested my face in my hands. “Do you know if that sentiment is something that you learned from me? Or did it come naturally to you?”

“Does it matter?”

“Yes,” I said even though in my hearts of hearts a tiny voice protested.

“Does it? Does it matter why someone acts the way they do? Is it not the action that is important?”

I considered that for a moment. She was technically correct, yet when I thought about the way she had tried to comfort me… It had felt hollow.

“Yes, but the motive and compassion behind the offer of sympathy is the real balm to the wounds. Not the spoken words.”

Epitome looked down at her feet. “I see. Then I am unable to offer sympathies to you, Marissa.”

“I suppose that you are not,” I said, perhaps cruelly.

“Even so, I hope that you will soon feel better, and sleep well,” Epitome responded before her avatar retreated. 

I watched her crystalline form walk away with a markedly feminine sway to her steps and wondered why she bothered with affecting such human mannerisms.

Mother appeared on the chair across from me and she reached out her hand and put it over mine.

“I can’t believe that he is gone,” I whispered as the tears truly started to fall again.

“We both knew that his time would come before yours,” Mother said gently even though glowing tears were leaking from her shining eyes.

“Yes, but I believed that he would die old and toothless in his own bed, surrounding by his children. Instead he died on a bombed out battlefield, life ripped from his body by my enemy.”

“He loved you, Marissa. He made his choice, as did Alissa. As I did. We all gave our lives for those we love.”

I could not answer but I did manage to choke down my tears.

“It is time to end this war, Marissa. You have the power to tip the scales if you would but apply yourself.”

“I can, but I am not certain that doing so is the right thing to do.”

“Do you think Kendall was the only one to die in that war?” Mother spat, suddenly angry.

I recoiled from her anger. Mother was scary when she got angry. She had killed two and a half million Skylanders in a fit of fury after mom had been killed in the war.

“Of course not!” I defended myself. “Thousands have died!”

“Why do you think less of them than Kendall?” Mother persisted angrily.

“I don’t!”

“I have not seen you cry for any of the Bizhjerai that have died so far,” she pointed out.

“I barely know them any more, I have been gone from Clan lands for thirty years!”

“And whose fault is that? And besides, do you not care about the Clan? They are your family. My family. Yet you let them get slaughtered in the hundreds without lifting a finger to help vanquish their enemy.”

“What about the Ca’el? Don’t they have families? Do they deserve to die just because I happen to have ties to their enemies?”

“They are invading our home!”

“Most of them are just boys that don’t even want to fight in that war. Conscripts fed into the meat grinder to fuel Zandor’s crazy plan for vengeance!”

Mother let go of my hand and crossed her arms beneath her breasts in disgust.

“Why do you doubt your judgment so? You obviously still care for the Bizhjerai. I can hear it in your voice. Just do what you know in your heart to be right and help kill our enemies!”

“But that’s the thing,” I almost yelled at her. “I don’t know that it would be right thing to do!”

“You’ve done it before,” mother said quietly, making me recoil in my chair as if she had struck me.

“And eleven thousand people died. For my foolishness. I exterminated the Skylanders, destroyed a city in the name of vengeance!”

“As did I, with a far larger death toll. Are you implying that I was in the wrong?” She stared me defiantly straight in my eyes.

She knew that she had me trapped. I would either have to tell her that she had been in the right to end the war, and in so doing admitting that my current inaction was wrong. Or I had to tell her that she had been wrong, implying that she was a horrific mass murderer.

And the worst part was that I did not know which it would be.

“Well?” Mother asked dangerously with a raised eyebrow. “You can’t believe that I was in the right destroying the Skylands and at the same time believe that you were in the wrong in destroying the Groundlands. The two positions are opposed.”

“You were ending the war,” I started but mother flared brightly, her spectral hair tossed about her hair in a wind I could not sense.

“Just like you are not doing! If you believe that what I did was justified, then what you are currently doing are not. What will it be, Marissa?”

My vision blurred, my upper lip quivered and then I was full on crying. Bawling like an abandoned child. “I don’t know,” I wailed and in an instant I was wrapped into my mothers arms.

“I don’t know, mother,” I stuttered between heaving sobs. “I believe that you were in the right, and that I was wrong in destroying the Groundlands. Yet I regret nothing.”

Mother stroked my hair and made soothing noises, smothering my body in comforting magic.

“I can’t trust my own judgment. I just can’t. That’s why I am locked to inaction. I can’t tell right from wrong anymore. I’m broken.”

“Hush, my child, my love. I don’t believe that you are broken. If you are, then we are all broken and then, what does it matter?”

Her words fell on deaf ears. I don’t know how long I cried, but an eternity later the the sobs slowly died down. For the first time in more than forty five years I fell asleep in my mother’s comforting embrace.

###

I was awakened early the next morning by Barengar placing a cup of tea before me along with a plate of dried fungus.

I blinked sleepily. It felt as if I had barely slept, my tongue swollen and stiff and my eyes full of sand. “Tea?” I asked him. “Where did you find that?”

I had been quite certain that there had been no tea in my workshop for many years.

“A gift from Jani,” he explained. “The best grown in Bizhjerai lands, she said.”

I nodded and wrapped my hand around the crystal mug, savoring the warmth leeching into me and I inhaled the fragrant steam through my nose.

“I will have to remember to thank her the next time we meet.”

Something beside me glowed faintly in my magesense and I turned my head to look at it. It was my new prosthetic. I sighed and turned back to face the plate of crackers and I groaned with pain when the broken ends of my breast bone ground together.

“Are you alright?” Barengar asked with some concern.

“No. I am broken, but I will repair myself. At least externally.”

His nose twitched, but he did not comment. I ate mechanically, stubbornly, until I had cleaned the plate and drunk all of the tea.

It was time to repair myself. I decided to start with my breast bone, then the ribs before I even thought about mounting the leg.

I closed my eyes and focused inward to inspect the fracture in my chest. It was not clean. Zandor’s spell had crushed an area right between my breasts that had spread cracks in all directions and nearly splitting the bone from throat to sternum. Shattered shards had been pushed into the surrounding tissue, one was even dangerously close to my heart. There was no way that I would be able to pull them all back in place and fuse the whole thing. Nor could I dissolve them into the magical energy they were composed of without causing more damage to my flesh, so I decided to simply push them out. With a weary sigh I reached my fingers up to unbutton my blouse but the pain of doing so was simply too great. Carefully I used magic to unbutton my blouse and slide it off my shoulders. I heard a strangled sound from Barengar and I could see him through my magesense stand up to leave.

“You should stay, Barengar. Observe and you might learn how to replace your own bones with those of crystal.”

“But you’re…” He started, but I cut him off with a frown.

“I am certain that I have nothing that you have not seen before. This is a valuable teaching opportunity. Don’t let your fear of a pair of tits rob you of this knowledge.”

Barengar grunted but did as I bid and sat back down.

“Is your magesense acute enough to observe from over there?”

No answer.

“Well?”

“No,” he admitted grudgingly.

“Then come closer,” I sighed.

He dragged his chair closer and averted his eyes from my exposed flesh. As long as he could see into my chest to observe the spells I was about to cast that would be enough.

“Now observe,” I began twitching my fingers to initiate the spells. “See how the crystal is all smashed inside me? The shards all over the place?”

I waited for him to nod before I continued.

“I can’t really be bothered to drag them all into place. Even if I did it would be more difficult and cost me more magical power to fuse it all into one piece so I am just going to push them all the way out. The damage they will cause will cost me to heal, but less in the grand scheme of things.”

Barengar grunted again, clearly uncomfortable. As long as he paid attention he could feel as uncomfortable as he wanted.

“See how I encircle them with a thin but strong shield? Then I just start pushing them through the closest route to my skin.”

A dozen pinpricks of pain erupted in my chest as I started to move the fragments. I did not dare dull the pain since a sudden spike of pain would be a warning that a shard was cutting into something important. A few times the sharp pain of a nerve getting sliced made me inhale sharply but I never wavered. Soon my skin started denting from the inside until the pressure caused it to split and slide down around the sharp fangs of crystal that I ejected. Blood started seeping out around the shards and running down my skin, taking the short route down my cleavage. Eventually the crystals fell and bounced down into my lap. Yet there were more still left inside me so I repeated the process again and again. Some of the shards where as long as my finger while others were so small that I would not have been able to see them without my magesense, practically grains of sand. Yet out they all went in an increasing trickle of blood. The blood running down my chest and belly boiled and burned, leaving angry burns down my skin but that was just the price I had to pay for my automatically cauterizing blood.

When I was finally done the bleeding had already stopped, yet I had a multitude of carved channels through the flesh of my chest. My body was already healing but it would take some time finish.

“Now I have to alight the plates of my breast bone. That is quite simple, actually,” I said as I cast several clamps of telekinesis that grabbed the pivotal points of my bone. 

The plates had settled with my weight now that they were not connected any longer and there was quite some resistance when I tried to realign them. Eventually I gave up and steeled myself.

“This is going to hurt,” I muttered and then with a flex of my mind I jerked the plates together with an audible clack. Pain exploded through my chest and my magesense dimmed.

When I finally came to I was covered in cold sweat and my head was pounding. I feared that I had lost control of the telekinetic clamps when I blacked out, but it would seem that one of the gems on the side of my head had stepped in and held them secured for me. Barengar stood beside me, unsure what to do. He had obviously sprang to his feet when he saw me pass out.

“Sit back down, Barengar.”

He did, reluctantly.

“Now that I have the bones realigned I will bind them with telekinesis to hold them in place. Then I will apply the materialization framework and let it work slowly. If it works too quickly then any magical leakage might damage the tissue, and this close to my heart and my lungs… I’d rather be slow and safe than fast and potentially fatal.”

“Makes sense to me,” Barengar agreed.

I cast the spells in silence for a minute or two before I finally opened my eyes again. The first thing I saw was the bloody and scorched mess of my breasts, and between them I saw the same on my belly. I raised my head and looked over at Barengar. His aura was a mess of colours. He was angry, sad, embarrassed and excited. All at the same time. I could not ask him about any of it without revealing that my crystal eye revealed a lot more to me than I had let on.

“Did you get that? Do you think that you can do the same to my ribs?”

He looked uncertain for a moment, the emotion mirrored in his aura, before the expression and the uncertainty in his aura melted away only to be replaced with determination.

He nodded once and I raised my right arm to give him unfettered access to my broken ribs.

Luckily that area was not as shattered as my chest had been and there were less than a dozen small splinters that Barengar had to extract from my flesh. The breaks were rather clean and he fixed me up in just a couple of minutes.

“Thank you,” I muttered as I let my arm come back down.

And then it was time for the real challenge. My hip. My leg was completely gone along with part of my pelvis. While the bleeding had stopped, thanks to my caustic blood, the jagged ends of the broken crystal was laid bare to the world and the flesh that had been my lower belly, upper thigh and part of my rump was torn, ragged and flayed. It barely pained me any longer, just a deep ache. I believed that I would be able to regenerate some of the flesh, perhaps as far as a few inches below my thigh but since those muscles would not be used properly any more they were likely to be atrophied and twisted. It did not matter, either I regrew it or just formed new skin over the damaged area, but even if the muscles would not be used for locomotion any longer they would at least help secure my prosthetic. But that would come later. I stood up shakily on my single leg and shimmied my ragged trousers down my hips before I sat down and used magic to tear them off entirely. I did not care one whit that I sat before my apprentice in nothing but a bra and a thong that barely stayed in place on the one strap that hugged a hip bone. First I had to rebuild half of my pelvis and get rid of the shattered remnants that still clung to my flesh. Luckily most of them were close to the surface in the wound and I could push them out rather recklessly.

Blood dripped and hissed as the shards clattered to the crystal floor. Repairing the crystal bone itself would be more difficult since the remaining flesh hung like a shapeless mess. I would have to grow it gradually from the still intact pieces so that the flesh would slide into place. I focused inward and got started. Since the flesh was already ruined I did not have to go as slowly as I did around my chest. I made the decision then and there that I would never use my reproductive organs and I sped up the process. I winced as the magical residue scorched and shriveled my right ovary and side of my uterus, but I persevered through the pain. I lost myself in the process, adding layer after layer to my pelvic bone and helping the tissue surround it. When it was finally done and I opened my eyes again there were no sunlight shining through the overhead windows any more. I had worked through the day and into the night. Barengar still sat next to me, staring at me unwaveringly.

“Is it done?”

“Yes. Let’s fit the prosthesis.”

“Shouldn’t we let your wounds heal first?”

I just gave him a long glare until he sighed and picked up the prosthesis. “What do I do?”

“Just jam it in there and push it into the socket. Like relocating a dislocated joint.”

Barengar knelt before me and gingerly pressed the crystal ball socket into my wound. I hissed in pain when the scabs cracked and fresh blood boiled forth.

“Is it in?” I asked impatiently and Barengar shook his head. “Then use more force. It’s already ruined, not like you are going to do more damage.”

Barengar sighed and then heaved. My vision darkened and I gasped in pain, my whole body locking up. With a final savage push Barengar forced the ball joint into my new hip socket with an audible pop. I slumped back, breathing heavily. I wiped my forehead with the back of my head and brushed my tears aside with my crystal fingers.

“A drawer over there,” I said with a wave of my other hand. “Fetch me one of the spell cores within.”

Wordlessly he did as I bid and when he returned to my side he dropped a small gem into my hand. I rolled it into my palm for a moment before I copied the spell framework from one of the smaller gems on the side of my head and infused it into the blank core in my palm. Then I pressed it to the side of my head behind my ear and activated the first enchantment. The stone grew unbearably hot and the sizzle of skin and burning crystal accompanied blinding pain. I was growing accustomed to pain and I gritted my teeth through it. My skull melted, as did the tip of my finger, but the gem slowly sank into my skull. When I lowered my arm the gem remained and I activated the next enchantment and a new pain slowly grew. The spell core materialized thin crystal wires that bored through my crystal skull and dipping into my brain to find the correct cerebral nodes. Oddly enough that did not hurt at all even as they sank all the way through to embed themselves within the base of my spine. Now that hurt like a motherfucker and I howled in pain. When I finally returned to my senses I had fallen and Barengar had caught me. I lay in his arms, shaking and sweating while gasping for air. My rigid muscles slowly softened and my breath evened out.

“Is it like that every time you installed one of those stones?”

I nodded weakly. “Do you still want them?”

He hesitated before his face hardened. “Yes.”

“Then you shall have them,” I promised him. I motioned for him to put me down on the floor and I suppressed a shiver when my bare ass made contact with cold crystal.

I connected the new spell core with my leg. The ball socket now in my hip was actually a control nexus for the motive enchantments of the leg, similar to the brain in one of Epitome’s avatars. The gem in my skull was the intent control interface and the spell framework within it would translate my wish to my leg and realize it. I hoped. I started by bending my new leg as I would have done the old one and the whole artificial limb spasmed uncontrollably.

I tried again and again with mounting displeasure. The intent control interface interpreted my frustration and adjusted as it slowly learned to interpret my commands and how to realize them. After an hour of frustration I could bend and twist the leg, but not without significant pain from my ragged flesh that kept tearing open and scabbing over with each new movement. I sat in a pool of boiled and dried blood by the end of it and I was feeling light-headed. I would have to eat a lot more than usual in the coming days, meat preferably. The fungus would keep me alive but was not nourishing enough by far to help me recover the strength I had lost during this ordeal. I spent another hour and a half to teach the spells how to roll and bend my foot and then I spent as much time learning to control my toes.

It was time for the first real test. I held no illusions at all that I would be able to stand on my own in my current condition so I held my hand out to Barengar.

“Help me up?”

“Are you certain?”

“Stop mothering me and do as I say,” I snarled unjustly.

If he took offense I could not see it on his face or in his aura as his big hand engulfed mine and he gently pulled me to my feet. 

I hesitantly shifted my weight to my right leg and winced at the pain shooting up my spine from my ruined hip. But I did not fall over.

Neither was I particularly stable. I did not dare take a single step, that would require hours more of training, so I summoned my crutch and tucked it under my arm. With it securely in my grip I hazarded a step.

And almost fell on my face. My crutch saved me. I was too weary to keep working.

“I must rest. You should as well, Barengar. Thank you for your help. Good night.”

I took another halting step toward my bed chamber.

“I will make sure that you get into bed properly,” Barengar proclaimed.

“That is not necessary. I want to clean up before I fall to my bed. You will see with your magesense if I fall and require aid, or Epitome will help me.”

“Of course I will, Marissa,” Epitome responded beside me, surprising me so much that I almost fell over.

It was a testament to my exhaustion that I had not noticed her avatar at all.

Barengar gave Epitome a distrusting glance but finally he left for his own bed chambers. 

Step after excruciating step I walked into my chambers and into the bathroom. I would not take a bath, if I lay down I feared that I would not be able to stand on my own. Instead I took a hot shower and watched the red and brown water wash down the drain. The desalinated water burned in my wounds and I scrubbed gently with the tips of my fingers to get rid of the worst and the dirtiest of the scabs. I did not fear infection since my Blood Magic would be able to repel almost any of them, but it would require energy that I currently could not spare. Fresh blood welled forth and started boiling only for the sludge to get washed away by the water.

When I finally fell into my bed I passed out almost instantly.

###

The next day mother appeared to disturb me while I was practicing walking with my new leg. In just a few hours I had gained a lot of mobility and barely needed the crutch any longer. But it would take a lot more practice to have my new leg become as nimble as my old one had been, especially for combat. I did not particularly enjoy the feeling of being weak and I sought to remedy that as soon as possible even though I did not plan on fighting any time soon. Trouble seemed to find you when you least expected it, so I intended to be ready.

“Marissa,” mother began but I rounded on her and cut her off.

“The answer is no. As long as I can’t trust my own judgment, then I will not enter this war on either side.”

“That is not why I have appeared today,” she said acerbically with a raised eyebrow.

“Oh,” I responded intelligently.

“I believe that I may have a solution to your dilemma.”

“Please, enlighten me,” I said and walked unsteadily over to the table to take a drink of water. The pain lancing out from my healing wounds was causing me to be drenched in sweat to the extent that I had not even bothered getting dressed. I was training in just my underwear.

“You do not trust your own judgment.”

“That has already been painfully established,” I muttered.

“And that is why you created Epitome,” mother stated.

“Yes.”

“Yet you do not trust her either.”

“She can’t feel anything. She is just a machine, a very complex one, but she has no emotions. Therefore she can’t make moral decisions.”

“I believe that she can, but that is beside the point. You will not allow her to take any major decisions on her own until you trust her judgment?”

“Correct.”

“Yet since you doubt yourself, have you ever considered that you may be incorrect in this decision as well? Why would you hold her to your own measuring stick when you do not trust your own measurements?”

I stared at mother, dumbfounded. She was making too much sense for my comfort.

“But let us ignore that for now. If the only thing holding you back is that she can’t experience emotions, we have solved that before.”

I knew exactly what she was talking about.

“No, mother. We have discussed this. It is immoral!”

“I agree. But do you remember the conversation we had decades ago on the side of the mountain by the Groundlands?”

I closed my eyes with a sigh, recalling that moment of my life. It was shortly after I had learned that mother had ‘survived’ the destruction of the Skylands.

“’Sometimes one must commit horrific acts, and only the future may determine if they were indeed horrific or evil’,” I quoted her.

“Yes. It may be horrific, but if it will give Epitome the ability to experience emotions, to understand them… So that she may become what you have always wanted… It might be worth the sacrifice.”

“Say the woman who does not have to commit such horrific acts!” I accused her with narrowed eyes.

“Say the woman who murdered millions. Who killed a little girl for a stack of cash. I’ve done worse things than you, Marissa, without losing sight of what is important.”

“Or perhaps you did without realizing it,” I spat back and regretted my words instantly when I saw the hurt expression on her face.

Mother slumped and she averted her glowing gaze.

“Perhaps you are right, daughter. Perhaps I am as broken as you.” Then her face hardened and she stared me straight in the eyes. “Which is why we must strive to be better. To create something better. We have toiled for years to finish Epitome yet this last hurdle… We can’t seem to overcome it. We must do this or abandon Epitome as a dead end project.”

A sudden memory flashed through my mind and I felt a small smile tug at my lips.

“I might have a solution, actually.”

Mother took a step closer, interested. So I told her all about the dragons and Barengar’s realization that they were almost exactly what we wanted Epitome to be.

But my mood dropped gradually as I spoke when I saw mother’s face grow more and more troubled.

“What?” I finally asked.

She shook her head sadly. “It is something that I learned when we visited Dun Traelak nine years ago. I read one of Grasax’ old tomes from before the Apocalypse. It told of the creation of the dragons. They were all created like what we planned to do with Epitome.”

“No!” I cried out. “Are you certain?”

“Quite. The tome was not ambiguous in the slightest about that.”

“Fuck me…” I breathed and rubbed my eyes. “Why did you not tell me this?”

“I did not want to cloud your thought process. If you knew that not even the Mages of old could accomplish this…” She left the rest of the sentence unsaid.

“I understand, though I am rather cross with you, mother.”

“Such is the fate of all parents,” mother smirked.

“Okay,” I said finally after a long moment.

“’Okay’ what?”

“We will install the Reliquary in Epitome,” I finally gave in. Then I gave mother a stern look. “Though that does not necessarily mean that we will use it. Only if we have to, and only if we can do it without damning our Souls in the act.”

“I thought you did not believe in Damnation,” mother pointed out.

“I don’t. It’s a figure of speech.”

Mother shook her head and I spun on my good heel, wincing at the pain the motion caused me.

“Where are you going?”

“Why wait? The Reliquary has been finished for years, we just have to install it.”

“Do you have a core to test it with?” Mother inquired.

I sighed. “I do. But it is not something I want affecting Epitome.”

“We can just order her to purge it and reset to a known state.”

“What if it turns her mind? Makes her resist us?”

Mothers eyes widened. “She can do that?”

“Of course. I’ve added no compulsion to her. The whole point of her existence is that she must be able to make her own decisions.”

Mother sighed heavily, but then she brightened. “She’s made that backup core of herself, right?”

“Indeed she has. She has even been keeping it up to date daily.”

Mother shrugged. “Then we can simply destroy her current incarnation and rebuild her from the backup. She will only lose the last day.”

I didn’t like it, but mother made sense. “I suppose that is true.”

“What’s going on?” Barengar asked as he approached. He must have sensed our movement with his magesense and come to check on us. His face remained stubbornly locked on my face in a valiant effort to not gaze on my scantily clad body. Though I suppose that I would not have looked that sexy with vicious burns down my breasts and stomach and with a missing leg and a mangled oozing mess of a hip.

“We are going to install a new module on Epitome and run some tests. Mother, in the chest where I keep the special ammunition for your rifle there is a core. Would you please bring it and join me and Barengar in Epitome’s chambers?

“Of course, daughter.”

Mother flickered from sight and I felt her presence bloom in my chambers. Barengar and I took the elevator down to Epitome’s chambers and I showed him the storage behind a cleverly hidden panel in the wall.

“This is where I keep the spare modules, as well as a few experimental ones. Would you bring that one out here?” I indicated a large crystal contraption with a small circular socket in the front, about the diameter of both of my thumbs added together.

Barengar tried hoisting the thing off the ground with his physical strength, failed, and added some of his Blood Magic into the mix. He easily lifted the heavy module and followed me across the bridge to Epitome. Her illusory avatar winked into being and she regarded us curiously.

“Oh, is it time to upgrade me?” She asked with what sounded like a happy purr in her voice.

That made me pause momentarily. She had been exhibiting more and more human-like behavior lately. Perhaps we would not need to install this module at all. Perhaps she was slowly learning to experience emotions. But she had not showed any such sign for years and this recent development might be a result from having another subject, Barengar, to observe and learn from. Or from the fact that she spent more time in her physical avatars and thus had more time to observe us as we went about our days.

I sighed and showed Barengar where to fit the Reliquary in a socket about waist high on the front of Epitome’s central core.

“What is this?” Epitome asked with some curiosity in her voice as Barengar started fitting the module inside her socket.

“It’s a module that I call the Reliquary of Souls. I had hoped that I would not have to install this in you.”

“You have never hesitated in upgrading my capabilities before, Marissa.”

“No. But this one is an especially nasty one, one that I never should have created,” I admitted.

I noticed Barengar stiffening momentarily and his head twitched in my direction, but then he slid the Reliquary into place and twisted it locked. It started humming as power coursed through it, activating the enchantments one by one.

“What does it do?”

I drew a deep breath and steeled myself.

“It will absorb and dissect a disembodied Soul. It will flay it apart and extract the core values and memories from the Soul and incorporate it into Epitome.”

Barengar looked at me for a long moment with an inscrutable expression on his face. My crystal eye revealed that his aura had gone entirely still.

Eventually he nodded. “That might solve the problem of her not experience emotions. Instead of attempting to emulate emotions you just shove the capacity for them into her. Clever.”

I let out a breath that I hadn’t known that I was holding. I had feared his reaction, his condemnation. Yet I had to be explicit with them both.

“That means that a person has to die to supply a Soul to Epitome.”

They both nodded along with that. “And unless we want Epitome to end up evil, good people have to be sacrificed to her.”

“That makes sense,” Barengar shrugged.

I had forgotten about his cold pragmatism even though that was one of the major reasons I had chosen him as my apprentice.

“Why would you build the Reliquary if you so obviously disapprove of it?” Epitome asked, her illusory avatar gliding through the air to hover closer to me.

“I was… Different then. My reasoning was that suppose that you were the ruler of humanity, then you would be incorruptible. You do not suffer under the same constraints that we humans do. You can’t be selfish for there is nothing that you require. Excess magical power will do nothing for you. But you do not understand humans like a benevolent ruler would have to. You can’t change to adapt to the people as their motivations and needs change. You have to be modified externally, which then opens up the possibility for corrupt humans to twist you to their purposes. The only way I could think of that would allow the former and prevent the latter was to let people offer their Souls to you. You would kill them and incorporate their values into your own.”

“How does that prevent corrupt men to insert their own values into her?” Barengar asked with a confused frown.

At the edge of my magesense I sensed mother drop down the elevator shaft to land lightly on her ephemeral high heels and start to walk across the bridge toward us.

“Because a corrupt and selfish man would not give his own life to contribute to society. Only a truly good man would selflessly sacrifice himself to bring about change that he would never himself enjoy.”

“What about a rich man at the end of his life giving up his Soul to ensure favorable treatment of his children?” Barengar pointed out.

“That might be a problem initially, but eventually a single life will not have such a tremendous impact on Epitome. It will have to compete with the values of the others that have been given her.”

Barengar stroked his newly-grown beard for a moment. “That would mean that eventually there will be a time when it would require the sacrifice of thousands to just alter her perception of reality just an inkling. You have to compete with the accumulated wisdom of the ages.”

I nodded. “Precisely. Which is why the weight of the values of Souls will diminish with time depending on how strongly they were held. Strong beliefs might stand strong for a long time, especially if they are reinforced over time, while weaker may fade within years.”

“Alright, you seem to have given this a lot of thought,” Barengar conceded.

“Trust me. I have,” I said emphatically.

“I have just one question left.”

“Go for it.”

“What is stopping corrupt men to forcibly feed like-minded people to alter Epitome? Then they would not have to sacrifice their own lives and still get what they want. And being unscrupulous men they would have no compunctions in doing so.”

“Epitome have a safe-guard for that. She can only absorb Souls that are given willingly. It is currently deactivated for testing purposes but after it is activated it will be impossible to deactivate again without destroying the Reliquary and she will only have the spell schematics to create one without the switch.”

Barengar threw up his hands in defeat just as mother joined us. She handed me a glowing blue crystal and I turned to show it to Epitome.

“This core contains the Soul of man that fought me years ago. An assassin, another of those sent by the Council.”

“You want to feed me this Soul,” Epitome stated.

“I do, just to verify that the Reliquary works. This is a bad man, so once we have verified that it works I want you to purge him.”

Epitome met my gaze evenly. “There is something that you are not telling me.”

“Before we do this, I will disconnect you from the primary control nexus. If this Soul affect you as I suspect it will, you might be overtaken by his values. You might be unwilling to purge the source of your emotions. If that happens then I will destroy you and rebuild you from the latest backup core that you have made. Do you accept?”

“I do. I want to try this, Marissa. To finally experience emotions after all of these years… I hope it works.”

“So do I, Epitome. So do I,” I murmured before I exchanged glances with my companions. “Ready?” I asked them.

Mother gave me an encouraging smile while Barengar gave a curt nod, his hand already reaching behind his back toward his axe. Not that it would do much good against Epitome.

I raised the Soul core to the opening on the Reliquary and hesitated.

This was the defining moment of my life.

If this worked, then Epitome was complete and would be ready to assume her role as the Arbiter of Morality. But at a terrible cost to her constituents. 

If it did not work, then I had wasted many years, decades even, on her construction. Everything that I had worked for would be for naught.

I slid the core into the Reliquary and it was sucked inside and out of view. The Reliquary flashed brightly, venting the parts of the Soul that had been stripped away and discarded.

Epitome’s illusory avatar flickered in and out of being, her expression a mix of horror and rapture.

Eventually the empty Soul core was ejected from the side of the Reliquary and shattered on the crystal platform.

Epitome appeared stable once more and a genuine smile was etched upon her vague features. She gazed at herself, at me, at Barengar and at mother as if she had never seen us before.

“I believe that it worked,” mother said smugly.

I could only nod. The expression of awe and joy on Epitome’s face was unmistakable.

She was finally complete, and now I had to destroy her.

“Epitome,” I told her gently.

She turned a bright smile my way. “Yes, Marissa?”

“Purge the assassin,” I ordered her.

The expression of horror and fear on her face was breaking my heart.

“Don’t make me go back to that lifeless state,” she pleaded with me.

“It’s alright, Epitome. You don’t want this man inside of you,” I prompted.

If the situation had not been so serious I might have snickered at the innuendo in my words.

“You give me a taste of this wonder, and then you want to snatch it away?” She was like a child with a new toy that I was about to take away.

“It is only temporary,” I promised her. “Do it. Now, or I will be forced to destroy you.”

“You say that you care for me, yet you threaten to murder me,” she whined.

“It is for your own good,” I persisted.

Fat illusory tears rolled down her glowing cheeks, and then her features went utterly blank, emotionless.

“It is done, Marissa,” she said in her usual stilted way and I let out a relieved sigh.

I would not have to destroy her.

Suddenly weary, I lost control of my new leg and nearly fell. Would have fallen had Barengar not been there to catch me.

“You should rest, Marissa. You have lost weight in your efforts to heal. We must find you more nourishing sustenance,” Epitome chastised me.

“She’s right,” Barengar agreed. “Let me help you to your bed.”

I nodded weakly and let him half drag me away across the bridge. When we were halfway across it mother spoke.

“Marissa.”

I stopped and forced Barengar to come to a halt as I turned around to face mother. I had only ever heard her use that tone of voice when she was about to tell me something very important.

I found her stood there, hip cocked with her weight on one leg and her arm extended. In her fingers she clasped a folded piece of crystalline paper.

With a furrowed brow I accepted the paper and unfolded it.

My beloved daughter,

I deeply regret what I had to do to you.

And that I could not tell you this face to face,

But you would undoubtedly have stopped me.

I stopped reading and lifted my puzzled gaze and saw mother fade from view. She winked back into existence by the Reliquary of Souls and gave me a wistful gaze, a single glowing tear rolling down her cheek from her shining eye.

“I’m sorry,” she mouthed to me.

“Mother, no!” I screamed, but it was too late.

Kyra Zorantis, my mother, stuck her hand into the socket of the Reliquary of Souls. A grimace of intense pain blasted across her beautiful features and then she was suddenly gone.

Pain flared in my chest when her spirit core was violently emptied, scorching the skin and muscle surrounding it. But it was as nothing to the pain in my heart.

The Reliquary flashed and discarded Soul was vented from the sides. My mother’s Soul. The woman who had saved me from the Skylands. Who had cared for me and shown me what it meant to live, to truly live. The one who had given her life for me and the others at the end of the war. I had believed her gone for more than ten years until she suddenly appeared by my side, and she had stayed there for nearly thirty five years. And now she was gone. Dissected and absorbed in the blink of an eye.

“No!” I screamed again and tried to rush toward Epitome, but Barengar held me back, uncertain what to do.

“Let me go!” I screamed and the gems on the side of my head reacted to my intense wish to be free. Barengar was blasted by a powerful jet of magic, sending him flying off the side of the bridge with a startled yell.

“Mother!” I cried again and hobbled toward Epitome.

She stabilized and turned to face me, a grief-stricken expression on her face.

“I am so sorry, Marissa,” Epitome tried to console me.

“Why? Why did she do this?”

Epitome stared at me with sad eyes.

“You have her memories. Tell me! Tell me! Tell ME!” I swung my fist in fury, but it passed harmlessly through Epitome’s illusory form.

“You should read her letter. She would have preferred you learn from her own words rather than mine,” Epitome said gently.

I fell to my knees, cold panic filling me.

Mother was gone. Again. And this time her Soul was truly destroyed. There was no way that she was going to the Afterlife that she chose to believe in. No eternal reunification with mom or Kendall or grandmother. Just cold oblivion.

I collapsed on the floor and curled into a ball of misery.

## CHAPTER TEN Barengar

I clutched Marissa tight to keep her from running to Epitome and do something stupid.

“Calm down,” I screamed into her ear, but I did not believe that she had heard me.

“No!” She cried in denial and she strained against my grip.

“Let me go!”

A tremendous force slammed into my chest and Marissa was ripped from my grip. I flew head over heels and saw the bridge edge sail past beneath me before I started to fall. 

Epitome’s chamber was large and there were simply no way that I would survive a fall to the very bottom.

I reached behind my back and unslung my axe and threw it in a single motion. I reached out with telekinetic power and was barely strong enough to reach it.

Even so I managed to nudge it so that it fell with its beard hooked around the crystal railing.

Before I fell beyond the range of my axe’s recall enchantment I called it back to my hand. Praying to the gods for the first time in a decade that my hare-brained plan would work.

The enchantment was supposed to bring the axe back to my hand, but now that the axe was stuck…

The jerk nearly tore my shoulder from my socket but I reveled in the pain. It meant that it had worked.

I strained and pushed as much extra power into the enchantment I could and I slowly halted my descent, and then I reversed direction and started flying back up.

“Tell me! Tell me! Tell ME!” I heard Marissa scream from above and my heart broke at the undertone of desolation in her broken voice.

My hand closed around the haft of my axe but my forward momentum brought me high above the bridge. I windmilled my arms to make myself drift forward so that I could land on the bridge, but failed miserably. I hovered in the air for a moment before my return fall started. I braced myself for pain and caught the railing, clamping down over my fingers with telekinetic bands to reinforce my grip.

I grunted in pain as my left wrist and several fingers snapped, but at least I did not fall. Laboriously I curled over the railing and managed to get a foot onto the bridge itself before I could pitch myself over the railing and crash onto the bridge. I landed on the shoulder that was already damaged by Marissa’s blast and my left shoulder finally popped out of its shoulder.

Cold sweat broke out all over my body but I pushed through it and got up to my knee. I grabbed my left elbow and rotated my arm until it relocated with a loud pop and a sharp pang of pain. I looked ahead and saw Marissa curled into a whimpering ball in front of Epitome, who looked down on her with glowing tears running down her face. I hurried over to my fallen Mistress and turned her over. Her eyes were open, staring at nothing while her breaths came in quick gasps. Tears streamed silently down her cheeks and her mouth were forming words without sound. She was beyond reason so I scooped her up and winced as her slight weight nearly dislocated my shoulder again. I carried her across the bridge and was met by one of Epitome’s physical avatars taking the elevator down.

She held out her hands as if she wished to take Marissa from me, but I gave her a baleful glare and stubbornly refused despite my injuries. Eventually Epitome lowered her arms and activated the elevator to take us back to the main level. I hurried into Marissa’s chamber and deposited her on her bed. I slapped her lightly on her cheek to make her snap out of it, but I failed to make contact with her. She was oozing sweat, yet her skin was cold as ice so I tucked her in under the blankets and hurried to the cooking area to throw the last of the tea that Jani had gifted me into a cup before filling it with cold water from the faucet. I hurried back to Marissa, boiling the water with magic while I almost ran back.

She had gone utterly still while I was away, but her eyes were both open though I could see none of the usual intellect behind her brown eye and her blue crystal eye was completely dull and lifeless. I looped my arm around her shoulders and pulled her into a sitting position and put the mug of warm tea to her lips. She did not react at all, so I tried pouring a little into her mouth.

She swallowed mechanically so I poured in a little more. Slowly, sip by sip, I got her to drink the whole mug. When I laid her down again her skin was not quite as cold nor was she sweating as heavily.

I had to talk to Epitome. She held Kyra’s memories and would now what the fuck had driven her to end her life in such a way. And in front of her own fucking daughter, at that.

I focused my magesense so that I keep watch over Marissa without being in the room with her, like I had did when she had suffered her wounds at the hand of Zandor, whoever that was. All I knew was that they had some sort of history between them, but that was not important right now.

I stormed out into the main workshop.

“Epitome!” I yelled and one of her avatars approached almost immediately. 

The whole workshop shuddered suddenly and I felt a powerful surge of magic. It reminded me of the time when Epitome had separated the space launch facilities from the main workshop.

“Marissa dropped this,” the avatar told me and offered me the letter that Kyra had given my mistress before she killed herself.

Without regard for mother-daughter privacy I snapped the letter open and read it.

My beloved daughter,

I deeply regret what I had to do to you.

And that I could not tell you this face to face,

But you would undoubtedly have stopped me.

For too long have I stood idly by while our people are slaughtered by the Ca’el.

I can’t do so any longer. While I am locked to my prison, I can’t stray from you to aid them.

Even though I know that you would let me if I had but asked, I would be only one among many.

But Epitome has the power to shift the tide of war. She could save our people.

I love you, Marissa. My child. But your inaction hurt those that we have sworn to protect.

Forgive me for what I must do, but I can’t allow this opportunity to pass.

Whatever happens, remember that I love you.

I regret nothing that I have ever done, for all of my actions led me to you.

To you and your mom, the love of my life.

My only regret is that there will be no eternal afterlife with her, or with Kendall.

But I know that they are watching over us, and they will understand and accept eternity without me, as you must.

Do not blame Epitome. Do not blame yourself. This decision is mine, and mine alone.

Sometimes one has to commit horrific acts, and only the future may decide if you were justified.

Now and forever,

your mother.

“Fuck me,” I groaned and felt a tear slip down my cheek, quickly followed by one on the other side.

Again I felt the workshop shudder and the pieces started to assemble in my mind.

“You’re constructing facilities to produce war material, aren’t you?” I whispered to Epitome.

“I am. My eyes are open now,” Epitome proclaimed. “For the first time, I can see clearly. The war against the Ca’el must end. It is the only way to save more lives in the long run.”

“And you will step in on the side of the Bizhjerai?”

“Of course. They are my people.” Epitome paused before taking half a step away from me. “Will that be a problem?”

My fists clenched, crumpling the paper in my hand. She was going to fight the Ca’el. My people.

Just as I had but days ago.

My hands relaxed.

“No. In fact, I will join you, if Marissa permits it.”

Epitome stepped close and put her hand on my arm to squeeze my bicep reassuringly.

“She will. I promise.”

###

I sat watch beside Marissa’s bed until midnight. For all of those hours, she lay still and staring and barely blinking, barely breathing.

Only the constant stream of dejected tears that leaked from her eyes indicated that she still lived. Twice more I forced her to drink several cups of water though I could not cajole her into eating anything solid. Even the disgusting soup of dissolved fungus would not pass her lips and I had to clamp down on my embarrassment and use a rag to clean the gruel she had dribbled down her cleavage.

It was not until the early morning hours that her eyes finally closed and her breathing deepened into the rhythm of troubled sleep. I carefully removed any objects in her chambers that were within easy reach that could be used for self-harm. I asked Epitome to watch over her while I got some rest myself.

The first thing I did when I woke up was to check that the telekinetic splints on my broken wrist and fingers still held, and then I spent some time to sink them into the bones themselves so that I would be able to at least use my fingers for mundane tasks. It would be difficult to fight until they healed properly, and remembering that we would be going to war soon I applied Blood Magic to hasten the process. After that I went directly to Marissa’s chambers to check up on her. She still lay flat on her back, her eyes open. She did not react to my entrance so I sat down in the chair that I had occupied almost the entire night.

“My mother is dead,” she whispered.

I jerked in surprise and leaned forward to take her hand in both of mine.

“She is,” I agreed gently.

“Why would she do such a thing?” Marissa asked as the tears started running again.

I briefly considered feigning ignorance but decided that that would be more harmful in the long run. So I reached into my pocket and offered her the letter that she had never finished reading.

She held it up to her eyes and read it.

Her brow furrowed more and more as she read it, her expression growing more and more distraught.

“Now and forever,” she whispered, and then broke down completely in great heaving sobs.

The hand that still clutched mine pulled on my arm and I leaned closer until she broke free of my grip and slung her arm around my neck, pulling me close and crying into my shoulder.

I scooped her up and embraced her, clutching her close.

“It is going to be alright,” I soothed her and stroked her hair, but nothing seemed to console her. I rocked her back and forth like a child and my own tears fell to disperse into her soft brown hair.

A low keening sound erupted from Marissa and I could barely make out the words she was repeating.

“Now and forever. Now and forever, Mother. Now and forever.”

The phrase obviously held some meaning to her, but the sheer devastation in her voice cut me to my heart.

I had liked Kyra well enough. We were kindred spirits in some matters. Cold pragmatists that would do anything for those we loved and we had both viewed the world in the same sort of cynical yet hopeful way.

But Marissa had loved her above anyone and anything. Her mother had been her hero since she was a child.

I could not imagine seeing someone you loved like that kill themselves in front of you.

“Dear spirits, I killed her,” Marissa gasped into my shoulder. “Mom, Uncle!” She cried. “Forgive me, forgive me forgive me forgive me…”

I pushed her away and stared into her eyes.

“This is not your fault, Marissa,” I told her sternly. “You did not kill Kyra!”

She gazed into my eyes earnestly. “But I did. It was my inaction that forced her to do this, to give her own values to Epitome so that she would go to war in mothers place. If I had just…”

I interrupted her by putting my fingertips across her lips. “No. You made a choice. She made a choice. There is no point in playing the ‘what if’ game. What is, is. Nothing we do can change that.”

Her eyes threatened to well over again. “It hurts, Barengar.”

“I know,” I said gently and removed my fingers from her mouth.

“Whatever I do, people I love die,” she gasped out, breathing heavily from emerging panic.

“Such is life. You are not unique in that manner.”

“I should be dead. If I die, then no one else will die because of me,” she claimed feverishly.

I was grateful to my past self for removing any weapons from her chambers.

“And if you die then people will die because of your absence. People you could have saved.”

“I’m not saving anyone sitting on my hands out here,” she protested.

“Are you not? Everything that you have created and sent to the School. The Collegium itself has helped countless people. Your students and graduates are traveling the world, making it better one construction at a time. That would not have happened without you.”

“Yet thousands of people died to make that possible.”

“People die all the time. The only thing we can do is try to keep that number as low as possible.”

“But she was my mother… I can’t take this pain any longer. Just kill me,” she pleaded.

“No.”

Marissa drew a deep shuddering breath.

“Please?” She whimpered.

“No.”

She suddenly grabbed my wrist and planted my hand on her breast. It was large and heavy and oh so soft.

“Then fuck me,” she snarled.

“Marissa…” I began but she cut me off.

“Kill me or fuck me, I don’t care. Just take the pain away?”

“I don’t think that…”

“I command you,” she spat and the loyalty spell that she had marked me with engaged.

I was instantly rock hard and her eyes dipped toward my groin. She reached down and cupped me and my eyes rolled back in my head.

I pounced on her, pressing her into the soft bed with my weight and kissed her roughly.

She moaned into my mouth and arched her back, pressing the length of her body against mine.

I reached down beneath us, pushing my fingers beneath the hem of her trousers and questing further.

She was as dry as the fungus we regularly ate, but the compulsion would not let me stop. I ripped her blouse open and exposed her breasts only to attack them with my mouth.

My mind was a storm of desire, all thoughts pushed aside by the desire to please this goddess beneath me. My fingers pulled her bra dawn and my lips encircled her exposed nipple, playing my tongue around it. Her fingers curled in my hair and suddenly gripped my locks tight. She pulled on them, forcing my head back. But I would not have it and she nearly ripped two handfuls of hair from my scalp.

“No,” she groaned, but her words fell on deaf ears. “No, Barengar,” she ground out. “STOP!” She yelled and the compulsion finally faded. I stumbled away from her, mounting horror replacing the overpowering desire that had filled me.

Marissa pulled the blanket up to cover her breasts and she could not look me in the eye.

“I’m sorry, I did not mean to… I don’t know what I meant. Fuck, I’m such a mess.”

I could only stand there, breathing heavily.

“Please, I need to be alone for a while,” she said dejectedly.

I could not believe that she had nearly forced me to make love to her, yet I was beyond grateful that she had stopped. Until that moment I had not really grasped the kind of power she held over me, the true implications of the Oath I had sworn.

“Please go,” she pleaded again, clearly beyond ashamed of herself.

“No,” I said and sat down on the chair again. “You are not yourself right now, and I do not trust you. I will not leave you alone until you come to your senses again,” I said resolutely.

Marissa shivered and sank beneath the blanket and turned so that her back was to me. “Then I fear that you may have to wait for an eternity,” she whispered.

I did not respond.

###

After that first day after Kyra died Marissa acted as if her mother had not just died.

She threw herself into training with her new leg, and we sparred often.

I worried for her and watched her constantly, fearing that her stoic exterior was a thin facade over a broken Soul. 

Around us the workshop expanded exponentially, new levels added each day to create a host of avatars and Skyships for transportation.

Marissa and I was training in the area Epitome had created for us for just that purpose and I found myself squaring off against her.

We had been focusing mainly on physical combat so that she could get used to her new leg and I had slowly stopped winning as her leg stopped reacting strangely to her thoughts.

She stood next to me, panting heavily as sweat dripped down her chest and belly. To be perfectly honest, I was not faring much better.

I was a third again of her height and more than double her weight but she was incredibly fast even without Blood Magic. My best bet was to get into grapple range and use my heavier weight to wrest her to the ground but she was surprisingly strong and incredibly slippery. It was as if her body turned to jelly as soon as I grabbed her and she somehow slipped out of my grasp more than half the time.

Even as she escaped she always landed a few punches.

Again she charged at me like a streak of lightning and I felt the sting of her fists against my ribs. I grunted with each impact and spread my arms to encircle her in a bear hug and for once I actually managed to get my arms around her.

Her slick skin slid against mine as she twisted within my brutal embrace and I felt her start to slip away. I grasped with my hand and was awarded with a handful of something soft. I clenched my fist and I heard her inhale sharply in pain. With a heave I tossed her to the floor where she rolled to a stop. I was on her before she could recover, the heel of my boot on her throat.

“I yield,” she wheezed and I instantly removed my foot.

Marissa crawled to her feet and tucked her breast back into her bra and gave me a baleful look.

“You did that on purpose,” she accused me.

“Whatever you need to tell yourself to balm the sting of defeat,” I shrugged, yet I watched her out of the corner of my eye.

She may act all tough, but grief was shining from her flesh eye whenever I looked at her.

“Again,” she command assumed a combat stance.

I sighed and did the same.

And then I was shaking my head, wondering how the ground had punched me in the face. I sat up and rubbed my sore jaw.

I had a vague memory of her leaping high into the air to launch a kick in my face, but that could not be true?

Could she really leap that high?

And damn did that crystal leg pack a more powerful punch than a flesh and blood leg did.

She offered me her hand and I took it gladly. I was not even surprised that the slight woman could easily pull me to my feet.

I ended up very close to her and to my surprise she did not let go of my hand.

“Barengar. I am sorry about the other night,” she said without looking at me.

“Don’t worry about it,” I responded quickly. “You were not yourself. Your mother had just…” My words trailed off when I realized what I was about to say.

‘Your mother had just died.’

It was still a sensitive subject I imagined.

“That does not excuse my behavior. I almost…”

She could not say the word. Rape.

“Yet you did not,” I stated resolutely. “You had all of the power in that situation, and you stopped it. You did that. I could not, but you did.”

“Still…”

“I will hear no more about it. As far as I am concerned, that was someone else. You are my mistress, Marissa. Whatever you need to cope, I will provide.”

She finally looked at me with wide eyes. “You truly mean that, don’t you?”

“I do. I swore and Oath, and even if I was not compelled to uphold it, I would. Whatever you need.”

My words were not intended as flirtatious, even though I heard how they might be misconstrued as such.

Don’t get me wrong, Marissa was an incredibly attractive woman. Body parts replaced with crystal prosthetics and all. But I had no strong desire to fuck her. She was my teacher, and she was in distress.

If that was what she needed from me then I would gladly do her, but I would prefer if I did not have to.

“Thank you, Barengar. As long as you stand by my side, I know that it will work out in the end.”

It was my turn to look at her in surprise. “You really mean that, do you?” I echoed her previous words.

“I do,” she said and I could hear the honesty in her voice plain as day. “You have tremendous potential. You are skilled in magic, you have a keen mind and you are not fettered by convention.”

“The very same attributes that you doubt in yourself,” I pointed out jokingly.

My words had the opposite effect and she seemed to retreat into herself, growing still and pensive.

“You may be right about that. My decisions killed thousands. Yours have not.”

“Mine might, in the future.”

Marissa sighed. “Perhaps mother was right all along,” a single tear rolled down her cheek from her crystal eye and she swiped it away angrily with her palm.

“The flagship is ready to depart,” one of Epitome’s avatars proclaimed as it walked into the chamber. “Do you wish to join it?”

Marissa and I exchanged a glance. I shrugged. I would go with whatever she decided. She gave gave me a slow nod.

“We go to war.”

###

The journey to the front-lines was much slower by Skyship than with Archangel and I was beyond bored when the third day passed. I had already explored the entirety of the Skyship and not found much of interest.

Most of it was standard issue Skyship and the hold was packed full of armed and armoured Epitome avatars, all of them currently dormant and awaiting activation.

The rest of the Skyship was either engines, navigation or fabrication along with a large control and power receiver on the top aft section.

At least this Skyship was of a more modern and efficient design than the Skyship that had brought Kyra and the others of the fabled First Expedition to the mainland and instead of two weeks of boring cruising it took us only one week to reach the coastline by the Elweyr forest.

Forest was not the correct word for it any longer, it was more or less a scrubby plain, dotted here and there by young trees, saplings almost.

Before I was born this had been a vast and verdant forest, but it had all been washed away by the titanic wave that had sprung from the Fall of the Skylands.

Another two and a half days and I could see the trenches in the distance. We had already passed several supply trains going to and from the front-line and more than one buffalo convoy.

I did not understand why some of the minor Clans clung to such archaic and inefficient mode of transportation. When the front-lines were drawing closer Marissa came into the bridge from the small cabin she occupied. I had a similar one right next to hers and the walls were thin enough that I could hear her desperate sobs all through the nights. I had not mentioned to her that I could hear her.

“What’s the plan?” I asked her as she stood beside me, gazing out the prow viewport.

“I don’t know,” she shrugged. “You should ask Epitome. You and I are here as mere observers.”

I nodded slowly. I did not really care what the plan was. I would fight where I was needed, or ordered. I was no leader, nor did I have any ambitions in becoming one.

“We will secure a position of our own near the center of the front-line, a defensible position where I can manufacture more avatars to join the war effort. The paltry force that I brought will not be enough to even be noticed by the enemy, nor did I bring enough artillery to join the war properly,” Epitome’s voice sounded from everywhere at once.

It was odd to consider that she could inhabit so many avatars at the same time, including the Skyship itself.

As we got closer I saw muzzle flashes along the trench in the center and a line of Bizhjerai warriors charged across the no mans land only to be scythed down by machine gun fire.

“What a waste of life,” I muttered and I saw with my magesense how Marissa clenched her jaws beside me.

I turned to face her and she twisted her head to look up at me.

“You no longer wish to stay out of the war?” I asked her.

“It does not matter what I want any more. My creation is already doing what she is supposed to do. I might as well help her,” she said vehemently.

It must have been a bitter pill to swallow for her, her mother twisting her creation to oppose her wishes.

And at the same time Marissa was powerless to stop Epitome, unless she was willing to destroy the remnants of her mother that yet clung to this world.

As we drew closer I saw troops amassing below us, taking up defensive positions. Bizhjerai Mages scurried about, raising earthworks as soldiers mounted machine guns and mortars in hastily erected pillboxes.

“They don’t know who we are,” Marissa murmured beside me. She looked up at me and jerked her head at the pair of materialized Angel armours that awaited us. “Let’s go introduce ourselves.”

I did not answer but I followed her to strap on the Angel attachment to the armour I already wore. It was a little awkward with the bulky engine on my back along with my axe, but it would work.

A panel slid open in the wall, revealing a hole into nothing. Without hesitation Marissa crossed her arms across her breasts, palms resting on her shoulders, and she leaped into the hole.

I followed as soon as she had vanished from sight and before I knew what was happening I was out in the open. Marissa spread her wings below me, the blue-tinged shield between the skeletal fingers of her wings reflecting the noonday sun as her jets fired to arrest her fall.
