I did the same and followed her down. Below us the soldiers started falling back into the trenches, abandoning their freshly constructed fortifications. Only the Collegium and the Clans used Angel armour and they had identified us as friendlies just by the virtue of using them.

I saw a flash of sunlight reflect on something red down on the ground and my heart skipped a beat. It had to be Jani.

I remembered the time we had spent together after her adoptive father’s unfortunate death. The late night conversations as we watched over Marissa, the midday sparring to keep our minds off our worries.

She was a skilled warrior, though her proficiency lay primarily in the area of magic. She could perform feats of magic that I would not have believed to be possible without physical manifestation of the spell frames, like the Angel armour. Granted, her spells were not quite as versatile as the Angel armour. In truth, it was more like the Valkyrie armour but even more powerful. Her spells would not allow actual flight, but for short distances and low altitudes the difference was imperceptible.

She used magic almost like Marissa did, without thought. But Jani did not have several spell cores embedded in her skull and interfacing directly with her brain and intent.

I almost shuddered to think about what she would be capable of if she had similar equipment to Marissa, and a part of me was eager to see such a thing.

Another part of me would kill anyone in the world to spare her the pain I had seen Marissa inflict on herself when I had watched her installing the latest gem on her skull.

That thought brought my mind back to Marissa. I had seen her suffer through incredible pain, often by her own hand when she was modifying herself.

I had forced myself to endure pain when necessary, but not like she did.

It was almost as if she did not place any effort in avoiding it, almost as if she was seeking it out.

As if she deserved it.

I closed my eyes at that realization. That was exactly what she did.

The Oath tattoo. The gem installation. They were more painful than they had to be. Marissa had mentioned to me that she might design a less painful Oath if she had the time, yet it had taken me less than a few moments to incorporate a pain dulling spell into it.

She just did not want to. Any pain she could inflict on herself, she would.

Even after thirty-five years she was punishing herself for destroying the Groundlands.

But the Skylanders had been her enemy. The enemy of her people. Why would she willingly suffer so for something that had been justified?

I did not have time to think any more since we touched down just then and Jani was approaching us on a wide flare of magical power, leaving the rest of the troop behind.

“Cousin!” She exclaimed and wrapped Marissa in a warm embrace.

My mistress returned the embrace awkwardly and then Jani surprised me by also embracing me around the waist.

“It is good to see you again, Barengar,” she purred into my chest and I hastily draped my arms around her.

I caught myself regretting the fact that we were both wearing armour and that I could not feel the softness of her curves pressed against my body. She stepped back from me and gave me her dimpled smile.

“I am glad that you have returned, but I am surprised that you arrive by Skyship and not that flying contraption you showed up in last time. That one seemed much swifter.”

“It was a decision removed from our hands,” I said cryptically, not certain how much Marissa wanted to reveal about our current situation. “They put you in charge of the war?” I asked her instead.

I had expected the new general to greet us out here and since she had come, I assumed that she was in command.

“Oh spirits, no. I am not a general. I was just father’s,” her voice broke on the word and her green eyes brimmed with sudden tears, “aide. Chief Marie’s second daughter is in charge now. I was demoted to platoon Sergeant.” She lowered her voice before she added, “with father… Gone. I consider it a promotion, even though it is more dangerous.”

Without thinking I reached out and put my hand on her shoulder and she shifted her weight into my palm.

We stared into each others eyes until Marissa cleared her throat.

Normally I would have expected her eyes to twinkle with amusement, but now they were blank and impatient.

“So, what’s with the Skyship?” Jani asked her cousin.

“It’s a relay node for my creation, Epitome.”

“Epitome? The Epitome of what?”

“Morality,” I supplied and caused Jani to crack a small smile.

“Hah. You always had a flare for the dramatic, Marissa. What is the Epitome of Morality?”

Marissa and I exchanged a glance and I shrugged. My mistress drew a deep breath and gave her the basics.

That Epitome was a construct with vast power that could supply a near endless supply of soldiers that in some ways was superior to a human.

Jani’s eyes widened with the implication but we had no more time to speak to her as the actual commander in chief arrived.

Marie’s daughter was a tall woman a few years older than myself. Her dark brown hair already had a few gray strands in it and her pretty mouth seemed to be set in a perpetual frown and her eyes were prematurely lined with crows feet.

She gave Marissa a cold glare. “Have you finally gathered your wits enough to find your loyalty to your kin?” She asked my mistress.

I took a step toward he with an angry growl but Marissa raised her hand to stop me in my tracks.

“That’s right, ‘cousin’. Leash your dog,” she said disdainfully.

Now it was Jani’s turn to growl as she fell in beside me defensively.

“Oh please, cousin. Don’t tell me that you like him,” the woman sneered at Jani.

My hand reached behind my back to reach for my axe.

“Barengar,” Marissa warned.

Reluctantly I forced my hand back down to my side.

“Anjali,” Marissa greeted the woman and it was obvious to me that she disliked the new commander.

It must gall her that such a sour woman bore the name of her mom.

“So, why have you come?” Anjali asked wearily. “If you are not here to fight the Ca’el, then fuck off to wherever you have been hiding all of this time.”

Marissa sighed. “I am here to fight, Anjali.”

“Fucking finally,” Anjali spat. “It just took you eight years to pull your head out of your fucking ass. You will reinforce the southern trenches and push forward to…”

“No. I am not under your command, Anjali.”

“I’m the General of the united Clans…”

“That may be so, but I am not in your command. I am in hers,” Marissa said and we all started in surprise as a crystal contraption slammed into the ground beside us. We were all sprayed in soil from the impact. Everyone but Marissa and I fell into a combat crouch, magic ready and weapons raised.

One of Epitome’s avatars climbed to its feet, one such I had never seen before.

Instead of the blank faceplate and the featureless body this one was sculpted like a human woman.

The chassis was all swelling curves and narrow waist below sleek ribs with a face that was somewhere between Marissa and Kyra, the only two human females she had ever met. Her hair was fine crystal strands and her left eye shone a bright violet while the other was solid blue crystal. But that was not the strangest part of her. She was wearing clothes. A corset was cinched tight around her waist and a loose blouse covered her breasts and arms while leaving her shoulders bare. Trousers sat snug on her hips and legs with plates of armour strapped to her thighs and shins above high heeled boots. Her skin was covered in a thin layer of illusion to give the appearance of pale skin and red lips. She even blinked with human-looking eyelids but no one with even the weakest of magesenses would ever mistake her for a human woman.

“And what the fuck is this,” Anjali demanded.

“I am Epitome,” Epitome responded and offered the warrior woman her hand.

Anjali stared suspiciously at the proffered hand for a moment before she shook it, wrist to wrist in the warrior style, without hesitation.

“You ain’t Bizhjerai, construct. You have no right to bear the Rune.”

Epitome looked at her shoulder where the Bizhjerai Rune glowed brightly.

“I am not Bizhjerai?” She said with confusion.

She sounded so lost that even I took pity on her.

“Where’s Kyra?” Jani asked suddenly, looking around expectantly for the Archon to appear.

Tears instantly sprang from Marissa’s eyes and she abruptly turned away, even though all of us present would be able to see them with our magesenses. 

“What’s wrong, cousin?” Jani asked and took a step closer to Marissa, undoubtedly to comfort her.

I raised my arm, blocking her path and I again found myself lamenting that we both wore armour so that I could not feel her soft breasts pressed into my arm.

Jani turned her big eyes up at me with the obvious question in their depths.

I focused my magesense on Marissa and I saw her eye focus on my invisible presence. Tears rolled down her face as she gave me a tiny nod.

I drew a deep breath. “Kyra sacrificed her Soul to infuse Epitome with her values and memories. Epitome is essentially an advanced spell construct motivated by what remains of Kyra.”

Jani gasped and threw her hands over her mouth, eyes brimming over with tears.

Anjali stared at the uncertain Epitome with something akin to respect in her eyes and Marissa let out a strangled sob.

Jani ducked under my arm and embraced her cousin from behind, sobbing quietly into the shorter womans back.

“If you truly carry the Soul of Bás Marbhadair, then you may carry the Mark,” Anjali conceded with something resembling reverence in her voice.

An uncertain but happy smile graced Epitome’s familiar features.

A hollow thump from above drew all of our attention and we watched a bulky construction fall from the unnamed Skyship to crash into the ground below. The box unfolded and a receiving dish bloomed like a the bud of a flower. A lance of power shot down from the heavens into the Skyship, and a smaller lance from the Skyship to the box on the ground.

Spells stormed out of the construction to form the schematics of what appeared to me to be a fortress. Smaller shapes dropped out of the Skyship and more of Epitome’s avatars, these ones standard issue soldiers, stormed toward the trenches. 

The construction of the war machine had begun.

“By the Spirits,” Anjali whispered. She stared in awe at the power lancing from the sky. Even though the artificial ley line was weaker than the massive one that speared toward the front lines from Dun Traelak, the sheer distance was staggering.

Anjali rounded on Marissa. “How is this possible?”

Marissa drew together all of the height she could muster out of her petite form and glared at her cousin.

“I have not been idle while I have been ‘fucking off’ to my retreat.”

“I can see that,” Anjali murmured before her eyes narrowed. “If you will not obey orders, then I have a war to run. Jani, make sure that they are not getting in the way.”

“But what about my platoon?” Jani asked, wide-eyed.

“There are plenty of sergeants,” Anjali said dismissively, already walking away.

“I’m sorry about your command,” I said to Jani awkwardly.

She let out a deep sigh and gave me a tight lipped smile. “It’s alright. Whatever helps bring an end to this war, I will do.”

I nodded. It was a sensible attitude. I turned to face Marissa, but she was still turned away from us.

“What now?” I asked her back.

“Now we wait for Epitome to get ready.”

“And when she is?” Jani asked.

“Then we help her in any way we can.”

Marissa started walking to the area that was already being covered in a layer of materialized magic.

I exchanged a glance with the beautiful redhead and shrugged before we both followed her.

## CHAPTER ELEVEN Marissa

Within the first day a series of rudimentary rooms had been established for me, Barengar and Jani. 

I was a little surprised that Epitome had considered Jani to be a part of our detachment, but I did not comment upon it.

Our rooms were furnished sparsely, essentially only a bed each was in place by the time night fell. I was astounded at the sheer number of spells at play. It must be hundreds of them, if not thousands, and Epitome was controlling them all at the same time. Her capacity was far beyond my estimation with her current framework.

“How is the fighting going?” I asked her main avatar, the one that looked so eerily like mother. The one I hadn’t had the heart to tell to change its appearance. Even though it felt like being stabbed in the heart every time I saw it I could not bring myself to erase the visage of mother. She was still in there, somewhere, in some form. She had given everything that she was to Epitome, and Epitome had become mother. Yet, she was not my mother, a fact that was painfully clear whenever I spoke with her.

But I could not forget the way she had looked when Anjali had told her that she was not Bizhjerai. She had looked crushed. Utterly devastated.

I had relived mother’s memories more times than I could count and I could clearly remember the happiness that had filled her when she was adopted into Clan Bizhjerai.

It had been one of her happiest moments, possibly the happiest, of her life.

All of that she had given to Epitome, and being told that she was not of the Clan…

It might have been one of the worst things that could have been said to her, especially from the daughter of the Chief of the Unified Clans.

When I woke up the next morning and stepped out of my chamber I found that a whole fortress had sprung up around me while I slept.

I was astonished at the speed Epitome could work. It had taken me weeks to build something similar to this structure when I was infiltrating the Groundlands, and Epitome had completed it in a handful of hours.

Granted, she had near unlimited power to channel into her creations and she did not sleep. Neither was she really constricted by the single-mindedness of a mortal. While I could only focus on two, maybe three, tasks at any given time Epitome could focus on thousands.

I shook my head slowly while a small smile curled my lips.

I had vastly underestimated her capabilities. She really had outgrown all of my expectations, and then some.

I stretched my enhanced magesense through the keep and found that a large section of the immediate underground had been excavated and a large factory was being constructed beneath my feet.

Segments of it was already operational, churning out parts for avatars and weapons. Several artillery pieces were already stationed on and beneath the wall surrounding the main keep and train tracks were stretching away into the distance.

“Did you sleep well, Marissa?” An avatar asked me pleasantly.

“As well as could be expected. Where are the tracks going?”

“Back to the workshop. While the long range transmission of magical power is quite efficient, there are significant losses due to the stabilization enchantments. Manufacturing materiel at the source of the power and transporting them to the front would be more economical when accounting for power spent, if not for time until deployment.”

I nodded along. What she was saying made sense.

“So I decided to use a combination of on location manufacturing bolstered by bulk transport.”

“Great job, Epitome.”

“Thank you, Marissa. I have also sent out scouting avatars to find ley line crossings. I am already reaching capacity of the Great Geyser collectors, even though I am scaling them up as we speak.”

What the fuck? She was already consuming all of the Great Geyser? The workshop must have grown exponentially since we left. Then the final part of her statement struck me.

She had only reached the capacity of the facility, and she was scaling it up. 

I let out a relieved breath. If she had been able to act that quickly then I would truly have been afraid of my own creation.

“Anything further to report?” I asked her.

“The avatars in the trenches have relieved a stretch of Clan warriors, which have freed them up to rest and aid the healers in their work. Do you want me to construct facilities to aid in the triage?”

I blinked in surprise. Why had I not thought of that? While Epitome could not use Blood Magic at all to heal injuries, she could easily extract bullets, stitch up wounds and splint broken bones. That would alleviate the pressure on the healers who could then focus on the more serious casualties.

“Yes. Top priority,” I responded with a firm nod.

“I expect to have a significant production of avatars and weapons by nightfall. Vehicles and fire support will take a little longer.”

A loud boom from overhead drew my gaze to the roof and my crystal eyes saw right through it to the Skyship above.

“Initial bombardment support of the ground troops have already begun,” Epitome added proudly.

“If you keep this up then you are going to make me feel utterly useless,” I joked and was surprised when her shoulders drooped.

“While that is not my intention, that was why you made me,” she said sadly.

I reached out and put my hand on her crystal shoulder. “I know, and when I am truly obsolete then I will more than happy.”

She raised her head and the blank faceplate seemed to regard me for a moment.

“Then I will strive to make it so as fast as possible.”

“How do you estimate that the enemy will react to your sudden appearance?” I asked, mainly to gauge her capabilities in military strategies.

“I am an unknown to them, but they are bound to connect my avatars in the trenches with this outpost. I suspect that they will attempt a counter-offensive before the defenses are fully operational.”

I nodded. “I suspect the same. Or they will attempt to push through another part of the trenches to flank us.”

“I considered that as well. Two more Skyships are en route from the workshop to shore up other parts of the front. I am also developing a beacon to be launched from the orbital stations to act as a seed for a fortress such as this.”

I felt my eyebrows climb my forehead. “Stations?” I asked, emphasizing the plural.

“Yes. Construction of a second station completed the day before last and orbital adjustment was successful. It is now nearly directly above us in geosynchronous orbit.”

I used my crystal eye to look through the structure again and saw that the lance of power arcing down from the sky had dramatically changed angle.

A sudden commotion at the edge of my magesense drew my attention and I focused on it. I immediately recognized Jani and the expression on her face was frantic.

I exploded into action, dashing toward the front gates where I found her. The doors opened on their own as I approached, courtesy of Epitome or the gems on my head I did not know, nor did I care.

Jani squeezed between the opening doors and came to a halt before me.

“The Ca’el are amassing for an attack. I’ve not seen so many in one place before,” she gasped out between heaving breaths.

“Suicide,” I scoffed. “They will not be able to cross the no mans land with all the machine gun nests that you have erected.”

“They must know that as well,” Jani argued. “They would not prepare for a charge just to throw their men away.”

She did have a point there, yet I did not have time to answer her before the first shells fell.

The whole trench line before me erupted in flames, shrapnel and death. Scores of Clansmen were shredded to unrecognizable red ribbons, those who survived and kept their wits about them dove into cover behind sandbags and emplacements. Those in the ditches would be relatively safe unless a shell found its way directly into it and even then it would have a limited effect due to the strategic bends in the trench-line.

“Fuck!” Jani shouted as she spun around and beheld the carnage.

The barrage just carried on, and on. In the distance I saw the smoke and flashes of the artillery, at least three batteries had been placed in the cover of night and out of sight.

“We have to silence those guns,” I barked at Jani and I saw her eyes flit between the batteries of enemy guns.

“They’re outside of our fields of fire,” she gasped. “That’s was why they were throwing their men away, to map out our guns positions.”

That was the first I heard of that and I closed my eyes in frustration. It would have seemed that Anjali had gotten greedy and fallen for the enemy’s bait.

Barengar skidded to a halt beside me, eyes narrowed through the eye slits of his helmet.

My Angel armour had fully materialized and I jerked my head at Barengar and Jani to follow me. I had only taken a step when Epitome’s main avatar spoke.

“What should I do?” She asked uncertainly.

“Can you strike one of the batteries with counter-fire?”

Her shining eyes pulsed once. “The south-west can be within range with only a few minutes of reconfiguration of the Skyship.”

“Do it,” I ordered tersely before I leaped into the air, followed closely by Jani and Barengar.

We did not fly far, only a few hundred paces to land outside the command tent. The guards hesitated when they saw me, clearly trying to decide if they should stop me or let me through.

I was not a part of the army and so I had no right to barge in on their commander in such a position. But I was also Marissa Zorantis Bizhjerai, a living legend to my people.

In the end their reverence won out and they stepped aside.

Inside I found Anjali shouting orders into her communicator and she looked up with a disdainful glare when I barged into her space.

“The fuck do you want?” She sneered.

“Your Angels. I want to take out those artillery batteries.”

“No. I can’t afford to risk my reserves on such a suicide mission.”

“If you don’t, then you will lose these parts of the trenches. You are not a fool, Anjali. You know as well as I that that barrage out there is to keep our people pinned so that the infantry can advance unmolested.”

“Our people?” Anjali said, indignant.

“Don’t you fucking start with that,” I snapped. “Not now. I can’t go out there myself, I need the Angels.”

“Get your fucking pet to protect you,” Anjali seethed.

Barengar bristled beside me, his hand reaching behind his back for his axe.

Without looking back at him I extended my arm across his chest, stopping him in his tracks.

“Epitome is doing what she can at this point. She is bringing the Skyship around to counter-bombard the south-western battery, but we need to stop the others.”

“Say you succeed, then. What about the third battery?”

I had no answer. Epitome could deal with one, me and the Angels a second. But she was correct. There was a third battery out there and as long as it was free to sustain the barrage it would be enough to keep our infantry pinned.

“I will go,” Barengar growled. “Give me as many Angels as you can spare, and I will silence those guns.”

I turned to look up at him. “You can’t defeat them, they will be protected.”

Barengar looked down at me and shrugged. “I don’t have to defeat them. Just disrupt them long enough.”

I stared up into his brown eyes, sudden worry clenching at my heart. He was going to his death without a moment of hesitation. To defend a people that was not even his.

But if I did not let him go, then the Ca’el would storm our trenches in force and even more people would die. I gritted my teeth and nodded before turning my head to await Anjali’s decision.

Without her approval the Angels would never follow me, regardless of how much they wanted to.

Anjali sighed and raised her communicator to her mouth. Without looking away from me she spoke into the mouthpiece.

“All Angels to report in by the command post.”

I let out a sigh of relief. “Thank you, Anjali.”

“Don’t. Just go.”

I gave her a shallow bow and retreated outside.

Angels were already dropping from the sky, the sunlight glinting off their purple crystal armour. One by one they rose to their feet from their landing postures and folded their wings behind their back.

They were well drilled, the elite strike force of the Clans. Each of them was handpicked from the most skilled warriors from each of the Clans to form this unit. Barely two hundred strong and quick to assemble. I quickly divided off fifty of them and gave them to Barengar to command.

“Angels!” I cried out. “Some of you flew with me at the Groundlands. Most of you had a parent that fought by my side when the last Skylanders fell. Today we will fly together, and we will silence those guns.” 

I pointed at the distant muzzle flashes.

As one they all pounded their fists over their hearts and without hesitation they unfurled their skeletal crystal wings.

They were professionals, veterans of years of warfare and they did not need more words or motivations.

“Don’t die,” I told Barengar and I could barely see his grin through the vertical breathing slit in his helmet.

To my surprise Jani hovered close by and when my gaze landed on her she straightened her back and squared her shoulders.

“I’m going with him,” she declared and with a single look into her eyes I could see that she would not be dissuaded.

“Wear your helmet,” I admonished her and with a blush she put it on her head.

I turned back to the assembled Angels and spread my wings.

“Strike from the skies, brothers!” I cried out and took to the air.

###

We started our suicidal flight by soaring high, well above the range of Ca’el infantry massing in the freshly dug assembly trenches. Some of the Angels dropped grenades to wreak havoc far below us, a technique that I had invented when I was defending the Groundlands from the savage horde more than thirty years ago. I had given that same idea to the Bizhjerai Angels a few years later and they had adopted that as their initial attack procedure. Drop high explosives to soften up the enemy and then drop down to engage in close range firefights or melee combat depending on the situation.

But we were not here to mess with the infantry and I shouted the order to conserve ammunition. We swooped across the landscape and left the trenches behind. A series of muzzle flashes from ahead drew my attention and the first flak grenades went off in our formation. My shield deflected one of the shots but the rest burst amongst us. Several Angels fell, their bodies ruptured by the blasts or pierced by shrapnel.

“Drop low,” I cried and put my words into action.

We swiftly dropped, the flak cannons struggling to adjust to our sudden maneuver. For a few breaths we were diving faster than they could compensate but they quickly figured out what we were doing and started leading their target.

More Angels fell, and even if they survived the fall they would not be long for this world. We were far beyond enemy lines and the likelihood of crossing the trenches back to friendly lines were close to nil.

I steeled my heart and carried on. All of the Angels knew what they had gotten into and were ready to die for the Clans.

As was I.

Epitome was well on her way to becoming what she was meant to be and my role in this world was swiftly growing smaller.

Perhaps I was already insignificant. Perhaps Epitome would be able to finish her evolution without me.

I had no time for further existentialistic pondering as we arrived at the artillery battery very suddenly.

I angled my wings and shot into the sky, exposed to small arms fire from the artillery crews and the battalion of defenders.

The Angels were in their element and knew exactly what to do. The bulk of the Angels veered off to the east and dropped down on the plains.

Mages among their ranks channeled power and earthworks sprang up from the ground behind which the Angels lay down flat to aim down their crystal sights.

The Mages sketched up amplification circles and prepared their spells to act as mortars. The defenders rushed to answer this threat while I and a part of the force swooped toward the guns themselves. The artillery crews trusted their defenders to keep them safe and they kept manning their guns without looking up. I flashed over the first gun and dropped an explosive spell into the small stack of shells next to it. I was far beyond the blast radius when the ammunition cooked off and I angled my wings to drop down next to another gun, coasting on the blast wave.

One member of the crew of this gun had been shredded by shrapnel, but the rest had been shielded by their piece. I drew my revolver and strode confidently around the huge machine. I shot the first Ca’el soldier in the head and did not even look at the woman as she fell. The second one met the point of my sword with his throat and the third squeezed off a shot with his side-arm.

The bullet flattened harmlessly against my shield and my foot lanced out to crunch into his temple, my crystal boot caving in the side of his head and twisting his body to the side.

With a flex of my mind I melted the breech of the gun, warping it so that it could no longer accept any shells. I cast about, seeing my detachment of Angels fighting the tank crews and destroying the guns. In the distance I saw the Ca’el infantry trading fire with the hastily entrenched Angels, some of them casting worried glances over their shoulders at the destruction we brought amongst their charges.

I smiled grimly at their predicament. They could not turn to fight us without giving up the defense of the hill, and then the Angels would advance to destroy the guns anyway.

Out of spite I drew my rifle and shot a nova crystal into the thickest cluster of soldiers I could find and laughed when the expanding ring of plasma bisected several men at chest level. In quick succession I emptied my magazine with equally deadly spells. The Ca’el were losing their will to fight and I saw several soldiers breaking ranks and running for the hills. I turned back to oversee the destruction of the artillery, the crew having been no match at all for the elite Angels. At a quick glance I saw only half a dozen fallen Angels, several of whom would live. In the distance Epitome’s Skyship belched flame, though I could not see if she hit her mark.

I just hoped that Barengar would be safe.

The last of the guns was disabled and I took to the air again. The Angels disengaged from the pinned Ca’el soldiers with practiced ease and joined me in the sky as we rushed toward the third battery.

A cold lump of dread formed in my belly. That had been too easy.

The dread solidified into borderline panic as we drew closer to the third battery and I heard the crack of gunfire. Too much gunfire. We crested a hill and a battlefield was revealed to me. The ground was littered with the dead and dying, Angels and Ca’el both. A wicked melee was in full swing near one of the redoubts surrounding the artillery battery and I instantly spotted Barengar. He was leading a dozen Angels through the lines of Ca’el, too many of them.

More than we had faced just now. More than should have been stationed here to defend the artillery.

It was a trap. I could read that plainly from how the corpses was strewn. Barengar had set down in the midst of the artillery, destroying a handful of the guns before the battalion had rallied. Hidden Ca’el reinforcement had arrived from the west, trapping him between them and the defending battalion. The Ca’el had charged through the gunfire to engage the Angels in a melee to prevent them from taking wing. Many had fallen to Barengar and his Angels, but they were now locked in close combat and encircled.

With a howl of range I threw spells in advance of my meteoric descent. My spells struck with thunderous force before I landed with both feet first into the back of a Ca’el soldier, my boots crushing his ribs and throwing him to the ground.

I angled my wings to land on my feet, revolver in hand while drawing my sword. Spells flashed out of the gems on the side of my head, eviscerating and burning the Ca’el in a path to Barengar. My revolver spat death and my sword clove life from flesh in a mad dash to reach my apprentice. When I drew closer I saw that he was wounded. He was dragging his right leg and his shield arm hung limply along his side. He swung his axe with only one hand, clumsily chopping into the shoulder of a Ca’el woman who fell with a high pitched shriek.

Barengar spotted me and his back straightened and with a roar he renewed his attack. The Angels I had arrived with descended on the Ca’el, but there were too many of them. We could not win here.

“We have to disengage!” I screamed.

“The artillery,” Barengar grunted as he cut off the arm of a man that had been about to bayonet him.

“Fuck! Disable the artillery, then break off!” I shouted to all of the Angels that could hear me.

“Can’t reach it,” Barengar grunted.

I leaned in and parried a bayonet aimed at his throat and with my magesense I saw a woman raise her rifle to aim at my head. At this distance it was unlikely that my helmet would absorb the shot. Even if it did it was more than likely that it would knock me out, and that was equal to death.

Barengar’s axe split the mans forehead with a solid crack and my apprentice wasted no time in recalling the axe to his hand. We exchanged a grin and fell in back to back, using magic and weapons to keep the attackers at bay. But it was a losing battle. Through my magesense I saw Angels fall around me, our circle shrinking by the moment. A shot struck my crystal leg, chipping the enchantments and causing my knee to suddenly seize up. I almost fell but managed to regain my balance just in time to skewer an attacker through the throat. He did not notice and kept swinging at me, his bayonet finding the gap between my gorget and my pauldron, sliding deep into my left shoulder.

I cried out and lost control of my arm. Luckily I had holstered mothers revolver when I ran out of bullets or I would have lost it when my fingers went slack.

We were losing this fight, but that was alright because the artillery was silenced. The Ca’el would not be able to charge across the no mans land and take the trenches today. Soon Epitome would be ready to disgorge a horde of avatars to bolster the Bizhjerai ranks and eventually push the Ca’el back through sheer attrition. My journey would end here, but my people would eventually win. Of that I was certain.

Another Ca’el woman raised her rifle and I claimed her life by enveloping her skull in a bubble of force before crushing her head like a fruit in a fist. Her body fell only to be replaced by another. There was always another.

I killed this one with a ray of incandescent fury that burned through his heart before I parried a bayonet with my sword, twisting my blade around the barrel to stab into his eye and further into his brain. A shot smacked into my hip and brought me to a knee. Though my armour had robbed the bullet of most of its force it was still enough to render my hip numb and useless.

I parried a bayonet but the force of the blow pushed me off balance and I fell on my side. The man raised his bayonet to finish me off but I focused magic through my crystal eye and burned through his forehead. I tried to get back up but neither of my legs would respond properly. A Ca’el woman aimed her rifle at me, well beyond my range, and I knew that my time had come.

I showed her my bloodied teeth through my helmet in a crazed grin, and then the sky fell down around us.

###

The world erupted in blue flames, the ground shaking with the force of the impact. My face was sprayed with soil and blood and other fleshier bits. When my vision and magesense finally cleared I saw Jani crouched beside me, her body wreathed in Soulfire, her eyes shining a violent blue. Her hair rose around her head as if in a gale and wherever she looked, Ca’el died. She rose to her feet and stomped her foot, a series of stone spikes erupting from the earth, impaling panicked warriors in a large swathe. A wave of her hand burned a score of Ca’el into ash in an instant as a heat haze flashed across the ground, scorching the earth and drying the mud until it cracked. A single man remained standing, encased in a powerful shield. A towering behemoth of a man.

He hadn’t bothered with his mask this time and he smirked with the side of his face that was not a scarred mess.

Zandor.

His hand shot forward and Jani grunted and planted her feet, the air around her rippling with a clap of thunder.

Barengar chopped down his last opponent and we found ourselves in an odd calm. Everyone outside of Jani’s protective circle was knocked from their feet, dead or dazed.

My apprentice spun and reached down to grab my gorget, pulling me to my feet roughly. My weakened limbs managed to find their balance and I stood there, gasping for breath.

Jani groaned and strained and I saw the skin around her eyes steaming. Her skin ruptured where it was visible and raw magical power leeched out of her.

She was overtaxing herself, just like mother had done in her battle against Grasax. If she was going to keep this up she would Archon.

She was undoubtedly powerful, incredibly so, but she was young and her technique was not flawless. Even an experienced Mage would have trouble containing the amounts of power she was channeling.

“Jani,” I warned her.

She did not heed my voice and she kept pushing against Zandor.

I was astounded that he had become this powerful. When I had known him thirty five years ago he had not shown much aptitude for magic and had been content with learning to throw the odd fireball around.

He must have studied hard and trained even harder than I for three decades. He was more powerful that I was, even with my magically charged skeleton.

Unless he had something similar fueling him.

“Jani,” I drawled again while I focused my crystal eye on Zandor.

I shifted through the various mode of vision that I had inscribed into the orb and layer after layer of information presented itself to me.

Barengar limped around me and placed his hand on Jani’s shoulder, focusing his power to stabilize her. I extended a channel of my own and lent him my power and I felt him tug sharply on my reserves.

The warriors around us were stirring, pushing to their feet. Mostly the Ca’el. Only a handful of the Angels still drew breath.

“I can’t believe that you fell for my trap again, Marissa,” Zandor called out to me as he walked forward.

As he came closer I saw the signs of strain on his face. He was putting more effort into his continuous attack on Jani than he was letting on.

I did not respond as I cycled my vision again.

There!

Several bands of power arced from the distance into him. Eight of them, to be precise.

He was being given power from others, that was why he was so strong.

Jani flashed brightly and she cried out in pain, her eyes opening wide.

She gasped and pitched forward, her crystal helmet tinkling like a bell. Barengar stared at her fallen form, his axe still raised in a reversed grip with the hammer head forward.

He had knocked her out before she would Archon completely.

He had possibly saved her life, but he had also removed her shield.

Zandor grinned and thrust both hands forward and both Barengar and I was flung across the ground, my breastplate cracking from the force of the impact.

We rolled to a stop side by side and I twisted my head to check on Barengar.

He was still moving, and he had retained the grip on his axe.

“We have to run,” I gasped out between coughs and blood dribbled down my chin where it instantly started boiling.

Something within me had broken.

“I’m not leaving without her,” Barengar snapped and pushed to his feet, snapping his shield open just in time to deflect a fireball.

“Of course,” I wheezed and sat up, throwing up my own shield to augment his.

“We can’t defeat him,” Barengar stated flatly, imbuing his axe with heat until the head glowed a bright red.

“No,” I agreed. “I will distract him, it’s me he’s after. You grab Jani and get the fuck out of here.”

“Marissa,” he began.

“Do it!” I snapped and then limped away from him, circling Zandor.

Predictably his eye tracked me, the milky one staring straight ahead.

Barengar hurled his axe at Zandor and the big man raised his shield to deflect it. But Barengar had not aimed at Zandor and the axe thunked into the ground between his legs.

Zandor did not even glance at the axe as he stepped over it, and that was when all of the heat trapped inside the axe was expelled.

Zandor’s shield was designed to repel physical force, not heat and the heat wave passed straight through it.

His trousers and boots caught fire and even across the distance separating us I could hear the sizzle of skin being scorched.

He howled in pain, but not just any pain. It was the howl of a terrified man.

Flesh burned twice.

Zandor fell to his knees, fingers grasping nothing as his face contorted in pain.

The flames crawled upward, setting his shirt ablaze.

This was it, this was my chance.

Mother’s revolver flew into my hands and I cocked the hammer with my thumb before taking sight down the crystal sights.

I remembered a moment many years in the past where I had stood similarly above my aunt, Alissa, forced to take her life.

I remembered mother standing similarly above a man she had hated with all of her Soul, yet her body had craved his touch desperately.

Both of those times she and I had hesitated, just like I hesitated now.

I had loved Zandor once. I still did in many regards. We had shared many pleasant hours in each others company, in each others arms.

Zandor and Valia were the only two people outside of my family that I had truly loved. I had envisioned a future together, my friend and I raising the children Zandor would give us.

That had changed when Valia died. She died because of me, because I had been too weak to save her.

Those children that she would have given birth to vanished from my vision and it became so much bleaker. But there would still have been happiness there.

Then I had betrayed Zandor and the Skylanders, destroying his hopes for a glorious future among his own kind.

I had believed that he died there, in the chaos of the Bizhjerai invasion of the Groundlands.

The future that I had hoped for had died there, slain by my own hand.

My finger tightened on the trigger as his howls of pain rang in my ears.

He had somehow poised an Empire to march to war, just to draw me out. To punish me for what I had done.

And I had deserved it. I deserved to suffer, to die, for what I had done.

Yet I did not regret it. I rid the world of the threat of the Skylanders, but my method was unforgivable. If I died because of it, that was a price I gladly paid to protect my people from any threat.

But Zandor was not acting in protection of his people. His people were dead. Mother and I had made sure of that.

He was acting purely out of vengeance, a selfish desire that would not bring his people back to life.

Thousands had died due to his efforts.

I may have loved him once, I may still love him, but this twisted creature before me was not Zandor. Not any more.

The true Zandor, the carefree and optimistic young man I had gotten to know and grew to love had indeed died at the destruction of the Groundlands and given birth to this vengeful and spiteful old man.

I had already killed him once, his body had just kept moving.

My shaking hand suddenly stilled, and I pulled the trigger.

My weapon spat fire and a small cloud of smoke that I quickly whisked away with a gust of wind.

My bullet clattered to the ground, flattened against his shield.

I felt my eyes widen and I took an involuntary step back.

Even in his current state, almost completely immolated and in tremendous pain he was still shielding himself.

He fell on all fours, his screaming suddenly stopping. His head snapped up and through the flames wreathing his form I saw his hateful gaze on my skin.

With a bellow of rage a clap of thunder erupted from my former lover, pushing an expanding ring of mud cascading out from him. The flames winked out and the horrible mass of burn wounds slowly rose to his feet, staggering.

His clothes were almost entirely burnt off, his skin charred and blackened. Patches of muscle and sinew glistened where the skin had burned away completely yet somehow his one remaining eye was unblemished from the flames.

His mouth stretched into a crazed grin, his lips cracking and spilling fresh blood.

I could not fight him. He was unbelievably powerful, bolstered by the artificial ley lines. He might eventually succumb to his wounds, but I doubted it. Just as I had enhanced my body to make it resistant to wounds I was certain that he had as well.

My magical power was nearly depleted and I had no doubt in my mind that even if I threw everything I had at him he would just shrug it off and then crush me.

Zandor coughed and spoke with a wheezing voice.

“And so the student has become the master.”

I took another step back, preparing to turn tail and flee. Zandor was unbelievably quick and he was upon me before I could take even a few steps. His foot thundered into my lower back and I was thrown to the ground, something cracking in my back.

I pushed up onto all fours when a big hand clamped down on my shoulder, lifting me up and his other hand encircling my throat.

He easily hoisted my into the air, my feet kicking for purchase as I struggled to breathe. He pushed his burned face close to mine and snarled into my face.

“I will not kill you quickly,” he sneered. “You will pay for what you have done, Marissa.” He gave my throat a squeeze for emphasis and I launched my foot at his groin.

He just twisted his hips and took my kick on the thigh as if it was nothing, less than the sting of a gnat.

“For such a horrid bitch, you are still rather beautiful. I wish that I had learned that Blood Magic trick from old Varl, but he was not skilled in that area,” he gloated and sent a thunderous punch into my side. Even through my armour I felt my ribs groan, not quite cracking.

“Varl?” I managed to wheeze out.

“You thought he died on the Prowler, did you not? No, I found him in the wreckage and tended to his wounds. He taught me much. He taught me how to defeat you.”

Zandor leaned in close and sniffed my neck and I tried to take a bite out of his cheek.

“Ah, you still smell like you did when we shared a bed. Perhaps we should revisit those old memories together?”

“I’d rather die than lie with you again,” I stuttered, the edges of my vision darkening.

Zandor chuckled darkly, a sound that I never would have expected to hear from him. A sound that sent chills down my spine.

“You think that you have a choice? Mark my words, Marissa, before you die you will taste my cum again.”

“I’ll bite your cock off,” I gargled defiantly, my head feeling ready to explode and my vision fading entirely. My heartbeat was thundering in my ears, loud enough to almost deafen me.

“Then I will break your jaw first,” Zandor snarled and the world exploded into stars and pain. A sharp crack at the side of my head filled my ear and then I felt wet mud on my cheek as I rolled across the ground. I could finally breathe again and I sucked in a deep breath through my bruised and stinging throat. I opened my eyes and the whole world was spinning, yet my magesense told me that Zandor was stalking closer, taking his time and savoring my downfall. If he grabbed me again I would die. I was certain of that. So I did something utterly stupid.

I snapped my wings open and fired my Angel jets, blasting across the ground, my breasts and legs digging furrows into the mud before I could gather my wits enough to adjust my attitude. I shot into the air, the winds buffeting me.

I struggled to stabilize my flight, but my vision was fucked. His punch to my head had pulverized one of the gems on the side of my head, driving the fragments into my skull and splitting it open.

My eye had cracked and the split bisected my vision, distorting my field of vision from that eye. My human eye was blurry and I could barely see anything. I had to recuperate.

But I could not fly in this condition. Not at all. I had to find a place to go to ground, to lick my wounds. Somewhere Zandor could not find me.

A sudden gust of wind almost sent me into an unrecoverable spin and I stretched my magesense out as far as I could. I had left Zandor far behind and I aimed myself at the forest far beyond the front-lines. I might be able to hide there until I regained my strength and could reassess the situation.

Fuck, everything hurt.

For the second time I had been bested by my erstwhile lover. Twice I had barely escaped with my life.

The next time we met one of us would die.

## CHAPTER TWELVE Barengar

“Do it!” Marissa snapped and the desperation etched in the smooth lines of her face made me act before I could think.

I had reacted purely on instinct when I knocked Jani out in order to protect her from herself, and I saw no way we could all escape.

I hurled my axe at the ground between Zandor’s legs, hoping to distract the big man with the blast of heat I had charged into the axe head.

I reached down and scooped Jani into my arms and engaged my Angel jets. I pushed as much extra power into my armour as I could, pushing it against the increased weight in my arms.

I twisted my head and looked over my shoulder just in time to see Marissa draw her revolver to aim at the immolated figure. The axe ripped out of the ground and soared through the air toward me and I just barely managed to shift Jani enough that I could catch it in my hand. Marissa hesitated for a long moment before she fired and I almost expected the big man to crumple to the ground.

Instead he pushed to his feet.

The man was unstoppable.

I had to go back and help my mistress, yet concern for the still form in my arms strained against my will to fight for my teacher.

I could not protect them both if I returned, and Jani could absolutely not defend herself in her current state.

I had to make a choice. Defy a direct order and risk all of our lives, or save myself and Jani and probably sacrifice Marissa.

In the end, it was thoughts of Jani that made my decision. Perhaps the Oath I had sworn to Marissa played a hand in it, but Marissa had not invoked the Oath directly so it would not dominate my mind fully.

Whatever pushed my into the decision, I left my teacher behind. I soared toward the front lines and I dearly hoped that I would have enough power to make it all the way.

I should have, but with Jani’s extra weight… It might be tight.

I did have enough power to cross the front lines at an altitude above the range of Ca’el rifles, but just barely. My people had begun their charge and nearly reached the Bizhjerai line when the artillery had been silenced and hundreds of mangled corpses lay strewn across the no mans land, their blood slowly seeping into the sunbaked mud.

Some of them were still moving weakly, here and there a soldier pushed to his or her feet in an attempt to beat a hasty retreat to the relative safety of their own trenches.

Every such warrior I saw was swiftly knocked from their feet by Azwaili snipers, the small Clan that specialized in long range combat supplying the vast majority of snipers.

It was then that I realized that each of the Clans had adopted to the introduction of Skylander magitech in different ways. Clan Azwail had discarded their bows and black powder muskets to embrace the long rifle and prided themselves in their accuracy. The Ithlaki, the former horse lords, had exchanged their horses for Angel and Valkyrie armour and adapted their hit and run tactics to work extremely well with their new wargear. The Bizhjerai, ever the stalwart warriors, had exchanged spear and axe with bolt action rifles and bayonets, many still armed with swords and wearing medium to heavy armour.

Halfway through my descent my jets flickered and spat, on the verge of breaking down. The landing was rough and I lost my footing, curling my body around Jani to shield her from the impact. My helmet rang like a bell when I struck a stone but it kept my skull intact.

Jani was ripped from my arms and came to a rolling stop a few steps from me. I staggered to my feet and stumbled to my knees next to her. I checked her body for broken bones, but everything seemed to be in its proper place. Unlike my own situation.

My left wrist was broken or severely sprained and the little and ring fingers on the same hand was just a dull ache. At least two of my ribs had been broken and I felt an odd cracking sensation in my hip when I moved my right leg. Possibly a shard of bone had splintered off inside my hip socket. But all of that could wait.

I carefully removed Jani’s helmet, her thick red braid spilling out from where she had piled it onto her head. Her eyes were closed and her breath was shallow and fast. The skin around her eyes were scorched and a faint white mist boiled out from between her closed eyelids. More mist seeped out from the gaps in her armour, parts of the crystal glowing from the rents within. Carefully I picked her up in my arms, wincing through the pain it caused my battered body, and I carried her toward the approaching Bizhjerai.

They tried to take her from me, but I refused. My mind was spinning, no clear thoughts forming. All I knew was that I would not surrender Jani to anyone that was not a healer that could fix her torn Soul.

She had almost lost her life when she defended Marissa and me. I would make sure that she lived.

A Bizhjerai Valkyrie exchanged a few words with the commander of this little troop before she leaped to the sky, her wing jets carrying her toward the command post. I ignored her and kept walking toward the triage section.

One of the healers looked up, his hands deep inside the guts of a woman, desperately trying to dig the bullet out of her. The healer immediately returned his attention to the task at hand.

“What’s wrong with her?” His assistant asked me.

“She almost Archoned,” I gasped out. “Her Soul is torn.”

The small Azwaili woman nodded sympathetically. “Put her over there in the emergency row and the healer will be with you shortly.”

“He will look at her now,” I growled, suddenly angry.

“Critical patients first,” she said softly but firmly.

“It’s alright, Ana,” the healer said as he pulled his fingers out of the woman lying before him. “I couldn’t save her,” he added sadly, finally tearing his eyes from his patient. She had stopped breathing.

The healer stood up wearily and looked me straight into the eyes.

“Unfortunately, I can’t help her.”

It felt as if the ground had opened up beneath me and I staggered to the side. I had abandoned Marissa to save Jani, and now this man told me that she was dying and that there was nothing he could do to save her.

“You have to help her!” I choked out, gazing down on her deceptively calm face, so still and unperturbed in my arms.

“I cannot,” the healer told me gently. “You must bring her to the healer station.”

My gaze snapped up to stare into his eyes. “Are you telling me that she can be saved?”

The healer took a step backward, holding his hands up.

I struggled to calm myself.

“No. I am not saying that. I am merely saying that she might have a chance there. No guarantees.”

“Jani!” A distraught female voice shrieked and with my magesense I focused on Anjali. She rushed up to me, taking in her still cousin.

Her furious face turned up toward me and she glared through narrowed eyes even as a tear trickled down her cheek.

“What did you do to her?” She asked me accusingly, her fingers curling around the hilt of her sword.

“It was a trap,” I snapped at her. “Zandor, the Demagogue, was there. He fucking killed Marissa!”

Anjali paled and her eyes opened wide, her hand falling from her sword.

“What? Marissa’s dead?”

“I… I don’t know. She was wounded, but not badly. She drew him away from us so that I could get away with Jani.”

“You fucking left her there? On her own?” Anjali’s voice was full of scorn, and it cut deep into my heart.

“She ordered me to,” I muttered.

“You fucking cretin,” Anjali began but my temper surged and I took a step to loom over her.

She was a tall woman, but I was freakishly so and could easily tower over her.

“I don’t have time for your bullshit, Anjali. Jani is dying and I must get her to the healers.”

Her furious expression changed in a heartbeat, turning into a wide-eyed look with parted lips and a faint blush slowly creeping up her neck and onto her cheeks. Her tongue darted out to lick her lip.

I kept scowling at her, willing her to help me. Finally she gave a single nod of her head.

“Go directly west from here, I will dispatch a guide to aid you.”

I was already walking away from her. “No need,” I snapped over my shoulder. “She does not have time to travel on foot or by train.”

If she replied I did not hear it and I pushed my injured hip to its limit to reach Epitome’s keep as quickly as possible. Even before I had crossed the gatehouse I was calling her name.

“Epitome! I need a pair of Archangels, now!”

One of her avatars nodded and almost immediately I heard the scream of jet engines as two of the large machines glided out from the Skyship above. They dropped rapidly and their impact cracked the thin layer of crystal that was the emerging floor. The Archangels opened up and I wasted no time tucking Jani into the one on the right. It gladly accepted her and swallowed her up and I stowed my axe before mounting my own Archangel.

“Epitome, slave Jani’s Archangel to mine,” I ordered the sentient construct.

“Are you certain, Barengar?” Her calm voice rang out inside my helmet. “You have never flown a slave-master configuration before.”

“I’m fucking certain. Now do it!”

She did not answer but an indicator that I had never before seen lit up on the side of my illusory viewport and I assumed that it meant that Jani’s Archangel was now slaved to mine.

I brought us high into the air before I oriented us to the west and slid our suits into flight mode. Within moments we were screaming across the plain and I kept my eyes peeled for the healer station. It should not be too far from the front lines, just far enough away to have time to evacuate the wounded if the front lines were lost.

###

“I need a healer to deal with a torn Soul!” I screamed as I spread my arms to catch Jani as her unconscious body fell out of her Archangel.

My hip screamed in anger when her slight weight settled on me and I nearly fell. A group of curious healers and walking wounded had surrounded the two crystal contraptions that had just fallen from the sky, yet no fear was evident in their demeanor. They must have heard about the strange flying machines that Marissa had been using lately. An older woman shouldered through the gaggle of onlookers and she angrily tucked a stray strand of gray hair into the tight bun on top of her head.

“What is the meaning of this disturbance?” She demanded, and then her eyes found Jani cradled against my chest.

She surged forward, placing her palm on her forehead and I felt magic flow into my friend.

“What happened to her?”

“Torn Soul,” I grunted.

“Bring her inside, quick,” she told me, already moving away. She turned her head and snapped at one of the walking wounded. “Fetch Ulma, quickly!”

The Ithlaki boy she had ordered dipped his head and dashed away, his wounded arm held stiffly against his side.

I followed the old woman into the nearest building. “Put her on the slab,” she ordered me and I hastened to comply.

Even before I had withdrawn my arms the woman was there with a knife and started sawing through the crystal straps that kept Jani’s armour on her body.

“Be useful and help me get her out her armour,” she snapped with a jerk of her head in the direction of a table upon which lay more sharp implements.

I reached out my hand and a knife sailed through the air and slapped into my palm and I got to work on the straps of Jani’s pauldron.

One by one the crystal armour plates clattered to the floor and just as I was tugging her boots from her feet another woman burst into the room.

Ulma, I presumed. Ulma was younger than the other but still many years my senior. Her lustrous brown hair was sprinkled with gray strands and her eyes were surrounded by a faint net of wrinkles, giving her soft brown eyes a matronly cast to them.

Ulma gave Jani a single look, her eyes flashing white, before she faced the still unnamed old woman. “Get him out of here.”

“I’m not leaving her side,” I growled and Ulma settled her gaze on me. I suddenly felt like a petulant child, chastised by mother and I hunched my shoulders.

“You will. This procedure will require the utmost concentration. Leave.”

Cowed, I bowed my head and beat a hasty retreat. Once outside I blinked into the bright sunlight and suddenly the world spun.

I nearly fell but a shoulder inserted itself under my armpit, steadying me. I felt a small hand on my chest and I lifted my head, struggling through the encroaching darkness.

“You’re hurt too, aren’t you?” A soft voice asked me.

“It’s nothing,” I said and tried to stand up. That was the moment my hip decided to betray me and I fell, nearly toppling the woman that was supporting me.

“Help!” She shouted and I heard boots rush closer.

Then darkness.

I woke up some time later to a loud snore. Before I opened my eyes I focused on my magesense and took stock of my surroundings. I was lying on a soft mattress on the floor in a long line of others. All of them bore wounds, mostly lacerations to the head and shoulders most likely caused by shrapnel from mortar or artillery fire. I took a deep breath and found that my chest had been tightly wrapped by soft crystal bandages. Right, broken ribs. I suppose that the Clans could not spare many Mages to telekinetically splint my broken bones, something that I should do as fast as I could. Marissa had spent several days honing my control enough for me to actually do it myself, and it had not been easy.

A sudden surge of memories brought my eyes wide open.

“Jani!” I gasped and sat up with a groan. I hobbled to my feet and nearly fell when my hip stung sharply.

The healers had not noticed, or failed to fix, the bone shard lodged in my hip. I ignored the pain and staggered toward the open door. A healer assistant saw me emerge from the gloom within and she hurried up to me with a frown on her face.

“What are you doing? Get back in there and rest!” She ordered sternly.

“I must check on Jani,” I muttered and shouldered past her.

“Hey! Get back inside or you will only make your broken ribs worse,” she tried to reason with me.

“Let them. I will fix them later.”

“Do you know how injured you actually were when you came here?”

“It does not matter. I’ll be fine.”

“You were bleeding internally. You still are, you need to rest or you will tear yourself apart again.”

I focused inward for a heartbeat, scrutinizing the tissue surrounding my ribs. I was not bleeding any longer.

It would seem that the alterations Marissa had taught me to apply to my body had worked and that I no longer needed to consciously apply Blood Magic to speed up my healing.

“I’m not bleeding internally,” I said without slowing down my laborious walk. “It’s already healed.”

“It most certainly is not!” She countered and I could tell that she was starting to get annoyed with me.

“I’m a Mage, proficient in Blood Magic,” I informed her calmly.

“Oh. Alright then,” she said and then walked away as if she had no care in the world. I blinked after her before I shook my head and resumed my walk. I moved slowly toward the structure where I had last seen Jani and I eventually pushed through the door.

The slab was empty but it was covered in blood. The old woman from earlier stood there, scrubbing the surface with a cloth.

“The woman,” I croaked. “The woman I brought in here. Is she…”

The old lady turned and gave me a look from head to toe before she sighed wearily. “Young men,” she muttered. “She’s stable, for now. She did not wake up but we managed to close the wounds in her Soul and flesh alike. Now it is up to the Spirits if she is to join us, or them.”

“Where is she?”

“She was taken to rest. You should not disturb her.”

“Where is she?”

The woman sighed again and tossed the bloody cloth into a bucket of water before she gave me a sharp gesture that I should leave. She fell in behind me and joined me by my side once outside. She pointed between two structures at a third one farther away. “She’s in there. Do not disturb her,” she admonished me before she returned into the operating building.

I gritted my teeth and started shuffling in the direction she had indicated. Slowly I made my way over until I could push the door open.

And there she was. Pale and still, her eyes closed, beneath a blanket that was draped all the way to her chin. By the way the cloth draped over her curves it was quite clear that she was naked beneath the blanket. I watched the swell of her breasts as they rose and fell with her even breath.

It was not until a loud clearing of the voice told me that I was not alone, and that I had been staring. I tore my eyes away and cleared my own throat.

“How is she?” I managed to get out.

“Stable, for now. She needs rest,” the female attendant informed me.

I walked further into the building. “I am sure that you have more pressing matters to attend to. I will look after her,” I told her.

“I shouldn’t…”

“Perhaps not. But you will.”

“It is not proper to have a man…”

“I am her friend,” I cut her off. “Her comrade in arms. Please, leave us.”

“You are also wounded,” she pointed out in an attempt to make me see sense.

“I’m a Mage,” I stated for the second time in a very short while. “I will heal myself.”

The woman’s pretty face pinched together before she finally nodded and stood to leave. “If she so much as twitches, you call for aid, alright?”

“Alright.”

With a sniff she left me, her steps hurried. I sank into the still warm chair she had vacated and I leaned into the uncomfortable backrest. I watched Jani for a long while, waiting for any sign of life apart from her breath but eventually I gave up and gazed inward. I started with fixing my ribs, the easy part, realigning the breaks and reinforcing the whole thing with magic.

My hip were a more difficult issue. I had been correct in my assumption that a fragment of bone had been knocked loose and it had lodged between the socket and the ball. My accelerated healing had already formed a cyst around it, filled with pus and fluid. I had to extract that before I could do anything else. As long as I got the fragment out of my body the fracture would heal in time.

I numbed the area before I got to work, using a spike of telekinesis to punch a hole from my groin and all the way into the joint. I carefully threaded the tip of my spike onto the cyst and then I applied pressure to it, squeezing the disgusting sludge out of me. Belatedly I realized that I was now filling my pants up with the pus that oozed out of the hole, but it was too late to do anything about that. My nose filled with the revolting smell of discarded tissue but I had to persevere. When the pressure in the cyst was alleviated I could focus on the bone fragment. I should have just crushed it into powder before I started so that it could have drained with the fluid. As it stood it was too long and jagged to be extracted through the tiny hole I had punched in one piece so I had to carefully snap it in several pieces. One by one I painstakingly extracted the pieces before I stapled the hole up from the inside out. Once done I directed my body to focus on healing the deep wound I had caused before I opened my eyes. My trousers were wet and foul, but I did not care. At this point I cared only about Jani and I rested my elbows on my knees, my head on my hands and I waited.

And kept on waiting.

###

I must have dozed off at some point because when I roused from my slumber the dawn was starting to shine its light through the small window on the far wall. When I had walked into this room it had been early morning and I knew that I had been awake past sunset. A motion in the corner snapped my head around, magesense piercing the gloom with acuity.

I relaxed immediately when I recognized her.

“What are you doing here, Epitome?” I asked wearily.

“I have come to tell you that I am ready to assault the Ca’el lines.”

“Why are you telling me and not Anjali? She’s in charge over there.”

“But you are Barengar,” she told me as if that would explain everything.

“So?”

“I have always reported to Marissa, and she is not here so I came to you. Where is Marissa?”

I sighed deeply. “She’s dead, Epitome. I’m sorry.”

“Dead?” She asked and at that moment I truly understood what she had become.

She had become truly sapient when Kyra gave up her Soul. That heartbroken and disbelieving tone of voice could not be faked.

Numbly I stood up and walked over to her avatar and wrapped my arms around her. She stood stiff and unmoving for a while before her arms settled around my waist and she leaned her head on my chest.

“Yes. Last I saw her she was wounded, exhausted, and she was facing Zandor. There is no way that she made it out alive.”

“My little girl is dead?”

“I am afraid so.”

She pushed away from me, taking a step back. “Did you see her die? Did you touch her dead body to make sure?”

“I think you already know that I did not. I had to focus on getting Jani away. It was Marissa’s orders.”

“I will not believe that she is dead until I see her corpse. She has been in many a tough situation and she has always made it out in more or less one piece.”

“Believe what you will, Epitome. It does not change anything.”

She shrugged in response, possibly the most human motion I had ever seen from her. “That is true. But I will fight through the Ca’el until I find her. I am launching my assault now. Would you come and help me?”

I gave Jani a long look and then shook my head with a sigh. “I will stay here and make sure that Jani is alright. When she is back on her feet we will both come and join you.”

“Very well,” Epitome answered and then the avatar collapsed to the floor.

“The fuck?” I exclaimed and leaped back, hand reaching behind my back for my axe. Then I sensed the spells in her avatar activating and the crystal that she was made of started evaporating, the magic drifting away in the general field.

She had destroyed her avatar instead of bringing it back. It made sense in a sort of way, when you had near unlimited power then time became your most precious commodity. It would be cheaper for Epitome to replace this avatar than it would be to spend her cognitive functions in bringing it back into the fold. I started turning back to sit down on the chair again when I noticed something odd. The magic drifting from the disintegrating avatar did not really disperse as I would have expected. Instead it drifted toward the bed. I watched curiously as the thicker concentration of magic slithered slowly toward Jani, and then into her. My brow rose. She was absorbing the power.

Was she unconscious because she was still so drained of power after the fight? Or was it because of something else, the tears in her Soul?

I did not know, but seeing her Soul thirstily drink the power I turned and dragged Epitome’s fallen avatar closer, almost draping it over Jani. The deconstruction spells absorbed most of the power, increasing the speed at which it dissolved. Soon enough it released more power than it required and the trickle into Jani turned into a stream. When the avatar resembled the corroded remains of a corpse that had been left in a stream for a couple of weeks Jani took a sudden deep breath, her head rolling from side to side. Her eyebrows twitched into an adorable little frown before her eyes fluttered open.

Her eyes found mine and then they closed again. I thought that she had passed out but then her lips moved soundlessly, a faint croak emerging from her throat.

She must have been parched. There were a carafe of water on the small table next to her bed, along with a mug. I quickly poured water into the mug and then I threaded my arm behind her head, raising her up so that she could drink. I put the mug to her cracked lips and slowly tilted it.

“Carefully,” I warned her. 

She drank greedily in small but insistent sips until she had drained the whole mug. She was breathing heavily by the end of it and her forehead was covered in a faint sheen of perspiration.

But some colour had returned to her cheeks and her eyes opened a crack again.

“Barengar?” She asked with what must have been the most beautiful little smile in the history of the world.

“I’m here,” I whispered, tears stinging the corners of my eyes as I grasped her hand in both of mine.

“You saved me,” she murmured, giving my hand a faint squeeze.

“You saved me,” I countered, electing not to mention that I had knocked her out.

Her hand gave mine a weak squeeze and her eyes drifted closed again. I thought that she had passed out again when her eyes opened and her smooth forehead was marred by a small frown.

“Marissa?” She whispered.

I opened my mouth, but no sound escaped my throat and I had to turn my face from her heartbroken expression.

When Jani didn’t move or speak for a long while I looked back at her and saw that her eyes were squeezed shut, a single tear dripping down her temple.

“Did you see her fall?”

“No. She drew Zandor from us, ordering me to flee with you.”

A sad smile twitched her lips. “Valiant to the last,” she murmured before her eyes suddenly sprang wide open and she tried to sit up in the bed.

The sheet covering her slid down her shoulder, revealing smooth skin and the soft swell of her upper chest before I could gently push her back into the bed. She was too weak to resist and fell back with a disgruntled grunt. Yet despite her weakness her eyes were sharp and bright as they narrowed in my direction.

“I saw the hatred in Zandor’s eyes, Barengar. He will not kill her quickly. She might yet draw breath. We can still save her!”

I shook my head slowly. “If she still lives then he will have brought her deep into Ca’el lands. We will not be able to breach far enough to get to her. Especially not in your current condition.”

“I’m just drained, I just need more magic. I’m getting stronger by the moment.”

For the first time she seemed to notice the partially evaporated avatar sprawled across her and her eyes widened with delight. She raised her arm and flicked the blanket aside and I caught a brief glimpse of her perfect breasts and taut stomach before I averted my gaze. I sensed a storm of magic and my magesense informed me that Jani was greedily dismantling the avatar, draining the power from the core inside and devouring the crystal flesh that had housed a shard of Epitome. Her spectral form thickened and blazed into light. Before she had even finished she stood from the bed, already clothed in a thin layer of pliable crystal, armour already forming on her slender form.

Her blue eyes burned with conviction and determination when she stared up into my face.

“I will not rest until we have brought Marissa home, dead or alive,” she said gravely.

I nodded slowly. “I will be by your side, Jani. Once more into the fray.”

## CHAPTER THIRTEEN Marissa

I had managed to fix most of my wounds, more or less, though I lacked the infrastructure to repair my eye or the cracked and crushed spell cores on the side of my head. Without the cores I would be weakened, but I hoped that I would be more than a match for anything but Zandor. I squinted through my twisted vision at the lone rider that slowly crossed the clearing of the forest, slowly approaching the tree I was nesting in. I had avoided Ca’el patrols several times already, yet this was the first time I saw one that was alone. It could be a trap, but I could not make out any details of the approaching rider. I had considered gouging out my crystal eye, the large crack down the middle of it warping my vision too much. But it still provide plenty of alternate modes of vision that still outweighed the impairment to my normal sight. I had grudgingly accepted that I would be partially blind for now and hoped that my decision would not return to bite me in the ass. I watched the lone rider approach and waited for proximity to reveal more details to my damaged eye.

Eventually I spotted the small flag suspended above the riders head on a slender pole mounted to the back of the saddle.

Ca’el courier. Apparently not on an urgent mission judging by the sedate pace of the horse. Soon enough the rider raised their head and I saw a glint of large blue eyes and full red lips through the wide eye slits and breathing channel of her helmet. A female courier. A plan started to form in my mind, but I still did not know if she was the bait in a trap. But I could not let this opportunity slip through my fingers. The rider came level to my tree and she did not even glance in my direction. I sent a quick prayer to the Spirits of the ancestors and I fired my Angel jets. Her head snapped around as I came screaming out of the trees and I had just enough time to see her eyes widen in surprise before I collided with her. I felt something snap inside her and I heard her cry out before we tumbled through the lush forest floor. The horse whinnied and danced away, but she was trained well enough to not bolt. The woman squirmed beneath me, her hand digging between us toward our groins. I felt her fingers curl around the hilt of the dagger that was digging into my midriff but I was faster. My hands found her chest, accidentally cupping soft breasts, and I heaved myself up to straddle her. Her hand yanked the dagger free but my magic clamped down on her, forcing her arms to the ground. My hand rose above my head before I slammed my own knife into her eye. With a wet squelch the pretty orb burst and the terrified woman gasped in pain. My knife lodged in the cartilage at the back of her eye socket and I slammed my other hand onto the pommel of my knife, forcing it into her skull. I heaved on the handle once, twice and the woman went limp abruptly. I sat astride the twitching corpse and strained my ears and magesense for any sign of attackers. When a minute passed without sign of anything living apart from me and the horse I relaxed and stood up. The horse watched me warily but allowed me to approach. I stroked her neck and murmured into her ear until she calmed down enough for me to rifle through the saddlebags. I found some provisions and tools related to horse care before I found a bundle wrapped in leather. I flipped it open. Missives. I leafed through them, hoping to find something useful. 

“Code,” I muttered to myself and incinerated the missives with a burst of magic. At least whoever these messages were for would not learn of the contents. I turned around and peered at the corpse behind me. I needed a way to infiltrate the Ca’el encampment to find the source of one of the artificial ley lines that fueled Zandor, and this woman had delivered a cover. I crouched by the body and wrenched the steel helmet off her head. Her one remaining eye stared sightlessly at me. Dark brown hair, blue eyes and a slightly darker complexion than what was common in the Skylands. Almost identical to the Bizhjerai. Perfect. I reached up and started unbuckling my armour, stripping down until I stood naked in the forest. Undressing a corpse was laborious, and dressing one in armour was even more strenuous. But magic helped, a lot, and a short while later I was dressed in Ca’el leathers and linens and light steel armour and the corpse was outfitted like a Bizhjerai Angel. It stung to relinquish the amount of magical power that was bound into the armour, but such was the price of admission to the Ca’el encampment. I cut a wide strip of fabric from the saddle blanket and wrapped it around my head to cover up my crystal eye.
