I carefully folded it to also cover the side of my head that was studded with magical gems and I used my fingers to comb my hair to further obscure my augmentations. Gloves hid my crystal fingers and a crude crutch would give a plausible excuse for the odd gait my prosthetic leg forced me into. All that done I loaded the corpse onto the back of the horse and mounted up, gently tapping my heels into the ribs of the horse to get her going. I had not ridden a horse in decades, but I had grown up on the backs of the noble animals and my body remembered well enough. I reached up and itched at my collar. While the dead woman now latched behind me had been of a similar length and stature as I, she had been a lot less curvy and the fabric stretched taut around my chest and hips, causing the coarse cloth and stiff leather to dig into my skin. There wasn’t anything I could do about that at the moment, so I just gritted my teeth and kept riding. I hoped that my improvised eye patch would properly hide my enchanted gems and their glow but just to make sure I popped a few buttons on my too small shirt, letting my breasts threaten to spill out. Hopefully that would divert attention from my unsightly fake wounds. It took me almost half a day to reach the Ca’el encampment and I was confronted by guards almost immediately.

“Halt!” They called out, but I could see them relax slightly when they saw the courier flag rising above my head. I saw the guards eyes take in my covered eye through the eye slit in my helmet before his eyes lingered on my exposed cleavage for too long a moment. Then his eyes saw the corpse beside me and his eyes widened. He cursed in his native tongue and I idly memorized the word, intending to ask Barengar what it meant if I ever saw my apprentice again.

“She attacked me in the forest, probably a survivor from the attack on the artillery,” I lied.

“You killed a Bizhjerai Angel? On your own?” The guard frowned suspiciously.

“She was wounded,” I shrugged. “Bitch stole my eye in the scuffle,” I spat angrily and thumped my fist into the corpse behind my saddle.

The man nodded appreciatively, his eyes fastened on my cleavage. “Carry on. I am certain that the lady will want to inspect that armour,” he said with a jerk of his thumb at the corpse.

I dipped my head and spurred the horse onward, and I was inside the Ca’el encampment proper. I wondered who this lady would be, and why she in particular would want to inspect the Angel armour.

Angels tended to fall where their bodies could not be recovered and the Ca’el would have had plenty of opportunities to inspect them.

Then it suddenly struck me. 

The Ca’el did not have any Mages. At least that is what we had all believed until Zandor suddenly appeared. Was this lady one of his apprentices? One of those bound to him, filling him with all of that power?

I glanced to the sky and shifted the perception of my broken yet still functioning eye. It saw through the cloth covering it easily and I instantly spotted the artificial ley line I had been tracking. It was arcing down toward the ground somewhere within the camp, and a second ley line descended to the east and a third to the west. All of them within this camp.

If my hunch was correct then one of these ley lines where bound to be the lady that the guard had mentioned. Before I had ridden far into the camp an official looking man approached me, stepping in front of my horse and grabbing the bridle to stop me.

“The lady have moved her quarters,” he informed me. “Let me guide you to them.”

I did not have time to respond for he started walking, his hand still firm on the bridle. Apparently the courier flag I carried heralded who I was supposed to report to.

I cast my magesense out, searching for anyone with the tell-tale aura of a Mage and when I did not find one I cast a couple of subtle spells to start dissolve the Angel armour within the hour. I did not want this mysterious lady figuring it out and starting to manufacture them for her own warriors. The Angel armour was one of the few advantages that the Clans had in this conflict. I doubted that this apprentice would be skilled enough to learn how to replicate the Angel armour but Zandor had mentioned that he had been trained by Mathias Varl. The old man had been an exceptionally skilled Construction Mage. He had in fact been the one that taught Uncle Kenny almost everything he knew.

The thought of Kendall made my throat constrict. The image of him falling to the mud, blood spurting out of his mangled neck, flashed before my minds eye and I had to swallow down a desperate sob. The sudden reminder of his death caused another memory to resurface, one that I had struggled to push down to the very core of my being.

Mother standing next to the Reliquary of Souls, my eyes lifting from the letter she had written me. The sorrow and determination of her face as she stuck her spectral hands into the socket before she allowed the Reliquary to absorb and dissect her Soul, feeding the core of what was her into Epitome. The dismal jets of discarded Soul that vented into the chamber. The parts of my mother that had been deemed non-essential. Waste.

I gritted my teeth and blinked an angry tear away. 

Within the span of days I had lost them both.

And it had all been my fault.

If I had engaged in this war earlier, both of them would still have been alive.

If I had not forsaken my people for so many decades, so many of my family would still be alive.

No more self doubt, I decided. From this day forward I would protect my people. Do what I believed was right.

And if I turned out to be wrong, that I would become the monster that I had feared that I was all of my life…

Then someone else who believed that they were right would stop me. The evolution of ideas, the strongest one would survive.

I was pulled from my reverie when my horse suddenly stopped and the official turned to gaze up at me. 

I was a little surprised, and more than a little miffed, that his eyes stayed stubbornly on my face.

Ever since I had blossomed from girlhood I had enjoyed men and women taking in my body with appreciation and now that I had purposefully displayed it as a diversion I was disappointed that I had come across a man that seemed immune to my charms.

I huffed and dismounted and before my boots had struck the ground a figure exited the wood hut I had halted next to.

My eyes widened in surprise. I had been so lost in thought that I had not noticed that I had come upon the terminus of the artificial ley line that I had been tracking. The thick umbilical of invisible energy connected to her chest.

My eyes stared at the connection and the sheer complexity of the spell. A distant part of my mind appreciated the full breasts that cradled the spell-work and with my magesense I saw the full lips of the woman curl with amusement when she saw where my eye had fastened. Her aura blazed with power. She was obviously a trained Mage, and powerful. Almost as powerful as me if you did not count the extra power I stored in my crystal skeleton.

I sent a silent prayer to the Spirits that my cloaking spells would fool her and that her magesense would not see the gems on the side of my head or my crystal eye or other prosthetics.

I may have become powerful with my augmentations, but subtle was not really within my repertoire. Not any more. I had not needed much subtlety since I had infiltrated the Groundlands and I had almost forgotten the lessons that Alissa had taught me. The stray thought of my Aunt sent a twinge of familiar regret through my heart and the memory of me shooting her through the head flashed before me.

I did not let that distract me and instead I focused on studying the woman.

I was already looking up at her chest and I let my eyes travel further up. She was tall. Almost freakishly so, only a few inches shorter than Zandor.

But she was also slender. So slender that she looked like she should have been shaped like me, short and curvy, but a giant had grabbed her by the ankles and neck and stretched her out to nearly twice her intended height. Her cheekbones were high and sharp, her chin narrow and her jawline aggressive. Her eyes were large and slanted and as dark as the night. I could not tell where her irises ended and her pupils began. The sides of her head was shaved and covered in magical tattoos that reminded me of my own and the rest of her thick hair was done up in a high ponytail that proceeded to flow down to her narrow waist. She was dressed in flimsy armour and even less clothing, yet I could easily discern the enchantments on the few scraps of steel she wore. She was even more well protected than a Bizhjerai decked out in full Skylander plate armour and warded against several types of magical attacks.

I suppressed a smirk as I let my magesense play over the enchantments. They were decades old, had been nearly obsolete when the Skylands fell. Clearly taught by Varl years ago.

Even though Zandor had learned a lot about magic he clearly retained his lack of imagination and creativity.

I realized that the woman had spoken, yet for the life of me I could not recall what she had said. 

I let my eye glaze over and I stumbled, bringing my hand up to the side of my head.

“Forgive me, my lady,” I mumbled, “this Bizhjerai wounded me in our scuffle.”

The tall woman sashayed around me, her heeled boots putting a sensuous sway to her slender hips that drew my attention.

“You killed an Angel?” She asked me, disbelief plain in her voice.

I turned around to face her. “I did, my lady. She was wounded and weak from the recent battle, I do not know why she chose to attack me. Perhaps she wished to deny you of the reports I carried.”

The tall woman turned to face me again, yet her long fingers stayed in touch with the Angel armour, an eager and thoughtful expression on her regal face.

“Carried?” She asked ominously.

I forced myself to cower before her. “Yes, my lady. She incinerated my saddle bag before I even registered her presence. It saved my life, she could just have easily killed me first.”

The woman pursed her full lips for a moment before she swept past me.

“Come, tell me what you know of the trap.” She entered the hut, her command post I assumed, and I hastened to follow and left the horse in the care of her manservant.

“Lady Amarah,” the man called through the still open door. “What of the Angel?”

“Take her to my private chambers. I will study it there,” Amarah commanded imperiously and within moments I heard the hoof beats retreat.

“Take a seat,” the lady instructed me with a gesture at a folding chair on the other side of a small desk.

The tall woman folded herself onto a much more comfortable looking chair on the other side and steepled her fingers and regarded me over them.

“Tell me of the trap. Is the bitch dead?”

I shook my head slowly. “Lord Zandor trapped Marissa alright,” I began. “He wounded her greatly, but not before suffering vicious burns himself.”

Amarah leaned forward suddenly, worry oozing from her in thick rivulets. “Zandor was wounded?”

I nodded quickly. “Aye. He was completely on fire, yet he fought through it. He almost killed Marissa but she managed to get away, probably hiding in the woods to the north-west.”

Her lips thinned and her eye twitched yet I could not tell from what emotion. Her eyes glazed over and the ley line spearing from her chest pulsed rapidly before her gaze refocused on me.

“Very well. You are dismissed.”

My fingers fell to my pistol and I almost winced when my gloved hand made contact with the unfamiliar steel weapon. My mothers revolver was stashed in the woods along with her sword. Both weapons were too conspicuous to bring into the heart of the enemy. I contemplated shooting the woman then and there but I knew that her enchanted defenses would easily absorb the shot no matter how flimsy her armour appeared. The sound of the shot would bring countless Ca’el warriors to my location, far more than I could ever defeat on my own. This was not a time for aggression

The patient hunter gets the prey.

I stood up and bowed, backing up two steps before turning and leaving. Once outside I had no idea where to go. I assumed that I was expected to find the barracks, or perhaps a healer to see to my supposedly freshly lost eye, but I had no inclination to such a thing. I had found one of my quarries. There where two more within this camp and I intended to assess them all before making any move.

Amarah was powerful, her wargear making her a difficult target. I would be able to kill her in a one on one fight, I had no doubt about it. But I would not be able to kill her quickly or quietly. I would bring hundreds of warriors onto me if I did. I would if I have to, my life for hers. I was not important any longer, if I ever was, but her death would weaken Zandor. But I would not throw my life away. I would make certain that my death was as costly as was possible.

So I ventured into the camp to find the other two umbilicals and see who they were attached to. Perhaps I could get rid of more than one of them before I died. Each that fell would rob Zandor of an eighth of his surplus power, assuming that they were all equally powerful. The sun was already about to set, I had to work quickly if I were to find the other two come morning.

###

I felt the corner of my lips curl in amusement as I beheld the third of Zandor’s apprentices. I had found the other quickly after I left Amarah’s tent. She had been a brutish woman, blonde of hair and powerfully built. Clearly not of Ca’el origin like Amarah had been. I did not know the blonde’s name but I did know that she differed from the tall woman in more ways than one.

She had been drinking with the soldiers, laughing and making raucous jokes. Her armour covered more of her body and was almost sensible in a fight. It was just as enchanted as Amarah’s but her aura indicated that she was magically weaker. Now that I had found the third one I had noticed a pattern for them.

They were all women. They were all beautiful. And they all had large tits.

It would seem that Zandor’s taste in women had not changed much in the last thirty-five years.

This third one that I was watching on her way back to her sleeping quarters was more like me in both shape and stature. We differed only in a few key points.

Her hair was raven black, mine was a dark rich brown.

Her eyes were startlingly and incredibly pale blue, mine was almost the same colour as my hair.

Her hips were a bit more slender than mine but her waist and ribs were positively tiny. I almost feared that the weight of her breasts, whose size dwarfed even mine, would snap her spine with all of that lack of supporting musculature.

I instantly and instinctively hated her, just based on the way she looked.

While my years had been kind to me, mostly thanks to prodigious use of Blood Magic to preserve my youth, I was not a young woman any longer. I had never considered myself vain, but seeing this perfect specimen of fertility cut something within me, something that I had suppressed for many years.

An old regret that I had never had the chance to bear Zandor’s children and raise them with Valia.

And this woman had him. Perhaps he had already fathered a score of children with his apprentices and concubines.

Granted, I did not know that they were his concubines, but the fact that they were all beautiful and young women led my mind in that direction.

That should have been me.

I should have stood by his side, Valia on his other, as the Groundlands fell before our feet. Eventually Clan Bizhjerai would have adopted them and we would have raised our family in the comforts of Dun Traelak.

Instead Valia had sacrificed her life to save mine even knowing who I was and what I was plotting to do.

Zandor had stood by me until that last fateful moment when I had forced him to choose between me and his people.

That had not ended well for me. I had destroyed everything that he held dear, and that had turned him away from me.

Understandable, but I had foolishly believed that our love was stronger than that. That he would have come to see the evil inherent in Skylander culture.

He hadn’t, and he had roused a powerful empire to wage war against my people. Somehow.

I gritted my teeth as I watched the young woman weave through the camp.

The blood of so many people covered his hands. At this point it might be even more blood than there were on mine.

I had only killed roughly eleven thousand. This war must have claimed more if you counted the casualties of both sides.

Most of them Ca’el, the true victims of this war. They had had nothing to do with me or the conflict between the Skylanders and the Clans.

They were but a tool for his vengeance, just like his current women were.

But tools they were, and they would need to be broken if I were to be able to defeat my former lover and end this war.

This third woman was wearing a silver dress and a heavily enchanted golden girdle. The other two appeared to be fighters, warriors.

I did not get that impression from this girl. She walked with confident steps but she lacked the deadly grace of a warrior born and bred and she carried only a small knife at her side.

But her aura blazed with magic and the umbilical connecting her to the distant Zandor was thicker and brighter than any of the others.

I believed that killing her would be easiest, and the most damaging to Zandor. I just needed an opportunity to do it quietly and quickly.

Preferably while she slept. The sun had set an hour ago and the streets of the camp were emptying. I could not hide in the crowd for long. Soon it would be obvious that I was following the woman.

It suddenly struck me that I did not have to follow her. I could find her anywhere, I just had to follow the thick umbilical that arced into the sky to fuel Zandor. With a grin I faded into the shadows, a subtle spell weaving the light around me to make me truly invisible to any but the harshest of scrutinies.

I strained with my magesense just to make sure that no one had sensed my little use of magic but it did not appear as if any of Zandor’s unbound apprentices, the Mages that had fought by his side in our first battle, were nearby.

It made sense to me that he kept his women far from danger and his lesser and more expendable apprentices in the thick of battle where they would be the most useful.

I supposed that he drafted them from the warriors and trained them as weapons, to use their powers only to destroy. Just like the Skylanders used to do.

Not like me.

My students were engineers first, Mages second. While they all were taught to fight that was not their primary function.

I had trained them to help. Sometimes helping meant building or improving. Sometimes it meant destroying or killing that or those which hindered people from living their lives.

I settled into the shadows, my attention split between monitoring my surroundings with my magesense and keeping it focused on the voluptuous woman that I had decided would be my first target. I watched her enter a larger than average barracks, one that only had a single soft bed inside of it instead of being packed full with bunk beds. I watched her undress and crawl into bed, and I waited for a long while as she tossed and turned as she struggled to fall asleep.

I was not close enough to use my crystal eye to observe her aura but I did not need to. My magesense showed with great clarity that her face was set into a perpetual frown of worry.

No doubt Amarah had told her about the wounds Zandor had suffered in our most recent fight.

I could hear his pained howl in my ears, smell the surprisingly pleasant scent of burning flesh and I could not keep my heart from tearing.

I had loved him once. Still did, regardless of what he had become. I still loved that youthful and optimistic young man that had enchanted my heart so many years ago.

I despised what he had become. In truth, he had died that day on the Groundlands when I crushed his dreams. He had died at my hands just like all the others had.

And in his death the new Zandor had been born. A twisted effigy of what he had been. All hopes and dreams for the future had been burned from him, twisted his optimism into cold cynicism and hatred.

I should have killed him that day. I should have shot him in the head like I did Auntie Alissa and saved so many lives.

I let out a deep sigh. I could not rewrite the past. But I could alter the future.

Could I foresee it? No. My actions could lead to something even more horrific to transpire, but I could not know.

It was what I had feared for most of my life, that I in my hubris would cause a cataclysm.

But those days were over. I would do what I had to do, and if I was wrong then I counted on someone to stop me.

I pledged that if I survived my current mission then I would teach Barengar everything I knew. I would teach all of my students everything I knew.

I would make them all as powerful as I could so that they could keep each other, and me, in check.

The power and knowledge was already there. I had opened a gate to power with my meta-magic research, one that I could not close again.

Even if I could, someone else would eventually discover it again and they might keep it to themselves. Not out of fear for what the knowledge might cause others to do like I had. But to keep it to themselves for personal power.

No, I could not allow that to happen. I would empower them all so that none might rise above the others as an oppressor. If everyone was powerful, then no one was.

But everyone would not be powerful. There would always be people caught in the middle.

I sighed again. That could not be helped. Better that the powerful fought on equal terms and a few got caught in the crossfire than only a few powerful oppressed the vast majority.

Like it had been on the Skylands. 

Like it was in the Union of Cities currently.

During my musings the beautiful young woman had finally fallen asleep. I did not know how long she had been sleeping, so I waited for a quarter of an hour to let her settle down.

I used my magesense to sweep the area. Most of the soldiers were sleeping as well and only the night watch was patrolling.

None of them glowed with the aura of a Mage, which was strange to me.

Perhaps Zandor did not believe that the Clans would send valuable magic users on suicidal infiltration missions, and he was probably correct in that assumption.

That would only make my job tonight that much easier. I could see with great clarity in a large distance from my physical form. Avoiding the patrols would be ease itself.

Only Zandor’s harem would be able to sense me. But only if they were awake, and alerted to what they should be looking for.

My cloaking enchantments should make me appear mundane in their magesenses, but with enough focus and the suspicion that I was more than I looked might allow them to pierce my defenses.

There was nothing I could do about that right now so I simply stood up and checked my weapons.

A pitiful single shot rifled pistol, a dagger and a cavalryman saber was all that I had. That and my magic. And several of my power cores were damaged beyond repair, I remembered sourly.

My head flashed with pain at the memory of Zandor’s thunderous punch that had split my skull and crushed my cores and cracked my eye.

I shook my head to clear it, and then I sneaked out of the shadows while dropping my shadowy disguise.

I had people to kill.

###

I made my way to the cabin that the beautiful young woman slept within without incident, but the door was quite obviously locked and warded.

It made me suspicious. The wards looked too simple, so easy to break. I was certain that these were just decoy wards and that the true wards were hidden beneath them.

Then I remembered that most of the magic that Zandor and his bitches had employed were outdated by decades, perhaps they just lacked the research capabilities to improve of their spells.

Either way I had to get rid of this outer layer of wards.

I did not even cast a spell, I just willed the wards to be broken and the intact spell cores on my head interpreted my will and found the most appropriate measure to dispel the wards.

A small surge of magic wafted out into the spell and the broken wards fizzled and faded. I saw no new wards being revealed when the outer layer vanished and I strained my magesense to find any hidden mundane locks or traps.

I found nothing that would tip off the sleeping woman inside so I shrugged and destroyed the mundane lock by heating the internal mechanism until the steel became soft and pliable before I twisted the knob, snapping the mechanism with a soft hiss, and entered.

The woman had not woken up, astonishingly enough. I crept across the dirt floor until I loomed over the woman, drawing my knife as I did. It was dark inside the windowless chamber but my magesense did not require light. A quick stab severed her vocal cords so that she could not sound the alarm and her eyes snapped open wide, breath sucking into her ruined throat. I quickly sank my blade into her throat and twisted, opening up a bloody smile from side to side. But I knew that a simple slit throat would not have killed me. Not with my augmentations. I was not certain how much Zandor’s bitches had been augmented, but I would take no chances.

I slammed my left palm onto her forehead, controlling her flailing body, and meticulously oriented the tip of my knife between two of the exposed vertebrae deep inside her wound.

A shove, a twist, a loud crunching crack.

The woman went utterly still, her brown eyes still tracking me, filled with pain and anguish.

I fisted her hair and with a savage jerk I ripped her head clean off the remaining few strands of tissue and sinew. The umbilical that had connected her to Zandor flared brightly and I sensed her magical power course skyward. Her body smoked and charred, cracks appearing in her previously flawless skin as her Soul was sucked out of her corpse.

Then silence and darkness apart from the light from the smoldering bed-cloth. Blood drenched my arms and my chest and dripped from the stump of the head I held by the hair. 

I had to get out of here, quickly. I dashed through the small room and shouldered the door open before dashing through the camp. I easily dodged the patrols as I set a course for the other umbilical.

They were moving. Undoubtedly the had sensed the death of their companion and true enough, a horn sounded the alarm but a moment later.

But I was fast, especially when my movements were powered by Blood Magic.

I rounded a barracks and came face to face with the blond bitch from earlier, yet this time she did not wear her armour. All she wore was a gossamer shift that clung to her curves. Her muscled arms coiled as she lifted the bastard sword in her hands and her gaze found me almost immediately.

“What’s going on?” She asked me, her gaze already flitting into the darkness behind me, assuming that I was being chased by something.

I smirked. Even though she was a Mage she was obviously a warrior first. A shield surrounded her body, but she was not relying on her magesense like she should have. If she had then she would have sensed the head bouncing beneath my hand.

“Invader,” I shouted back, still approaching her.

The woman threw up a light spell above her head, bathing the immediate area in a harsh white light.

Her blue eyes snapped to my blood-drenched chest and arms and her eyes went wide, her sword coming up in a defensive posture.

I pivoted my arm back and hurled the head at her.

To my surprise the woman reflexively dropped her sword and caught the head, her shield winking out of existence in order for her to catch it.

She cradled the head in her hands, brushing the bloody hair aside with her thumbs.

“Nahara!” She gasped, and then she died.

The back of her head exploded in a torrent of brain, skull, blood and hair and her body started burning even before she started to fall.

I slammed my single shot pistol back into its holster and kept running. A casual flick of my wrist severed her head from her shoulders for good measure before the umbilical winked out.

Two down, one more to go.

I had no doubt that the last one, lady Amarah, would be a much greater challenge.

I had only managed to kill the first two ones that easily due to stealth and surprise.

A Mage properly trained in magic would never have been fooled like I had tricked the blonde. They’d either let their shield deflect the head or they would have knocked it aside with a sword or a buffet of power.

The blond one must have been a recent addition to Zandor’s harem, or she had been a particularly inept Mage. Probably chosen because of her beauty and innate magical strength. The camp was coming awake, soldiers stumbling out of their barracks in various states of dress. Most of them carried weapons though.

I ducked into an alley, using my magesense to avoid the thickest clusters. Once I had to bowel through a trio of men but I did not even have to slow down to kill them.

Non-Mages were as nothing to me. One of them stumbled back, pushed by a telekinetic shove into the bonfire outside the barracks and his clothes caught fire instantly.

The second fell with my blade between his ribs and the third grasped the invisible band of force that clamped down around his neck with unrelenting strength. I left his weak wheezing behind, knowing that even if the spell dissipated it would have crushed his larynx beyond repair. The third and final umbilical in the area was drawing closer and I knew that Amarah was powerful and clever enough to use her magesense to scout for me. I had stood face to face with her, gazed into her eyes, and seen that she was intelligent and capable.

I would be able to defeat her one on one, but she was bound to have rallied support by now. A quick scan of the horizon would have informed her that her two companions had been slain in rapid succession.

I briefly considered retreating and letting her live and hunt the other five umbilicals, but I quickly discarded that notion.

Amarah would die. She had to die if I was to have a chance fighting Zandor.

After this night it was likely that Zandor was going to withdraw his harem closer to himself.

At least that is what I would have done once it was proven to me that they were not safe. Better to have them close where they could lend aid rather than waiting to be picked off.

I slammed into the door of an armoury and came face to face with a surprised guard, stumbling back from the impact with the door. My sword stabbed through his chest and piercing his heart. I threw my shoulder into his chest, wresting him off my blade without pausing and I snatched up a bolt action rifle. A loaded magazine floated from a box and into my hand and I slammed it into the weapon as I burst through the far armoury wall in a shower of wooden splinters.

I looked in both directions and saw the umbilical to my left and I turned in that direction and ran. I racked the bolt of the weapon, chambering a round, and then leaped onto the roof of one of the barracks. I rolled on my shoulder to kill off my momentum and came to a breathless stop. I could see Amarah near a bonfire, rallying her warriors in a defensive circle. Improvised barricades were being thrown up and patrols hastened through the camp in search of intruders. I shifted my cracked vision and saw that she had surrounded herself in a shield. It was designed to repel physical force, heat and kinetic magic. I even saw strands of air filtration spells layered through the complex weave.

She was really hedging her bets. I could not sense her with my magesense at this distance so I was fairly confident that she would not be able to sense me back given that I was stronger than her and had augmented my senses with enchantments. I shouldered my purloined rifle and squinted down the iron sights and I aligned them right onto her ample cleavage. I steadied my breath, exhaled, and squeezed the trigger. Amarah flinched and her head pivoted to face the bright muzzle flash that had given away my position. But I was already running and leaping for the roof of the next barracks. Amarah extended her hand and a bolt of lightning leaped from her fingertips to destroy the barrack I had been perched upon. I leaped across the gap to the next barracks and gave her a glance. She was unharmed, but my shot had had its desired effect. I had not meant to kill her. I knew that a mundane rifle would not punch through her shield. But I had spooked her and she had instinctively reconfigured her shield. It was now a near impenetrable dome for any kinetic attacks, but it no longer protected her against heat or gas.

Amarah screamed an order and a large section of her defenders filed out from their improvised barricades to search the rubble of the barracks. I silently stole past them above their heads, none of them catching the shadowy figure above their heads. I only had two more barracks to cross before I would be upon her and I could see her eyes fasten on my shadowy form. She had sensed me. Could see me clearly. Her arm rose, power surging through her and her long and slender fingers splayed toward me.

Invisible force coursed toward me and her lips curled into a cruel smirk.

But she had not realized that I was a Mage. And not just any Mage.

I was Marissa fucking Zorantis, daughter of the most dangerous Mage to have ever lived.

I had destroyed the Groundlands. I had exterminated the Skylanders.

And now I would exterminate this bitch.

I channeled power that twisted me aside, a focused shield erupting impossibly fast to deflect her deadly spell.

Impossible, if you did not have a pre-enchanted spell core on hand.

Now it was my turn to smirk when her eyes widened and her smirk faded. I leaped from the barrack and soared above the bonfire.

The remaining warriors looked up at me in astonishment.

I flung my arm forward and threw a spell toward the bonfire and it ignited a few meters above the flames. I shielded myself from it’s effects and poured power into the barely visible vortex.

A swirling orb of blue and white flames, pulling and tugging at everything within range. Weak at first but it rapidly grew in ferocity, tugging at the clothes of the soldiers, their hair, even the mud beneath their feet.

Small loose objects went first. Pebbles, loose ammunition and weapons. Grenades. All surged toward the core of the spell to adhere to it as I reversed and turned the direction of gravity. Soldiers slowly lifted from the ground, shrieking in alarm and confusion. Some of them had the wherewithal to hold on to something, but none could maintain their grip for long. One by one they all fell toward my spell, stuck there by the power of gravity. I landed beyond the bonfire in a crouch, one arm raised to feed the gravity well and the other surrounded by voracious flames. I threw my flaming hand forward and launched a thick streamer of fire into the bonfire, turning the merry flames into an inferno. The flames leaped skyward, turning blue from heat. The ball of men and equipment disappeared into the flaming inferno and I heard screams for only a few moments. Soon enough their emptied lungs would inhale the flames, and there would be no coming back from that. For only half a breath I maintained the spells before ammunition and grenades started to cook off in the ball of flesh and flame. Gobbets of meat and charred blood, fragments of steel and wood from equipment burst out of the roiling fireball to fall as a heavy rain around me. A body came tumbling out and rolled through the mud.

Amarah. Her left arm was gone halfway from her shoulder and the bone of her thigh was exposed among a mess of scorched flesh. Her hair was singed short and her raw and bleeding scalp poked through at places. Her formerly smooth and pale skin was red or charred and covered in burns, yet she somehow drew a shuddering breath.

Even more astonishingly she managed to climb to her feet.

I stood there, gaping at her stupidly as I panted with exhaustion. That display had almost drained my reserves completely. 

How the fuck had she survived?

Amarah snarled through her lipless maw, her one remaining eye burning with pain and hatred. She raised her arm to point at me and a writhing snake of orange lightning surged toward me.

I just barely had time to raise a shield, but it could not block the blast entirely. The ray of power struck my chest and my heart seized in my chest. My vision darkened and I fell to a knee, wheezing for breath.

I slashed my arm through the ray of power, dispelling it with the enchantment in my little finger. The ray winked out but my heart was still silent.

It hurt. A lot. Pain suffused my chest and shot down my left arm and thunder roared in my ears.

Amarah stumbled closer, glaring down at me disdainfully. She raised her arm again but only orange sparks fizzed from her hand. She moaned and fell to her knees before me.

“You’re dead, bitch,” she wheezed through her mangled lips.

She must have sensed that my heart had stopped.

I felt a tiny bit of the power that still remained within me surge toward my heart, felt the shearing pain as the telekinetic cocoon wrapped around my heart, severing connective tissue. The cocoon constricted once, twice, and kept going. The pain receded, the thunder in my ears lessened and my vision brightened.

Amarah’s eyes widened, the burnt one bursting and dribbling down her scorched cheek.

“What?” She breathed and raised her hand to rake me with her long nails.

I raised my hand and caught her wrist, pushing to my feet.

I slammed her arm down on my knee, breaking her elbow with a crunch.

A strangled groan of pain escaped the ravaged woman and I planted my foot on her tits and kicked her onto her back.

“Who… The fuck… Are you?” She gulped between difficult breaths, clear liquid mixed with blood dribbling from her mouth.

“I don’t know how much of your Soul returns to Zandor but if you are able… Tell him that his bitch is sick of his bullshit and that I’m coming for him.”

“Marissa…” Amarah breathed, her eye focusing and the last shreds of her power formed within her.

I raised my foot and stomped on her face. Again and again and again and again. The crack of bone and cartilage was like music to my ears. Her skin split asunder, her remaining eye popped out of her fractured skull and I did not stop until my boot made contact with the ground through her head.

Amarah did not burn at all as the umbilical merely winked out. She’d had almost no power left when she’d died.

I stood there for a moment, getting my bearings and breathing hard. Blood and brains and skull fragments covered my leg.

I was drained. I was technically dead since my heart would not beat on its own, but my spell would keep me alive until I could heal the damage that orange spell had caused. If I could heal it.

But I would not have time for that if I remained here. I was astonished that more soldiers had not arrived yet to inspect the tumultuous blast.

But then again, non-Mages tended to stay clear of magical duels. It was not safe to be close to them.

But I could sense them move cautiously in the fringes of my magesense, slowly leapfrogging closer and keeping solid lines of overlapping fire.

I turned and staggered into the darkness, too drained to use Blood Magic. I would have to use my magesense to avoid the soldiers and find somewhere to go to ground to recover. Possibly steal a horse and get the fuck out of this camp. My job here was done anyway.

I let the night swallow me.

## CHAPTER FOURTEEN Barengar

“Get these assholes off me,” Jani cried out as she ducked her head down into the trench, bullets narrowly missing her helmeted head.

For anyone else it would have been suicide poking your head out like that, but her magic seemed to work without her conscious mind and diverted the bullets just enough to not harm her. I had seen shots aimed squarely at her face somehow miss to do no more harm than just cut a few strands of her hair away.

I sank my axe into the shoulder of a Ca’el woman with a grunt and with a twist of my wrist I ripped it loose.

Suddenly there were no more warriors before me inside the Ca’el trench. 

We had pushed through two lines of trenches already, Epitome’s avatars carrying large crystal shields to protect the rest of us from the barrage of bullets.

I twisted my head to the side and saw a dark haired Bizhjerai female regarding me with wide eyes. I was not interested in her at the moment and I looked beyond her at one of the purple crystal avatars.

“Epitome, can you do something about those machine gun nests?”

The blank faceplate regarded me for a moment before it shook its head. 

“The artillery are out of range. Relocating them now but it will take a while.”

“What about the Skyships?”

“The wind is coming from the wrong direction. It will take them some time to recover from the last maneuver.”

Her words caused me to gaze to the sky to watch the crippled Ca’el airship. It was almost completely on fire and it was slowly sinking toward the ground as it was carried away on the winds. Just moments ago it had been beaten into submission by Epitome.

“These machine guns are the only thing standing between us and Marissa,” I reminded her, raising my voice to be heard over the roar of the guns.

“I know,” Epitome responded calmly. “I could rush them, but I don’t have any shield bearing avatars on hand. It will cost me many avatars to charge.”

“Shit,” I cursed.

“What about the Valkyries?” The Bizhjerai woman asked me with gleaming eyes.

I shook my head, recalling what had happened back at the last trench a few days ago. A group of Valkyries had taken to the air to drop grenades on the machine gun nests but the gunners had been up to the challenge.

None of the Valkyries had reached the machine guns, and only one of them had returned alive.

“They’ll be cut to ribbons, like last time,” I said tersely.

“There’s only one thing we can do,” I continued. “Jani! To me!”

I had barely finished the request before a blue flash of magic streaked through the trench and Jani appeared next to me, panting with exertion. The small veins around her eyes were glowing again, a sure sign that she was overextending.

I put my hand on her shoulder and gazed deeply into her faintly shining eyes. Almost like Kyra’s eyes had shone with inner magic.

“Calm down, Jani. You’re doing it again,” I admonished her gently.

Jani took a deep breath and seemed to force herself to relax. “Thank you.”

I gave her a lingering look, making sure that she was alright before I turned back to Epitome.

“You got grenades?”

The avatar reached into a crystal pouch on her belt and withdrew a crystal the size of an egg.

“Good. Follow me, and stay close. We’re gonna do this slow and methodical like. Epitome, can you give me a boost?”

Her answer came in the form of an artificial ley line that speared from the overhead Skyship and lanced into the power core of my armour. It was rapidly charging and would shatter if I did not find an outlet for this power.

“Over the top and charge the Ca’el!” I grinned maniacally and a burst of Blood Magic carried me over the parapets. I sensed a flush of magic and Jani soared gently after me, landing in a crouch. Epitome merely leapt and landed with a heavy thunk, her feet sinking into the mud. Far ahead of us I saw the Ca’el gunners jerk in surprise at our sudden and suicidal appearance, screaming orders and directing the gunners to shoot us. I raised my left arm and deployed my shield, activating the enchantment and extending the shield to cover my companions. I had only taken a single step when the first shots slammed into the shield and the impacts nearly pushed me back. The bullets soon became a torrent of metal and I reinforced the shield enchantment with the power poured into my wargear through the artificial ley line. Step by step we walked closer, Jani and Epitome poking their rifles through the shield to take pot shots at the entrenched enemy. I saw a few soldiers go down, but it was not enough to disrupt the gunners.

Epitome landed a lucky shot into the eye of one of the gunners and the stream of bullets relented some, but another Ca’el soldier quickly rolled the corpse aside and took her position. I gritted my teeth and pushed through the flood until we were within grenade tossing distance.

“Epitome, take the nest straight ahead. Jani, the one on the right. I’ll deal with the left. On my mark.”

We pushed a few more steps and I felt the power core in my armour scorch the skin of my chest beneath it. It could not take much more punishment.

“Mark,” I barked and fired my Angel jets. I trusted that my companions would act and I soared to the left, going high enough quick enough that the gunners could not adjust their aim in time. The artificial ley line cut off and I raised my own personal shield to deflect the scattered small arms fire that the gunners and loaders unleashed. It did not matter. I struck in their midst like thunder, expelling a blast of force to knock them off balance.

My axe smote the top of the head off a commander before I reversed my swing and stabbed the pommel blade through the chest of one of the gunners that had stood up to defend.

My wrist pressed into her breast before I jerked my weapon free and she fell with a gasp and a ruptured heart. A shot to my back knocked me forward but my armour absorbed the shot. I spun around and clipped the shooters jaw with the edge of my shield and he stumbled back. I pressed forward and swung my axe one-handedly and just barely managed to slice his throat open.

A bayonet pierced my calf and I fell to a knee with a roar, a lance of raw power shooting from my left index finger as my shield retracted and the ray bored a steaming hole through his chest. He fell, breath wheezing out of the smoking hole. The final Ca’el soldier turned tail and ran but my hurled axe took her between the shoulder blades and she pitched face first and slid through the mud before she came to a final stop. I held out my hand and her body lurched as my axe ripped free and returned to my hand with a thwack. A bullet struck my helmet and I fell, reeling. That probably saved my life as a stream of bullets salted the sand bags I had fallen behind. I peered through an opening and saw that the middle machine gun nest was unharmed and a mangled mess of shattered crystal lay before it. Epitome had failed to reach her nest. The one beyond was awash with blue flames. Jani.

I looked around the blood-spattered nest I hid in and swore. Grenade ditch. No doubt all of them was equipped with one. Throwing one of the Ca’el grenades that I had within reach over there would probably be useless. I could not count on them reacting too slowly.

But it would by me some time while they threw themselves into cover.

I reached out and snagged a grenade off the belt of the commander that I had killed and with a twist of magic I pulled the pin.

No need to place myself in the line of fire and I used magic like a low power mortar to launch the grenade across the twenty paces that separated the machine gun nests.

“Grenades,” came the muffled cry.

The plural gave me an insidious idea. They would be scrambling to grab the grenade and toss it into the ditch before throwing themselves prone to avoid injury.

I hastened to launch a second grenade. I hoped that they would not be able to dispose of two of them. With a cruel smile I sat up and let my Angel armour carry me across.

When I was halfway there the first grenade detonated and through the smoke of the first blast I saw that my assumption had been correct.

They were all lying prone, except for one woman who had just closed her fist around the second grenade. Her eyes were wide and her breath was rapid.

I could clearly make out the panic on her face. Her back was to the grenade ditch and I knew that she would not have time to toss the grenade into it before it detonated.

I knew it. She knew it. I could see the despair in her eyes before she closed them with resignation.

She curled up into a ball on the ground, the grenade pressed to her belly as if she was cradling an infant protectively.

I saw her draw a final shuddering breath before she was torn apart by the blast.

Blood and body parts were scattered around the machine gun nest and the remaining gunners were dazed.

That’s when I dropped down among them, just a moment before Jani joined me.

The remaining gunners did not live long. 

Jani tilted her head back and smiled up at me through the mist of smoke and blood and I returned the grin.

I looked back at the trench that we had just conquered and saw a multitude of avatars and Clan warriors climb over the top to take the now disable machine gun nests. To either side of me the Ca’el were falling back now that their defensive line had been broken. The main battle line had been broken and according to Epitome she had broken through on several other stretches to the north and south.

Yet our victories were not without loss. In two places along the trench line the Ca’el had pushed through. One of the advances had been halted at the third reserve trench but the other…

Zandor had led that attack personally, the Demagogue finally entering the fray personally. He had led more than a thousand Ca’el elite warriors behind our lines and we had no idea where they were.

Anjali had fallen back with a large contingent of Clansmen, primarily Ithlaki Angels and Bizhjerai mechanized infantry, to deal with them. Somehow she trusted Epitome enough to hold the main lines.

If Zandor and his contingent were left uncontested there were no telling how much damage they could cause. I ached to hunt for him, but I knew that I was no match for the hulking brute on my own. Even with Jani by my side he would kill us easily.

We needed Marissa.

But Marissa was dead.

I knew in my heart that she was. But Jani refused to believe it, and for her sake I followed her in her mad quest to save her cousin.

“We’re through,” Jani said, her arm snaking around my waist.

I draped my arm around her shoulders and pulled her close, sinking my head to touch my helmet to hers.

Even through the armour I could smell her sweet scent.

“Let’s go and find her, then,” I murmured. “Do you have the scrying core?”

Jani pushed back and frowned up at me disapprovingly. “Of course I do.”

She shook her head and walked away from me, muttering about dense men and their stupid questions.

I hastened to follow her, shoulders slumped. I had not meant to ask a stupid question, merely to ask her if she was ready for our mission.

But then she looked over her shoulder and smiled radiantly, albeit wearily, at me and gestured for me to catch up.

We were both tired. We had fought almost without pause for days, yet we had more work to do.

While Jani could fly, in a limited fashion, without a set of Angel armour our current mission required more stamina. She spread her wings and activated her jets, just enough to scorch the mud behind her.

I gave her a nod and leaped to the sky, the beautiful Bizhjerai warrior by my side.

“We’re coming, Marissa,” I murmured despite my belief that she was dead. “If you are still alive, hold on.”

###

I woke up at dawn and stretched languidly. Jani heard my movements and looked over her shoulder and graced me with a pretty smile.

“Morning,” she greeted me.

I yawned loudly and shook my head vigorously. “Morning,” I grumbled.

My belly ached and I rummaged around in my pouch and my fingers grazed a single strip of jerky. The last of our provisions.

Two days we had been searching for Marissa, but the area that we had to search was beyond vast. At least the scrying enchantment that Jani had was guiding us in the correct direction.

Marissa was moving, slowly. She was not flying. I believed that she was dead and Zandor was moving her corpse for some morbid reason, but Jani was convinced that she was still alive and staying on the ground to stay hidden.

I tore the piece of jerky in two and offered half of it to Jani. “Hungry?” I asked.

She shook her head slowly, her smile never wavering.

I shook the meat at her. “Don’t lie to me, Jani. You must eat to keep your strength up.”

“I still have some in my pack. I’ll eat later.”

We both knew that she was lying. I could tell with my magesense that she had already finished all of the provisions that she had packed.

I stared at her, frowning, until she sighed and reluctantly accepted the small strip of jerky and chewed on the stringy meat.

Within moments I had gobbled up my breakfast and felt it do nothing at all to alleviate my hunger.

We would have to hunt for food today, which would slow down our search greatly.

I washed down the meat with a swig from my nearly empty canteen.

“Have you scried for Marissa yet?” I asked my pretty companion.

“No,” she admitted and hastily produced the enchantment. She frowned into the blue crystal and I felt a surge of magic.

Instead of shining an arc of power in the direction we should travel, nothing happened.

Jani gasped and nearly dropped the crystal. She shot me a frightened glance.

“It didn’t work!”

I frowned at the idle crystal. “Even if she was dead it should still give a return from her body,” I drawled.

Jani pushed the crystal in my direction. “You try it!”

She was blinking rapidly, fighting back tears at the thought of losing her venerated cousin.

Even though they had not spent much time together, Jani had idolized Marissa all of her life. 

Marissa was larger than life to her.

Even though Jani was stronger than me and thus she would have greater range with her scrying I gave it a try.

Still nothing.

“What do we do?” Jani whined, wringing her hands.

“First of all, calm down,” I told her. She swallowed deeply, nodding. “And then we will keep going in the same direction we went yesterday. She’s not moving quickly, we are far swifter. Perhaps we will get a return once we get closer.

Jani nodded again, magic already oozing from her skin in preparation for flight. She was rearing to go.

I had been hoping to wake up fully before we hit the proverbial road, but such was not my fate this morning. With a sigh we took to the skies and approached the forest near the main Ca’el encampment. Epitome and her army could not be far behind us, a few days at the most. Unless she had been forced to retreat to deal with Zandor and his army of elite soldiers that was behind our lines.

I doubted that even Epitome could defeat him. He seemed like such an unstoppable force that even the vast magic that Epitome had at her disposal would not faze him. We traveled the rest of the day until I sensed a deer in the forest below us. Before Jani could react I was diving, plunging through the branches to drop with both feet first on top of the startled woodland creature. My heavy boots broke its back on impact and it lay bleating in pain for a few moments before my axe parted its neck from its shoulders. Jani dropped down, lightly, next to me and without a word she drew her knife to help me dress the animal.

I liked that she was not afraid of getting her hands dirty. We made camp there and feasted on roasted deer while our armours recharged from the power in the ambient magical field. It was a slow process to recharge, but it could not be helped.

Again we split the night in two halves to keep guard. We were close to the Ca’el lines and patrols could happen through at any moment.

Since I had slept in this morning, I took the first guard and Jani went to sleep curled up to my back, facing the fire. 

If it hadn’t been for our precarious situation or our mission to rescue a dead woman I would have enjoyed this hike with a beautiful and lovely woman immensely.

As it was I had to fight my nerves at every moment.

## CHAPTER FIFTEEN Marissa

It was after me. It never seemed to stop, to rest, and it always found me. I had first spotted it a few hours after I fled from the Ca’el main camp. I had been drained of magical power, weary but triumphant. And then I had sensed it behind me. At first I had believed it was a human, a tracker or assassin sent by the Ca’el to hunt me down. I had waited in ambush and thrown a fireball at it when it drew within range.

It had stared at me with glowing violet eyes.

Mother.

Mother had returned to me, somehow, and had come to my aid.

I had desperately tried to withdraw the fireball, but it had been too late.

I had expected mother to deflect with a casual wave of her hand before giving me an amused smirk.

Instead it hadn’t even flinched and the power that fueled the fireball had been sucked into it.

I only knew one type of creature that could do that. Deoch draoidheil.

That explained the violet eyes. The savages to the north of the Groundlands had had the same type of eyes that mother had had.

But this was obviously not a savage. It had the arms and legs of a human, not the elongated limbs of the deoch draoidheil.

And it wore clothes. I had never seen a deoch draoidheil with anything more than a loincloth, even their females loped around with their tits out.

This one was clad from neck to toe in black leather, reinforced to deflect bladed slashes.

The head was wrapped in a cowl and the face veiled behind thick cloth.

The only visible part of its flesh were those eyes. Eyes that stabbed into my heart with their familiarity.

And then it had hurled my own fireball back at me.

I was so stunned that I barely had time to raise a shield to defend myself.

Deoch draoidheil could not use the magic that they drank. Never in more than thirty years had such an instance been recorded.

This was something else, something more.

That was when I felt a tug on my power, slowly but ever faster draining me of my magic.

I had felt such an aura in my youth. Reavers.

This thing had the powers of the deoch draoidheil as well as the Reavers.

Who the hell had created it? I knew that it was most certainly not Zandor.

It did not matter, for it drew a revolver and shot me.

The first shot struck my flesh and bone hip and caused me to fall. The second and third bullet whistled through the air where my chest had been a moment ago and the fourth would have emptied my brain.

Fire belched from the wound in my hip and I had activated my Angel armour and left the creature far behind.

I had forced the bullet out of my flesh and used Blood Magic to initiate rapid healing.

But the creature had found me again, this time wounding me in the shoulder before I could get away.

I was too weak to fight it hand to hand, fearing that it would be as strong as the deoch draoidheil, and it turned my magic back on me.

So I fled and tried to recover enough strength to fight it.

But whenever I believed that I had managed to secrete myself away or that I had put enough distance between us… It always found me.

That first encounter had been three days ago and I had barely slept for longer than an hour at a time since then. I had long since consumed the provisions that I had pilfered from the camp and my stalker would not let me have enough time to hunt. I had found a few shriveled bushes with sour berries that I had shoved down my throat. But I feared that they were poisonous and they made my belly ache and cold sweats to break out all over my body.

I had thrown up a thin soup of partially digested berries and blue bile and I’d had to suffer through two sessions of explosive bowel purges with panic rising in my throat that my stalker would catch me with my trousers around my ankles. One of the times it almost had and I had barely gotten away. It had appeared right behind me without being visible in my magesense, an impossibility in itself. I had only been warned by the slight rustle of cloth that heralded the sword swinging to claim my head. Since then I had not been able to trust my senses. If it could hide from my magesense, what said that it could not also become entirely invisible?

I stumbled into a large clearing, knowing that my time was up. I could run no further. My Angel jets had failed me just a few moments ago, my power cores exhausted. My crystal skeleton was nearly drained and my body was a shriveled husk from my overuse of Blood Magic. I had burned all of my body fat away, my skin taut around my muscles, my usually full breasts empty flaps of skin crowned by unused milk glands. Even my muscles had weakened but I had stubbornly refused to use them too much to fuel my Blood Magic.

I had nothing left to give, had been given no respite to recuperate enough to fight back.

I realized then that I had made a grave mistake. I should have fought my pursuer when I was still strong, not waste my strength running from it.

Now I was at an even more disadvantageous position.

My hackles rose suddenly and I just knew that my stalker was behind me.

I turned around and true enough, it was there. Calmly watching me with those familiar eyes. I swirled my tongue around my dry mouth in an attempt to wring out some saliva.

“What the fuck are you?” I croaked, at least wanting to know the instrument of my death.

It tilted its head curiously but then it chuckled.

“You have not changed much since last we saw each other,” it responded with a raspy voice. It’s words were shaped oddly, as if it was struggling to form them. “A pity that not all of us could say the same.”

It reached up with gloved hands and pulled back its cowl and lowered its veil.

I recoiled in horror. 

The face that smirked at me had once been human.

Now it had a gray pallor and had a rough texture. The lips were stretched across a nest of sharp teeth that were too many and too large for the human jaws they grew out of. Lank strands of hair poked through a series of bony quills that grew out of the scalp to sweep back across the crown of the head. The features had been twisted almost beyond recognition, but something struck a note of familiarity deep within me.

But I would have remembered encountering this beast before.

I slowly drew my sword.

“Why are you hunting me?”

The woman took a languid step closer with the easy gait of a predator that was not at all worried.

“Revenge,” it whispered and its eyes flared brightly.

“I would have remembered slighting you,” I said, taking a step to the side as we circled each other.

“Of course. The mighty Marissa have forgotten all of those she slew in order to achieve greatness. All those corpses she made and quickly forgot.”

She took a step closer.

“You killed Valia,” it stated with a growl that sent chills down my spine.

“You killed Micah,” she said and increased her pace.

She drew her sword and raised it high.

“You killed Felaan!” She screamed and swung at me.

I raised my sword and blocked hers with a resounding clang, binding it. She snapped at my face with her mouthful of fangs and I finally realized who she had been.

“Wylah,” I breathed, shock stunning me to the core.

She smirked, foetid breath hitting me full in the face.

“I’m glad that you have not forgotten all of your friends,” she snarled and somehow managed to get her leg in between us to give me a firm kick in the lower belly.

I stumbled back, keeping my sword up in a guard.

“What happened to you?” I gasped as she advanced again.

We exchanged a flurry of blows, her blade coming inches within the gaps in my tattered armour.

“You happened,” she sneered.

She swung again and I nearly reached out with what little magic I had left to snag her sword and run her through. But she would just have eaten my magic and turned it against me like she had so many times before.

Instead I drew the Ca’el revolver I had taken and jammed it into her belly and squeezed the trigger in rapid succession.

Wylah grunted and jerked with each bullet that ripped through her abdomen and then blinding pain shot up my left arm. 

I fell to the ground with a scream, cradling the stump of my arm, already steaming as my caustic blood sealed the wound.

Wylah smirked at the sight. “Ever the resourceful bitch. Good. I don’t want you to die too quickly. You will suffer for what you did to Felaan. What you did to me.”

“I didn’t do anything to either of you,” I sputtered through the pain. “The savages killed Felaan! I wasn’t even with you. You saw them kill him!”

“I did,” she said wistfully. “But it was your fault that we were even there in the first place.”

She squatted next to me and pulled her own revolver. She placed the muzzle against my leg and I tried to scramble away. I screamed when I accidentally put my weight on the stump of my arm, already forgotten that I had lost the limb. I fell back to the ground, panting with pain.

“When the Bizhjerai came and blocked off the valley mouth I knew that there would be no escape south. So I fled north, into the caves. When I emerged after the soldiers you left behind left, there you were.”

Her voice wavered as she recounted the painful memories. “I was too weak to fight you head on. You had already constructed defenses that were too powerful for me. For years I clawed survival from that valley, fighting the savages whenever they found me. Ate their flesh when nothing else was available.”

She gestured at her twisted body. “It slowly turned me into this. A blessing in disguise.”

“Blessing?” I could barely get the word out.

“Blessing. For I learned that as my body changed, I gained some of their abilities, their strength. Whatever had turned them into what they are transferred to me. I became immune to magic, could twist it for my own purposes.”

She smiled widely. “I became the perfect instrument for destroying Mages such as you. An anti-mage, if you will. You created the instrument of your death!”

She squeezed the trigger. The shot punched into my prosthetic leg, shattering it.

Wylah blinked in surprise when I did not cry out in pain. She extended her hand and poked my other leg and grinned when her finger made contact with soft flesh.

“If at first you don’t succeed,” she murmured and shifted her pistol to press it into my thigh.

“Wylah…” I breathed and she refocused her attention on my face.

“And there it is. Beg for your life, Marissa. Beg like so many Skylanders did just before you had your mongrels slaughter them in their homes.

I gasped in a deep breath while Wylah waited expectantly. “I’m sorry for what happened to you,” I managed to get out. “I never wanted to hurt you, or Zandor, Felaan and Valia. I hoped that after… We could retreat to Dun Traelak, to have a life together.”

“Either you are a fool for believing that, or you are lying. It does not matter. I finally have you at my mercy, and you will not escape it!”

A loud and high-pitched screech rapidly approached and Wylah spun around. Her finger tightened on the trigger but her motion had pulled her weapon to the side.

Instead of the bullet shattering my femur it instead tore through my thigh.

Barengar and Jani dropped from the sky, a powerful blast of magic already emerging from my young cousin.

Barengar’s axe embedded itself in Wylah’s chest with a solid thunk and she stumbled back a step with a surprised grunt. And then, shockingly, she stood up straight again.

“Jani, don’t!” I tried to scream the warning, but only a groan escaped my throat.

Wylah grinned as the spell approached her, and then it winked out of existence and her eyes flared brightly.

Then she threw the spell back at Jani. I saw my cousin’s sudden expression of shock and surprise before she was hurled back, tumbling head over heels across the grass.

“Jani!” Barengar screamed and the pain and fury in his voice cut me to my soul.

Barengar was almost upon Wylah and she raised her pistol to shoot him. His shield deployed in the nick of time to absorb the shot and then he recalled the axe to his hand. It wrenched Wylah to the side before it came loose and her next two shots went wide. She only had a single shot left and she pulled it back in line.

Barengar charged up a spell and Wylah smirked, waiting for him to throw it so that she could kill him with his own magic, just like she had done with Jani.

Her hubris became her downfall for Barengar kept running and dismissed his spell, it having served its purpose as a distraction. His axe lashed out and severed Wylah’s arm at the shoulder, the blade biting into her ribs.

She blinked in surprise, clearly not having anticipated that his spell had been a feint. Barengar ripped his axe out of her ribs and deflected her sword with his shield arm. He did not even bother deploying the shield, he just knocked her sword to the side. He rammed the spike of his axe into her belly and forced her back, away from me and forced her onto her back. He pulled his axe back and Wylah immediately tried to rise but failed when her severed arm was not there to prop her up.

With a roar Barengar swung his axe with both hands and severed the head of my old friend. Her head rolled to a stop at my feet, fanged jaw forming words without sound, her shining eyes glaring balefully at me.

And then the light faded and winked out.

As did the light inside my own head.

###

I woke up with a deep inhalation, my mind rousing quickly and my magesense spreading out.

The first thing I noticed was the man standing beside my bed. For a moment I was ready to fight, muscles tense and spells boiling beneath my skin.

But then I recognized him. Barengar. I relaxed instantly and turned my attention to my immediate surroundings. A stone room, but there were something peculiar about the walls.

Materialized magic shot through the stone walls in neat lines. Plumbing.

I knew where we were. Dun Traelak. Home.

Even though I had not lived here for decades, this would always be my home. The place closest to my heart..

Barengar must have brought me here after he killed Wylah and had the healers work on my body. I slowly opened my eyes and found him staring down at me.

“Thank you,” I whispered.

He did not respond for a long while and when he spoke his voice was thick, as if he had not been using it for a long while.

“I thought that Zandor killed you.”

I blinked and shifted my crystal vision to look at his aura. 

Relief was paramount within it, with traces of hope and despair. He had truly believed that I had died.

“He damn near did,” I croaked. 

Barengar jerked and hastened to bring me water which he helped me drink.

Why was his aura suffused with despair?

A memory flashed past my minds eye. Jani casting a powerful spell only to have Wylah turn it back on her.

That’s why. I had noticed how they looked upon one another, something was definitely going on between them.

Or had been going on between them. It pained me greatly that she had died, even though we had only met a handful of times. 

She had always been a happy child, compassionate and affectionate and a very skilled and powerful Mage.

And now she was dead, because I had led her astray.

I closed my eyes and fought back the tears. It felt as if I had done little else but fighting grief since mother sacrificed herself to awaken Epitome.

First Kendall, then mother and now Jani. The people I cared about kept dying, one by one.

A movement by the far wall caught the attention of my magesense and I zoomed over there, wishing for anything to distract my maudlin thoughts.

I opened my eyes and twisted my head.

“Hey, cousin,” Jani murmured from across the narrow aisle between our beds.

“Jani! You’re alive!”

“Uh huh,” she responded weakly. “That thing threw my spell back at me but I managed to shield most of it. I’ll be right as rain in no time. How are you feeling?”

I took stock of my body and found that I had almost no aches. I was still malnourished and underweight, but my hurts were gone. I felt too good.

“I’m fine. Famished, but fine. What happened?”

Barengar cleared his throat. “You passed out after I killed that thing and I carried you both until I came across a squad of Epitomes. She summoned Archangels to bring you back here for healing.”

“How long have I been out?”

“Three days. You were so depleted of power that your Soul could not recharge itself. I just gave you a magic transfusion to wake you up.”

That would explain why I felt fine. I still needed a lot of time, or access to a source of magical power, to recharge but I would recover. 

I always did.

The enticing smell of roasted meat reached my nostrils just a moment before a pair of maids arrived with two trays laden with food. The female maid wordlessly laid the tray across my legs and offered me a kind smile before silently retreating. The male guard treated Jani the same but the smile he offered her was much more interested.

Until he heard Barengar’s deep growl behind him. The man blanched and beat a hasty retreat from the room.

I suppressed a chuckle at Barengar's jealousy and he gave me an odd glance as if he did not understand what I was laughing at. I ignored him and started eating.

After scarfing down a couple of mouthfuls I spoke.

“How goes the war?” I asked with a gesture that Barengar should sit down.

He sat on the edge of Jani’s bed, his elbow naturally resting across her knees and his hand dangling so that his fingertips grazed the side of her thigh.

Jani smiled radiantly at him as she chewed a piece of bread. Barengar did not seem to notice her happy reaction to his touch.

I would have to beat him over the head and stuff him into her bed if he was going to keep up being this ignorant. Perhaps I should take Jani on as my second apprentice.

Having them both cooped up close together at my workshop for months on end might force them to deal with whatever was blossoming between them.

“Epitome is pushing forward hard. After the main trenches were broken the Ca’el has not been able to dig down properly. Hundreds of avatars are created each day and ferried to the front lines by her Skyships.”

He let out a slow breath before continuing.

“There is no way the Ca’el can compete with that kind of reinforcements or logistics. Epitome does not sleep, does not rest. Her advance is implacable and relentless.”

“She gets that from mother,” I smiled proudly.

“I suppose she does,” he eventually agreed.

“What about Zandor and his army that broke through. Has Anjali engaged them?”

“That’s the thing. She can’t find them. There are plenty of tracks and discarded camps but then… Nothing.”

“That’s impossible,” Jani protested. “You can’t hide a thousand men that easily.”

“Obfuscation magic,” I mused thoughtfully.

“What’s that?” Barengar frowned. “I’ve never heard of that kind of magic.”

“Kendall told me about it many years ago. It was something that he and Mathias Varl had researched back when Uncle Kenny was still his apprentice on the Skylands. A method of either confounding the senses of your opponents or rendering oneself invisible by fucking around with light.”

“Fucking around with light?” Barengar asked incredulously.

“Yup. Everything we see is apparently light reflecting of an object. Bend that light around yourself and whoever is looking at you will only see what’s behind you.”

“I’ll take your word for it,” he responded and rubbed at his eyes with the back of his hand.

“They wanted to create a better assassin, one that would be undetectable, but they never managed to figure it out and gave up to move onto more promising prospects.”

“And Varl taught Zandor,” Barengar finished my train of thought.

“Precisely. Perhaps he managed to figure it out in the years after Kendall left his apprenticeship, or during the war or the decade that followed before he finally died.”

“Why would Zandor want to hide his movements? If he could strike Anjali without her seeing him coming, or taking out one of Epitome’s factories…”

“I don’t know why. I don’t even know if that is what he is doing. This is pure speculation.”

“He might be awaiting reinforcements before making his move,” Barengar mused.

“Reinforcements?” Jani queried, a cute frown on her forehead. “The Ca’el are finally being pushed back. They can’t spare the men to punch through Epitome.”

“Actually, Jani, that is not completely true,” Barengar said slowly. “Just before I came here to wake Marissa up Marie had just received word from Epitome.”

Both Jani and I sat up straight at that announcement.

“What did she say?” My young cousin exclaimed impatiently when Barengar offered nothing further.

“There has been several breaches of the advance. Two of them completely circumvented the advancing troops while two of them actually managed to push through. Not without severe casualties, though. A fifth contingent was completely decimated to a man.”

“Five breaches,” I murmured, my hackles rising. I had killed three out of eight of Zandor's bitches. That left five.

“What are you thinking?” Barengar asked me, knowing me well enough to know when I was having a suspicion.

He leaned forward toward me, his hand sliding off Jani’s thigh. My cousin slumped and pouted but neither of us paid any attention to her.

“During our most recent fight with Zandor I made a discovery. The source of his vast power.”

Now I had both of their undivided attention. “This eye,” I started to explain and tapped my temple. “It allows me to see spectra that not even our magesenses will reveal. I had to shift through many different layers but then I found something. Something similar to artificial ley lines, but different. Eight of them, to be precise.”

Barengar stared at me, not comprehending the implication. Of course, he had no idea that I had found and slain three of the whores.

“He was bound to eight powerful Mages, all of them supplying him with significant power.”

Jani’s eyes widened. “That would make him nearly unstoppable!”

Barengar held up a hand. “That would make him powerful, yes. But there is no way that his body could withstand to channel that amount of power if it was not his to begin with. He’d burn, become an Archon.”

I raised my eyebrow at him. “Just like I do every time I exceed my own limits?”

Jani gave me a surprised look for she had never seen me do anything of the sort. Barengar had.

“You’re cheating,” he scoffed as it was no big deal.

“Cheating?” Jani asked curiously but her question went unheeded.

“Yes. If I can do it, why can’t Zandor?”

“But he’d burn,” Barengar persisted.

“We have observed that he did not. I don’t know how he does it, perhaps it is part of the enchantment that binds his bitches to him, or perhaps it is something else. It does not matter. We have seen that he can do it and we must act accordingly.”

“Bitches?” Jani interjected. “How do you know that they are women?”

“The three that I found all were, and they were all beautiful and powerful. I know the man.” I looked away, unable to face their scrutiny as I confessed. “I was his lover once, decades ago when we were still youths.”

Jani gasped and covered her mouth with her hand and I awaited her condemnation. That I had lain with the enemy, gobbling on his cock as if it was the only thing that kept me alive.

“That’s so sad!” Jani exclaimed and suddenly surged out of her bed, practically throwing herself across the small distance between us to envelop me in a hug. She got tangled in the sheet that was pinned beneath Barengar and lay sprawled, the night shirt that was all she wore riding up her hips to expose her bare bottom. I wrapped my arms around her and saw Barengar appreciating the fine piece of ass proffered at him.

He caught me seeing him and he turned his head away, blushing. I couldn’t help but smirk.

They should just fuck and get it over with.

“Jani,” I drawled slowly.

“Mhm,” she murmured into my neck, still clutching me tightly.

“Your ass is exposed.”
