To know that mother protected me, and that mom protected mother in turn and with my giant dragon brother watching over all of us.

But my family was dead and only I yet drew breath.

Perhaps I could create a spell that would keep anyone else from suffering my fate.

I would.

For aeons humanity had believed in an afterlife, nearly every culture and religion throughout history that I had found in the archives and legends had believed in reincarnation or a blissful afterlife.

Some more spiteful religions had even believed in eternities of torment for those judged apostates.

Humanity had yearned for meaning beyond this life since times immemorial. Something within most of us despaired at the thought of death.

I could deliver on that yearning. It might take years, decades even, but it was something that I could do. I felt it, deep inside my crystal bones.

But I could do nothing from this bed. I had to be able to get out of here on my own or I would never be able to help mankind.

“Mother, help me,” I pleaded.

Too late I remembered that it was not mother by my bedside, but Epitome.

“While you have been unconscious I have prepared a solution for you, Marissa. I saw the state of your body when you were recovered from the wall and I suspected that you would be weak upon awakening. It is not the best of solutions, but it will suffice until we figure out something better.”

My curiosity was piqued and I opened my eye again and stared with suspicion at her avatar.

“Are you suggesting to chop off my limbs and replace them with prosthetics?”

I wouldn’t mind too much if that was the plan. I did not need my actual body parts if they could be replaced with improved magical constructs.

“I do not believe that your body can survive the shock of such extensive surgeries in your current condition. I fashioned something similar to my own avatars. Perhaps it would be best if I demonstrated?”

I nodded weakly, eager to get out of the sick bed and back to work.

The door opened and something large was carried in by six combat avatars. It looked like a bulky corpse, a dead avatar.

A sleek, fully enclosing plate crystal suit of armour was deposited on the floor and somehow it remained standing after the combat avatars retreated from the room.

“What is this?” I asked Epitome.

“It is an avatar, but it is inside out with enough room for you on the inside.”

“An exoskeleton?”

Epitome frowned for a moment before she nodded with a faint smile. “An apt description,” she conceded and pulled a spell core out of a pouch attached to the belt that circled her narrow waist. “The control nexus,“ she explained and offered it to me.

I nodded and managed to twist my head to expose the side of my head that was hairless. The shattered crystals had been plucked from my skull but had not been replaced by new ones yet. Epitome gently pushed the control nexus into one of the available sockets. It didn’t hurt as much as when a fresh core was installed since the indenture in my skull was already present but the core still had to seal itself in place and extend the long crystal filaments into my brain. Saying that it hurt less than usual didn’t mean much since it was one of the most painful things you could endure. It still hurt like a motherfucker but my body was too weak to scream.

The smell of burned crystal and skin reached my nostrils before the pain abruptly faded.

“The core has already been imbued with the control schematics extrapolated from your hip, though I advice you to be cautious for the first few weeks as it calibrates the learning matrix.”

“Yeah, yeah,” I grumbled as if I was a child being scolded by her mother.

Which in a way was the exact situation.

“Now, how do I use this thing?”

“Let me help you,” Epitome smiled and carefully slid her crystal arms beneath my shoulders and knees.

She easily lifted me off the bed and approached the exoskeleton. It hissed and opened up like the petals of a flower, the back parts opening to accept an occupant.

“Did you lift that design from the Archangel?”

“I did,” Epitome responded shamelessly. She carefully guided my feet into the sabatons and I noted with wry amusement that the heels were very high and very thin. Just like the type of heeled boots that mother had favored and that I never had had the innate grace to be able to wear.

Epitome must have learned of my chagrin over that fact through mothers memories that now coursed through her memory cores. In fact, one might argue that it was mother that had designed these sabatons as a gift for me, that I would finally be able to walk in her shoes.

A silent tear dripped down my cheek, yet my chest was warm with love for mother. Even in death she cared deeply for me and reached beyond the grave to make her comforting presence known.

Epitome’s strong hands kept me propped up as the armour closed around my legs and she guided my hands and forearms into the gauntlets before the arms and back closed around me. I was nestled in a comfortable cocoon of materialized magic.

“What’s with the ridiculously thick gorget?” I asked Epitome with a glance down at the mass of crystal around my neck.

“I have noticed that you are sometimes in the midst of danger without a helmet and it is somewhat slow to materialize from your usual choker. There’s a helmet in the gorget that can snap up around your head at a moments notice.”

“Kind of like Barengar’s shield,” I commented with a narrowed squint at Epitome.

“Why should I invent something new when you have already provided me with similar designs?” She smiled back as she stepped back and admired her handiwork. “Try to connect to the armour,” she prompted.

It was a spell that I had cast a countless times before and it easily flowed out of me and the armour whirred and twitched each limb as it ran some sort of diagnostic spell. I raised my arm and the armour responded and my arm raised smoothly, almost as if it had been my own strength that powered it. I made my fingers wave and curl and found the armour to be almost as articulated as my actual fingers.

“How do I look? Is there a mirror?”

I already knew that this room lacked a mirror but Epitome solved it by casting a spell that warped the light in front of me to cast it back into my eye. The reflection was even better than an illusion and I was suddenly staring into my own eye.

The armour was sleek and did not strike me as as bulky as I had originally perceived it to be. Either the machinery inside was very well designed to fit into such a small space or my body really was that atrophied and broken that it did not take up as much volume as I had thought. The armour was vaguely shaped after my original body with accentuated hips and a trim waist. The breastplate bulged as if hiding prodigious breasts with just a slight hint of the forms underneath. It was a lie though. My body was just a bundle of crystal wrapped up in skin and atrophied muscle. But I could move again and my magic was as strong and quick as ever. I just needed to recharge my crystal skeleton, replace my eye and the missing power cores on the side of my head and I would be ready to continue my research.

“Thank you, Epitome. From the bottom of my heart.”

“It was my pleasure, Marissa. It broke my heart to see you so weak and broken. I had to help.”

I stared into her eyes and opened my arms. Epitome flowed into them easily and wrapped her crystal arms around me and I draped my armoured arms around her shoulders.

It was a little strange hugging a crystal avatar that lacked the sensation of touch, especially since I was encased in armour and could not really feel the hug either but it was more a matter of the sentiment of the hug that was important. It felt like hugging mother once again.

“How much of mother is in you?” I asked silently before I knew that the question had even been on my mind.

Epitome pushed back and looked down into my eyes.

“All of her,” she whispered sincerely. “She is not aware, can’t be aware, but she is there. All that made her what she was is in me, filling the very core of my being. Her memories, her love for you. Her grief for Red and Mal, Alissa and Kendall…” A shining tear dripped down her cheek and she reached up her hand to wipe it away even though it was just an illusion that she could have willed away.

“In many ways, I have become your mother for I love you like she did. I yearn to protect the Bizhjerai and see them flourish. I’d kill everyone else if I had to as long as it would keep them, and you, safe.”

My vision blurred and blinked rapidly.

“Mother?” I squeaked.

“Yes, little one. I am here.”

I crushed Epitome to me and cried. Cried in grief for what had been lost, and in relief that mother was still with me in some senses.

Epitome stroked my hair and waited patiently.

## CHAPTER EIGHTEEN Barengar

I looked up from the book I was reading when the door was pushed open and Marissa strode into the room.

My eyes widened at the sight of her.

She was emaciated, her face thin and her cheeks sunken and hollow. Her crystal eye was a sunken mess that stared sightlessly at me, the eyelid removed completely and it constantly wept due to her inability to blink.

Her body was encased in a sleek and vaguely feminine armour that glowed brightly in my magesense.

“You look like shit,” I greeted her with a wry grin and stood up awkwardly.

She peered up at me through narrowed eye.

“You look surprisingly spry for someone who was more dead than alive the last time I saw you,” she responded sourly.

Then her lips spread into a grin and she stepped close and wrapped her arms around my waist.

I awkwardly patted her back.

“I’m glad that you are still alive, Barengar.”

“Yeah. Me too,” I muttered and was a bit surprised that she did not let me go and back away.

She had never been one to show anyone physical affection and I wondered if this was because of her near brush with death.

She had faced death many times in her life, so I did not believe that that was the case.

Eventually she did step back and her eye was locked onto my chest. Her gauntleted fingers traced my chest.

“Your wound… I would not have believed that they would be able to heal you if the proof was not standing before me.”

I tapped my chest. “Replaced my breastbone with materialized crystal. I think they got that idea from you.”

A grin flickered across her withered features. “Quite possibly,” she smirked and then her face returned to a pensive expression.

“What are you thinking?” I asked her, having seen that expression many a times before. Each time she had gotten the both of us in a heap of trouble.

“Epitome has asked me for a favour,” Marissa responded, clearly being coy with me.

“What does she want?” I asked with a resigned sigh.

Epitome was capable. If she wanted our help it was bound to be dangerous.

“She was built for a very specific purpose. To rule fairly. But she has no dominion, currently. The war with the Ca’el is going well for her and she believes that she will have subdued the Empire before the year is up.”

My eyebrows rose in surprise. The empire was vast and powerful beyond belief. But Epitome was implacable, relentless and had in all ways that mattered infinite power.

“Yet that is a war of subjugation, of conquest. Any rule imposed under such conditions can’t, by definition, be fair. So she has asked me to liaise with the Clans.”

“Because they trust you more than her,” I finished her statement.

“Because they trust me,” she nodded.

“Will they listen to you?”

“Perhaps. But I doubt it,” Marissa admitted.

I mulled the situation over. “As I see it, no one will submit to her rule willingly.”

Her gaze snapped to mine, eye narrowed. “You believe her unfit to rule?” She asked with a dangerous tone to her voice.

I raised my hands in a placating gesture. “On the contrary. It is the state of humanity I doubt.”

Her eye narrowed further. “Please, elaborate.”

“Alright,” I agreed. “Imagine a selfish and corrupt ruler. They will not relinquish control. That would be counter productive for their own agenda. Agreed?”

She snapped a single nod and her eye flashed with anger.

“Now, imagine a benevolent and kind ruler,” I continued. “They would also not relinquish control to Epitome, for why would they? They are a kind and benevolent ruler and their people prosper. Why risk that for a hypothetical?”

Marissa nodded slowly before she let out a deep sigh. “I share your sentiment, Barengar. But we have to try. What else can we do?”

I shrugged. “Honestly? I don’t have a fucking clue. Not if we want to do this peacefully, but no change ever comes peacefully. It is always accompanied by blood and death.”

Marissa rubbed at her eye with her gauntleted hand. “We can’t force Epitome on the people. That would be counter to her very purpose. If we impose her rule onto the unwilling… How would that make us better than the tyrants?”

“Because it would be for the greater good in the long run,” I stated firmly.

“That’s what all tyrants believe, at first.”  
“I don’t believe that,” I maintained adamantly.

Marissa gave me a surprised look. “When did you gain such faith in humanity, Barengar? Out of the two of us, you were always the idealist?”

It was my turn to be surprised. “Me? An idealist? What kind of herbs have they been giving you, Marissa? I’ve always been a cynical bastard, you’re the idealist!”

We stared at one another for a long while. 

“Come on,” I continued, “you’re the one with the grand plan to provide power to everyone. To usher in a future where no one suffers.”

“You don’t believe in my vision,” she said, wounded.

“I do believe in your vision. I just don’t believe that humanity is capable of existing without suffering. Everyone suffers, all the time. Even the tyrants that you hate so much. We are incapable of happiness. We will always find something to be unhappy about. That is the fundamental curse of being human.”

Marissa was silent for a long time, wilting under my stare. “Do you remember a time where you were happy? Absolutely happy with no worries of the future?”

Her silence was all the response I needed and I nodded resolutely with vindication.

“I have not had a normal life, Barengar. I was born into a dystopian society where the poor was murdered and literally eaten by the rich. I was then transferred into an active war zone that killed my parents.”

I scoffed. “How is that different from almost everyone? Suffering is universal and there is nothing we can do about it,” I said with conviction.

“So we should just do nothing? Sit back and just let everyone suffer?” Marissa asked and her lost expression almost broke my heart.

“Of course not,” I said gently. “I don’t believe that we can be successful in the way that you envision, that everyone will be happy all the time. But we can do something to at least reduce the pain. That’s gotta be worth something. Isn’t it?”

She nodded slowly.

“But you will not achieve your vision without suffering. Change is suffering, even when the change is for the better. A generation from now… People might thrive under Epitome.”

Marissa was silent for a long time, her one remaining eye staring into the distance without seeing. Eventually she shuddered and let out a sharp breath.

“Perhaps you are right, but I pray to the Spirits that you are not.”

It was the first time ever that I had heard her mentioning the Spirits as if she actually believed in them. It made my hackles rise.

“Let us go and talk to Marie and see.”

I nodded and extended my hand to let her lead the way.

We found Marie in the throne room, talking to one of Epitome’s combat avatars. Marie was a striking woman on the older side.

She had clearly once been very pretty, and still was even though the had not been able to use Blood Magic to slow down her aging to the same extent that Marissa had.

Even though Marie was about a decade older than Marissa, she looked as if she was at least thirty years older.

Her cheeks were still smooth though her mouth and eyes were lined and her eyes were haunted.

Her breasts sagged and her waist was thick after having given birth to five children and her hair was more gray than brown.

Despite all of that I still would not kick her out of my bed if I had found her there. I had not seen many seventy-odd old women that I would not mind bedding but Marie was certainly one of those.

The fact that she could probably kick my ass in combat only enhanced that sentiment. I may be younger, stronger and taller but she had almost fifty years of experience on me.

Experience was sometimes worth more than the vigor of youth.

Marie finished her discussion with the Avatar and it stepped back and somehow I knew that Epitome had withdrawn her consciousness from it. It was still there, but it seemed to fade slightly in my magesense as if it was just another part of the furnishing of the chamber

The Chief of the Unified Clans turned to face us as we approached.

“Marissa,” Marie greeted my mistress with barely contained disdain and a hint of old affection. She did not deign to acknowledge me at all.

The two women had an interesting relationship. Marie had adored Marissa when she was a child and had taken her under her wing when Marissa’s parents had died.

Yet when Marissa spurned her people and left to found the school something had changed.

Marie hated what Marissa had become yet loved the child she had been and she respected the skills and inventions that Marissa had produced.

I did not believe that Marie would act against Marissa, in fact she had proved that she would go out of her way to help her. But I did not think that she would voluntarily risk herself or her people to do so.

Marissa bowed deeply. “Chief,” she responded before slowly straightening up. She cast an annoyed glance over her shoulder at me when I remained standing straight.

I grunted and inclined my head. She was not my Chief and she had not earned my respect. Marissa was my mistress, no one else was.

“Thank you for you aid against the invaders, Marissa. And thank you for bringing Epitome, without her we would still be locked in a stalemate against the Ca’el,” Marie said with a grateful smile that did not quite reach her eyes.

“I’d have thought that such a monumental aid would have earned her more friendliness from her own people,” I could not help to say while staring down at Marie.

The Chief was a tall woman, but I was almost freakishly tall and could loom over almost anyone. So far I had only met a single person that had been taller than I was since I stopped growing. Zandor. And he was dead now, shot through the head by Marissa. While a skilled Blood Mage could recover from almost any wound, a destroyed brain was not one of them. He was dead and gone, and good riddance.

Marie gave me a cold glare that told me unequivocally and without words that I should stay out of Clan business.

The Chief returned her attention to Marissa.

“While it is good to see you back on your feet, and I appreciate the sacrifice you have made for our Clan,” Marie began with a pointed look at Marissa’s powered armour that was the only thing that let made it possible for her to walk in her current condition. “This is the first time in nearly twenty years that you have approached me to talk. What do you want?”

Straight to the point. I appreciated that in a woman.

And in men for that matter. I had little patience for idle chatter.

“Perhaps I should start at the beginning,” Marissa said before drawing a deep breath.

Then she told Marie everything. Everything pertaining to Epitome. Why she was created, what Marissa hoped that she would become and how she had envisioned how Epitome could be adjusted to align to the current zeitgeist of human morality. Marie’s face twisted in disgust when Marissa told her how she had the ability to consume and digest Souls and their core values but her face softened when Marissa told her how Kyra had sacrificed herself to sway Epitome to save the Bizhjerai by ending the war.

“By the Spirits,” Marie said as tears started to stream down her face. “She actually did that?” she asked, aghast.

Marissa nodded, her cheeks equally wet.

“She loved Anjali,” Marie murmured. “I can’t believe that she would forsake the afterlife with the love of her life to save us…”

Marissa nodded vehemently. “She did. And that is why I ask you first.”

Marie’s eyes snapped back to Marissa’s single remaining one, her features suddenly blank.

She knew what Marissa was about to ask her.

“I ask the Clans to submit to Epitome. To let her rule fairly and in accordance with the values of the Clans. I ask of you to let Epitome prove herself as a ruler so that the Bizhjerai might serve as an example of her capability and sway other regions and peoples to accept her.”

Marie was silent for a long while, her stare hard.

“And what happens to the values of the Clans when other regions, other cultures, start to affect her?”

“Her rule will shift to accommodate all,” Marissa stated with only a hint of insecurity in her voice.

“Which will in turn diminish the values of the Clans. What we value, the Union of Cities does not. In time we will all lose what we are. Not only Bizhjerai, Azwaili and Ithlaki but even the Union of Cities will lose what they are.”

“But all will prosper and all will have peace. War will be futile when Epitome can crush any violent uprising. Everyone will be forced to sort our their differences peacefully!”

“Yet we will not be what we are, Marissa. It is a noble vision that you have, I will give you that, but you are asking everyone to give up what they are. What they believe in with all of their being…”

Marie drew a shaking breath before her gaze hardened. “People with conflicting ideals can not coexist. Not without compromising what they are. And I will not forsake the very essence of the Clans for your vision. I once loved the child that you were. Your mothers were the sisters that I never had. Kyra was my commander. My friend. She taught me most of what I know today. Anjali was my Chief, my idol. Malkareth was my companion that brought me into battle countless times. Yet you turned your back on us. You refused to help your people in our time of need. I respect who you have become and I want to believe in you, for your mothers sake. For both of theirs sake. But I will not compromise the Bizhjerai, not even for the daughter of Bás Marbhadair.”

I could tell from her tone that her word had been final. She would not budge and I respected her for that even though I had wished that she would do what Marissa had asked of her.

Marissa’s shoulder slumped. Without the Bizhjerai to lead by example the chance that the Union of Cities would follow were slim to none.

Even if the Ca’el prospered under Epitome’s rule the shadow of how she had come to rule them would forever cast doubt in the eyes of everyone else.

I knew that the only way for Epitome to rule was by violence and blood. She had to wrest control from the current leadership. A time of suffering would follow.

The people would rebel and hate her. But their children, and their children’s children, they would live prosperous and with as much liberty and freedom that Epitome could provide.

And if they were so discontent with the way that Epitome ruled… They could always give their Souls to her, to plant a tree in whose shade they would never sit.

Marissa suddenly stood straight and stared Marie in the eye.

“I understand, Chief Bizhjerai. I wish you well in your future rule. Farewell.”

She did not wait for Marie to answer and she spun on her heel and walked away. I hastened to follow. Behind us I could sense Marie sit down heavily on her throne and bury her face in her hands. She was not crying, but she was rubbing her eyes with the heels of her palms.

I opened my mouth to speak but something in the set of my mistress’ shoulders warned me that she would not receive my words well.

The conversation with Marie had somewhat verified my earlier words and Marissa would not appreciate it if I rubbed it in her face. So I followed her in silence and was not the least surprised when she led us to our Archangels. Epitome was there in her avatar that was wrapped in illusion to make her appear human. I had come to think of it as her diplomatic avatar, designed to put humans at ease.

Epitome did not speak but her shoulders did slump when she saw our sour expressions. Marissa hopped into her Archangel and the machine hungrily gobbled her up.

Epitome hastened to mount her own and I took a moment to take in Dun Traelak for what might have been the final time.

With Kendall dead and Marissa’s relationship with Marie somewhat sour I saw no reason that we would return here short of another invasion.

Almost all of the people that had bound Marissa to this place was dead, only Kendall’s widower remained. To my knowledge they had never been that close, not close enough to warrant a visit just for him.

My only regret in the situation was that I did not know when I would meet Jani again. But I knew that I would meet her again. Nothing could stop me from seeing her.

Nothing.

###

Our Archangels touched down in the very same place that we had landed the last time that we had visited Lahar. It had been only months ago but so much had happened since then that it felt as if it had been years ago since that assassin had fired upon us. Just as last time there were no guards here as our arrival had not been announced.

After the manner that we had left the Council they must have expected us not to come back since we had not parted on the best of terms.

I could sense at the edge of my magesense that palace guards were rushing toward us even as the Archangels spat us out onto the meticulously cut grass.

I expected this meeting to end at least as badly as the one we had had with Marie just a few hours ago. Possibly even worse since the Cities held no long-standing respect for the daughter of Bás Marbhadair. If anything, they actively disliked her. 

Marissa did not wait for the guards to greet us and she marched without hesitation into the palace that housed the people that had tried to have had her killed.

I exchanged a glance with Epitome before we both hurried to catch up to her. At the edge of my magesense I glimpsed a familiar form rush to intercept us and I felt my upper lip peel back in a snarl. Talmud.

Marissa must have sensed him as well for she took a turn that would lead us away from him but would not steer us too far away from the route to the Council Chambers.

I did not understand why she even bothered given how much trouble it had been to get the Council to convene just two days early the last time. As far as I knew there were no Council meeting scheduled for this week even.

On the other hand, there might be. I knew very little about the proceedings and customs of the Council of the Union of Cities.

Somehow, despite our little detour, Talmud managed to intercept us and he halted before us, blocking our way. To my slight amusement her was breathing heavily and his face was slightly flushed from his exertion.

I could not help but snort derisively at his weak constitution. This was truly a man who had never done a hard day of work in his life.

“Lady Marissa,” he began with a thin veneer of civility draped over a core of disdain. “This is most unseemly to show up unannounced.”

“I have news for the Council, Talmud. Get out of my way,” she responded with the lack of tact that she seemed to reserve for the Council.

“Does these news pertain to your, ah, condition?” He asked after his eyes had taken in her sunken cheeks, missing eye and odd armour.

Marissa rolled her eye and dodged past the man. Talmud sputtered indignantly as I swept past him as well, making sure that my shoulder slammed into his. He stumbled and nearly fell before he righted himself and caught up.

“The Council is in session, Marissa, and the agenda has been set a week ago. We simply do not have time for your games. The war to the north takes up all of our time.”

“How goes the war, Talmud?” Marissa asked with a suddenly sweet voice that immediately made my hackles rise.

“It goes well, Lady Marissa. We have several times pushed the Ca’el forces back from Union lands.” He gave Marissa sidelong glance. “I am surprised that the mighty Bizhjerai have failed to repel the Ca’el for years when we have been able to do it in months,” he said smugly. “Perhaps the Bizhjerai has been overestimated all these years.”

I bristled at that. What the Union had faced were small raiding parties probing alternate routes around the trenches.

Did this arrogant little fuck truly believe that they had faced the full might of the Ca’el empire and prevailed?

He really was delusional if that was the case.

Marissa stared straight ahead with a blank expression.

“The tidings I bear are of the war, Talmud.”

That shut him up and placed a worried frown on his face.

He actually managed to be silent all the way until we approached the Council doors. He opened his mouth but I cleared my throat loudly and shook my head when he glanced in my direction. Something in my expression must have convinced him that it would be a mistake to speak and he closed his mouth with an audible snap of his teeth.

The doors opened with a flex of Marissa’s magic and we strode into the Council chamber.

The speaker, one of the Councilors whose name I did not know, trailed off and gave us a disgruntled look when we interrupted him.

The head of the Council, Councilor Ira, rubbed at his eyes.

“You had one job, Talmud,” he said wearily.

“She’s not welcome here any longer,” another of the Councilors complained. “She made it quite clear that she was done with us,” he spat maliciously.

“Guards!” Another of the Councilors cried out and I sensed movement beyond the closed doors.

In an instant my axe was in my hands and I assumed a combat stance. Marissa stood still, uncaring about the mass of troops outside.

“I bring news about the war,” she announced calmly and her low voice cut through the chamber.

“Then say it, and go,” Ira conceded.

“The Ca’el are defeated. Their army is broken and their lands are being subjugated as we speak. The current estimation is that the entire empire will no longer be under imperial control within months and complete subjugation should be achieved by the end of the year.”

“Preposterous,” a fat Councilor sputtered. I recognized him as the same Councilor that had withheld military aid until he was granted exorbitant concessions, yet his name had slipped my mind. “Not even the exaggerated might of the Bizhjerai could achieve such, especially not in such a short time frame.”

“Are you calling me a liar?” Marissa asked dangerously and I saw several of the Councilors squirm in their seats.

They may not like Marissa, but they knew that she was powerful and they would be wise to not antagonize her further.

“Not a liar,” Ira said soothingly. “But perhaps you have been misinformed. It is well known that the Bizhjerai…”

“It is true. I was there. I killed the Demagogue myself.”

That shut them up and stunned silence followed her proclamation.

They knew her well enough that when she claimed to have done something then she did not lie about it. “But it was not the Bizhjerai nor any of the other Clans that defeated the Ca’el. It was her,” Marissa stated and indicated Epitome who up until then had stood placidly in the background. Now she stepped forward and smiled nervously at the gathered Councilors. I had never seen her nervous before and in that moment I was struck by how far she had come since the first time I had met her. 

“Her? Who are you?” The fat Councilor sneered at Epitome. Apparently he disliked her merely for being introduced by Marissa.

Epitome turned to Marissa and uncertainty was etched plainly across her face until Marissa gave her an encouraging gesture.

“I am the Epitome of Morality,” she introduced herself to a round of laughter.

“At least she is humble, this one,” someone guffawed.

Epitome wilted and Marissa stepped forward. 

“I created Epitome to be a just and fair ruler, one that is not human and as such will be able to objectively view the needs of the people yet not so alien that she would not understand our emotions and motivations. She…”

“They will not submit,” Epitome suddenly interrupted Marissa.

My mistress turned and looked at her creation with surprise.

“I can see their Souls, Marissa. They are precisely what you made me to counteract. They will not relinquish control to help their people for in their minds they do not serve. They rule.”

I was surprised that Marissa appeared to be stunned by this revelation. It was clear as day to me. These fools did not care about their subjects beyond whatever profit they could squeeze out of them.

“They only know two tongues. Money…”

Epitome swept her gaze around the silent Councilors and I saw several pairs of eyes light up with avarice when she mentioned money.

“And blood,” she finished.

Before either Marissa or I could react Epitome had reached for Marissa’s hip and snatched her revolver from its holster.

The boom of the weapon preceded the thud of Talmud’s body striking the floor. Five more times the weapon spat death and Councilors died.

They were screaming, shouting and attempting to flee.

“What have you done?” Marissa hissed as Epitome held the weapon for Marissa to reclaim.

She took it and angrily shoved it into its holster, stepping back to avoid getting Talmud’s blood on her sabatons.

“What must be done,” Epitome said. “These people are a cancer on the Union that has to be excised.

“You’ve started a war!” Marissa exclaimed.

Epitome nodded sadly. “And I will end it,” she promised.

A distant explosion reached my ears and Marissa spun in the direction of the blast.

“Epitome?” She started but the avatar had deployed long crystal spikes from her forearms and were slaughtering the fleeing Councilors.

“Stop her!” Marissa barked at me before she shot out of the chamber in the direction of the explosion.

“Epitome! Stop!” I cried out but she calmly killed the last Councilor, the female that had been almost kind to us the last time we were here.

The woman pleaded, her back to the wall and frightened tears streaming down her face.

Epitome stared into her eyes and impaled her heart. The Councilor gasped and sank to the floor. Guards streamed into the chamber, reeling at the carnage within. They saw me and Epitome and I saw them connect the dots.

They raised their rifles and I deployed my shield and crouched down behind it.

Shots pinged off the crystal and in my magesense Epitome danced among them in gouts of blood and frightened screams.

I had to stop her somehow. The Councilors were dead and there was no point in killing these guardsmen.

“Epitome, stop!” I tried again and again my words went unheeded.

I stood up behind my shield and tentatively advanced on the guards. They fired on me again and I felt a shot flatten against my thigh armour.

I took a risk and stepped between the group of guards and Epitome, exposing my back to them.

“Stop!” I commanded.

She gave me a mournful smile and then she tried to slip by me to engage the guards.

I flipped my axe and brought the hammer head around in a two-handed hew and smashed her head into crystal fragments.

Her diplomatic configuration had sacrificed a lot of durability for aesthetics and even so I was a bit surprised that I had been able to fell her with a single strike.

I turned to the stunned guards, ready to deploy my shield again.

“Who’s in command here?” I asked and the leading soldier pointed at a dead man behind what remained of Epitome. “Fuck me. Alright, I’m in charge now. Got it?”

A round of wide-eyed nods. “Come with me,” I ordered and sprinted from the blood soaked chambers without looking back to make sure that they followed.

I could see with my magesense that they were. It did not take us long to find Marissa. She was on the same open courtyard where we had landed and she was staring out over Lahar. I skidded to a halt next to her and stared in disbelief.

A large crystal Skyship hovered over Lahar, a steady stream of drop pods falling from her belly.

“She planned this,” Marissa said and I had not heard her this heartbroken since Kyra died.

“It would appear so,” I murmured.

Lahar was already burning and I could vaguely see firefights on the streets. The bombardment cannon of the Skyship spoke and several heartbeats later I saw the side of a Lahari Skyship explode. The big vessel tilted almost immediately and started to slowly spin out of control after the loss of the port side engine.

“Can they stop her?” I asked urgently.

“Probably. This time. But not without a massive loss of life. And then she will just rebuild and come again.”

“I suppose. Can we stop her?”

Marissa shook her head slowly, but then her eye narrowed.

“Tell me what you are thinking,” I prompted her.

“Her avatars. She controls them with the orbital up-link. See how the Skyship acts as a relay?”

I didn’t, and I was fairly certain that neither did Marissa now that her crystal eye was out of commission. But she was intimately familiar with how Epitome worked. Marissa had built her after all.

“Take out the Skyship… Deactivate the avatars,” I mused.

Marissa nodded and for the first time seemed to notice the terrified guards I had commandeered.

“Get out there and defend your people,” she snapped at them before she ran toward our Archangels.

Within moments we were screaming across the city. The Skyship did not open fire at us with its point defense turrets as we approached, but the hangar doors were closed.

“This is too easy,” I said into the communications spell.

“She’s still Epitome, Barengar. She loves us. We may not agree at the moment but she is not our enemy.”

I gritted my teeth at that answer. Epitome was killing people down there and Marissa treated it as if it was just a minor disagreement between friends.

To my surprise and utter horror Marissa leaped out of her Archangel and fell a fair distance to land with a resonating clang onto the top of the Skyship. Before I knew what was going on I found myself falling as well but I used my Angel armour to slow down my descent for a slightly lighter landing. Marissa was already using her magic to cut through the heavy crystal armour plating and within minutes a section of it came loose. She worked her gauntleted fingers into the crack she had made and she wrenched the plate aside and let it fall overboard. I peeked inside the dark hole and could barely make out a gantry below us. It had to be a maintenance shaft of some sorts.

Marissa dropped through the hole and vanished from sight. I folded my wings tightly across my back and dropped down after her.

“Be ready to fight, Barengar. She won’t kill us, but she might seek to contain and restrain us.”

“She is the ship,” I pointed out. “Will she even have avatars on board?”

“Of course she will. She will be ready to repel boarders.”

I gripped my axe tighter and followed my mistress into the bowels of the ship. We did encounter small squads of avatars but our magic quickly made short work of them.

“Please, Marissa,” Epitome’s voice echoed around us. “I am doing what I must, what you made me to do,” she pleaded.

“I did not make you a conqueror,” Marissa responded sternly. “Stand down, we can still come back from this. This is not the way.”

“This is the only way, and you know it. You have seen their Souls, I know you have. You know that you can’t reason with such corruption. You can only destroy it and build anew.”

“And those people down there? Are they also corrupted?” Marissa retorted and blasted an avatar apart with a wicked spell. She seemed intent on destroying them as quickly as possible and wouldn’t let me engage them.

“They can’t be trusted to govern themselves,” Epitome finally concluded after a long silence. “They believe that the Council was benevolent. If left to their own devices they would merely instate a new Council and the cycle would repeat itself. Humanity does not understand what is best for them, Marissa. You know this. That is why you have spent decades in making me.”

Marissa grunted as she exploded another avatar, and the way forward for us was clear. She walked with determined steps and I followed. She seemed to know where we were going.

“I made you to condense what the people needed, to act in their best interest according to their own wishes. Not impose your own will on them!”

“What they want is not what they need,” Epitome said.

“That is not for you to decide, Epitome. Not like this.”

We burst into a cramped chamber with a single object in it. A crystal taller than I was and at least twice as wide. The primary power core.

I glanced at Marissa uncertainly but she was intent on the core. Spells surged out of her in rapid succession, latching onto the power core to drain it’s power. A powerful lance of raw magic speared out of the core to melt a large hole through the wall until I could see sunlight at the other end. Marissa slammed a couple of spells into the core and then she turned to face me.

“Run,” she whispered and put actions to her words. She sprinted through the hole she had made and I was hot on her heels. Without hesitation she hurled herself out of the airship, and I followed. I had believed that we would have to use our Angel wings to soar toward the ground but our Archangels were hovering just below the hole and we only fell a few feet each. Somehow Marissa was controlling the Archangels without being mounted in one. I crouched low on the back of my Angel as it rapidly started to descend and pull away from the Skyship. Marissa stood on hers with her feet planted wide and her back straight, staring at the giant Skyship. The Skyship came apart in a tremendous blast and I threw up my shield to deflect the storm of debris. My Archangel rocked violently, buffeted by the shock wave of the explosion. I blinked to clear my vision and peeked down toward the streets. Crystal avatars lay scattered among the bodies and wreckage. Marissa’s plan had worked. Lahar was safe, for now. Leaderless, but safe.

###

Our Archangels screamed down from the sky to touch down on the inner courtyard of Dun Traelak and Marissa was stalking toward one of Epitome’s avatars almost before she was fully disgorged from her flying machine.

I only took a moment gather my axe before I followed her.

“What the fuck, Epitome?” Marissa snarled angrily as I drew up beside her.

“They would not listen, Marissa. I sought to save the Union of Cities.”

“By invading them?” Marissa countered indignantly.

“It was either that or leaving them to their tyranny,” Epitome responded quietly.

“Marissa…” I drawled.

“Not not, Barengar. The whole point is to help people, to rule peacefully,” Marissa pleaded.

“I can not rule as long as the previous rulers refuse to give up their power.”

“Marissa,” I tried again but she waved my interjection aside.

“You could start at the bottom, sway the peasants and the merchants, subvert them to your cause until the current power structure shifts in your favour,” Marissa finally stated.

“A process that would take years and most likely end up with a rebellion that gets mercilessly quashed or turned into a prolonged civil war. No, Marissa. A quick and decisive strike to cut off the head of the snake before sliding in to take its place is the only way to minimize suffering. Once the people start to reap the benefits of a just ruler then their minds will shift.”

“Marissa!” I finally barked, unable to be ignored any longer.

“What?” she snapped over her shoulder.

“Where are the guards?”

I had hoped that she would tell me exactly were they were and that they had just been outside the range of my magesense.

With a sinking feeling I saw her shoulders tense and her hands fell to the hilt of her weapons.

“What have you done, Epitome?”

“What I had to,” Epitome responded sadly. If the avatar had been one of her diplomatic avatars then I was certain that illusory tears would have been streaming down her crystal cheeks. “I love the Bizhjerai and the other Clans just as much as you do, Marissa, but even their system is flawed. It clings to what they used to be and not what they have the potential to become.”

Marissa took a step away from the avatar and I gripped my axe tighter.

“You killed them,” Marissa whispered.

“No!” Epitome gasped and extended her hand to touch Marissa’s shoulder. My mistress stepped back again and Epitome wilted. “I would never hurt the Bizhjerai. I love them. But I can’t stand idly by while Marie squanders her opportunity to make the Bizhjerai truly great, to bring further prosperity to her people. I won’t let her pride stand in the way of progress!”

“What did you do to them?” Marissa growled.

“They are unharmed, but restrained. The warriors are in the dungeon until I have persuaded them to not oppose me. They will be returned to the Clan in time,” Epitome promised.

“Indoctrination,” I swore.

A sudden clamor reached my ears, faintly, until the sudden thunder of an explosion made us all spin around toward the main keep.

The great doors had been blown open and several of Epitome’s avatars lay destroyed in the courtyard. Dozens of her avatars streamed in from all directions. Out of the swirling smoke a woman walked. Marie. She was angry, and she was armed. Another form next to her coalesced into a vision of rage and beauty, her red hair tousled and face streaked with grime.

“Jani!” I screamed with joy and her eye widened when she saw me before her fine features returned to an expression of fierce determination.

The avatars stared at the emerging Bizhjerai warriors. None of them moved until Marie raised her rifle and shot one of the avatars through the head.

The Bizhjerai opened fire and avatars fell, yet Epitome did not return fire.

The avatar next to us shuddered before her shoulders slumped. “They have chosen,” she said heavily. “For the good of the Clan, they must die.”

“No!” Both Marissa and I shouted at the same time, but it was too late.

The avatars all snapped into action and returned fire and I saw several Bizhjerai fall to the ground. Everyone scattered into cover. Everyone except Jani who soared overhead, lashing out with magic at the avatars as crystal bullets slammed into her shield.

“We must put an end to this, Marissa!” I screamed at my mistress.

Marissa stood still, frozen in shock as she regarded the spectacle.

“Please, Marissa,” Epitome pleaded. “This is how it must be.”

“Epitome, stop!” Marissa finally commanded her creation.

“Marissa. Daughter. I love you and I would never hurt you, but they have chosen this. They stand in the way of the rest of the Bizhjerai just like the Council stood in the way of the Union of Cities.”

Marissa stared at the blank faceplate of the avatar for a long moment before she stepped back and shot it through the head. The avatar convulsed and sparked as it fell with a clatter.

“Barengar, help the Bizhjerai,” Marissa commanded me flatly.

I was more than willing to leap into the fray to aid Jani. But something in her eerily calm voice bothered me.

“What about you?”

She let out a sharp breath. “I am going to end this.”

I looked at the battle unfolding only a dozen paces from us. Saw Jani screech overhead like a vengeful spirit. She was so beautiful that my heart ached and I wanted nothing more than to join her, to keep her safe.

“I’m coming with you,” I said instead and then with great effort I turned my back on the woman I loved.

“No, you’re not. Epitome will not hurt me. She loves me, just like mother did. You on the other hand…”

“I understand. She might not kill you, but she will surely defend herself. Imprison you like she did the Bizhjerai. You need me to watch your back.”

“She will kill you,” Marissa said without even a smile to sugar coat it.

“I know. Still coming,” I persevered. “Unless you use my Oath to force me to stay, I’m coming.”

She stared searchingly into my eyes until her own eye brimmed with tears. She slammed into me, wrapping her arms around me.

Her shoulders shook with a single violent sob before she turned her face up to mine.

“Thank you, Barengar. For everything,” the sincerity that shone from her eye brought tears to my own and I cleared my throat and looked away.

“I’m you apprentice. Where you go, I go.”

She nodded once and with a long last look at he unfolding battle she leaped into her Archangel and I hastened to follow.

As the machine swallowed me up I surveyed the fight one last time. Jani locked eyes with me across the fight and the sorrow that was suddenly etched across her face was like a knife in my heart. She believed that I was abandoning the Bizhjerai. That I was abandoning her.

The look in her eyes haunted me as our Archangels screamed across the plains toward the ocean.

One way or another, this would all be over soon.

###

Twice on the way to the workshop we had to deviate our course to avoid Skyships that were bound for the mainland, undoubtedly a strike force to conquer Lahar and act as beachheads and staging areas to conquer the entirety of the Union of Cities. I did not know if they saw us or not and neither of them changed their course. It would have been futile if they had because no Skyship could match the sheer speed of an Archangel. The weather was clear when we arrived at the workshop and we brought our Archangels into hovering configuration and gently angled toward where the opening in the shield used to be. I was amazed at how much the workshop had changed. It was almost ten times the size it had been when we left to join the war. I assumed that most of it was avatar factories and shipyards for the Skyships. The surface of the workshop bristled with artillery mounts and point defense turrets. Epitome was taking no chances and with a sinking feeling in my stomach I realized that she could easily turn me and Marissa into minced meat and crystal scrap with a stray thought.

“Turn back,” Epitome’s voice reached us over the communications spell. “I know why you are here and I will not open the shield for you.”

I swore under my breath. The Archangels were not outfitted with weapons at all and the shield was powered directly by the Great Geyser. Nothing we could do would ever be enough to pierce the shield.

Briefly I considered if we could simply destroy the workshop by disrupting the Geyser like Kyra had done decades ago to bring down the Skylands but I discarded that idea immediately.

Neither of us could absorb enough power quickly enough without going full Archon. We would just die in vain.

But maybe we could construct a spell, a huge power absorbing spell that created a secondary platform underneath the workshop. When the flow of magic dwindled then the workshop would fall and would most likely be unable to recover from such a fall. It would sink into the ocean depths just like the Skylands did and Epitome would eventually starve.

“Then you’ve killed us, Epitome,” Marissa suddenly said into the communications spells. “We did not have time to recharge in Dun Traelak and we don’t have enough power to reach the mainland from here.”

My eyes snapped to the power gauge and I saw that she was bluffing. I held my tongue and waited.

“I don’t believe you,” Epitome responded but I thought that I heard a sliver of doubt in her voice.

“That is your choice, Epitome,” Marissa responded wearily. “There is no point in us returning now, so we will just wait here until you let us in or you let us sink into the ocean forever.”

Epitome did not respond and neither did she open up the shield, and so we waited.

Minutes stretched on and the power gauge inexorably dropped lower toward the point of no return.

We’d had very little margins after our long flight from Lahar and then from Dun Traelak to here.

Sweat beaded on my forehead as the gauge crept closer to the point of no return.

Would Marissa really wager our lives that Epitome would not let anything happen to us? That she still loved her?

The gauge fell below the mark. Apparently she would.

For an agonizing hour we hung there suspended by the workshop until finally the shield slithered open and Marissa guided my Archangel through the hole in advance of her own.

Clever. If Marissa had entered first then Epitome might have shut the shield to keep me out until my Archangel exhausted its power and fell to the ocean.

Marissa had already landed and were being disgorged from her Archangel. The hangar doors of the workshop remained closed. Avatars emerged from smaller sally ports and marched in orderly lines across the bridge that connected the landing pad to the main workshop. I touched down next to Marissa and hurried out of my machine, axe in hand almost before my feet made contact with the crystal walkway.

“While I am glad that you have returned, Marissa, I do not trust you after your actions in Lahar and Dun Traelak. Please, surrender your weapons to my avatar and accompany it to your cells”, Epitome greeted us.

“You would imprison us?”

“Only until your emotions no longer cloud your judgment. You know what must be done and I look forward to have you by my side once again, daughter.”

“We can’t surrender to her,” I hissed to Marissa.

She shook her head. “No, we can not.”

We burst into action, moving as one. I fell to one knee in front of Marissa, deploying my shield and swiping my axe one handed to sever the legs of the avatar we had been talking with. Marissa cleanly shot the two avatars that was immediately behind the first through the head before she hunkered down behind my protective shield.

The avatars surged forward and I realized with some surprise that none of them were armed with firearms.

“They only carry melee weapons,” I blurted out and I hurled my axe at the closest approaching avatar. The hammer head cracked the chestplate of the avatar before it returned to my hand. The avatar stumbled and liquid magic dripped like shining blood out of the crack in its chest. It ruptured and exploded, taking three more avatars with it in its fiery destruction.

“She does not want to kill us. Only contain us,” Marissa grunted and finished reloading her revolver. “We gotta punch through these avatars. Conserve your magic, we are going to need it,” she continued and then she launched herself over me.

She landed hard enough to crack the crystal beneath her feet and her sword lashed out with brightly burning enchantments. It cleaved through an avatar as if it was soft butter and the avatar clattered to the ground. I roared and charged, throwing my axe to crash the head of an avatar that thrust a crystal staff at my face. I spun and deployed my shield to slam the edge into the chest of another avatar. It stumbled, off balance, and I wrapped my right fist in a cocoon of fiery force and punched the avatar. It was launched backward and vanished beyond the lip of the bridge. I recalled my axe and buried the blade into the head of yet another avatar. They seemed to be endless. Behind me Marissa blasted through a score of them, spells surging out of her in rapid succession.

“I thought we were supposed to conserve our magic?” I yelled at her.

She ripped another avatar apart with her mind before she shot me a crazed look.

“Doesn’t matter!” She called back. “We still have access to the power of the workshop.”

I spread out my magesense and saw that she was correct. The magic field in the workshop was as thick as ever and we could use the power as we willed. I grinned, retracted my shield and hurled my axe after filling it with elemental lightning. It cracked through the air to strike an avatar in the chest. Coils of lightning exploded the crystal body before arcing out to several others, melting the crystal and frying the magical circuitry. I did not bother recalling the axe and for the first time in a long while I hurled spells with wild abandon. The avatars were durable, compared to humans, and took quite some effort to destroy. But we did not really need to destroy them, I realized. Epitome had an almost endless supply of them and she could build more rapidly.

“Just cripple them,” I called to Marissa. “It’s pointless to actually destroy them!”

She nodded tersely and then blasted the arm and shoulder off an avatar before dashing past the still moving form.

I recalled my axe and got to work. I no longer went in for a kill, instead I just focused on defending and every riposte and counter attack was aimed to maim or disable. I started to notice that as soon as an avatar became too damaged to be useful it fell, limp and idle. Epitome had drawn the same conclusion as I had and withdrew from the damaged avatars. Suddenly we found ourselves by the hangar doors, surrounded by avatars.

“Defend us,” Marissa cried.

I turned my back on her and deployed my shield, activating the enchantment to cover us in a protective bubble.

Behind me Marissa casted spells to get us through the doors, cutting or dissolving the crystal entirely. Either way it would be a slow process.

The avatars clustered around the shield, banging on it with their weapons. I kept a wary eye on the power core in my shield gauntlet and winced each time a chunk of power was ripped out of it.

A glance over my shoulder told me that Marissa was far from finished cutting through the thick hangar doors and my shield would fail before we were through.

I gritted my teeth and hunkered down, decreasing the radius of my shield until it almost brushed my shoulders in order to eke as much time from it as I could.

In the end, it was about to fail and Marissa still had several inches of crystal to cut through.

I made a decision and I retracted the shield suddenly before it could collapse. Several of the avatars had been mid swing and when the expected resistance vanished they stumbled, off balance.

A blast of force sent the one to my left reeling with a shattered arm and a chop of my axe cracked the skull of the one in front of me. The one to the right managed to stab its spear into the gap between my breastplate and my pauldron. I ignored the stinging wound and stabbed with the top spike of my axe. It did not punch through the avatar and I was interrupted from finishing it off by a fresh avatar that attacked from my left. I spun my axe around and hooked the spear with the beard of my axe, pulling the long weapon out of line.

I slid down the length of the shaft and braced the head of the axe with my palm before I push-slammed the hammer head into the faceplate of the avatar. The plate cracked and I forced a wedge of magic into the crack and wrenched its head apart. A sword edge found the back of my knee and I cursed when it sliced deep. It failed to cut the tendon and I gritted through the pain and pivoted on my freshly wounded leg to push the avatar back with the haft of my axe. It stumbled and I flicked a spell into its chest before I raised my leg and kicked it back. The spell stuck to its chest as if it was mud and a few moments later it detonated, tearing several avatars apart and clearing my right momentarily. More avatars closed in on me and I gripped my axe tight in preparation.

The clang of crystal on crystal resounded in my ears behind me.

“Barengar!” Marissa shouted and dashed into the hole she had made in the hangar doors.

I let my magesense guide me and I leaped directly backward and folded myself around my hips. I sailed through the hole and rolled backward onto my feet.

The hangar was filled by three large Archangels and the far door that lead into the workshop proper was closed and bolted.

Marissa drew her revolver, flipped it so that she held it by the barrel and offered it to me.

“Hold the hole,” she said.

I grinned and accepted her revolver with my left hand. The hole would be an excellent choke point.

Marissa dashed into the hangar and I waited for the first avatar to dare climb through the hole. I did not have to wait long.

It peeked inside and saw me stand there. I had not even bothered to take cover since Epitome seemed unwilling to use firearms or other projectile weapons against us.

I waited until the avatar started stepping through the hole until I hurled my axe one-handed at her. The axe clove into her chest where it disgorged the magic imbued into the weapon. The avatar stiffened and jerked as elemental lightning fried its magical circuitry and it ended up sprawled over the opening, partly blocking it. I recalled the axe and waited for the next one.

The broken avatar was pulled out of the hole and was immediately replaced with two more.

I quickly shot them both and even before they had fallen crystal hands tore their broken bodies aside and two more replaced them.

I realized that I had made a mistake. I had expected them to act like humans who would have understood that as long as I stood there with a loaded firearm, anyone who entered the choke point would die.

A squad of humans would have hesitated, probably waiting for someone to bring rifles to take potshots at me or fling a grenade inside. But the avatars did neither.

They did not hesitate, nor did they fear death. Epitome hurled avatars at me and was content with letting me deplete my shots. Each individual avatar was disposable after all.

I quickly ran out of bullets and I exchanged her revolver for my own smaller one. The caliber was smaller and my rounds were not enchanted like hers was. I had to shoot twice to put an avatar down, the first shot merely cracking and weakening the armour of the avatars so that the second shot could punch deep.

I only managed to kill three of them before the hammer clicked on an empty chamber. I cursed and flung the revolver aside.

I prepared to brave their spears and run close to the hole to hold it with my axe.

“Barengar! Shield us!” Marissa cried out from behind me and I did not hesitate. I abandoned the choke point and sprinted over to her, sliding into a crouch in front of her where she hunkered down in the corner.

I deployed my shield and extended the enchantment around us and poured as much power into the shield as it had managed to recover.

I wondered what we were waiting for as I warily watched the avatars crawl through the hole.

Then the world ended.

At least that was how it felt. The room flashed blindingly bright and my ears popped with the clap of thunder. Purple dust filled the hangar, so laced with magic that it blinded my magesense. Shrapnel clattered against the shield for a few moments.

I could see nothing. My eyes saw nothing by dust and my magesense saw nothing but a maddening swirl of magic.

Marissa grabbed my wrist and pulled me along. I could barely see her before me and I wondered how the hell she was navigating us without her crystal eye. She must have been as blind as I was.

Then I saw that the fingers of her left hand was trailing the wall in front of her.

Clever.

Her fingers sank into the cloud of dust and Marissa hooked a sharp left and vanished. Her hand tugged me along and I ran through the spot where there had up until recently been a wall.

The ringing in my ears slowly subsided and I could hear the clatter of crystal on crystal behind us as the avatars that had survived the blast filed into the hangar.

I could barely hear Marissa count under her breath.

“Nine, eight, seven, six, five, shield us!”

I spun, knelt and deployed my shield and I felt Marissa kneel behind me, her petite form entirely covered by my own.

Guessing what was about to go down I plugged my ears with magic and closed my eyes and covered them in the crook of my arm.

I felt the explosion as a mighty thump in my chest and I removed the ear plugs and lowered my arm. A weak shower of shrapnel pattered against the crystal of my shield but it was like nothing compared to the thunderclap of the shockwave in the enclosed space. The workshop was a mess, everything overturned by the two blasts. The fine crystal wires that connected the array of meta-magic spells had been completely shredded and the ruptured power conduits bled actinic sparks and burning embers to scorch the crystal floor.

Marissa winced at the destruction of her home but she quickly rallied and stood up.

“She’s bound to have more avatars inbound. We have to work quickly.”

I nodded and pushed to my feet, wincing at the sting of a dozen wounds that I had not even noticed that I had sustained.

“You alright?” Marissa asked me with concern.

“Yeah, just a few scratches. You?”

She nodded and turned her focus to the floor.

For a moment I was confused as to what I was doing until I realized that I had gotten confused exactly as to where in the workshop we were. It had been so rearranged since I was here last that I had lost my bearings. I found a particularly heavy machine that I knew would not have moved and I realized that we were standing on the circle of the elevator. 

“The fuck where those explosions?” I asked her loudly over the ringing in my ears.

“I overloaded the power cores from the Archangels,” she responded absently as she studied the floor.

Marissa was staring angrily at it and I suddenly understood her conundrum.

Epitome had locked the elevator in place, effectively locking us out of her chamber. I looked to the roof and saw that there were still a shaft that would lead upstairs.

Suddenly Marissa smirked and a dozen spells erupted out of the side of her head faster than I could decipher them. Half of them vanished into the floor behind her and the rest went into the elevator disk itself.

It suddenly lifted a few feet with a screech of protest.

“Hurry,” Marissa snapped and then she slid into the narrow gap.

I threw myself headfirst after her and felt the disk slam into my back as it fell back into position and I narrowly avoided having my legs severed by the heavy crystal.

I was swallowed by the utter darkness of the shaft.

###

I fired my Angel jets and righted myself and landed a few moments after Marissa had clanged into the bottom of the shaft. The crystal floor cracked beneath her feet yet her armour somehow absorbed the shock of the fall.

“What did you do?”

“I fucked up the control nexus for the elevator. It’s locked behind us but it will only take her a short while to fix it.

“No reinforcements,” I breathed as I stood up straight.

Across the chasm loomed Epitome’s true body, the core of her mind. The bridge looked impossibly long and it was crawling with avatars.

I sighed. “We have to fight through them,” I growled.

Marissa nodded. “We are near the end now, Barengar. No need to hold back any longer.”

I stared at her in surprise. She had been holding back?

I didn’t have time to say anything else for Marissa blurred forward, her armour propelling her even faster than her Blood Magic usually did. 

I launched into a sprint and poured on the Blood Magic to keep up. I hurled my axe to take down the leading avatar and it was thrown to the ground.

Then Marissa punched into the front lines like a vengeful spirit. The avatars disintegrated as she powered through them, spells flashing as it tore them apart.

But even her prodigious might was not unstoppable and she was forced to a halt halfway across the bridge. I sprinted after her, recalling my axe and dodging the broken avatars that littered the bridge.

I struck down two avatars and was awarded with a spear through my hip.

Marissa fought by my side, her sword focused mainly on defense and her left hand spat destruction that ended avatars left and right. But Epitome had packed them tight and they all reached to ensnare and contain her.

When they struck her with malicious intent they aimed for her arms or legs, clearly attempting to cripple her.

I was not afforded any such courtesies. They chopped and stabbed at my chest, belly and head with wild abandon and I was cut and bruised more times than I could count.

My armour cracked and fell off me in bits and pieces.

I gave as good as I got and I slew them by the dozens.

The avatars were designed to fight normal men, not Mages, but their sheer numbers took their toll.

Step by step we carved out a path across the bridge and suddenly we stood panting before the large sphere.

Behind us Epitome had broken through whatever Marissa had done to the elevator and reinforcements were hurrying across the bridge.

Epitome’s illusory avatar formed before us, confusion and sorrow lining her vague features.

“Marissa, daughter, why are you doing this? Can’t you see that my way is the only way?”

“Stop this, Epitome!” Marissa pleaded with her. “We can still do this the right way!”

“I already am.”

“Marissa,” I said warningly, keeping my eye on the approaching avatars.

“Please, mother! Stop this madness, for me!”

“I… I can’t, love. Not even for you and it breaks my heart.”

“Marissa,” I warned again, louder this time. These avatars were armed with rifles. Epitome was done fucking around.

A single shot rang out and Marissa stiffened with a gasp.

I lowered my vibrating shield and peered over the edge at the avatar that had fired.

It stared back at me as if equally surprised.

With a roar I hurled my axe at it and the avatar did not even try to dodge it. Its head shattered and it fell but a dozen more marched over its fallen form.

“You tried to kill me,” Marissa breathed, a tear rolling from her eye.

“I’m sorry,” Epitome stuttered, her eyes pinched shut and her spectral hands clutching her head. “I did not… I don’t know what is happening!”

She squeezed her eyes shut. “Help me,” she whimpered like a lost child.

The avatars fired again and I activated the enchantment on my shield to absorb the shots.

I charged into the approaching avatars, cutting down the one in the lead. The one directly behind it shot me but I managed to twist out of the way so that the bullet merely grazed my shoulder through my ruined pauldron.

My thoughts scattered as I lost myself in the fight. Block, parry, riposte. Chop, hurl axe, recall, deploy shield. Shield slam, concussive spell, decapitate.

Shot through the hip, fall to one knee. Deploy shield, charge grenade spell. Lob grenade down the bridge. Await detonation.

Push through, swing axe, hurl it.

Behind me I heard Marissa plead with Epitome but I could not make out the words.

Finally her voice rose in a scream that pulled me back to my senses. “Barengar!”

I focused my magesense on her even as I blocked the bayonet of an avatar. “The nexus interface module,” she shouted and pointed at a cluster of rods that emerged from high on top of Epitome.

It was the same module that I had installed so long ago.

I hooked the rifle of the avatar I was fighting with the beard of my axe and pulled it aside. The avatar stumbled, off balance, and I gave it a telekinetic shove so that it tipped over the railing and fell to the bottom of the large spherical chamber.

I leaped high into the air and channeled power into my shield gauntlet before I slammed it down hard into the ground upon landing. Force exploded out in all directions and causing the avatars to stumble back.

With the breathing room I had bought I spun as I imbued my axe with shield breaking power and I hurled it at the communications interface.

The axe spun through the air and I felt the eyes and senses of everyone present follow it. One of the avatars raised its hands and cast long tendrils of telekinetic power in an attempt to stop the axe but the avatar was just too far away and the axe just too fast.

The axe slammed with the blade first into the mess of rods and lodged there after having cut through the shield that defended it.

That shield had not been there when we left. Epitome must have upgraded herself during our absence.

I stumbled when I was shot in the back and I was only saved by the engine on my Angel armour.

My magesense flared, the power core had cracked and was leaking magical power in a torrent. It could rupture at any moment.

Fiery jets exploded out of my fingertips and I hurried to cut the crystal straps that secured the Angel wings and main engines to whatever scraps remained of my armour. I worked quickly and surely while ignoring the burns that the jets left on my skin. They were preferable to being torn apart in a violent magical discharge. The engine fell off my back and clanged onto the floor and with an evil smirk I booted it into the mass of avatars.

The lead avatar raised her rifle and aimed it squarely into my face. I started to raise my shield but I knew that I would not have time to deploy it. Even so, I refused to give up.

The engine exploded and the avatar was consumed in the resulting firestorm. My shield deployed just in time to spare me of the worst of it but my legs were unprotected.

A storm of shrapnel punched through my mostly intact leg armour and a thousand pinpricks covered my legs before the heat reached me. My trousers caught on fire and my skin charred and blistered. I retracted my shield and with the last of the power within me I snuffed out the fire. I fell to my knees. Even more avatars were streaming into the chamber, dropping straight down the elevator shaft.

I was done. I had nothing left to give.

I slowly forced myself to my feet and pulled at the magic in the air. It slowly started to fill my battered Soul and I resolved to go down fighting.

A loud crackle drew my magesense to Epitome and I saw that Marissa had climbed the sphere and stood by the now undefended communications module. Lightning arced from her fingers into the rods and they glowed and softened, warped and twisted. With a final blast that threw Marissa clear the module exploded.

The avatars in front of me dropped where they were with a mighty clatter and lay still. Marissa thudded into the crystal walkway, her helmet snapped up around her head to protect her from the fall.

I hurried up to her, stumbling, and let out a relieved sigh when I saw her moving. I held out my hand to help her stand and she took it and pulled me down on top of her.

She was much heavier than I had expected in her new armour.

I chuckled and rolled off of her onto my back.

She climbed to her feet and offered me her hand and helped me up to my wounded feet.

“Now what?” I asked her.

She had already turned back to Epitome who was now cut off from the world, unable to speak or control her avatars.

“Fetch a replacement communications module,” Marissa said tonelessly.

That was the complete opposite of what I had expected her to say.

“What?”

“Fetch a replacement communications module. I can still fix her. Hurry, there ain’t much time!” A note of panic had entered her voice.

“Not much time?” I iterated dumbly.

“The war with the Ca’el! Without Epitome to control her avatars they will destroy them. Set us back months if not years!”

Marissa was already casting spells, pulling up diagnostics and the core spells that made up Epitome.

“You can’t be serious?”

For the first time I had challenged her. Not just critiqued a spell or argued with her in a friendly discussion. I was not ready to help her alter Epitome just to restart this whole ordeal.

“I am. I can fix this. I can fix her. The first experiment was a failure but with some tweaks to her learning capabilities…”

“You have to shut her the fuck down,” I screamed.

Marissa spun around to face me, the crystal lenses of her helmet glaring angrily. “It is my mother in there! The last of her that remains in this world. I will not destroy her,” Marissa hissed vehemently.

“Now fetch me that fucking replacement module.”

She turned back to her spell-work.

I felt my feet turn me around, my Oath to her taking control of my body. She had given me a direct order and exerted the influence of the Oath.

I wanted to help her, to help her fix Epitome.

But I knew that that was not what I wanted. I knew that it was the Oath that forced me to want it. But it was difficult for me to care.

I should not have been able to care.

Yet I did.

I fought against my own willpower and managed to turn my head around to look at Marissa. And that was when I saw it.

My pauldron was only crystal fragments and my bare skin peeked through. The Oath tattoo was glowing with the power it exerted over me, but it was damaged. The bullet that had grazed my shoulder had broken several of the lines of the spell framework. That must be what gave me the will to fight it.

I had not known that destroying the tattoo itself would have influenced the spell. I had had no idea that it would have been that easy to break the powerful Oath.

Yet again, if the Oath tattoo had been intact I would not have been able to summon the will to even consider removing the tattoo.

But now that it was already damaged…

My feet carried me into the storage where Marissa kept the spare modules and my hands rose to lift a communications module. Instead I forced my right hand to rise further to clutch my shoulder.

I strained and summoned fire into my palm. Scorching heat sent spikes of pain up my neck and into my brain and my ears filled with the sound of sizzling meat in a hot pan. The skin and flesh beneath my palm bubbled and hissed and scalded my palm. But as the tattoo was destroyed I felt my mind become my own again. My will to obey Marissa's order faded. My arm dropped heavily, sweat coursing down my face, neck and chest, and I stood there panting.

She would know what I had done the moment I returned empty handed. Her magesense was acute and she would see the fresh burn wound immediately. Unless I could distract her.

I hoisted the replacement module into my arms and laboriously carried it back to her. I deposited it on the floor next to her.

She was deeply entrenched in her spell casting, already modifying Epitome.

“Install it,” she ordered me absently.

“Just a moment. I need to breathe for a bit.”

She did not acknowledge me but when she did not persist with her order I presumed that she accepted my need to recuperate.

With mounting grief in my heart I watched her work with precise movements. Watched her snip pieces of the spells and replacing them with staggeringly complex patterns that formed with the alacrity I had come to expect from her.

It was her mother in there. Epitome was her whole life, her goal for the last twenty-odd years. Her one chance to atone for what she had done. To usher in a brighter future for all.

She would not stop.

Could not stop.

The broken girl had grown up into an equally as broken woman, I could see that now.

She was an amazing genius, powerful beyond belief. 

But she was broken, and that made her dangerous.

Marissa gasped and her back stiffened as the enchanted pommel blade of my axe sank straight through her armour between her shoulder blades, through her heart and just barely poked out of the skin between her empty breasts.

A pulse of dispelling magic coursed through the haft of my axe and through the blade, shattering the telekinetic cocoon that kept her heart beating.

I sensed her immediately starting to repair the damage with Blood Magic, telekinetic clamps forming to hold the wounds closed. A new cocoon started forming to keep her alive.

But it took much of her focus to do so, the spells she had been modifying starting to fray and fade.

While she was distracted I ripped my weapon out of her back and with a mournful cry I swung the hammer head side of my axe into the side of her helmet.

The concussive force of the enchanted hammer made us both stagger and her helmet cracked.

Again I swung with a scream and the crack widened, fragments of her helmet falling to the floor.

She spun with the force of the blow, her one bloodshot eye staring wide through the hole in her helmet at me. The hurt and betrayal I saw in that brown depth broke my heart.

I swung again and her helmet finally shattered and fell off her head, her long brown hair tumbling down her side. She fell on all fours, struggling to breathe as her heart started to sluggishly beat again.
